# Cheeky one-paragraph summaries, one per day's reading.
#
# Format, tab-separated:   BOOK CH:FIRST-LAST<TAB>summary
#
# The verse range must be exactly one of the day splits the plan makes at its
# default settings (5 min x 180 wpm). ./build-summaries.py validates that and
# refuses to build otherwise, so a boundary can never quietly drift out of step
# with the pacing code.
#
# A chapter that splits mid-narrative ends every part but the last on episodic
# TV continuation text ("Next time on ..."). A chapter that splits mid-poem
# does not: a psalm has no cliffhanger.

EST 1:1-22	Ahasuerus rules 127 provinces, spends six months showing off his things, then throws a week-long open bar for the capital. Vashti hosts the women. On day seven the king, thoroughly marinated, sends seven eunuchs to fetch her in the crown so the nobles can admire her looks. She declines. The king's legal team panics that wives everywhere will get ideas, so an unrepealable law goes out in every language: Vashti is fired, and every man is officially the boss of his own house.
EST 2:1-23	Single again, the king lets his staff run an empire-wide replacement search. Every eligible young woman is rounded up for a twelve-month spa protocol in exchange for one night. Mordecai, an exile raising his orphaned cousin Esther, tells her to keep the Jewish part quiet. Esther asks the eunuch in charge what to wear and wears that. Crown, feast, tax holiday. Then she passes up his tip about two doorkeepers plotting murder; they hang, it goes in the royal diary, and nobody thanks him.
EST 3:1-15	Haman gets promoted and everyone at the gate has to bow. Mordecai won't. The staff nag him until he admits he's Jewish, then run to Haman. Haman decides one man is beneath his standing and goes for the whole ethnic group. He rolls dice for a date and sells the king a vague story about a people with inconvenient laws, plus ten thousand talents of silver. The signet ring changes hands with no follow-up question, and the two sit down for a drink while Susa is left perplexed.
EST 4:1-17	Mordecai puts on sackcloth and ashes and wails through town. Esther sends a change of clothes, which he declines, so the whole crisis is conducted by courier: here is the decree, go beg the king. She notes that walking in uninvited is a capital offence and the king hasn't sent for her in a month. Mordecai's reply is not comforting: you won't survive this either, and maybe this is the entire point of you. Three days of fasting, and "if I perish, I perish".
EST 5:1-14	Day three, Esther stands in the one courtyard where standing is a capital offence. The king holds out the scepter and offers her half the kingdom. She asks for a dinner party. With Haman. At dinner she asks for another dinner tomorrow. Haman floats home delighted, spots Mordecai still not standing up for him, and brags about his money and his invitations before admitting none of it counts while one man sits at a gate. His wife suggests a seventy-five foot gallows.
EST 6:1-14	King can't sleep, has the royal minutes read to him, and learns he never thanked Mordecai for saving his life. Haman arrives to request a hanging and is asked instead how to honour a man the king likes. Assuming this is himself, he describes the most extravagant parade, and is told to do it all for Mordecai. He spends the day leading the horse of the man he built a gallows for, then goes home and his own wife tells him he is finished.
EST 7:1-10	Dinner, round two. Esther finally names the plot and the man behind it, and the king storms out to the garden to have feelings about it. Haman throws himself on Esther's couch to beg, the king walks back in, sees a man on his wife's furniture, and that settles it. A helpful servant mentions there's a seventy-five foot gallows already standing in the yard. Haman gets to try it out.
EST 8:1-17	Esther inherits Haman's estate, Mordecai gets the signet ring, and everyone discovers Persian law has no undo button. So instead of cancelling the genocide they write a second decree letting the targets fight back. Mordecai walks out in royal blue and white, Susa throws a party, and a great many locals decide this is a fine week to become Jewish.
EST 9:1-17	The appointed day arrives and goes very badly for everyone who showed up planning a massacre: five hundred dead in Susa, Haman's ten sons among them, and nobody touches the loot, which the text is very keen you notice. The king asks Esther whether she wants anything else, which by now is a dangerous question, and she asks for one more day. Seventy-five thousand later they stop and have lunch. Next time on Esther: the paperwork.
EST 9:18-32	Mordecai writes it all down and invents a holiday. Purim, named after the dice Haman rolled to pick the date, observed with feasting, presents, and food for the poor. Ratified by letter, confirmed by a second letter, and entered into the book, because nothing says "we survived" like getting it properly minuted.
EST 10:1-3	The king celebrates by raising taxes. Mordecai ends up second in the empire and genuinely well liked, and the narrator points you at the official chronicles for the details, which is ancient for "see the extended edition".
GEN 1:1-31	God starts with a formless nothing and a wind on the water, says "light" and gets light, then spends six days separating things from other things: sky from sea, land from sea, plants, sun and moon, and "he also made the stars" dropped in like an afterthought. Then fish, birds, livestock, and finally humans, made in the divine image and handed the deed to everything that moves. The whole planet goes plant-based. God files the work as very good, the last performance review anyone passes for a while.
GEN 2:1-25	God knocks off on day seven and makes the day off holy. Then the camera pulls in: a man shaped out of dirt and started with mouth-to-nose, a garden planted in the east, plus a detour about four rivers and where the good gold is. One tree is off-limits, penalty death. God decides solitude is the first thing that isn't good, parades every animal by to be named, and none work out. So: anaesthetic, one rib, and a woman. The man's reaction is the oldest poem on record.
GEN 3:1-24	A talking snake opens with did God really say, and works it until the fruit looks nutritious, attractive and educational. They eat, discover they are naked, and invent clothing out of shrubbery. God comes walking in the cool of the day and asks where they are, which is not a geography question. The man blames the woman and, in the same sentence, God for supplying her. The woman blames the snake. The snake says nothing. Everyone is cursed, given leather clothes anyway, and evicted.
GEN 4:1-26	Two brothers, two offerings, one accepted. Cain takes the feedback badly, is warned that sin is crouching at the door, and invites his brother into the field anyway. Asked where Abel is, he tries am I my brother's keeper, history's first alibi. He is exiled and gets a protective mark anyway. His descendants invent tents, music and metalwork, and one writes his two wives a song about killing a young man for bruising him. Adam and Eve have Seth, and people start saying God's name out loud.
GEN 5:1-32	A genealogy, and a punishing one: ten generations from Adam to Noah, each entry the same three facts, had a son at X, lived another Y, died. Everyone is impossibly old. Two names break the rhythm. Enoch walks with God for three hundred years and then simply isn't there anymore, no death recorded. And Methuselah, at nine hundred sixty-nine, takes the record and does nothing else with it. Lamech names his son Noah hoping he'll be a relief from the hard work.
GEN 6:1-22	Divine beings marry human women, giants turn up, and God caps the human lifespan at a hundred and twenty, all inside four verses with no explanation offered, ever. Humanity's every thought is evil, God is sorry he made any of it, and calls for a reset. Noah is the exception. He gets blueprints: gopher wood, three decks, tarred inside and out, one door, one window, and his family plus two of everything that breathes. Noah says not a word in this chapter. He just builds it.
GEN 7:1-24	Boarding call: seven pairs of the clean animals, two of everything else, seven days' notice, and the rain arrives exactly on schedule. The deep bursts open from below while the sky opens from above, which is day two of creation being undone from both directions at once. God shuts the door personally, a detail worth noticing. Forty days of rain, water over the mountaintops, and everything breathing outside that boat is gone. The flood sits there for a hundred and fifty days.
GEN 8:1-22	God remembers Noah, which the text mentions in a way suggesting it was briefly in doubt, and sends a wind. The water drops, the boat parks on Ararat, and Noah opens the world's first weather bureau: a raven that is useless, a dove that comes back with nothing, a dove that comes back with an olive leaf, and a dove that decides it has better places to be. Noah's first act on dry land is to barbecue a selection of the animals he spent a year saving.
GEN 9:1-29	Reboot terms: be fruitful, animals are now afraid of you and also on the menu, minus the blood, and murder is capital, since people are made in God's image. Then the rainbow, a knot in the divine handkerchief so God remembers his own promise. Then Noah, the one righteous man on earth, plants a vineyard, gets drunk, and passes out naked in his tent. Ham sees and tells his brothers, who reverse in with a blanket. Noah wakes and curses Ham's son, who wasn't even there.
GEN 10:1-32	The table of nations: seventy-odd names sorted under Japheth, Ham and Shem, functioning as a map of the known world with every people filed under whoever they came from. Most of them you will never hear from again. Exactly one gets a story: Nimrod, a mighty hunter before Yahweh, who founds Babel and Nineveh and ends up as a proverb. Everyone else is a country wearing a person's name.
GEN 11:1-32	Everyone speaks one language and decides to build a tower to the sky to make a name for themselves, specifically so they won't get scattered. God comes down to have a look and makes everybody unintelligible to everybody else. Then another genealogy, Shem down to Terah, with the lifespans quietly collapsing from five hundred years to two hundred. It lands on Abram and his wife Sarai, who has no children. Terah packs the family for Canaan, gets as far as Haran, and stops.
GEN 12:1-20	God tells a seventy-five-year-old to leave his country and his father's house, and promises him a great nation, which is bold given his wife can't have children. Abram goes. A famine sends him to Egypt, where he looks at his beautiful wife and calculates that "she's my sister" will keep him alive and comfortable. Sarai is taken into Pharaoh's house, Abram collects livestock, and God has to plague an innocent Pharaoh to sort it out. Pharaoh's telling-off is justified, and Abram has no reply.
GEN 13:1-18	Abram comes back from Egypt considerably richer than he left, and he and Lot now have too many animals to share a postcode. Rather than fight, Abram offers his nephew first pick of the land. Lot takes the well-watered Jordan plain, and the narrator immediately adds "this was before Yahweh destroyed Sodom and Gomorrah", the literary equivalent of ominous music. Lot pitches his tent toward Sodom. Abram, left with the leftovers, is told to look in every direction, because all of it is his.
GEN 14:1-24	A war of four kings against five breaks out over unpaid tribute, the losing side falls into tar pits while fleeing, and the winners carry off Sodom's goods and, incidentally, Lot. Abram arms three hundred and eighteen men of his household, attacks at night, and gets everything back. A mysterious priest-king called Melchizedek turns up with bread and wine, blesses him, collects a tenth, and vanishes from the story. Abram then refuses Sodom's plunder down to the last sandal strap.
GEN 15:1-21	God appears offering himself as a shield and a great reward, and Abram says the quiet part: I have no children and my household manager inherits everything. So God takes him outside to count the stars. He believes, and it is credited as righteousness, a sentence the Bible never stops quoting. He asks for proof and gets a strange one: halved animals, birds of prey to shoo off, a terrifying darkness, four hundred years of slavery foretold, and a smoking firepot walking between the pieces alone.
GEN 16:1-16	Ten childless years in, Sarai proposes a workaround: use my slave. Abram agrees without comment. Hagar gets pregnant, Sarai blames Abram for her own plan, and Abram says do what you like with her and leaves. Hagar runs into the desert, where an angel finds her at a spring, sends her back, and promises descendants beyond counting and a son who will be a wild donkey of a man. Hagar names God, the only person in the Bible to do it: the God who sees me.
GEN 17:1-27	Abraham is ninety-nine when God turns up handing out new names: Abram becomes Abraham, Sarai becomes Sarah, and the sign of the covenant is going to be surgical. Every male, permanently, from eight days old, staff included. Told he'll have a son at a hundred by a ninety-year-old, Abraham falls on his face laughing, then tries to redirect the promise onto Ishmael. God says no, it's Isaac, this time next year, and gives Ishmael a blessing of his own. Abraham circumcises the whole household that afternoon.
GEN 18:1-33	Three men appear at Abraham's tent in the midday heat and he mounts a full state banquet in ten minutes. Over lunch one of them mentions that Sarah will have a son next year. Sarah, eavesdropping from the tent door, laughs at the mechanics of it, then denies laughing, and is told, gently but firmly, yes you did. The visitors look toward Sodom, and God decides to tell Abraham. Then the great haggle: fifty righteous people, then forty-five, forty, thirty, twenty, ten, with Abraham apologising harder each round.
GEN 19:1-17	Two angels reach Sodom and Lot insists they stay with him. Every man in the city surrounds the house demanding to rape them. Lot steps outside and offers his two virgin daughters instead, a sentence nobody has managed to improve. The crowd turns on him and moves on the door until the angels haul him inside and blind them all. Told to collect his family and run, Lot dawdles until the angels drag four people out by the hand. Next time on Genesis: somebody looks back.
GEN 19:18-38	Told to run to the mountains, Lot negotiates. He would prefer a nearby town, it's only a little one, and he gets his way, which is how Zoar gets its name. Then sulfur and fire, the plain flattened, and Lot's wife turns around and becomes a pillar of salt. Abraham gets up early and sees smoke rising like a furnace. Lot then gets frightened of Zoar and moves into a cave, where his two daughters get him drunk two nights running. The sons are Moab and Ben-Ammi.
GEN 20:1-18	Abraham moves to Gerar and runs the sister routine again, unchanged, on a new king. Abimelech takes Sarah, then God turns up in his dream with the opener you are a dead man. Abimelech, who hasn't touched her, argues his case and God concedes it entirely, admitting he is the one who kept him away. Abimelech then delivers the second righteous pagan lecture of Abraham's career, and Abraham's defence is that she is technically his half-sister. He walks away with livestock, servants and a thousand pieces of silver.
GEN 21:1-34	Isaac is born and Sarah says God has given her a laugh. She then sees Ishmael being a teenager at the weaning party and demands he and his mother be thrown out. God backs Sarah. Hagar goes into the desert with one skin of water, and when it runs out she puts the boy under a bush and sits a bowshot away so she won't watch him die. God shows her a well. Then a change of subject: Abimelech, a treaty, and seven ewe lambs at Beersheba.
GEN 22:1-24	God tells Abraham to take his son, his only son, and offer him as a burnt offering. Abraham gets up early and saddles the donkey, which is either faith or shock. The worst conversation happens on the way up: Isaac notices they have fire and wood but no lamb, and Abraham says God will provide one. He ties up his son and has the knife in the air before an angel shouts twice. There is a ram in a thicket. He names the place Yahweh Will Provide.
GEN 23:1-20	Sarah dies at a hundred and twenty-seven, and Abraham, who owns none of the land he was promised, has to buy a grave in it. What follows is a negotiation conducted entirely in compliments: the Hittites insist he take their best tomb, Ephron insists on giving him the field free, Abraham insists on paying, Ephron mentions in passing that the land is worth four hundred shekels, and Abraham weighs it out on the spot. The first square foot of promised land the family owns is a grave.
GEN 24:1-32	Abraham makes his senior servant swear an oath with his hand in an anatomically memorable position: find Isaac a wife from the old country, and do not take my son back there. The servant loads ten camels and goes. At the well he invents a test, the woman who offers to water the camels too is the one, a large ask given a thirsty camel drinks like a bathtub. Rebekah arrives before he finishes praying and waters every camel. Next time on Genesis: the servant makes a speech.
GEN 24:33-67	Dinner is served and the servant refuses to eat until he has told the whole story, which he does at length, repeating the last thirty verses back to the family almost word for word. Laban and Bethuel concede that God arranged it. More gifts appear. The family asks for ten days, the servant says no, so for the first time somebody asks Rebekah, and she says "I will go". She rides out, sees a man walking in a field at evening, and veils herself. That is Isaac.
1JN 1:1-10	John opens without a greeting, name or address, just a pile of clauses about something heard, seen and handled from the beginning. The message: God is light with no darkness in him at all. What follows is a set of if-statements: claim fellowship while walking in darkness and you are lying, claim to have no sin and you are deceiving yourself, claim you have never sinned and you have made God a liar. The one door left open is confession, and God is faithful enough to handle it.
1JN 2:1-29	The point of writing, he says, is that you would not sin, but if you do there is a Counselor. The test of knowing God is keeping his commandments, old and new at once. Anyone who hates his brother is walking in the dark. A poetic aside follows, addressed to children, fathers and young men in turn. Do not love the world, it is passing. It is the final hour, antichrists have already appeared, and the giveaway is that they left: never having belonged.
1JN 3:1-24	Look at the love, he says, that we should be called children of God, and then he admits nobody knows yet what that turns into. Sin is lawlessness. God's children and the devil's are told apart by two things: doing what is right, and loving your brother. Cain is the counterexample; John asks why he killed him, then answers his own question. Hating your brother is filed under murder. Then the practical version: seeing someone in need, having the means, and shutting your heart. Love in deed, not in vocabulary.
1JN 4:1-21	Do not believe every spirit, test them: does it confess that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh. Then the chapter becomes the one everybody quotes. God is love, and the evidence offered is not a feeling but a sending. Nobody has ever seen God, so the visible proof is people loving each other. Perfect love throws fear out, since fear has to do with punishment. The closing argument is blunt: saying you love God while hating your brother is a lie.
1JN 5:1-21	Believing and loving are welded together here: love the Father and you love his children by definition. His commandments, he adds, are not burdensome. Faith is what overcomes the world. Three witnesses agree, the Spirit and the water and the blood, and God's testimony outranks anyone's. The purpose statement arrives near the end: this was written so you may know you have eternal life. Then a genuinely puzzling passage about a sin leading to death, and a last line, keep yourselves from idols, with no follow-up.
1PE 1:1-25	Peter writes to people scattered across five provinces and calls them foreigners, setting the tone for the letter. An inheritance is kept in reserve, and meanwhile grief in various trials, compared to fire testing gold, since gold perishes and faith does not. The prophets, he says, were researching this for somebody else's benefit, and angels are craning their necks to see it. So be holy, and remember what you were bought with, which was not silver. Then Isaiah: all flesh is grass, the flower falls, the word stands.
1PE 2:1-25	Drop the malice and gossip, want the pure milk of the word the way a newborn does. Then a building metaphor: Christ is the living stone the builders rejected, and readers are stones stacked into a house, staffed by themselves as the priesthood. Same stone, two outcomes: a cornerstone for some, a thing to trip over for others. They are a chosen race and also resident foreigners, so behave well enough that accusations do not stick. Servants are told to endure even harsh masters, with Christ as the example.
1PE 3:1-22	Household advice: wives who might win an unbelieving husband without a word being said, and a preference for a gentle spirit over braided hair and gold. Husbands get one verse with a penalty attached: honor your wife as a joint heir, or your prayers are obstructed. Everyone else is told not to return insult for insult, and to always give an answer for the hope in them, with humility. Then the strange part: Christ preaching to spirits in prison, Noah's eight, and baptism.
1PE 4:1-19	You have spent enough time doing what the Gentiles wanted, he says, and lists it in some detail. Your old friends now find it strange that you will not join in, and take it personally. The end of all things is near, so be sober enough to pray, be earnest in love, and be hospitable without grumbling. Do not be astonished at the fiery trial. Do not suffer as a murderer, a thief, or a meddler, a list where one item does not belong.
1PE 5:1-14	A word to elders from a fellow elder: shepherd the flock because you want to, not for dishonest gain, and do not lord it over anyone. The younger defer, and everybody puts humility on like clothing, since God resists the proud. Throw your worries at him, because he cares. Stay watchful: the devil is prowling like a roaring lion looking for someone to eat, and the comfort offered is that believers everywhere are getting the same treatment. Then the greetings, from Silvanus, Babylon, and Mark, and a kiss.
2PE 1:1-21	Peter starts with a ladder: faith, then moral excellence, then knowledge, self-control, perseverance, godliness, brotherly affection, love. Lacking them makes you shortsighted and forgetful that you were ever cleaned up. He explains, in advance, why he keeps repeating himself: he knows he is putting off this tent shortly, and wants it to stick after he has gone. No, he says, we were not passing on cunningly devised fables. We heard the voice on the holy mountain ourselves. Prophecy is not somebody's private invention.
2PE 2:1-22	An entire chapter of invective against false teachers, and Peter is clearly enjoying himself. Three precedents get stacked up: angels dropped into Tartarus, the flood that spared Noah and seven others, and Sodom and Gomorrah reduced to ash with Lot pulled out worn down. God knows how to rescue and how to hold the rest for judgment. Then the abuse proper: unreasoning animals, revelling in daylight, following Balaam, corrected by his own donkey. Wells without water. Promising liberty while owned themselves. It ends with a dog and its vomit.
2PE 3:1-18	Mockers will show up asking where this promised coming has got to, since everything carries on as it always has. Peter's answer: they are willfully forgetting the flood. The delay is not slowness, it is patience: one day is a thousand years and the reverse, and he does not want anyone to perish. The day will arrive like a thief, elements dissolving in heat, followed by new heavens and a new earth. Then a footnote about Paul, whose letters contain things hard to understand and get twisted.
BEL 1:1-20	Cyrus wants to know why Daniel will not worship Bel, given how much the idol eats: twelve measures of flour, forty sheep and six firkins of wine a day. Daniel laughs and says it is clay within and brass without and has never eaten anything. Seventy priests stake their lives on it, the king seals the temple with his own signet, and Daniel has the floor strewn with ashes first. That night the priests come in through the trapdoor and eat the lot. Next time on Bel: the floor.
BEL 1:21-42	Footprints in the ash, of men and women and children. The priests show the king their private entrance, he has them killed, and Daniel demolishes Bel and his temple. The king produces a dragon that indisputably eats, and Daniel kills it without sword or staff, using boiled pitch, fat and hair. Babylon riots and the king caves, and Daniel spends six days in a den of seven lions deliberately kept hungry. An angel carries Habakkuk over by the hair with dinner. On the seventh day the accusers go in instead.
GEN 25:1-34	Abraham remarries, fathers six more sons, gives everything to Isaac and pays the rest off to move east, then dies at a hundred and seventy-five. Isaac and Ishmael bury him together. Ishmael's twelve princes are listed and he dies at a hundred and thirty-seven. Rebekah's twins fight before they are born, and the younger arrives gripping the elder's heel. Esau, back from the field and dramatic about starvation, trades his birthright for lentil stew and goes on his way, having eaten.
GEN 26:1-35	Famine, and Isaac heads to Gerar with his father's old routine: she is my sister. It survives until Abimelech looks out of a window and sees him being affectionate with his sister. Isaac reaps a hundredfold and gets rich enough that the Philistines fill in his wells out of spite, so he digs them again with his father's names, argues over two more, and reaches Rehoboth, which nobody contests. Then Abimelech arrives wanting a treaty with the man he expelled. Esau marries two Hittite women.
GEN 27:1-26	Isaac is old and blind and wants venison before he blesses his firstborn. Rebekah has the plot assembled before Esau is out of sight: goat meat cooked to taste, Esau's clothes and goat skins on Jacob. Jacob worries only about getting caught, and his mother volunteers to take the curse. He walks in and credits God with the speed of the hunt. Isaac hears the wrong voice, feels the right hands, and goes with the hands. Next time on Genesis: the other son comes home with dinner.
GEN 27:27-46	The kiss, the smell of the borrowed clothes, and the blessing goes out: dew, grain, wine, lordship over his brothers. Esau walks in with the real venison a minute later. Isaac trembles violently and confirms the blessing anyway. Esau cries bitterly and notes that his brother's name has now been earned twice. He gets a leftover blessing about swords and yokes, and starts planning a funeral followed by a murder. Rebekah sends Jacob to Haran and tells Isaac it is about the local wives.
GEN 28:1-22	Isaac sends Jacob to Paddan Aram to marry inside the family. Esau, working out at last that Canaanite wives are the problem, fixes it by adding a third wife from Ishmael's line. Jacob camps overnight with a rock for a pillow and dreams a stairway with traffic on it and God at the top repeating the family promise. He names the place Bethel and stands the pillow up as a monument. His vow is impressively conditional: food, clothing, a safe return home, and then God gets a tenth.
GEN 29:1-35	Jacob reaches a well, rolls the stone off single-handed, kisses Rachel and bursts into tears. He offers Laban seven years of labour for her, and they pass like a few days. At the wedding Laban substitutes Leah, and Jacob only discovers it in the morning: the man who impersonated his brother is out-conned by his uncle, who explains that the younger simply does not go first here. Another seven years. Leah, unloved, bears Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah.
GEN 30:1-25	Rachel, childless, demands children of Jacob, who points out that he is not God. So the sons arrive by proxy: Bilhah bears Dan and Naphtali, Zilpah bears Gad and Asher. Reuben brings his mother mandrakes, and Rachel buys them with a night of her own husband, who learns of the transaction as he comes in from work. Leah gets Issachar, Zebulun and Dinah. Then God remembers Rachel and Joseph is born. Next time on Genesis: a discussion about wages.
GEN 30:26-43	Laban, who has divined that his prosperity is entirely borrowed, does not want Jacob leaving and asks him to name his price. Jacob asks only for the speckled, spotted and black animals, which Laban agrees to and then removes from the flock and parks three days away. Jacob answers with peeled poplar rods propped in the watering troughs, a breeding theory the text reports without comment. He ends up with flocks, servants, camels and donkeys.
GEN 31:1-29	Laban's sons start saying out loud that Jacob has taken everything, and Laban's face stops being friendly. Jacob calls his wives out to the field for a business meeting: ten changes of wages, a dream, the God of Bethel. Rachel and Leah agree, on the grounds that their father sold them and spent the money. So they go, Rachel quietly taking his household gods. Laban chases them for seven days and is warned in a dream to say nothing. Next time on Genesis: somebody searches the luggage.
GEN 31:30-55	Laban's real grievance surfaces: someone has taken his gods. Jacob, who has no idea, cheerfully offers the death penalty for whoever has them, and Laban ransacks every tent while Rachel sits on the idols in a camel saddle and apologises for not standing up. Nothing is found, so Jacob lets twenty years out at once: fourteen for the daughters, six for the flock, the drought and the frost and the lost sleep. They pile up stones, name the heap in two languages, and part.
GEN 32:1-32	Angels meet Jacob and he names the place God's army, then hears that Esau is coming with four hundred men and splits his camp in half on the logic that one half might survive. He prays, then hedges the prayer with an enormous staggered gift of goats, ewes, camels, cows and donkeys, so Esau meets the apology several times before he meets the man. Alone at the ford, Jacob wrestles someone until dawn and limps into the sunrise with a new name.
GEN 33:1-20	After all that preparation, Esau runs up, hugs him, and they both cry. Jacob has still arranged his household in ascending order of how much he would mind losing them, with Rachel and Joseph safely last, and bows seven times on the way in. Esau tries to refuse the gift and is talked into taking it, then offers company for the road. Jacob pleads the pace of children and nursing livestock, promises to follow him to Seir, and goes the other way.
GEN 34:1-31	Shechem rapes Dinah and then asks his father to arrange the marriage, as though that settles it. Jacob says nothing until his sons come in from the fields. They answer with deceit: we will intermarry, but every male in your city must be circumcised first. Hamor sells it to his townsmen as a business opportunity. On the third day, while the men are still in pain, Simeon and Levi kill every one of them and take Dinah out of Shechem's house.
GEN 35:1-29	God tells Jacob to go back to Bethel, so he collects the household's foreign gods and earrings and buries them under a tree. The altar goes up, and God renames him Israel again, in case the first time did not take. Then the losses come one after another: Deborah, Rebekah's nurse, and then Rachel, who dies in childbirth naming the boy Benoni before his father renames him Benjamin. Reuben sleeps with his father's concubine. Isaac dies at a hundred and eighty.
GEN 36:1-43	This is the Edom chapter, and it is a list. Esau's three wives, his five sons, and then the chiefs, who are largely the same names again with a title attached. The Horites of Seir get a roster of their own, including Anah, remembered forever for finding hot springs while pasturing his father's donkeys. Then eight kings of Edom, then eleven more chiefs. The only actual news is at the top: Esau moved away because two brothers with that much livestock could not share a country.
GEN 37:1-36	Joseph is seventeen, brings his father a bad report about his brothers, and is dressed in a way that settles any question about who the favourite is. He also tells them both of his dreams, in which they bow to him, and repeats the second to his father. They see him coming across the field and decide the dreamer is best discussed in a pit. Reuben talks them down to abandonment, Judah improves the plan into a sale, and Joseph goes to Egypt.
GEN 38:1-30	Judah leaves his brothers, marries, and has three sons. Two of them die on the page, and rather than risk the third he sends Tamar home to be a widow indefinitely. So Tamar veils herself, sits by the road to Timnah, and takes his signet, his cord and his staff as security for a payment that never arrives. Three months later Judah hears she is pregnant and orders her burned, until she sends his own belongings back. He concedes that she is more righteous than he is.
GEN 39:1-23	Potiphar buys Joseph and eventually stops supervising anything except his own dinner, because everything Joseph touches prospers. The narrator then mentions, immediately before the next scene, that Joseph is well built and handsome. His master's wife makes the same request daily and is refused daily, on grounds of trust and of God. She finally gets hold of his garment and he runs out of the house leaving the evidence in her hand. He lands in prison, where the warden promptly stops supervising anything too.
GEN 40:1-23	Pharaoh's cup bearer and baker offend him and land in the same prison, where Joseph is assigned to look after them. Both dream on the same night, and neither has anyone to explain it. The vine with three branches means three days and a restored job, and Joseph asks, reasonably, to be mentioned upstairs. The baker, encouraged by how well that went, offers his three baskets and the birds, and is told it also means three days, in a much worse way.
GEN 41:1-30	Two years later Pharaoh dreams of seven fat cattle eaten by seven thin ones, then seven good heads of grain swallowed by seven withered ones, and every professional in Egypt draws a blank. The cup bearer chooses this moment to remember his faults. Joseph is hauled out of the dungeon, shaved and re-dressed, and opens by saying the skill is not his. Pharaoh retells both dreams, adding that he has never seen cattle that ugly. Next time on Genesis: the interpretation keeps going.
GEN 41:31-57	Joseph finishes interpreting and then, entirely unasked, adds a national policy: find a discreet and wise man, appoint overseers, take a fifth of everything for seven years. Pharaoh looks around the room for such a man and hires the consultant. Signet ring, fine linen, gold chain, the second chariot, an Egyptian name and a priest's daughter, all in one afternoon, and Joseph is thirty. When the famine arrives, Pharaoh's only instruction to a starving country is to go and ask Joseph.
GEN 42:1-21	The famine reaches Canaan and Jacob asks his sons why they are standing about looking at one another. Ten go to Egypt, Benjamin kept home in case of accidents, and they bow to the governor without recognising him. Joseph knows them at once, speaks roughly, and accuses them of espionage, which conveniently extracts a full family inventory: twelve brothers, the youngest at home, and one who is no more. He holds them three days, then proposes terms involving a hostage. Next time on Genesis: the sacks get opened.
GEN 42:22-38	Reuben takes the opportunity to say he told them so. They still do not know that Joseph understands every word, since he has been using an interpreter, and he steps out to weep before coming back to bind Simeon in front of them. Then he has their money secretly returned in their sacks, which turns a mercy into a terror the moment one of them finds it. Reuben offers his own two sons as a guarantee, a proposal his father does not address.
GEN 43:1-34	The food runs out and Jacob tells his sons to go and buy a little more, as though nothing else were attached to the request. Judah lays out the terms and puts himself up as collateral, and Israel gives in, sending balm, honey, spices, nuts and double the money in case the first lot was an oversight. In Egypt they assume they are being arrested, but the steward produces Simeon. Joseph sees Benjamin and has to leave the room.
GEN 44:1-34	Joseph has his silver cup planted in Benjamin's sack, along with everyone's money again, then sends the steward after them with an accusation. The brothers are so certain of their innocence that they volunteer a death sentence, which the steward moderates to enslaving whoever holds it. The sacks are searched oldest to youngest, stretching the moment out on purpose, and the cup is in the youngest. Judah, who once sold a brother for silver, offers to be the slave in his place.
GEN 45:1-28	Joseph clears the room and gives up. He weeps loudly enough for Pharaoh's household to hear, announces who he is, and asks whether his father is alive, which his brothers are too terrified to answer. He does the theology for them: you sold me, but God sent me, and five years of famine are still to come. Pharaoh sends wagons. Joseph loads them with provisions and sends them off with the only instruction that matters: do not quarrel on the road.
GEN 46:1-34	Israel stops at Beersheba to sacrifice and is told not to fear Egypt, in a promise that ends on the detail that Joseph will close his eyes. Then the census, sorted by mother: thirty-three, sixteen, fourteen and seven, seventy souls in all. Judah is sent ahead to find the road into Goshen. Joseph drives out in his chariot and weeps on his father's neck a good while, and Jacob says he can die now. Then Joseph coaches the family: say livestock, because Egyptians cannot abide shepherds.
GEN 47:1-17	Joseph presents five of his brothers to Pharaoh, who asks their occupation, gets the rehearsed answer, and hands over the best of the land plus a job offer for anyone competent. Then Jacob is brought in and blesses Pharaoh, twice, and when asked his age gives one hundred and thirty and calls the years few and evil, which is a remarkable thing to tell a head of state. Joseph gathers every coin in Egypt and Canaan into Pharaoh's house. Next time on Genesis: the money runs out.
GEN 47:18-31	Year two of the money-free economy: the Egyptians come back with nothing left but their bodies and their fields, and propose the sale themselves. Joseph buys the whole country for Pharaoh, moves the population into the cities, and exempts only the priests, who were already on a royal allowance. Everyone receives seed and a permanent fifth to Pharaoh, and thanks him for saving their lives. Jacob lives seventeen years in Egypt, reaching a hundred and forty-seven, and makes Joseph swear not to bury him there.
GEN 48:1-22	Jacob is dying and strengthens himself enough to sit up for the occasion. He adopts Joseph's two Egyptian-born sons outright, so Ephraim and Manasseh now count like Reuben and Simeon, then asks who the boys standing there are, because his eyes have gone. Joseph carefully arranges them so the firstborn falls under the right hand. Jacob crosses his arms anyway. Joseph tries to correct the mistake and gets I know, my son, I know.
GEN 49:1-33	Jacob gathers his twelve sons and delivers twelve verdicts dressed as blessings. Reuben loses his standing over his father's bed. Simeon and Levi are cursed for their anger and told they will be scattered. Judah gets the lion and the scepter, Zebulun the harbours, Issachar a strong donkey lying down under a load, Dan a snake biting a horse's heels, Benjamin a ravenous wolf, and Joseph the longest and warmest passage by a distance. Then he gives exact burial instructions, draws his feet into the bed, and dies.
GEN 50:1-26	Joseph has his father embalmed, which takes forty days, and Egypt mourns for seventy. The convoy that goes up to Canaan carries chariots, horsemen and every elder in Egypt, and the Canaanites watching it name the place after the Egyptians. Once Jacob is buried, the brothers panic and send Joseph a message reporting a deathbed instruction from their father to forgive them. Joseph weeps, tells them he is not in the place of God, and says they meant evil while God meant good.
JAS 1:1-27	James opens with count it all joy about trials, a lot to ask in verse two. Ask God for wisdom, but without doubting, because a doubter is a wave and gets nothing. The rich fade like a flower in scorching wind. Nobody gets to blame God for their own temptation: desire conceives, sin is born, death follows. Quick to hear, slow to speak, slow to anger. A hearer who is not a doer checks the mirror and instantly forgets his own face. Real religion: widows, orphans, staying unstained.
JAS 2:1-26	Picture it: the man with the gold ring gets the good seat, the man in filthy clothes gets told to stand by the footstool, and James asks what that makes you. God chose the poor to be rich in faith, and it is the rich who drag you into court. Break the law once and you have broken it all. Telling a cold, hungry person to be warmed and filled while handing them nothing is faith without works. Demons believe God is one, and shudder. Abraham and Rahab acted.
JAS 3:1-18	A warning to aspiring teachers that they will be judged more strictly, then three pictures of a tiny thing steering a large one: a bit in a horse's mouth, a rudder on a ship, a spark in a forest. The tongue is the spark. Humanity has tamed every animal and cannot tame this one item. The same mouth blesses God and curses people made in his image, absurd, like a spring running fresh and bitter at once. True wisdom is gentle and merciful; the jealous, ambitious kind he calls demonic.
JAS 4:1-17	Where do the fights come from? From wanting things. You do not have because you do not ask, and when you ask you ask to spend it on yourself. Friendship with the world is declared open hostility toward God, little middle ground offered. Resist the devil, draw near to God, and, unusually, turn your laughter into mourning. Stop judging one another, since there is one lawgiver and it is not you. And stop planning a profitable year in that city: you are a vapor, and do not know tomorrow.
JAS 5:1-20	The rich are told to weep and howl, their gold corroded, the corrosion set to eat their flesh, and the withheld wages of their harvesters shouting into God's ears. Then a turn: be patient, like a farmer who cannot hurry the rain, because the judge is already at the door. Job is named for endurance. Do not swear oaths, let yes mean yes. Suffering, pray; cheerful, sing; sick, call the elders for oil. Elijah had a nature like ours, and it did not rain for three and a half years.
JDT 1:1-16	Nebuchadnezzar turns up here as king of the Assyrians reigning in Nineveh, which is not where history left him, and the narrator does not blink. Four verses go on the cubit-by-cubit dimensions of Arphaxad's walls at Ecbatana. He summons the entire west to his war, and every nation refuses and sends the messengers back in disgrace, because he seemed to them like one man. He wins anyway, runs Arphaxad through with darts in the mountains of Ragau, and goes home to banquet for a hundred and twenty days.
JDT 2:1-28	Eighteenth year, first month, twenty-second day: the king holds a meeting and decrees the destruction of everyone who ignored him. Holofernes gets a hundred and twenty thousand foot and twelve thousand horse, plus orders that the west should prepare earth and water. The logistics are itemised, and the army goes out described as locusts. Then a travelogue of arson: Put and Lud destroyed, Midian's tents burned, Damascus fired at wheat harvest. The coast is terrified, correctly.
JDT 3:1-10	The coastal cities send messengers with a surrender so total it is nearly a poem: our houses, our fields, our flocks, our sheepfolds, our cities and the people in them all lie before you, use them as you please. Holofernes accepts, garrisons the high cities, conscripts their best men, and is welcomed with garlands and dances and timbrels. Then he cuts down their sacred groves, since the assignment is to abolish every god so that all nations call on Nebuchadnezzar alone. He camps a month near Esdraelon gathering baggage.
JDT 4:1-15	Judea hears what happened to everyone else's temples and reacts as a nation newly back from exile with a barely reconsecrated sanctuary would. They occupy the hilltops, fortify the villages and store the harvest. Joakim writes to Bethulia to hold the ascents, narrow enough for two men. Then everyone puts on sackcloth: men, women, children, servants, foreigners and, worth pausing over, the cattle. Ashes on every head, and the Lord hears them.
JDT 5:1-24	Holofernes wants to know who these hill people are and why they alone have not come out to meet him. Achior the Ammonite gives him the honest answer, which turns out to be a summary of most of the Old Testament: Chaldean ancestry, Egypt, plagues, a dried sea, conquest, exile and return. His military conclusion follows from it. Find out whether they have sinned against their God, and if they have not, my lord should simply pass by. The tent immediately starts saying he should be killed for it.
JDT 6:1-21	Holofernes answers with the question the book is built around, who is God but Nebuchadnezzar, and promises mountains drunk with blood. Rather than kill Achior he sentences him to being right: tied up and dumped at the foot of Bethulia to die with the people he defended. Israelite slingers drive the escort off, the townspeople untie him and bring him in, and he repeats to the assembly exactly what was said in the war council. They fall down and pray, praise him exceedingly, and give him dinner.
JDT 7:1-17	The army moves on Bethulia, now a hundred and seventy thousand foot and twelve thousand horse, and fills the valley. The defenders say these men will lick up the face of all the earth. Holofernes rides out to survey the springs. His allies advise against fighting at all: seize the water, sit still, and thirst will hand over the city without costing one man. He likes it. Next time on Judith: the cisterns run dry.
JDT 7:18-32	The siege becomes arithmetic. Thirty-four days, cisterns emptied, drink rationed by measure, children discouraged, women and young men fainting in the streets and the gateways. The whole population turns on Ozias and the elders with a case that is hard to answer: you should have made peace, slavery is survivable, watching our children die is not. Ozias asks for five more days on the grounds that God will not forsake them utterly, and promises to open the gates if nothing comes. Then he sends everyone back to the walls.
JDT 8:1-17	Enter Judith, with a sixteen-name genealogy, a husband who died of sunstroke standing over the harvest crew, three years and four months of widowhood, and a fasting schedule with careful exemptions for sabbaths and feast days. She is rich, beautiful, and nobody has a bad word for her. Hearing about the five-day ultimatum, she tells the elders that putting God on a deadline is not faith but a test. Next time on Judith: she is not finished.
JDT 8:18-36	She keeps going. No one in this generation worships idols, unlike the fathers who fell before their enemies for it. If Bethulia falls the sanctuary falls with it, and the blood will be required of them. And this is testing, the kind God does to the people he keeps close, not vengeance. Ozias concedes every word, then explains that the people were thirsty and made him swear. So Judith stops arguing: stand at the gate tonight, I am going out with my maid, and do not ask what I intend.
JDT 9:1-14	Judith prays face down in ashes, timed to the hour the evening incense goes up in Jerusalem. She opens on her ancestor Simeon and the sword given to avenge a violated sister, then works through God's foreknowledge and the Assyrians' trust in horse and spear. The requests are specific: break their strength, strike the prince with his servant, and bring down their pride by the hand of a woman. She asks that her deceit be the weapon.
JDT 10:1-23	The transformation is inventoried item by item: sackcloth off, washed, anointed, hair braided, tiara, the good clothes from her married life, all to deceive the eyes of every man who sees her. Her maid packs food she can lawfully eat. The elders are struck dumb. The Assyrian patrol takes her, swallows her story about betraying the hill country, and escorts her in with a hundred men while the camp turns out to stare.
JDT 11:1-23	Holofernes opens gallantly, explaining that the war is Israel's own fault for slighting him. Judith delivers a masterpiece with a false bottom: she flatters the king and the general, endorses Achior's theology, and reports that the starving city is about to eat the consecrated food and forfeit its protection. Every sentence is technically defensible and the whole thing is a lie. The room is enchanted.
JDT 12:1-20	Offered the general's own food, Judith declines and eats what she brought, which establishes the routine that lets her leave camp each night to bathe and pray. On the fourth day Holofernes throws a private party and sends the eunuch Bagoas to fetch her, reasoning that letting such a woman go untouched would make them a laughing stock. She agrees at once, calling it her joy to the day of her death.
JDT 13:1-20	Everyone clears out and Holofernes is unconscious. Judith takes his own scimitar off the bedrail, prays for strength, and strikes his neck twice. The head goes into the food bag, and the two women walk out through the camp on the nightly prayer walk nobody questions any more. At Bethulia's gate she wakes the town and states plainly what happened: he never touched her. Ozias blesses her above all women on earth.
JDT 14:1-19	Judith issues the tactical orders: hang the head on the battlement, arm at dawn, march down as if to attack, and do not actually attack. The enemy will run to Holofernes' tent, find nothing, and panic. First she has Achior brought in. He sees the head in a man's hand, faints, and on being revived converts and joins Israel. At sunrise the plan runs exactly as written. Bagoas knocks discreetly, assuming his lord is occupied, then goes in and finds the body on the threshold with the head gone.
JDT 15:1-13	The panic works. The camp empties, every fighter in Bethulia comes down after them, and Jerusalem, the hill country, Gilead and Galilee chase them past Damascus. Plundering the camp takes thirty days and enriches everyone. Joakim comes up from Jerusalem to call Judith the exaltation of Jerusalem and the great glory of Israel. They give her Holofernes' entire tent, which she loads onto a mule, and she leads the women in a dance.
JDT 16:1-25	Judith's song makes the point plainly. No young man, no son of the Titans, no giant brought their champion down: the daughter of Merari did, with her face. Her sandal ravished his eye, and the scimitar concluded matters. Then thanksgiving in Jerusalem, three months of feasting, and Holofernes' canopy dedicated to the Lord. She refuses every suitor for the rest of her life, frees her maid, and dies at a hundred and five.
LEV 1:1-17	The book opens with God speaking from a tent about how to burn a bull correctly. The animal must be male and without defect, the offerer lays a hand on its head, and then everything is specified: flay it, joint it, wash the legs and innards, stack the wood just so. The poor bring birds, whose heads are wrung off and whose crops are thrown east onto the ash heap. Three times the phrase 'a pleasant aroma to Yahweh' arrives like a chorus.
LEV 2:1-16	Now the grain offering, which is fine flour, oil and frankincense, and comes in oven, griddle and pan variants for reasons never explained. The priest burns a token handful and keeps the entire rest, an arrangement worth noticing. Yeast and honey are banned from the fire; salt is compulsory, called the salt of the covenant, and required with every offering you bring. First fruits go in as roasted grain still in the ear.
LEV 3:1-17	Peace offerings, and here for once the animal may be male or female, which counts as flexibility. What matters is the fat: the fat over the innards, the two kidneys, the cover on the liver, and with a sheep the entire tail cut close to the backbone. The same anatomical list is recited three times, once for cattle, once for sheep, once for goats. It closes with a permanent rule: no fat, no blood, ever.
LEV 4:1-19	Sin here is the accidental kind, the rule you broke without noticing. If the anointed priest did it, the price is a bull: blood flicked seven times toward the veil, dabbed on the incense altar's horns, poured out at the base, and the carcass hauled outside the camp and burned. If the whole assembly sinned without realising, the elders lay hands on a second bull and it goes the same way. Next time on Leviticus: that bull, still.
LEV 4:20-35	The scale slides by rank. A ruler who sins unwittingly owes a male goat; an ordinary person owes a female goat, or a female lamb instead. The procedure barely changes, blood on the horns of the altar with a finger, the rest poured at the base, the fat burned like a peace offering. The sentence that closes each case is the one that matters: he will be forgiven. Said four times, in case anyone was reading only for the anatomy.
LEV 5:1-19	A list of ways to be guilty without meaning to: hearing the call to testify and staying quiet, touching a carcass and forgetting, swearing something rash. Confession comes first, then the offering, which scales with what you have. A lamb, or two pigeons, or a tenth of an ephah of plain flour with no oil and no frankincense on it, since it is a sin offering and not a treat. Mishandle holy things and it is a ram plus restitution plus a fifth.
LEV 6:1-30	Fraud is filed as a sin against God rather than as paperwork: return what you took, add a fifth, and do it on the day you are found guilty. Then housekeeping. The altar fire must never go out, which is stated three separate times. The priest changes clothes to carry the ashes outside the camp. Priests eat the leftovers of everyone else's grain offering, but a priest's own offering is burned entirely and never eaten. An earthen pot that boiled a sin offering is smashed; a bronze one is scoured.
LEV 7:1-19	Trespass offerings first, then a note on priestly income: the priest who handles your burnt offering keeps the hide, and whoever bakes the grain offering keeps the baked thing. Peace offerings come with an expiry date. A thanksgiving sacrifice must be eaten the day it is offered; a vow or freewill offering gets two days, and anything left on the third day is burned. Eat it on day three anyway and the whole sacrifice is void, retroactively. Next time on Leviticus: who is allowed to eat any of it.
LEV 7:20-38	Eat sacrificial meat while unclean and you are cut off from your people, which is the strongest sanction available and gets used twice on one page. Fat and blood are permanently off the menu, though fat from an animal that died on its own may be used for anything except eating. Then the priests' portion is fixed by gesture: the breast is waved, the right thigh is heaved, and both belong to Aaron's family forever. A closing line files the entire set of laws under Sinai.
LEV 8:1-21	After seven chapters of instruction, someone finally does something. Moses assembles the whole congregation to watch him dress his brother: coat, sash, robe, ephod, breastplate with the Urim and Thummim inside it, turban with a gold plate on the front. Then oil on the tabernacle, seven sprinkles on the altar, oil poured over Aaron's head. A bull dies for sin and a ram is burned whole, and the refrain 'as Yahweh commanded Moses' lands at the end of nearly every step. Next time on Leviticus: there is a second ram.
LEV 8:22-36	The second ram introduces the strangest gesture in the book: blood dabbed on the right earlobe, the right thumb and the right big toe, first of Aaron, then of each son. Cakes and fat and the right thigh are loaded into their hands and waved. Moses keeps the breast for himself, which the text notes and does not defend. Then the closing instruction: seven days and nights at the door of the tent, do not go out, so that you do not die.
LEV 9:1-24	Day eight, and Aaron works the altar himself for the first time, beginning with a sin offering on his own account before he can do anything for anyone else. Calf, ram, goat, bull, grain, in that order, all of it exactly as prescribed. Then he lifts his hands and blesses the people, Moses goes in with him and they come out together, and the glory appears. Fire comes out from Yahweh and takes the offering off the altar. The people shout and fall on their faces. Remember that fire.
LEV 10:1-20	Nadab and Abihu offer fire God did not ask for, and the fire that came out yesterday comes out again and kills them. Moses offers a theological explanation. Aaron says nothing. The bodies are carried out of the camp, and Aaron and his remaining sons are forbidden to mourn. Rules follow: no wine before entering the tent, and teach the difference between holy and common. Then Moses finds a goat burned rather than eaten and is angry, and Aaron asks whether eating it today would have pleased anyone.
LEV 11:1-28	The dietary rules run on a two-part test, chews the cud and has a split hoof, and the memorable part is the near misses: camel, cony and hare all chew but fail on feet, and the pig has the feet but does not chew. Water creatures need fins and scales. The forbidden bird list files the bat among the birds. Insects are out unless they hop, which lets locusts and grasshoppers through. Touch a carcass and you are unclean until evening. Next time on Leviticus: the lizards.
LEV 11:29-47	A roll call of eight small unclean creatures, mostly lizards, then a surprisingly careful set of rules about where one might land after it dies. Onto a wooden bowl: soak it. Into a clay pot: smash the pot, and whatever was inside it. Onto an oven: break the oven. Into a spring or cistern: the water stays clean. Onto dry seed: fine. Onto seed you had already watered: not fine. The chapter then stops listing and gives a reason instead: be holy, because I am holy.
LEV 12:1-8	Eight verses on purification after childbirth. A son costs seven days of uncleanness and thirty-three more of purification, with circumcision on the eighth day in the middle of it; a daughter doubles both numbers to fourteen and sixty-six, and the text offers no explanation for the doubling. At the end she brings a lamb and a bird, or two birds if she cannot afford the lamb. The concession is the one this book keeps making: the rule holds, the price does not have to.
LEV 13:1-30	The priest becomes a dermatologist. The criteria are specific: has the hair in the patch turned white, is it deeper than the surrounding skin, has it spread since last week. When nothing is certain, shut the person away seven days. The strangest ruling comes early: a man covered head to foot and entirely white is declared clean, and one patch of raw flesh reverses it. Burns, boils and an itch in the beard each get their own protocol. Next time on Leviticus: it gets into the laundry.
LEV 13:31-59	More diagnostics, with a shave involved: everything comes off except the patch itself. Two rulings are pure reassurance, since a dull white rash is harmless and going bald is explicitly clean, stated twice. Then the chapter turns hard. Anyone genuinely infected wears torn clothes, leaves his hair loose, covers his lip, calls out 'Unclean, unclean' and lives alone outside the camp. The remaining verses transfer the whole procedure to a piece of cloth: quarantine the garment seven days, wash it, tear out the spot, burn it if the colour returns.
LEV 14:1-25	The healed person is met outside the camp, and the rite is strange and rather beautiful: two birds, one killed over fresh water in a clay pot, the other dipped in its blood along with cedar, scarlet yarn and hyssop, then released into open country. He shaves, washes, waits seven days, then shaves everything again including his eyebrows. On the eighth day, blood on the right ear, thumb and toe, then oil on the same three places. Next time on Leviticus: that oil, again, slower.
LEV 14:26-57	The budget cleansing is written out again in full, ear and thumb and toe and oil, on the principle that being poor should not buy an abbreviated ceremony. Then mildew in a house, which God says he will personally put there. The priest orders the house emptied before he inspects it, so its contents are not condemned along with it. Seven days shut up, then pull the affected stones, scrape, replaster. If it returns, the house comes down. If it clears, one bird goes free over the city.
LEV 15:1-33	A full chapter on bodily discharges, worked out with the same unhurried thoroughness as everything else: the bed, the chair, the saddle, anyone who touches any of them, and anyone the affected person spits on. Clay vessels are broken, wooden ones rinsed. Men and women get parallel treatment, seven days and then two birds in each case, and ordinary marital sex simply means both of them bathe. The stated reason arrives at the end and is not disgust: keep them separate so they do not defile the tabernacle.
LEV 16:1-17	This chapter is dated: after the death of Aaron's two sons. The high priest may not walk into the Most Holy Place whenever he likes. He bathes, wears plain linen, and brings a bull for his own sins before he touches anyone else's. Two goats are set before God and lots are cast, one for Yahweh and one for the scapegoat. He carries in coals and incense so the smoke covers the mercy seat and he does not die. Next time on Leviticus: the other goat.
LEV 16:18-34	The live goat carries the whole weight of it. Aaron lays both hands on its head, confesses every iniquity of Israel over it, and a man appointed in advance leads it out into the wilderness, to a solitary land, and releases it. Everyone involved washes afterward, including the man who took the goat and the man who burns the carcasses outside the camp. The date is fixed and permanent: seventh month, tenth day, no work of any kind, for the native-born and the foreigner alike, once a year.
LEV 17:1-16	Every animal slaughtered has to be brought to the tent door, and killing one out in the open field counts as shedding blood, which is a startling way to describe dinner. The stated purpose is to stop people sacrificing to the goat idols they have apparently been sacrificing to. Blood is forbidden four separate ways, with the reason stated plainly: the life of the flesh is in the blood, and the blood is what makes atonement. Hunted game must be drained and the blood covered with dust.
LEV 18:1-30	The chapter opens by naming two places, the Egypt behind them and the Canaan ahead, and telling Israel to live like neither. Then a long list of relatives no one may approach, worked through methodically by degree: mother, father's wife, sister, granddaughter, aunt by blood and aunt by marriage, daughter-in-law, sister-in-law. Child sacrifice to Molech sits on the same list, and so does adultery. The closing image is deliberately physical: the land grew sick of the people living there before and vomited them out, and it can do that again.
LEV 19:1-37	The most quotable chapter in the book, and the most miscellaneous. Leave the corners of your field unharvested for the poor. Pay a hired worker before nightfall. Do not curse the deaf or trip the blind, since neither can catch you at it. Judge without favouring the poor or the great. Love your neighbour as yourself, and sixteen verses later, love the foreigner as yourself, since you were foreigners in Egypt. Filed in among all this: no mixed seed, no mixed cloth, no tattoos, and honest weights.
LEV 20:1-27	Chapter eighteen listed the offences; this one attaches the penalties, and the escalation is the point. Giving a child to Molech means stoning, and if the community hides its eyes, God says he will deal with the community. Cursing a parent, adultery and several degrees of incest carry death. Some cases get a lesser sentence, being cut off, or dying childless, a real punishment in a book this concerned with descent. It ends where eighteen ended, land ready to vomit, and the call to be holy.
LEV 21:1-24	Rules for priests, tightening as the rank rises. An ordinary priest may attend the funeral of a parent, child or sibling, no one further out. The high priest may not go near a dead body at all, including his own parents. Then a list of physical defects barring a descendant of Aaron from the altar, from blindness and lameness to a flat nose and scabs. The chapter is careful about one thing: he is disqualified from serving, not from eating. He still gets the holy bread.
LEV 22:1-33	Two halves, both about not treating holy things casually. First, who may eat the priests' portion: not a guest, not a hired hand, not a daughter who married outside, but yes to a slave the priest bought and to a daughter come home widowed without a child. Then the animals, which must be flawless, with disqualifying flaws down to warts and running sores. Two rules at the end are almost tender: a newborn stays with its mother seven days, and no animal is killed with its young.
LEV 23:1-21	The festival calendar opens with the Sabbath, so the first appointed feast is the one that happens every week. Passover on the fourteenth of the first month, unleavened bread from the fifteenth for seven days. Then the sheaf of first fruits, waved, before anyone may eat the new harvest. Count seven Sabbaths, fifty days, and bring two loaves, baked with yeast, in a book that has spent chapters keeping yeast off the altar. Next time on Leviticus: the autumn ones.
LEV 23:22-44	The gleaning law reappears, dropped into the middle of the calendar, as though the festivals do not count without it. Then the autumn cluster: trumpets on the first of the seventh month, the Day of Atonement on the tenth with no work and self-denial, and on the fifteenth the feast of tents. That comes with instructions to take palm branches and willows from the brook, rejoice for seven days, and sleep in shelters, so the next generation learns what their ancestors lived in.
LEV 24:1-23	It begins with housekeeping: oil for a lamp that never goes out, twelve loaves in two rows on the gold table, replaced every Sabbath. Then a fight breaks out and a man blasphemes the Name. He is held in custody until God's will can be asked, unusual in a book of fixed penalties. His mother is named, Shelomith daughter of Dibri of Dan; his father was Egyptian. Everyone who heard him lays hands on his head and he is stoned. The verdict widens into eye for eye.
LEV 25:1-29	The land itself gets a Sabbath: sow six years, leave the seventh alone, and whatever comes up on its own is open to the servants, foreigners, livestock and wild animals equally. Count seven of those cycles and the fiftieth year is jubilee, announced by trumpet on the Day of Atonement, when everyone returns to their property. Property is priced in remaining harvests, not acres. Someone asks what they will eat in year seven, told year six will carry three. Next time on Leviticus: houses, and people.
LEV 25:30-55	A city house sold and not bought back within a year stays sold forever, while a village house counts as farmland and comes back at jubilee. Levite property is always redeemable. Then the poor: hold your brother up, lend him nothing at interest, sell him nothing at a profit. An Israelite who sells himself is a hired worker and not a slave, and goes free at jubilee with his children. Foreign slaves are inheritable property, which the text says flatly. A sold Israelite is redeemed by years left.
LEV 26:1-25	The covenant terms, in two columns. Obey and the rains come on time, the threshing runs into the grape harvest and that into sowing, five of you chase a hundred, and God will walk among you. Refuse and it escalates in rounds, each opening with 'if you still will not listen' and adding seven times more: fever, enemies eating your crop, fleeing when nobody pursues, iron skies and bronze soil, wild animals, then sword and pestilence. Next time on Leviticus: it gets worse before it turns.
LEV 26:26-46	The curses reach their worst here, and the images are precise rather than rhetorical: ten women baking in a single oven and handing bread back by weight, cities in ruins, people scattered and so frightened that a driven leaf sends them running. Cannibalism is named plainly. One consolation runs underneath it: the emptied land finally gets the Sabbaths it was never given. Then the chapter turns. If they confess, God remembers Jacob, Isaac, Abraham and the land, and says he will not reject them utterly even there.
LEV 27:1-17	The book ends on an appendix about vows, mostly the price list. A person promised to God has a fixed valuation by age and sex, from fifty shekels for a man in his prime down to three for an infant girl, and anyone who cannot pay is assessed by the priest. Animals may not be swapped, and an attempted upgrade forfeits both. Houses are appraised, and a field valued by how much barley seed it takes to sow. Next time on Leviticus: the paperwork, concluded.
LEV 27:18-34	More valuations, all prorated to the jubilee, all redeemable at the assessed price plus a fifth. A firstborn animal cannot be dedicated, because it already belongs to God, which quietly closes a loophole. Then the tithe of the herd, counted by whatever passes under the shepherd's rod, with the owner forbidden to inspect the tenth animal or to trade it for a better one. So the book that opened with a bull at the tent door ends with a shepherd counting sheep and being told not to cheat.
MAN 1:1-15	Fifteen verses of one man's apology, front-loaded with everything he can say about God: heaven and earth made, the sea bound by a word, the deep shut up and sealed. Then the pivot. Repentance was not appointed for Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, who did not need it, but for me. His sins outnumber the sand of the sea, he is bowed under iron bands, he set up abominations. He bows the knee of his heart, says he has sinned twice over, and asks not to be destroyed.
MRK 1:1-45	Mark skips the nativity entirely and opens at a run. John arrives in camel hair on a locust diet, announces someone stronger, and baptizes him; the sky tears open and says so out loud. Forty days in the wilderness get one sentence. Four fishermen abandon their nets immediately, a word Mark will be using constantly. Demons recognize him and are told to be quiet. A cleansed leper is told to say nothing and immediately tells everyone, which ends Jesus walking into towns.
MRK 2:1-28	The house is so full that four men take the roof apart and lower their friend through the hole, which Jesus reads as faith rather than vandalism. He forgives sins first, which the scribes silently object to, and heals the man largely to settle the argument. Then he recruits a tax collector, eats with the wrong crowd, declines to make his disciples fast, and observes that nobody patches old cloth with new. On the Sabbath his disciples snack, and he cites David doing worse.
MRK 3:1-35	A man with a withered hand becomes a test case, and Jesus answers with a question nobody touches, then heals him while angry. The Pharisees team up with the Herodians to kill him, which is quick work for chapter three. He keeps a boat on standby, and appoints twelve, two nicknamed Sons of Thunder and one flagged on the spot as the betrayer. His family arrives to collect him on grounds of insanity. The scribes go with demon possession. He points out that Satan evicting Satan is bad business.
MRK 4:1-41	He teaches from a boat because the crowd will not give him room, and the sower parable does what parables do: everyone hears it, nobody asks. Privately he explains: the seed is the word, the soils are people, and nothing hidden stays hidden. Two more seed parables, including the mustard seed that starts smallest and ends big enough for birds. That evening the boat crosses in a storm, he sleeps through it, is woken with a complaint rather than a request, and tells the weather to be quiet.
MRK 5:1-43	A man living in the tombs, who has snapped every chain, meets Jesus, and the demons introduce themselves as Legion. They negotiate a transfer into two thousand pigs, which drown. The town weighs a healed man against its livestock and asks him to leave. On the other shore a synagogue ruler begs for his dying daughter, interrupted by a woman who has spent twelve years and her savings on doctors and is healed by his coat. The girl dies meanwhile. He raises her, then says feed her.
MRK 6:1-27	The hometown can only see the carpenter whose sisters they know, so he does almost nothing there and marvels at their unbelief. He sends the twelve out in pairs with a staff and nothing else. Herod hears the reports and concludes, unhelpfully, that John the Baptizer is back from the dead. Mark rewinds: Herodias wanted John dead, Herod protected him because he liked listening, then a birthday, a dance and a public oath handed her what she wanted. Next time on Mark: the platter arrives.
MRK 6:28-56	The head is delivered and John's disciples bury the body. The apostles come back full of reports, so Jesus proposes a rest, which the crowd defeats by running around the lake and arriving first. Five loaves and two fish feed five thousand men and leave twelve baskets. He sends the disciples ahead, prays, then walks out to their struggling boat and intends to pass by, which is not a detail anyone expects. They take him for a ghost. Mark's verdict on the amazement: they had not understood about the loaves.
MRK 7:1-37	The Pharisees open on hand washing, and Mark stops to explain the custom. Jesus escalates from washing to Corban, the loophole letting a man dedicate to God the support he owed his parents, and calls the whole thing voiding God's word with tradition. Nothing going in defiles a person; the problem is what comes out, followed by a list. Then a Syrophoenician woman out-argues him about dogs and crumbs and wins, and a deaf man with a speech impediment gets fingers, spit and one word: Ephphatha.
MRK 8:1-38	Four thousand this time, seven loaves, seven baskets left. The Pharisees ask for a sign, and he sighs deeply and refuses. In the boat he warns about the yeast of the Pharisees and of Herod, and the disciples conclude he is annoyed about the forgotten lunch, which prompts the sharpest quiz in the gospel: how many baskets, and do you still not understand. A blind man at Bethsaida needs two attempts and sees men like walking trees. Peter says Christ, then rebukes Jesus about dying and gets called Satan.
MRK 9:1-26	Six days later three of them watch him change on a mountain, Moses and Elijah alongside. Peter, whom Mark excuses as having no idea what to say, proposes tents. A cloud settles the matter. At the bottom the other nine have failed with a boy with a mute spirit, and the scribes are enjoying it. The father says if you can do anything, and Jesus catches the if. The father's reply is the chapter's best line: I believe, help my unbelief. Next time on Mark: the boy looks dead.
MRK 9:27-50	He stands the boy up, then tells the disciples privately that this kind takes prayer. He predicts his death again; they do not understand, are too afraid to ask, and spend the walk arguing about which of them is greatest. He tells them first means last, and puts a child in the middle. John reports stopping an outsider casting out demons, expecting praise, and is told not to. Then millstones, and the hand, the foot and the eye, and a closing note about salt, nearly a relief after all that.
MRK 10:1-29	Pharisees open with divorce, and he takes it past Moses to the beginning: two become one, so nobody separates them. Children are brought and the disciples manage the diary; he is indignant. Then a man asks about eternal life, has kept everything since childhood, and is loved, and told to sell the lot. He leaves sad, owning a great deal. Camels and needles follow, and who could possibly be saved. Peter notes that they, at least, have left everything. Next time on Mark: what that gets you.
MRK 10:30-52	A hundred times over, houses and family and land, and then with persecutions, which is not how anyone else would pitch it. Walking up to Jerusalem he spells out the mocking, the spitting, the flogging and the killing. James and John respond by asking for the two best seats. The other ten are furious, presumably for not asking first. He explains that greatness here runs the other way. At Jericho a blind beggar shouts, is hushed, shouts louder, throws off his cloak, and follows him down the road.
MRK 11:1-33	Two disciples are sent to untie a donkey with a cover story ready. Coats and branches go down, hosannas go up, and he reaches the temple, looks around, notes it is late, and leaves. Next morning he curses a fig tree that was never going to have figs, then clears the temple, quoting a house of prayer at the market inside it. The tree is dead by morning, which Peter mentions. Asked who authorized any of this, he asks one question about John and they cannot answer it in public.
MRK 12:1-23	A parable about a vineyard whose tenants beat the servants and finally kill the owner's son, on the theory that this gets them the inheritance. The chief priests identify themselves as the tenants and would arrest him but for the crowd. Next Pharisees and Herodians arrive with compliments and a tax question built to have no safe answer; he asks whose face is on the coin. Then the Sadducees, who do not believe in resurrection, propose a woman widowed by seven brothers. Next time on Mark: whose wife is she.
MRK 12:24-44	He tells the Sadducees they are mistaken: the raised do not marry, and God calls himself the God of Abraham in the present tense. A scribe asks which command is greatest, gets love God and love your neighbor, and is told he is not far from the kingdom. Nobody dares ask anything after that. He raises a puzzle about David calling his own son Lord, warns about scribes who like long robes and devour widows' houses, then watches a widow put two small coins in the treasury.
MRK 13:1-37	A disciple admires the stonework; not one stone will be left on another, which ends the tour. Four ask when, and get an answer that declines to say when. Wars, earthquakes, famines: beginnings, not the end. They will be handed over, and should not rehearse a defense. When the abomination stands where it should not, run without stopping for a coat. False christs, a darkened sun, the Son of Man in clouds. As for the day, not even the Son knows, so the instruction is to watch.
MRK 14:1-34	Two days before Passover the chief priests look for a quiet way to take him. At Bethany a woman pours costly nard over his head and is scolded for the waste; he defends her: she has anointed his body for burial. Judas goes to the priests, who offer money. At the table he says one of the twelve will hand him over, then gives them bread and a cup. At Gethsemane he says his soul is sorrowful to the point of death. Next time on Mark: he prays alone.
