MRK 14:35-72	He prays that the hour might pass, then says not what I desire but what you desire. Three times he comes back and finds them asleep. Judas arrives with an armed crowd and identifies him with a kiss. Someone cuts off a servant's ear. Everyone runs, one young man leaving his linen cloth behind. The witnesses contradict one another, so the high priest asks him directly, and he answers directly. They condemn him, spit on him and strike him. Peter denies him three times and weeps.
MRK 15:1-47	Pilate asks if he is the King of the Jews, gets three words, then nothing. Offered a choice, the crowd asks for Barabbas, a killer. Soldiers dress him in purple, crown him with thorns and beat him. Simon of Cyrene is pulled from the crowd to carry it. He refuses the drugged wine. Darkness covers the land from the sixth hour to the ninth, when he cries out that God has forsaken him. The veil tears from top to bottom. A centurion says what nobody else has said.
MRK 16:1-20	Three women come at sunrise with spices and one logistical problem: the stone. It is already rolled back. A young man in white tells them he has risen: tell the disciples, Peter by name. They run and say nothing, afraid, which is a strange place to end a gospel. The appendix records the appearances anyway: to Mary Magdalene, who is disbelieved, to two walking into the country, also disbelieved, and finally to the eleven at dinner, who get told off. Then the commission, the signs, and the ascension.
PS2 1:1-7	Seven verses of David giving his own account of himself. Youngest brother, sent out to the sheep, made his own instrument and tuned the lute himself. Who would tell the Lord about it? The Lord hears it himself. So an angel fetches him from the flock and anoints him, passing over brothers who were taller and better looking. Then the whole Goliath business compressed into two lines: the Philistine cursed him by his idols, and he drew the man's own sword and beheaded him.
PSA 1:1-6	The book opens with a posture chart: don't walk with the wicked, don't stand with sinners, don't sit with scoffers, a slide into bad company measured by how comfortable you get. The alternative is a man who chews on the law day and night and grows like a tree planted by water, fruit on schedule, leaf intact. The wicked get one image and it is chaff, which the wind handles without effort.
PSA 2:1-12	Nations rage, kings hold a summit, and the agenda item is throwing off God's ropes. The response from heaven is laughter, which is a rougher answer than anger would be. Then the decree: a king installed on Zion, told to ask for the nations as an inheritance and issued an iron rod for the pottery. It closes by advising those same kings to be wise, serve with fear, and take refuge before the fuse burns down.
PSA 3:1-8	The complaint is a headcount: enemies multiplying, and a chorus insisting God has no help for him. The rebuttal is a man who lay down, slept, and woke up, offered as evidence of protection and, in fairness, hard to argue with. Ten thousand surrounding him do not change the arithmetic. The prayer for the enemies is specific: cheekbones struck, teeth broken.
PSA 4:1-8	A night prayer, filed under grievance. He asks how long his reputation will be someone else's entertainment, tells the offenders to lie on their beds and think about it quietly, and notes that plenty of people are asking who will show them anything good at all. His own answer is unfashionable: more gladness than a bumper harvest of grain and new wine. Then he goes to sleep, which is the point.
PSA 5:1-12	Morning shift. He lays out his requests and then watches, like a man who expects a reply. What follows is an inventory of things God cannot stand: arrogance, lies, bloodshed, flattery. Their throat is an open tomb, which is not a remark about their breath. He asks that their own schemes trip them, and that everyone taking refuge get to shout about it. He is going to the temple either way.
PSA 6:1-10	He asks not to be corrected while God is angry, which is a reasonable request in any relationship. Then the case for staying alive: nobody thanks you from Sheol, so healing him is arguably in God's own interest. He floods his bed nightly and his eyes are wearing out with grief. In the last three verses the tone flips completely. God has heard, and the workers of iniquity can leave.
PSA 7:1-17	He offers to be trampled into the dust by his enemy if he actually did the thing he is accused of, which is either innocence or excellent bluffing. Then he asks God to convene court and rule on his integrity. God turns up as a judge with a standing daily grievance, a sharpened sword and arrows already lit. The wicked man's downfall is self-inflicted: pregnant with mischief, he digs a pit and falls into it.
PSA 8:1-9	A man looks up at the moon and stars, calls them finger-work, and asks the obvious follow-up: why would anything that big pay attention to us. The answer is not modest. Humans come out a little lower than the angels, crowned, and handed the management of sheep, cattle, wild animals, birds and everything swimming through the sea. The psalm opens and closes on the same line, which is how you know it means it.
PSA 9:1-20	Thanksgiving with receipts: enemies routed, nations rebuked, their names blotted out, whole cities now remembered by nobody. The throne is described as permanently occupied and permanently in session. Then the turn to himself, asking to be lifted up from the gates of death. The nations, he notes, sank into a pit of their own construction. His closing request is small and devastating: let them find out they are only men.
PSA 10:1-18	It opens by accusing God of standing too far off, and it is not polite about it. Most of the psalm is a portrait of the predator: prosperous, sneering, ambushing from the outskirts, dragging the helpless off in a net while assuring himself God has forgotten and will never look. The psalmist wants the arm broken, specifically. The ending insists God is king anyway and does hear, which reads like an argument he is slowly winning against himself.
PSA 11:1-7	Somebody advises him to flee to the mountains like a bird, since the archers are already stringing up in the dark and the foundations are going. He declines. His answer is that God is still in his temple and still on the throne, and more to the point still watching, eyes examining everybody. The forecast for the violent involves coals, fire, sulfur and a scorching wind, served as the contents of their cup.
PSA 12:1-8	An emergency call about a shortage of honest people. Everybody flatters, everybody keeps a second heart in reserve, and the boast is that their lips belong to them and nobody is lord over them. God interrupts in the middle of the psalm to say he is getting up, because of the groaning of the needy. His words get weighed against theirs: silver refined seven times against pure surface. The vile, meanwhile, are being promoted.
PSA 13:1-6	Four how longs in two verses, which is the sound of someone who has stopped pacing himself. Forgotten forever, face hidden, sorrow every day, enemy on top. He asks for light in his eyes before he sleeps the other kind of sleep. Then, with no explanation offered and nothing visibly changed, he ends on trust and a song.
PSA 14:1-7	The fool's position is stated in four words, and the rest is God leaning over the balcony of heaven to see whether anyone down there understands anything. The audit result: nobody, not one. The wicked eat his people the way you eat bread, casually and without thinking about it. Then a flicker of dread on their side, and a closing wish for rescue to come out of Zion.
PSA 15:1-5	A question about temple admission, answered as a character reference rather than a ritual. Speak truth internally, do not slander, do not harm a friend, honor the right people, and keep an oath after it has turned out badly for you. No lending at interest, no taking money to convict the innocent. Anyone who lives like that, the psalm says, will never be shaken.
PSA 16:1-11	Other gods get declined with a detail that stays with you: he will not pour their drink offerings of blood or even take their names on his lips. What he has instead is described in property terms, a portion, a cup, boundary lines that happened to fall in a pleasant part of the map. He gets counselled by his own heart at night. The last verses are pure confidence: not left in Sheol, shown the path of life, joy at capacity.
PSA 17:1-15	The case goes in for audit. He notes that God already tested him overnight and found nothing, then asks to be kept as the apple of an eye and hidden under wings. The opposition is a lion crouching in secret. Sharper still are the men whose whole portion is in this life: full bellies, well-provided sons, wealth stored for the grandchildren. His closing line quietly wants something else, to wake up and see God's face.
PSA 18:1-50	Fifty verses and it never lowers the volume. He calls out of distress and the answer arrives as weather: earth shaking, smoke out of God's nostrils, a cherub used as transport, hailstones and lightning, the sea floor laid bare. The rescue itself takes one line, drawn out of many waters. Then a long stretch of self-defence about clean hands, and a victory lap of leaping walls and grinding enemies into street mud. He credits it all to God's gentleness.
PSA 19:1-14	Two halves, welded. First the sky preaches without saying anything, in a language with no words that everyone somehow hears, and the sun comes out of its tent like a bridegroom with somewhere to be. Then the law, rated in six adjectives and priced above fine gold and honeycomb. The ending goes personal and slightly nervous: forgive the errors I cannot even see, and keep the deliberate ones off me. Then the famous request about the words of his mouth.
PSA 20:1-9	A blessing said over a king before a battle: may God answer you, send help from the sanctuary, remember your offerings, hand you what your heart is after. Then the banners go up. The famous line arrives late and does the work of the whole psalm, some trusting in chariots, some in horses, and this lot trusting a name. The scoreboard is given in advance. They fall, we stand upright.
PSA 21:1-13	The thank-you note for the previous psalm's prayer. The king asked for life and received length of days forever, plus a gold crown he did not pay for. Then the tone turns and the second half is what happens to the plotters: a furnace, fire, their descendants cut off, and a drawn bow aimed at the face. It lands finally on a request that God be exalted, which after all that is a heavy note to finish on.
PSA 22:1-31	It begins with the question that has no answer inside it: my God, my God, why have you forsaken me. He cries day and night and gets nothing back, calling himself a worm, mocked by people who tell him to let God rescue him if God is fond of him. Then the body: poured out like water, bones out of joint, heart melting like wax, hands and feet pierced, his clothes divided by lot. Only near the end does it turn, insisting God did not hide his face after all.
PSA 23:1-6	The most famous six verses in the book, and they earn it by staying small. A shepherd, green grass, quiet water, a soul put back together. Then the valley with death's shadow over it, walked through rather than avoided, with a rod and a staff for company. The picture changes at the end into a table laid in full view of his enemies, oil on his head, and a cup that will not stop overflowing.
PSA 24:1-10	First a deed of ownership covering the entire planet and everyone standing on it. Then the entry requirements for the holy hill, and they concern hands and heart rather than paperwork. The second half is written to be shouted at a gate: lift up your heads, doors, the King of glory is coming in. Someone on the other side asks who that is, twice, and gets told twice, louder. Yahweh of Armies.
PSA 25:1-22	A prayer that keeps alternating between asking for instruction and asking for forgiveness, as though the two were one request. He wants his youthful sins forgotten and his current one pardoned on the grounds that it is great, which is an unusual defence and possibly a good one. Between the pleas he drifts into calm statements about God teaching the humble. He is desolate, netted and cruelly hated, and the last line widens out to cover all of Israel.
PSA 26:1-12	He invites God to examine, test and try him, which takes a certain confidence. The evidence is largely a list of chairs he has not sat in: not with the deceitful, not with hypocrites, not with the assembly of evildoers. He washes his hands in innocence and walks around the altar. What he genuinely dreads is being filed with the bribe-takers when the sorting happens, and he says outright that he loves the house.
PSA 27:1-14	Half of this is unshakeable and half is frightened, and the psalm makes no apology for the seam. In the first part an army could camp around him and he would be fine. In the second he is begging God not to hide, not to abandon him, and admitting his own father and mother might. In the middle sits the one thing he actually asks for, which is not victory but a permanent address in God's house. It ends telling itself to wait, twice.
PSA 28:1-9	The opening worry is not that God will refuse but that God will say nothing, which he rates as equivalent to being in the pit already. He asks specifically not to be dragged off with the men who speak peace to their neighbors with mischief already loaded. Their sentence is to be paid exactly what their hands built. Then the psalm flips into thanks mid-stream, because the petition was heard, and closes asking for the whole nation to be shepherded.
PSA 29:1-11	A thunderstorm, tracked from the waters inland, and the word voice lands seven times. It splinters the cedars of Lebanon, makes whole mountains skip like calves, throws lightning, shakes the wilderness of Kadesh and strips the forests bare. Inside the temple the only appropriate response is one word, repeated. Then the last verse does something unexpected: after all that noise, what God hands his people is strength and peace.
PSA 30:1-12	A recovery song with an embarrassing confession in the middle. When things were going well he announced he would never be moved, and then God hid his face and he came apart immediately. His prayer from the bottom is frank negotiation, asking what profit there is in his destruction, since dust makes a poor choir. The famous line sits in verse five, weeping for the night and joy in the morning. It ends with the sackcloth off and dancing.
PSA 31:1-24	Two images do most of the work here and both are about being discarded: forgotten like a dead man, and broken pottery. His neighbors find him contemptible and people who see him on the street run. He commends his spirit into God's hand in verse five, a line later borrowed. Then it turns. His times are in God's hand, and there is a shelter specifically from the strife of tongues. He panicked and said he was cut off, and was heard anyway.
PSA 32:1-11	The physical cost of not confessing, described like an illness: bones wasting, a hand pressing down day and night, strength drying out in the heat of summer. Then he says it out loud and the whole thing lifts in a single verse. What follows is advice offered gently right up until it isn't, since do not be like the horse or the mule, animals with no understanding that need a bit and bridle, is not gentle at all.
PSA 33:1-22	Sheet music first: a new song, a ten-string harp, played skillfully and with a shout. The theology is creation by speech, heavens made with a word and their armies with a breath. Then a run of demolition. God cancels the counsel of nations, no king is saved by the size of his army, no strong man by his strength, and a horse is flatly called a vain thing for safety. What is left is an eye on those who hope, and a soul waiting.
PSA 34:1-22	Praise for a dozen verses, then it turns into a teaching session without warning. The testimony first: he asked, he was answered, and the people who looked to God went away radiant. Taste and see, he says, which treats God as something to try rather than something to agree with. Then the children get sat down for the syllabus: keep your tongue off evil, depart from it, do good, seek peace and pursue it. The quietest line is the best, that God is near the broken-hearted.
PSA 35:1-28	He asks God to pick up an actual shield and spear and take the field in person, and then to lean over and tell his soul it is safe. His grievance has a sharp centre: when these people were sick he wore sackcloth, fasted, and mourned as though for his own mother, and when he went down they gathered to watch and tore at him without stopping. The word Aha appears twice, which is somehow worse than a threat. He wants their hidden net to catch them.
PSA 36:1-12	The first four verses are a diagnosis: the wicked man flatters himself so thoroughly that he can no longer detect his own sin, and does his plotting lying in bed. Then the psalm changes register entirely and goes vertical, kindness reaching the heavens, righteousness like mountains, judgments like a great deep, and animals covered by the same care as people. There is a river to drink from and a spring of life. It closes with one small request, keep the foot of pride off me.
PSA 37:1-40	Forty verses aimed at one specific temptation, envying people who are getting away with it. The counsel repeats on purpose: do not fret, trust, delight, commit, rest, wait, stop being angry. The argument underneath is entirely about time, which is why God laughs, having already seen the day coming. Best of all is an old man's eyewitness note about a wicked man spreading like a green tree in native soil, who went back to look and found nobody there.
PSA 38:1-22	A full medical report submitted as a confession. No soundness anywhere, wounds gone rotten, a burning waist, a throbbing heart, the light of his eyes gone, and he traces all of it to his own foolishness. Friends and relatives have arranged themselves at a distance. His strategy against the accusers is to go entirely deaf and mute, which is either dignity or exhaustion. The last verses shorten into three requests: don't forsake me, don't be far, hurry.
PSA 39:1-13	He decides to say nothing at all in front of the wicked, holds it until the silence heats up like a fire, and then says something far bleaker than whatever he was suppressing. Life measures out in hand widths, a man stands as a breath, a walking shadow heaping up wealth without knowing who gathers it. He calls himself a stranger and a foreigner, as all his fathers were. The last line asks God to look away long enough for him to recover before he exists no more.
PSA 40:1-17	Rescue first: a horrible pit, miry clay, feet set on a rock and a new song issued. Then the theological turn, that what God wanted was not sacrifice and offering but an opened ear, and the answer given is simply here I have come. He is careful to record that he did not seal his lips about it. Then, having testified at length, he admits his iniquities outnumber the hairs on his head and asks God to hurry, which undercuts the confidence in exactly the right way.
PSA 41:1-13	The promise up front is that God will sit at the bedside of anyone who considers the poor. Then it gets specific about the psalmist's own sickroom. Visitors turn up, speak falsehood to his face, and go straight out to tell everyone. The whisper campaign has already concluded he will not rise again. Worst of all, the familiar friend who ate bread at his table has lifted his heel. His prayer to be raised so he can repay them is honest rather than admirable. The amens close Book One.
PSA 42:1-11	Homesickness written as thirst. He remembers going with the crowd into God's house, leading them, singing, and now his food is his own tears while people keep asking him where his God has got to. He is somewhere north, near the Jordan and Hermon and the hill Mizar, which is not where he wants to be. Deep calls to deep and the waves go over him. Twice he stops to ask his own soul why it is in despair, and tells it to hope.
PSA 43:1-5	The same poem continuing, same refrain, but the request has sharpened into a legal one: plead my cause against an ungodly nation and a set of deceitful men. He wants God's light and truth sent out ahead of him as guides, specifically to lead him back to the holy hill and the altar, where the harp is waiting. Then he asks his soul the same question a third time and gives it the same answer.
PSA 44:1-26	Grandfathers speak first: God drove out nations and planted his own people, and no, not by their own sword. Then the word but arrives in verse nine and the rest is a complaint made under oath. Sold for nothing, scattered, made a byword and a shaking of the head, regarded as sheep for the slaughter, and they insist they have not forgotten him or been false to the covenant. The last verses shake God awake and ask why he is sleeping. No answer comes.
PSA 45:1-17	A wedding song, and the poet tells you up front that his tongue is a skillful writer's pen, which is the least modest opening in the collection. The groom gets a sword strapped to his thigh, sharp arrows, an eternal throne and garments smelling of myrrh, aloes and cassia. The bride is told to forget her own people and her father's house, offered as advice and landing as a cost. She arrives in gold and embroidery with her companions behind her. Sons are promised as princes.
PSA 46:1-11	The scenario is the worst one available: the earth changing, mountains shaken into the sea, the waters roaring. What is promised is not that it will not happen but that they will not be afraid when it does. In the middle of all that there is a river and a city, quietly glad. God ends wars by breaking the equipment, bows snapped, spears shattered, chariots burned. The famous command to be still is addressed to the raging nations, not to anyone anxious.
PSA 47:1-9	Applause is demanded from every nation, and then the occasion is given: a coronation, God going up to the throne with a shout and the sound of a trumpet. Sing praises appears four times inside a verse and a half, which is not an accident. The final verse gathers the princes of the peoples in with the people of the God of Abraham, a larger claim than the volume lets you notice.
PSA 48:1-14	A city guide with a military anecdote in the middle. Enemy kings assemble, pass by together to look at Zion, take one look and hurry off, their panic compared to labour pains. Then the instruction that shapes the psalm: walk around the city, count the towers, mark the bulwarks, consider the palaces, and tell the next generation what you counted. The tour is not really about architecture. The last line is about a guide who goes all the way.
PSA 49:1-20	A wisdom riddle set to a harp and addressed to rich and poor together, on the theme that money does not work where it matters. Nobody can redeem a brother from death, the ransom being permanently out of range. Wise men and fools die at the same rate and leave it all to others. The rich name their lands after themselves, which is treated as touching rather than impressive. The bleakest image is Death as a shepherd. The line about the animals is used twice.
PSA 50:1-23	God convenes a court, summons heaven and earth as witnesses, and makes clear the charge is not that the sacrifices stopped. They did not. The complaint is the assumption behind them: he owns every animal of the forest and the livestock on a thousand hills, and if he were hungry he would not mention it. Then he turns on people who recite his statutes while consenting with thieves and slandering their own mother's son. The worst line is the quietest: I kept silent, and you thought I was like you.
S3Y 1:1-29	Three men are walking about inside a furnace praising God, and the narrative treats this as the setting rather than the event. Azarias prays: you are righteous, we sinned, you handed us to the most wicked king in the world, and we have no prince, no prophet, no altar, so take a contrite heart instead of ten thousand fat lambs. The servants keep feeding the fire, the flame goes up forty-nine cubits and kills the Chaldeans standing near it, and an angel turns the inside into a damp whistling wind.
S3Y 1:30-68	The song is a roll call of creation and it leaves nothing out. Heavens, angels, the waters above the sky, sun and moon and stars, showers and dew, winds, fire and heat, frost, snow, lightning, mountains, growing things, seas and rivers, whales, birds, cattle, people, Israel, priests, and finally the three of them by name, thanking God for pulling them out of the fire. Every line ends the same way, a hymn built entirely of repetition, sung by men standing in a furnace.
SUS 1:1-33	Susanna is devout and married to the most honourable man in Babylon, whose garden doubles as the local courthouse. Two elders appointed judges that year both become obsessed with her, hiding it until they collide doubling back to the same garden and admit it. They lie in wait, and when she sends her maids for oil and shuts the doors, they corner her: consent, or we testify we caught you with a young man. She refuses and cries out. So do they. Next time on Susanna: the judges speak.
SUS 1:34-64	The two elders lay hands on her head and give evidence: a young man, too strong to hold, who got the doors open and escaped. The assembly believes them because they are elders and judges, and condemns her. Daniel interrupts the execution to point out that nobody examined the witnesses, then examines them himself, one question each. Under what tree? A mastick, says the first. A holm, says the second. The pair receive the sentence they had intended for her.
TOB 1:1-22	Tobit opens by listing his ancestors back five generations, then explains he alone of Naphtali kept going to Jerusalem while everyone else sacrificed to a heifer. Deported to Nineveh, he refuses Gentile food, is promoted to royal purveyor, and leaves ten talents of silver with Gabael in Media. Then he takes up burying the king's victims by night, illegal, and loses everything he owns. Sennacherib is murdered, and Tobit's nephew, who now runs the accounts, gets him recalled.
TOB 2:1-14	Restored and comfortable, Tobit sits down to a Pentecost dinner and sends Tobias to find a poor man to share it. Tobias reports a strangled corpse dumped in the marketplace, so Tobit leaves food untouched, buries the man after dark, and gets mocked for it. He sleeps by the courtyard wall, sparrows drop dung into his eyes, and he goes blind. His wife earns a goat weaving. He accuses her of stealing it, and she asks where his righteousness got him.
TOB 3:1-17	Blind and mocked, Tobit prays to die. The same day, Sarah prays the identical prayer: seven husbands, and a demon named Asmodaeus killed every one on the wedding night, and the maidservants blame her for it. She considers hanging herself, decides it would shame her father, and asks for death instead. Both prayers are heard at once, and Raphael is dispatched to scale the films off Tobit's eyes, bind Asmodaeus, and marry Sarah to Tobias.
TOB 4:1-21	Having just asked for death, Tobit remembers that nobody knows about the money, so he calls Tobias in for a farewell speech that runs to twenty verses. Bury me, honour your mother, give alms, marry inside the family, pay your workers the same day, avoid drunkenness, take advice from anyone wise, and do to no one what you yourself hate. Almsgiving comes round roughly five times. The actual point, ten talents of silver on deposit with Gabael at Rages, arrives at verse twenty.
TOB 5:1-22	Tobias points out the flaw in the plan: he has never met Gabael. Told to hire a guide, he finds Raphael, who is an angel, and does not notice. Tobit interviews the applicant about his family, is satisfied, settles on a drachma a day plus a bonus if both come home safe. The dog comes too. Anna weeps that no amount of silver is worth her son, and Tobit assures her a good angel is on the job.
TOB 6:1-17	At the Tigris a fish comes at Tobias, the angel says grab it, and they roast and eat it, keeping the heart, liver and gall. Raphael explains: burn the heart and liver and any demon flees, smear the gall and blindness clears. He mentions that tonight they lodge with Raguel, whose daughter Sarah is legally Tobias's to marry. Tobias raises the small matter of seven dead husbands. Raphael tells him to bring the fish parts. Tobias falls in love on the spot.
TOB 7:1-18	Sarah answers the door. Raguel notices the family resemblance, learns that this is Tobit's son, kisses him, then weeps when he hears Tobit has gone blind. Dinner is served, and Tobias refuses to touch it until the marriage is agreed. Raguel tries eat, drink and be merry first, then admits that seven previous husbands all died in the night, then hands his daughter over anyway, writes the contract and seals it. Edna prepares the other chamber, cries with her daughter, and tells her twice to be comforted.
TOB 8:1-21	In the bridal chamber Tobias burns the fish heart and liver on the incense, the demon flees to the uppermost parts of Egypt, and the angel binds him there. Then the couple get up and pray before anything else. Outside, Raguel is digging a grave on the assumption he will need it, and sends a maidservant at first light to check. The report: both alive, both asleep. He blesses God, has the grave quietly filled in, and throws a fourteen-day wedding feast.
TOB 9:1-6	Sworn to stay at his own wedding, Tobias sends Raphael to run the errand the entire journey was for: take a servant and two camels, collect the silver from Gabael, and bring him back for the feast. The reason given is that his father counts the days and will grieve. Raphael goes, produces the handwriting, receives the bags with their seals unbroken, and is back by morning with Gabael. The money that has driven the whole plot changes hands offstage, in six verses.
TOB 10:1-12	Back in Nineveh, Tobit is counting days and working through explanations: detained, perhaps, or Gabael has died. Anna skips to the conclusion that her son is dead, stops eating by day, and walks out to the road every morning to watch. When the fourteen days end Tobias asks to leave. Raguel offers to send word to his father instead, is refused, and hands over Sarah, half his goods, servants, cattle and money. Edna's farewell is half blessing and half warning: she is my daughter, do not cause her grief.
TOB 11:1-19	Raphael proposes they run ahead of the caravan to prepare the house, and adds: bring the gall. Anna sees her son coming and announces she is ready to die. Tobit stumbles at the door, and his son rubs the gall on his eyes. They sting, he rubs, the white films scale away, and he sees. He walks out to meet his daughter-in-law, blessing God in front of neighbours who cannot believe it. Seven more days of feasting.
TOB 12:1-22	Tobit tells Tobias to pay the guide well, and they agree on half of everything they brought back. The guide declines it, delivers a short sermon on prayer, fasting and almsgiving, then explains that he carried their prayers before the Holy One and stood beside Tobit at every burial. He is Raphael, one of the seven. He adds that he never actually ate or drank, they only saw a vision. Both fall on their faces, and he is gone.
TOB 13:1-18	Tobit writes a hymn, and its argument is that the scourging and the mercy are the same hand: he leads down to the grave and brings up again. Praise God in front of the nations, he says, precisely because you were scattered among them. Turn back with your whole heart and he turns back. Then he reaches Jerusalem and loses all control of the vocabulary: sapphires, emeralds, walls and towers of pure gold, streets paved with beryl and stones of Ophir, and every street of it saying hallelujah.
TOB 14:1-15	Tobit went blind at fifty-eight and got his sight back eight years later. Very old now, he calls in Tobias and six grandsons for closing instructions: leave Nineveh, because Jonah was right about it, bury me and your mother properly, and remember what Aman did to Achiacharus and how that ended for Aman. He dies mid-speech at a hundred and fifty-eight. Tobias moves to Ecbatana, buries his in-laws magnificently, inherits twice over, and dies at a hundred and twenty-seven, having first heard that Nineveh has fallen.
JOS 1:1-18	Moses is dead, so Yahweh's first act is a pep talk: be strong and courageous, repeated so many times it starts to sound like a warning. Joshua orders three days of rations and reminds Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh that they promised to fight for the rest before going home. The people answer with total obedience, on penalty of death for anyone who doesn't fall in line.
JOS 2:1-24	Two spies scout Jericho and end up at Rahab's, a prostitute whose house doubles as an intelligence safehouse. She hides them under flax on the roof, lies smoothly to the king's men, and cashes in her risk for a guarantee: spare my family when you take the city. They agree, on condition of a scarlet cord in her window, and she gets them out the wall by rope.
JOS 3:1-17	The ark leads the way to the Jordan, which is at flood stage, not exactly cooperative conditions for a river crossing. The moment the priests' feet touch the water it piles up in a heap upstream at Adam, and the whole nation walks across on dry ground while the priests stand in the riverbed holding the line.
JOS 4:1-24	Twelve men haul twelve stones out of the exact spot where the priests stood in the Jordan, set up as a monument so future kids will ask what they mean and get the whole crossing story. Joshua sets up a matching twelve inside the riverbed itself. Forty thousand armed men cross over, the water resumes flowing, and Israel camps at Gilgal.
JOS 5:1-15	With the enemy kings' morale already gone, Joshua's first order of business is mass circumcision of an entire generation born in the wilderness, using flint knives, at a hill now named for the occasion. Passover follows, the manna stops the day they eat the land's produce, and Joshua meets an armed stranger who turns out to outrank him: take off your shoes, this ground is holy.
JOS 6:1-27	Seven days of marching around Jericho, silent except for trumpets, seven laps on the seventh day, then a shout and the wall comes down. Israel kills every person and animal in the city except Rahab's household, and burns everything but the metal, which goes into the treasury. Joshua curses whoever rebuilds it, a curse specific enough to be unsettling.
JOS 7:1-26	One man, Achan, keeps loot he was told to destroy, and Israel loses thirty-six men in an otherwise routine attack on Ai as a result. Joshua tears his clothes and asks God why they even crossed the Jordan. The lot lands on Achan by process of elimination; he confesses to a robe, silver, and gold buried in his tent. He and his family and everything he owns are stoned and burned in the valley named for the trouble.
JOS 8:1-18	Round two at Ai, this time with a plan: thirty thousand men hide in ambush while Joshua leads the rest in a staged retreat to pull the defenders out of the city. It works exactly as drawn up, down to Joshua's javelin as the signal to spring the trap. Next time on Joshua: the trap closes.
JOS 8:19-35	The ambush takes the empty city and torches it; caught between two forces, the men of Ai are wiped out, twelve thousand dead in total, men and women both. The king is hanged, then his body is dumped at the gate under a heap of stones. Joshua builds an altar, writes the law on stone, and reads all of it aloud to the whole assembly, women and children included.
JOS 9:1-27	The Gibeonites fake a long journey with stale bread and cracked wineskins to talk Israel into a protection treaty, and it works because nobody thinks to ask God first. Three days later the truth comes out and it's too late; the oath holds. Israel's compromise is to let them live as permanent woodcutters and water carriers instead of killing them outright.
JOS 10:1-23	Five kings besiege Gibeon for allying with Israel, so Joshua marches all night to rescue them. Yahweh throws hailstones that kill more men than the Israelite swords do, and the day stretches long enough at Joshua's word for the rout to finish. The five kings are found hiding in a cave and sealed in with stones while the battle continues. Next time on Joshua: the cave gets opened.
JOS 10:24-43	The five trapped kings are brought out, killed, and hanged, their bodies sealed back in the cave. Joshua then moves south through Makkedah, Libnah, Lachish, Eglon, Hebron and Debir, one after another, and the pattern repeats at every city: total destruction, no survivors. The chapter states plainly that he left no one remaining, as commanded.
JOS 11:1-23	A northern coalition under Jabin of Hazor gathers at the waters of Merom with horses and chariots, and Israel routs them, hamstringing the horses and burning the chariots as instructed. Hazor alone is burned, since it had been the head of those kingdoms; the rest of the cities are left standing. The land finally has rest from war.
JOS 12:1-24	A tally of the defeated: Sihon and Og, taken care of under Moses east of the Jordan, followed by a bare list of thirty-one kings Joshua defeated on the west side, Jericho and Ai included. No narrative, just names and a final count.
JOS 13:1-33	Joshua is old, and Yahweh points out there's still a long list of unconquered territory, the Philistines and Geshurites among it, before turning to bookkeeping: what Moses already assigned to Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh east of the Jordan, restated in border by border detail. The Levites get no land, since the offerings are their inheritance instead.
JOS 14:1-15	Land division west of the Jordan starts, and Caleb goes first, unprompted. At eighty-five he reminds Joshua he was forty when he scouted this land and still believed it, unlike his fellow spies, and asks for the hill country of Hebron, Anakim and all. Joshua gives it to him. Nobody argues with a man that stubborn.
JOS 15:1-14	Judah's border is traced in exhausting detail, south to east to north to west, mostly place names that mean nothing without a map. Buried in the middle of it: Caleb collects on his promise and drives the three sons of Anak out of Hebron himself. Next time on Joshua: Caleb offers his daughter as a prize.
JOS 15:15-63	Caleb offers his daughter Achsah to whoever takes Kiriath Sepher; his nephew Othniel does, and marries her. Achsah then asks her father for something more useful than a husband: springs of water to go with the dry southern land, and gets both the upper and lower springs. The rest of the chapter is a long roll call of Judah's towns by region, closing on the note that Jerusalem's original residents were never actually driven out.
JOS 16:1-10	The border of Ephraim and Manasseh, Joseph's two tribes, gets traced the same way Judah's did, town by boundary marker. One detail breaks the pattern: the Canaanites in Gezer are never driven out, just put to forced labor, which becomes a recurring theme.
JOS 17:1-18	Manasseh's territory is divided, and Zelophehad's five daughters, named individually, collect the inheritance Moses promised them since their father had no sons. Several towns' Canaanite residents are again left in place as forced labor rather than driven out. Joseph's tribe complains about getting only one allotment; Joshua tells them to go clear forest land themselves if they're so numerous, chariots of iron or not.
JOS 18:1-28	The tabernacle moves to Shiloh, and Joshua calls out the seven tribes still dragging their feet on claiming land. He sends surveyors to map what's left into seven portions, then casts lots. Benjamin draws first, with a border description followed by a list of twenty-six cities, Jerusalem among them, though it's still Jebusite at this point.
JOS 19:1-51	The remaining six lots get assigned: Simeon tucked inside Judah's oversized territory, then Zebulun, Issachar, Asher, and Naphtali by list of towns. Dan struggles to hold its assigned land and ends up conquering Leshem instead, renaming it Dan after their ancestor. Joshua himself finally gets an inheritance, the town he asked for, and the whole distribution is declared finished.
JOS 20:1-9	Six cities of refuge are designated, three on each side of the Jordan, where someone who kills accidentally can flee the victim's avenging relative and get a fair hearing instead of a knife in the back. He stays there until the current high priest dies, then goes home.
JOS 21:1-20	The Levites, who own no territory, are given forty-eight cities carved out of everyone else's land instead. Aaron's descendants get thirteen of them from Judah, Simeon and Benjamin, Hebron among them, though its farmland had already gone to Caleb personally rather than the priests. Next time on Joshua: the remaining forty-eight, and a closing line worth the wait.
JOS 21:21-45	The rest of the Levite cities are handed out to the remaining priestly families, forty-eight total, three of them doubling as cities of refuge. The chapter closes by stepping back: Yahweh gave Israel everything he promised, all of it, and not one word of it failed.
JOS 22:1-18	Joshua sends the eastern tribes home with a blessing and a share of the plunder, their duty fulfilled. On the way, they build a massive altar by the Jordan, and the rest of Israel, hearing about it secondhand, assumes rebellion and mobilizes for war, sending Phinehas to confront them before anyone strikes first. Next time on Joshua: the altar turns out not to be an altar.
JOS 22:19-34	The eastern tribes explain themselves: the altar isn't for sacrifice, it's a replica, a witness so future generations won't tell their children they have no share in Yahweh. Phinehas and the delegation accept the explanation, war is called off, and the altar gets named accordingly: Witness.
JOS 23:1-16	Old and near the end, Joshua gathers Israel's leaders for a farewell speech that's mostly warning: don't marry into the remaining nations, don't mention their gods, or the very blessings Yahweh delivered will flip into curses just as completely. He's seen the promises kept in full; he's equally sure about what happens if they don't hold up their end.
JOS 24:1-16	At Shechem, Joshua recites the whole national story in Yahweh's own first person voice, Abraham through the conquest, then puts the choice plainly: serve the old gods or serve Yahweh, but choose. His own answer is the famous one, as for me and my house, we will serve Yahweh. The people answer that they will too. Next time on Joshua: Joshua tells them they cannot do it.
JOS 24:17-33	The people confirm their choice, and Joshua, oddly, pushes back: you can't actually serve Yahweh, he's holy and jealous and won't forgive a casual betrayal. They insist anyway. A covenant is made, a witness stone set up, and Joshua dies at a hundred and ten, buried in the hill country he was given. Joseph's bones, carried out of Egypt generations earlier, are finally buried too.
RUT 1:1-22	Famine sends Elimelech's family to Moab, where both sons marry local women before Elimelech and then both sons die, leaving three widows. Naomi heads home and urges her daughters-in-law to stay behind; Orpah does, but Ruth refuses with the line that gets read at weddings, your people will be my people. They arrive in Bethlehem at harvest time, and Naomi asks to be called Mara, bitter, instead of her own name.
RUT 2:1-23	Ruth goes gleaning to feed them both and happens, by no plan of her own, into the field of Boaz, a relative of her dead father-in-law. He notices her, offers protection and extra grain left behind on purpose, and she comes home with far more than a normal day's gleaning. Naomi recognizes the name immediately: Boaz is family, a close enough relative to matter.
RUT 3:1-18	Naomi sends Ruth to the threshing floor at night with a plan: wait until Boaz falls asleep, then uncover his feet and lie down there, a request for him to take on the role of family protector. He wakes startled, agrees in principle, but discloses there's a nearer relative with first right of refusal. He sends her home before dawn, discreetly, with six measures of barley as a message to Naomi.
RUT 4:1-22	Boaz takes the matter to the city gate and offers the nearer kinsman first right to redeem Elimelech's land. The man agrees until he learns Ruth comes with it and it would complicate his own inheritance; he backs out, formalized with the old shoe-removing custom, and Boaz takes both the land and Ruth. Their son Obed becomes David's grandfather, and the book closes with the genealogy connecting Perez to David.
NAM 1:1-15	Nahum opens on Yahweh as a jealous avenger and spends the whole chapter describing what happens to mountains and seas when he arrives angry. The target is Nineveh, and the comfort in it is aimed entirely at the people Nineveh has been standing on.
1KI 1:1-27	The book opens with a king who cannot get warm. Adonijah, next in line by age, runs Absalom's playbook exactly: chariots, horsemen, fifty runners. The narrator adds that his father had never once asked him why. The guest list at his coronation barbecue is the giveaway, everyone except the prophet, the general's rival and the brother. Nathan's counter-move is choreography. Neither he nor Bathsheba argues policy. They argue that she and her son are about to be executed. Next time on 1 Kings: the old man wakes up.
1KI 1:28-53	Given a clear question, David answers in one sentence, then organises a coronation with the precision of a man who has run a revolt: my mule, that spring, those two clerics, one trumpet. The mule matters, being a public loan of the king's own status. Adonijah's party is still eating when the noise arrives, and Joab, who has heard a lot of trumpets, asks what it means. The guests do not say goodbye. Adonijah, clinging to the altar, gets a conditional oath and an instruction to go home.
1KI 2:1-23	David's deathbed speech has two parts that do not match. The first is a fine exhortation to keep the law of Moses. The second is a hit list: Joab, who shed blood in peacetime, and Shimei, whom David personally swore not to kill and who is therefore to be killed by someone else. Then Adonijah, let off once already, asks for the woman who shared his father's bed, and sends the request through Solomon's mother. Next time on 1 Kings: the list gets worked through.
1KI 2:24-46	The list is settled in one chapter, with an efficiency meant to be admired. Adonijah dies the same day. Abiathar is spared for old service and exiled, closing an account opened against Eli's house. Joab grabs the altar as Adonijah did, and this time the altar is not a loophole; he says he will die there and is obliged. Shimei keeps his house arrest three years, until two runaway slaves make him forget. The last line is the point: the kingdom was established in Solomon's hand.
1KI 3:1-28	A dynastic marriage to Egypt, and a note that king and country alike still sacrifice on hilltops, no temple existing yet. At Gibeon God offers a blank cheque and Solomon spends it on an understanding heart, calling himself a child who does not know how to go out or come in. He gets that, plus wealth and honour he did not request. The demonstration follows, and it works because it is a trap rather than a judgment: nobody who wants the child alive will accept half of one.
1KI 4:1-34	An administrative chapter, and the interesting part is the plumbing. Twelve officers, one month each, feeding a court that gets through thirty cors of flour and a hundred sheep a day before you count the gazelles. Two of the twelve are married to Solomon's daughters, which is how you keep a supply chain loyal. Then the wisdom: three thousand proverbs, a thousand and five songs, a curriculum running from Lebanese cedars to the hyssop in the wall. Somebody is paying for all this.
1KI 5:1-18	A trade negotiation conducted entirely in compliments. Solomon explains that his father was too busy fighting to build, and that no Israelite can cut timber like a Sidonian, which is flattery and also true. Hiram is delighted and proposes floating the cedar down the coast in rafts. The price is food, twenty thousand cors of wheat and twenty of pure oil a year. Then the labour figures, stated without comment: thirty thousand conscripts in monthly shifts, seventy thousand carriers, eighty thousand cutting stone.
1KI 6:1-18	The temple gets a precise date and precise dimensions, and if the numbers slide past you the book does not mind. The detail it wants you to keep is the silence: every stone was dressed at the quarry so that no hammer, axe or iron tool was heard on the site. God interrupts the construction report with a conditional, keep my commandments and I will live among my people. Then cedar over everything, until no stone is visible. Next time on 1 Kings: they start gilding it.
1KI 6:19-38	This is the gold section, and the word appears in almost every verse: gold walls, gold altar, gold floor, gold chains across the doorway, gold on the carvings and the doors and the hinges. Two olive-wood cherubim ten cubits tall stand in the inner room with their wings spanning the whole width, wingtip to wall on either side and touching in the middle, so the ark sits under a ceiling of wings. Then the flat closing accountancy: founded in Ziv, finished in Bul, seven years.
1KI 7:1-26	Note the arithmetic the book leaves unsaid: seven years on God's house, thirteen on Solomon's. His complex includes a hall so full of cedar columns it is called the House of the Forest of Lebanon, a courtroom, and a separate house for his Egyptian wife. Then Hiram of Tyre arrives, and the tone changes into loving technical description. Two hollow bronze pillars eighteen cubits high, named Jachin and Boaz. A bronze sea holding two thousand baths on twelve oxen. Next time on 1 Kings: ten wheeled basins.
1KI 7:27-51	Ten bronze stands on ten bronze chariot wheels, cast to one measure and one pattern. Somebody designed mobile washing units for a building nobody was going to move, and the text describes their axles with genuine affection. Then the inventory, all cast in clay moulds down in the Jordan plain and hauled uphill. The line that gives the game away is that Solomon left the bronze unweighed because there was too much of it. Then the gold, down to the door hinges.
1KI 8:1-24	The ark comes up to the temple a detail the writer cannot resist: the carrying poles are long enough to be seen from the holy place, and they are still there. Noted with no comment at all, the ark contains nothing but the two stone tablets, a very quiet sentence in a very expensive building. Solomon's opening remark is the best in the chapter: God said he would live in thick darkness, so here is a house. Next time on 1 Kings: the prayer itself.
1KI 8:25-44	The prayer's foundation is a concession no builder wants to make: heaven cannot contain you, so obviously this cannot either. Then it becomes a list of contingencies, and the list is what makes it great, because it assumes failure throughout. Perjury, defeat, drought, famine, plague, blight, locusts, siege, and every man knowing the plague of his own heart. Each case ends the same way, hear, and forgive. Even the foreigner from far away gets an answer. Next time on 1 Kings: he plans for exile.
1KI 8:45-66	The prayer ends by planning for the worst thing that can happen, which is the thing that eventually happens: deportation. His premise is that there is no man who does not sin, so his answer is that repentance works from anywhere, prayed toward a place rather than in it. The accountancy afterwards is preposterous and probably meant to be: twenty-two thousand cattle, a hundred and twenty thousand sheep, and an altar too small to hold the offering.
1KI 9:1-28	God's second appearance is the first promise with the conditional made explicit and the failure case spelled out: the house cast away, the nation a proverb, passers-by hissing at the ruin. That paragraph is the whole rest of Kings in advance. Hiram gets twenty Galilean towns for twenty years of timber and gold, inspects them, dislikes them, and names the district something rude. A Red Sea fleet crewed by Tyrians brings back four hundred and twenty talents of gold.
1KI 10:1-29	A visiting monarch arrives to test the famous wisdom with hard questions, and the text is more interested in the tour: the table, the seating plan. Then the inventory piles up: gold shields in a cedar hall, an ivory throne with fourteen lions, gold cups because silver had stopped being worth counting, apes and peacocks. The annual gold intake is six hundred and sixty-six talents. And chariots and Egyptian horses, which the law of the king specifically forbade.
1KI 11:1-23	Seven hundred wives and three hundred concubines, and the charge is not the arithmetic but the shrines, since Solomon builds hilltop sanctuaries for Chemosh and Molech within sight of the temple he built for Yahweh. The verdict comes with an aggravating factor attached: God had appeared to him twice. The sentence is the kingdom torn away, deferred to his son's reign and softened by one tribe, both concessions made for David rather than for him. Next time on 1 Kings: a prophet ruins a good coat.
1KI 11:24-43	Two adversaries and one employee. Jeroboam is the more dangerous, and the reason is in his job title: Solomon promoted him for being industrious and put him over the forced labour of the northern tribes, so the man who knows what the building programme cost is now the man with a following. Ahijah's prophecy is performance art, a brand new coat torn into twelve pieces in an empty field. Solomon's response to a divine announcement is an assassination attempt. Forty years, and the obituary is one line.
1KI 12:1-17	The kingdom is lost in a single meeting. The north's request is reasonable: your father worked us too hard, ease it and we are yours. Rehoboam consults the men who actually ran the country and receives the best political advice in scripture, be their servant today and you will have them forever. Israel answers with the old rebel slogan, and this time nobody puts it down. Next time on 1 Kings: he sends out the forced-labour minister.
1KI 12:18-33	Rehoboam's response to a general strike is to send the minister for forced labour, who is stoned to death. The army musters and is stood down by a prophet on the grounds that the split is God's doing. Jeroboam's problem is genuine, since a pilgrimage route to a rival capital is a security threat, and his solution becomes the sin the book measures every northern king against: two gold calves, an improvised priesthood, and a festival moved a month.
1KI 13:1-17	A prophet from Judah interrupts a royal sacrifice by talking to the furniture. Jeroboam points at him and his arm locks in that position, and the altar splits on cue. The king then asks the man who just cursed him to pray for his hand, it works, and then offers him dinner. The refusal is specific: no bread, no water, and go home by another road. Next time on 1 Kings: the lie.
1KI 13:18-34	The old prophet's line is that he is a prophet too and an angel gave him a countermanding order, and the narrator states flatly that he lied, without ever explaining why. The man of God accepts a second-hand revelation over the one he received himself. The death is strange and precise: a lion that kills him and then stands guard, eating neither the body nor the donkey. The old prophet buries him in his own grave. Jeroboam, given every possible sign, changes nothing.
1KI 14:1-14	Jeroboam sends his wife in disguise to the prophet who made him king, which tells you he knows what the answer will be and hopes anonymity improves it. What follows is the template every northern dynasty gets: I raised you up, you were not David, and now the whole house goes like sweepings. The one mercy is the child, who dies before she gets home and is the only one with some good found in him. Next time on 1 Kings: the door.
1KI 14:15-31	The sentence widens from one family to a whole nation: Israel shaken like a reed. Then the domestic detail that makes it land, the child dying exactly as his mother crosses her own threshold. The camera turns south, where Rehoboam is no better, and Shishak arrives in year five and simply removes the treasury, including Solomon's gold shields. Rehoboam has bronze replicas made for the door guard to carry in and out, which is the perfect image for the reign and for the rest of the book.
1KI 15:1-19	Two Judean kings, one paragraph each. Abijam is bad and survives anyway because of a promise made to his great-grandfather, and the narrator sums up David as faultless except in the matter of Uriah the Hittite, which is a remarkable amount of weight for one subordinate clause. The one blemish is that he leaves the hill shrines standing, the standard qualification for every decent king here. Then he bribes Syria with the temple treasury to attack Israel from behind. Next time on 1 Kings: Damascus takes the money.
1KI 15:20-34	Damascus takes the money and burns its way through the north, and Baasha drops his blockade. Asa then does the thing that makes him competent rather than merely pious, conscripting the whole country to carry Ramah away stone by stone and rebuild it as two fortresses of his own. The north begins its cycle: Nadab lasts two years before an officer murders him at a siege, and the officer's first act as king is to exterminate the previous family. Then Baasha does exactly what Jeroboam did.
1KI 16:1-17	The prophet Jehu delivers Baasha the speech Ahijah gave Jeroboam, almost word for word, which is the point: the north keeps producing the same king under different names. Then the machinery accelerates. Elah gets two years and is murdered by his own chariot commander while drunk, and Zimri's first official act is exterminating the family, exactly as Baasha had done. Zimri then holds the throne for seven days, which the text states with a straight face. Next time on 1 Kings: seven days is up.
1KI 16:18-34	Zimri's exit is to lock himself in the palace and burn it down around himself, and even he gets the standard verdict for walking in the way of Jeroboam, over a reign of one week. the one durable thing anyone manages in this stretch: buys a hill for two talents of silver and builds Samaria on it. His verdict of worse than everyone before him lasts exactly one paragraph, because Ahab arrives and takes the record.
1KI 17:1-24	Elijah arrives with no introduction and no genealogy, announces a drought, and vanishes. He is fed by ravens, which is not a detail anybody would invent, until the drought he announced dries up his own water supply. Then he is sent to a widow in Sidon, Jezebel's own country, who is down to one meal for herself and her son, and asked to be served first. She does it, and the flour and oil hold. The child lives, and her closing line is that now, finally, she knows.
1KI 18:1-24	The best character here is Obadiah, who runs Ahab's household while secretly keeping a hundred prophets alive in two caves, and whose reaction to meeting Elijah is professional terror: the Spirit will move you, Ahab will find nothing, and I will be the one who dies. Then the challenge, its terms calculated: their choice of bull, their turn first, four hundred and fifty to one, no fire laid by anybody. Next time on 1 Kings: they hop for a whole day.
1KI 18:25-46	Baal gets a full working day and the home advantage, and produces nothing. Elijah's heckling at noon is the funniest thing in the Old Testament and also a serious argument: perhaps he is thinking, or away, or asleep. Elijah's own preparation removes every excuse, twelve stones, a trench, twelve jars of water, and his prayer asks one thing, that the people know. Then fire takes the offering, the stones, the dust and the standing water. Then the Kishon, a very small cloud, and a prophet outrunning a chariot.
1SA 1:1-28	A household with two wives, one fertile and cruel about it, one childless and adored, and an annual pilgrimage that reopens the wound. Eli's assessment of a woman praying silently in his own temple is that she is drunk. She bears Samuel, then does the astonishing thing: weans him and hands him over permanently.
1SA 2:1-20	Hannah's song is not a thank-you note, it is a manifesto: bows broken, the barren bearing seven, the poor moved to the throne. It ends by mentioning a king, chapters before Israel has one. Then Eli's sons, whose priesthood is a servant stabbing the pot with a fork before God's portion burns. Next time on 1 Samuel: someone says it to Eli's face.
1SA 2:21-36	Eli's response to his sons sleeping with the women at the Tent door is a mild why do you do such things. Then a man of God does the job properly: a withdrawn promise, no old men left in the family line, and a dated receipt attached, both sons dead on the same day. The replacement is already in the building, wearing a small ephod.
1SA 3:1-21	Visions are rare, Eli's eyesight is going, and the first word from God in years arrives as a child mistaking it for his boss calling him, three times. What God then says is a message about Eli, delivered to Eli's apprentice. Eli insists on hearing his family's ruin, and answers only that God should do what seems good to him.
1SA 4:1-22	Down four thousand men, Israel's plan is to fetch the ark, on the theory that God travels and can be deployed. The second battle is worse: thirty thousand dead, the ark captured, both of Eli's sons killed on the same day exactly as promised. Eli takes the casualty list without falling; it is the word about the ark that kills him.
1SA 5:1-12	The Philistines install their trophy next to Dagon, which is either piety or gloating. Morning one, Dagon is face down before the ark. Morning two, he is face down again and has lost his head and both hands. Then the tumors start, Ashdod makes the ark Gath's problem, Gath forwards it to Ekron, and it takes three cities to notice.
1SA 6:1-21	Seven months in, the Philistines consult their own religious professionals, who advise compensation: five golden tumors and five golden mice, plus a pointed reference to how well hardening your heart worked out for Pharaoh. The test is neat. Two cows that have never pulled anything, calves locked up at home, nobody steering. They walk the highway to Beth Shemesh in a straight line.
1SA 7:1-17	The ark sits in a private house on a hill for twenty years, which is what a national emergency looks like once everyone has adjusted to it. Samuel's terms are unpopular: get rid of the other gods first, then talk about deliverance. He is halfway through a lamb when God thunders. He plants a stone and names it Help Up To Now.
1SA 8:1-22	Samuel's succession plan is his sons, and his sons take bribes, which is precisely the failure Eli's sons demonstrated. God's reading is that the rejection is his, not Samuel's, and has been running since Egypt. The disclosure is a job description written as a bill of costs: your sons, your daughters, your best fields, a tenth of everything, and no answer when you complain.
1SA 9:1-13	Israel's first king is introduced by two facts: he is very tall and he cannot find his father's donkeys. Saul's instinct after three fruitless regions is to give up in case his father starts worrying about him rather than the livestock. It is the unnamed servant who knows there is a seer in town. Next time on 1 Samuel: the seer has been expecting him.
1SA 9:14-27	Samuel had a day's notice, so the meeting only looks like a coincidence. Saul walks up to the most famous prophet in Israel and asks him for directions to his own house. Samuel implies rather grandly that everything desirable in Israel is his, at which Saul takes refuge in his tribe's insignificance. The kingdom is hinted, seated, fed, and arranged for privately at dawn.
1SA 10:1-27	The anointing comes with three verifiable predictions: two men with news, three men with bread, and a procession of prophets whose music sweeps Saul up so completely that his neighbours coin a proverb. The first thing he does with his new heart is not mention the kingdom to his uncle. The lot at Mizpah then narrows to a man hiding in the luggage.
1SA 11:1-15	Nahash's peace terms are the right eye of every man in Jabesh Gilead, offered specifically as a humiliation to Israel, and he is confident enough to grant them a week to look for help. Saul hears the news while walking home behind his oxen, which is where the newly crowned king apparently still is. Then the oxen chopped up and posted around the country.
1SA 12:1-25	Samuel's retirement speech opens with an audit of himself, which he passes, having invited anyone present to name a single ox he ever took. Then the history lesson, in which every rescue comes without a king, arriving at the awkward observation that they asked for one while God was already holding the post. The proof is meteorological: thunder and rain in wheat harvest.
1SA 13:1-23	The war starts because Jonathan attacks a garrison and Saul blows the trumpet as though he had planned it. Against thirty thousand chariots the national response is to hide in caves, thickets, rocks, tombs and pits. Saul waits the appointed seven days, watches his army leak away, and performs the sacrifice himself, and Samuel walks in on the last word of it.
1SA 14:1-26	Jonathan's plan is two men against a garrison, with a decision procedure attached: if the enemy says wait, they wait; if the enemy says come up, God has handed them over. The Philistines choose the second option purely to be insulting. Twenty men die in the space of half a furrow, and the panic spreads until the ground shakes. Next time on 1 Samuel: honey.
1SA 14:27-52	Jonathan, having missed the oath entirely, eats honey off the end of his stick and delivers the verdict on his father's leadership in one line: he has troubled the land, and a fed army would have won bigger. God then declines to answer about a night attack, so Saul goes hunting for the sin, swearing to kill even his son. The army stops him.
1SA 15:1-35	The order is total destruction of Amalek, and Saul carries out most of it, which turns out to be the problem. He keeps the king and the good livestock, destroys everything worthless, and reports full compliance to a prophet who can hear sheep. Then the excuses, in order: the people did it, and it was for sacrifice anyway, to your God.
1SA 16:1-23	God's instruction is to stop mourning and start anointing; Samuel's first response is that Saul will kill him, so the trip is disguised as a sacrifice. Samuel then repeats Israel's original mistake by admiring the tallest son present, and is corrected on the spot. Then the boy anointed to replace Saul is hired to soothe him, and Saul loves him greatly.
1SA 17:1-31	Two armies on two hills doing nothing for forty days while one enormous man in an inventory's worth of bronze proposes to settle the war by single combat, and nobody accepts. Into this walks a delivery boy whose first act on hearing the challenge is market research: what exactly does the man who kills him get? Next time on 1 Samuel: the interview.
1SA 17:32-58	David's job application consists of two references, a lion and a bear, and the argument that a giant who has defied the living God is functionally another predator. Saul lends him the royal armour, and David takes it straight back off on the grounds that he has not tested it, which is the most professional line in the chapter. Then it is over in one throw. The strangest detail comes last: Saul, who employed this boy as his armour bearer, asks Abner who he is.
1SA 18:1-30	Jonathan, the crown prince, hands the newcomer his robe, sword, bow and belt, which is either friendship or an abdication in mime. Then the women invent the worst possible chant: Saul his thousands, David his ten thousands. Saul does the arithmetic out loud and concludes only the kingdom is left. Two spears thrown at a man playing music for him. A daughter promised and quietly married off elsewhere. Then a bride price of a hundred Philistine foreskins, which David pays at twice the asking rate.
1SA 19:1-24	Saul's plan to have David killed is undone by his own family, twice in one chapter. Then Michal lowers her husband out of a window, dresses the bed with an idol and goats' hair, and tells her father that David threatened her, a good lie efficiently deployed. The chapter ends strangely and rather beautifully: three waves of arresting officers arrive at Naioth and start prophesying instead, and when Saul goes himself he ends up stripped on the ground for a day and a night.
1SA 20:1-20	David wants to know what he is guilty of; Jonathan's defence is that his father tells him everything, which is the kind of thing a son believes right up until it stops being true. Jonathan's own request is stranger and sadder: keep faith with my family when your enemies are all destroyed, which is his way of saying he knows how this ends. Next time on 1 Samuel: three arrows.
1SA 20:21-42	The code is set first: Jonathan suspects which signal he will need. Day one, Saul lets the empty seat pass as a purity problem. Day two he asks, hears the Bethlehem story, and the mask comes off: an insult aimed at Jonathan's mother, a flat statement that he will never inherit while David lives, then a spear thrown at his own son. That answers the question. Jonathan leaves hungry, says the code words aloud to a boy who has no idea what he is carrying, and David weeps the most.
1SA 21:1-15	David arrives at Nob with no escort and an explanation nobody asked for, the mark of an improvised lie: secret royal business, cannot say more. What he wants is food, and the only food is the bread reserved for God, handed over after a purity question. Then he wants a weapon, and the one available is Goliath's, kept behind the ephod. The chapter notes quietly that Doeg the Edomite was present, and leaves it there. In Gath the giant killer saves his life by drooling into his beard.
1SA 22:1-23	David's first command is assembled from everyone in the country who is broke, desperate or angry, four hundred of them. Saul, meanwhile, is under a tree with a spear, telling his Benjamites that nobody feels sorry for him, and Doeg answers the invitation. Ahimelech's defence is perfectly good and completely irrelevant. Then the atrocity: Saul orders the priests killed, his own guards refuse, and Doeg kills eighty-five and destroys Nob entirely, its children and its animals. One son escapes. David says plainly that the deaths are on him.
1SA 23:1-29	David asks God about Keilah, is told to go, then asks again because his men are frightened, and God repeats himself without complaint. He wins, and the town he just saved becomes the trap: Saul is delighted that David has shut himself behind gates and bars. So David puts two practical questions to the ephod, will Saul come and will Keilah hand me over, and gets yes and yes. Jonathan visits to say out loud that David will be king. Then a Philistine raid saves David by accident.
1SA 24:1-22	Three thousand men hunt David, and their commander walks alone into the one cave David is hiding at the back of. His men read this as prophecy fulfilled. David's compromise is vandalism on the royal wardrobe, followed by guilt about it. The speech from the hillside is a masterpiece of having it both ways: here is the hem, your life was in my hands, and also I am a flea beneath your notice. Saul weeps, concedes David will be king, and asks that his family not be wiped out.
1SA 25:1-23	Samuel gets one verse. Then a story about a rich man's sheep. David's message to Nabal is protection-racket phrasing: long life, peace, and by the way your shepherds came to no harm while we were around, so anything you can spare. Nabal's reply is the most efficient insult in the book. Four hundred swords are on the road within a verse. A servant, correctly judging his employer unreachable, goes to Abigail, who loads donkeys and rides out without mentioning it to her husband. Next time on 1 Samuel: she talks.
1SA 25:24-44	Abigail's speech is the best piece of negotiation in the book. She takes blame she does not owe, dismisses her husband on etymological grounds, then asks whether the king he is going to be will want a massacre in his memoirs. David agrees on the spot and admits he was hours from killing everything male in the house. Nabal, oblivious, is throwing a party. She waits until he is sober, tells him, and something in him gives out. Ten days later he is dead, and David proposes.
1SA 26:1-25	The Ziphites inform on David a second time, and this time he goes on the offensive, walking into a sleeping camp of three thousand with one companion. Abishai's offer is precise: one stroke, no second. David refuses and takes the spear and water jug, worth more as evidence than a corpse. Then he stands on a hill and roasts Abner for failing to guard the king. Saul's confession is fuller this time, and worth as much as the last.
1SA 27:1-12	David's solution to being hunted by his king is to defect: Saul stops looking the moment he hears. He asks for a town of his own rather than a room in the capital, the request of a man who does not want his movements observed, and gets Ziklag. What follows is sixteen months of double bookkeeping: he raids the desert peoples, leaves no witnesses, and reports them as attacks on southern Judah, his own people. Achish congratulates himself on a servant for life.
1SA 28:1-25	Saul, who expelled every medium in Israel, spends it looking for one, God having gone silent in all approved channels. He goes in disguise and swears by the God not answering him that she will not be prosecuted under his own law. She is the professional, and the only one kind to him. Samuel arrives annoyed and adds nothing: the kingdom is David's, and tomorrow you will be with me. Saul falls flat, and the witch of Endor bakes bread for the man who outlawed her.
1SA 29:1-11	David marches to war against Israel in the Philistine rear guard, an impossible position their command solves. Their argument is excellent: the surest way for this man to buy back Saul's favour is with our heads, and we know the song. Achish, fond of David, gives him a glowing reference and dismisses him anyway. David's protest is masterfully ambiguous: why may he not fight the enemies of my lord the king, a phrase that could name either. He rides away from the battle that kills Saul.
1SA 30:1-15	David comes home to a burned town and no families. The raiders took everyone alive, the chapter's only mercy. His men weep until they cannot, then discuss stoning him. He consults God and gets a generous answer: pursue, and you will recover all of it. Then the rescue's pivot turns up in a field: an Egyptian slave abandoned when he fell ill, who needs only bread and figs before walking his old employers into an ambush. Next time on 1 Samuel: the party.
1SA 30:16-31	They find the Amalekites scattered across open ground over their loot, the worst possible formation. The fighting runs dusk to next evening; only the four hundred with camels get away. Everything is recovered, nothing missing. The real test: his harder men propose the brook two hundred get their families and nothing else. David overrules them: the man with the baggage shares equally with the man in the line. It becomes law. He posts plunder to Judean towns, generosity and also campaigning.
1SA 31:1-13	The battle is reported without comment. Israel breaks on Gilboa, Jonathan dies with two brothers, and Saul, wounded, asks his bearer for the one favour left, is refused out of terror, does it himself, and the bearer follows. Everything Samuel said the night before happens on schedule. Saul's head and armour go on tour. The last act belongs to Jabesh Gilead, the town he rescued in his first week, whose men walk all night to take his body off a wall and fast seven days.
2SA 1:1-27	A messenger arrives with the correct clothes, the correct dirt, and an improved version of events in which he personally finished Saul off at Saul's own request, plus the crown and bracelet as proof. He has misjudged his audience completely. David mourns, fasts, then asks the one question that matters, why he was not afraid to kill Yahweh's anointed, and has him cut down on the spot. The lament that follows is the finest poetry in the book and mentions Saul's failings not once.
2SA 2:1-32	David asks where to go and is told Hebron, where Judah crowns him, and his first political act is a thank-you note to the town that buried his predecessor. Abner installs Saul's surviving son as a rival king, and the civil war opens with an atrocity dressed up as sport: twelve men each, let the young men play before us, twenty-four dead in one exchange. Asahel is warned off twice, then killed with the wrong end of a spear.
2SA 3:1-21	The war grinds on in one sentence, and the family expands in six. Then the alliance collapses over a question of etiquette: Ishbosheth accuses his own general of sleeping with his father's concubine, and Abner's reply is the sound of a man who has been holding a kingdom together and is done. David's only condition is the return of Michal, which is a claim on Saul's line as much as a marriage. Next time on 2 Samuel: Joab gets back.
2SA 3:22-39	Joab comes home to find his brother's killer entertained and released, his briefing to David thin cover for what he has already decided. He recalls Abner from the road, takes him aside in the gateway, and kills him. David's response is a masterclass in managing a murder he did not order: a public curse on Joab's house, sackcloth, a walk behind the bier, a quotable lament, a fast until sundown. It works. The last line is the honest one: he is king, and the sons of Zeruiah are stronger.
2SA 4:1-12	Two of Ishbosheth's own officers kill him asleep at noon, remove his head, carry it overnight to Hebron and present it as a service rendered. They have not been paying attention. David has already executed one man for claiming to have finished off a king, then draws the conclusion about murdering a defenceless man in bed. What happens to them is thorough and public. Tucked into the middle is the note that Jonathan has a surviving son, dropped by his nurse at five. Remember him.
2SA 5:1-25	The tribes arrive with a speech about bone and flesh and a reminder that David led while Saul was king. He takes Jerusalem despite a Jebusite taunt about the blind and lame keeping him out. Hiram of Tyre sends cedar and craftsmen, a foreign king assessing the situation. More wives, more sons, one named Solomon, filed without comment. Then two Philistine campaigns in the same valley, and David asks before each: go, then do not go, circle behind, and wait for marching in the treetops.
2SA 6:1-23	Thirty thousand men, a new cart, a party. Then the oxen stumble, a man steadies the ark, and he is dead. David's reaction is not piety but anger and fear, and the ark sits three months at a stranger's house. He tries again on hearing the stranger is getting rich. Then sacrifices every six paces, a king dancing in a linen ephod, watched from a window by Michal. Her line about the servants of his servants cuts, and his reply, more undignified still, changes everything between them.
2SA 7:1-29	David notices he has cedar and God has curtains, and proposes to fix it. Nathan says yes, corrected overnight, a lesson about answering prophecy off the cuff. The reply is a refusal around an enormous promise: God never asked for a building, and has been content in a tent since Egypt. You wanted to build me a house; I will build yours. The son builds it, the dynasty outlasts him, discipline comes without the withdrawal that ended Saul. David sits down, an odd posture for prayer and exactly right.
