2SA 8:1-18	A chapter of conquests written like a ledger. Moab is defeated and measured out with a rope, two lengths executed to one spared, the most chilling sentence in the book in the flattest possible voice. Hadadezer loses seventeen hundred horsemen and every chariot horse but a hundred, hamstrung. Damascus sends help and loses twenty-two thousand men. A king who hated Hadadezer sends congratulations in silver and gold, dedicated rather than banked. The refrain repeats twice, Yahweh gave victory wherever he went, and the cabinet list arrives.
2SA 9:1-13	David asks whether anyone of Saul's house is left to be kind to, not a safe question for the person being looked for, since new kings usually ask it for the other reason. Ziba produces Jonathan's crippled son from a place called Lo Debar, and the man arrives on his face calling himself a dead dog. What he gets is his grandfather's land, a permanent place at the royal table, and a staff to farm it. The chapter ends by repeating that he was lame in both feet.
2SA 10:1-19	David sends a condolence delegation to the new Ammonite king, whose advisers decide the sympathy is reconnaissance. Hanun's response is a diplomatic insult with real teeth: half a beard each and their clothes cut off at the waist. David quietly parks them in Jericho until they are presentable. Then the bill for the joke arrives. Ammon hires thirty-three thousand mercenaries, Joab finds himself with enemies front and back, and splits the army in two, telling Abishai they will each rescue the other.
2SA 11:1-27	The chapter opens with the season when kings go out to war and the note that David stayed home, and everything follows from that sentence. He sees a woman from the roof, sends for her, and she is pregnant. The cover-up is where the horror is. Uriah is told three times, increasingly bluntly, to go home to his wife, and refuses each time because the army is sleeping in a field. He then carries his own death warrant to Joab.
2SA 12:1-16	Nathan does not accuse, he litigates. A rich man with flocks, a poor man with one lamb that ate from his plate and slept in his arms, a guest to feed, and the obvious theft. David, who has spent his life judging cases, delivers a furious verdict, and then hears the four words. The sentence is not death but consequence: the sword stays in his house, and what he did behind a curtain will be done to him in daylight. Next time on 2 Samuel: seven days.
2SA 12:17-31	For a week David lies on the ground and will not eat, and when the child dies his servants are too frightened to tell him. Instead he washes, dresses, worships, and asks for food, and explains himself with a clarity nobody around him has: while there was a chance I begged, and now there is none, and I will go to him rather than him coming back to me. Then Solomon is born, and God loves him. The chapter ends with forced labour, saws and brick kilns.
2SA 13:1-18	The consequences promised in the last chapter begin inside the family. Amnon wants his half-sister and makes himself ill over it, and his cousin Jonadab supplies the plan, which relies on David doing as he is asked without asking why. Tamar is sent to cook for a man pretending to be sick, and the room is cleared. What she says is careful and clear: this is not done in Israel, ask the king for me properly. He does not listen. Next time on 2 Samuel: her brother.
2SA 13:19-39	Tamar tears her robe and cries aloud in the street, the only voice raised in the whole affair. Absalom tells her to keep quiet, and she lives desolate in his house. David is very angry, and that is the entire extent of David's response, and that failure sets up everything that follows. Absalom says nothing to Amnon for two years, then throws a sheep shearing party and works his father into sending his brothers along. Amnon is killed drunk.
2SA 14:1-17	Joab, reading his king correctly, hires a professional. The woman of Tekoa arrives in mourning with a case designed to look like a legal problem: two sons, a field, a killing, and a widow about to lose her last heir. David rules in her favour twice, until she has a royal oath in hand, and then she springs it, asking why the king applies one rule to her son and another to his own banished one. Next time on 2 Samuel: David asks who wrote it.
2SA 14:18-33	David spots the ghostwriter in one question and the woman admits it while managing to compliment him, which is why Joab hired her. Absalom is fetched home and then held at arm's length: back in the city, barred from the king's presence, for two more years. The narrator inserts here that Absalom was the handsomest man in Israel and cut his hair annually because of the weight of it, five pounds, which will matter later. When Joab twice ignores his summons, Absalom's solution is arson, which works immediately.
2SA 15:1-19	Absalom runs the most efficient campaign in scripture: a chariot, fifty runners, and a spot by the gate where he tells every litigant that his case is excellent and the crown has failed to provide a judge, followed by a personal embrace. Two years of that and the country is his. The coup itself is launched under cover of a religious vow at Hebron. David's response is immediate flight, not to save himself but to spare the city a sword. Next time on 2 Samuel: Ittai declines.
2SA 15:20-37	Ittai, a foreigner who arrived yesterday, gives the most loyal speech in the book, and David stops arguing. The whole country weeps at the Kidron. Then David starts making decisions that show he is a better general in flight than he was at rest: the ark goes back, because he will not use it as a talisman, and the priests go back with it as an intelligence network. On hearing his best adviser has defected he prays a single sentence.
2SA 16:1-23	Two men meet David on the road. Ziba brings food and a story about his master hoping to get the crown back, and gets Mephibosheth's entire estate on his word alone, which will look worse later. Shimei throws stones and calls the king a man of blood, and David's answer to Abishai is the most interesting thing he says in the whole revolt: my own son wants me dead, so why not this man. Ahithophel's first advice is to make the break public.
2SA 17:1-29	Ahithophel's plan is correct and would have worked: twelve thousand men, tonight, kill the king only, bring the country home. Hushai's counter-plan is designed to sound better while wasting the one thing David needs, which is time, and he sells it with flattery and a bear robbed of her cubs. Absalom takes the worse advice, and the narrator says plainly why. A woman at Bahurim hides the couriers in a well under grain. Ahithophel rides home, tidies his affairs, and hangs himself.
2SA 18:1-17	The army will not let David fight, on the grounds that he is worth ten thousand of them, which is both flattering and tactically true. He gives one order, loudly, in front of everyone, and it concerns his son. Then the forest of Ephraim, where the terrain kills more men than the fighting does. Absalom, whose hair the text made a point of weighing, is caught by the head in an oak while his mule walks on. Next time on 2 Samuel: somebody has to tell the father.
2SA 18:18-33	The chapter opens with the pillar Absalom built because he had no son to remember him, which the narrator places directly after burying him under rocks. Then a scene about who has to deliver the news. Joab will not send the priest's son, because the messenger's job is dangerous today, and sends a foreigner instead. Ahimaaz runs anyway and arrives first with nothing useful to say. Everything after that is one line repeated: my son, my son, and the wish to have died instead.
2SA 19:1-22	An army comes home from winning a war and has to sneak in, because the king is upstairs weeping for the man who tried to kill him. Joab's speech is brutal, accurate, and the only thing that works: you have humiliated the men who saved your family, and if you do not sit in that gate tonight you will be alone by morning. David sits in the gate. Next time on 2 Samuel: the other claimants.
2SA 19:23-43	Mephibosheth arrives having not washed, shaved or changed his clothes since the king left, which is an alibi you can see, and says Ziba lied about him and stole his donkey. David, who gave Ziba the estate on Ziba's word alone, splits the difference rather than admit he was conned, and Mephibosheth's reply, let him take all of it, you are home, is the most graceful thing in the chapter. Then the reunion sours into a quarrel about who owns the king.
2SA 20:1-26	The kingdom lasts about one chapter. Sheba blows a trumpet, says the north has no share in David, and takes Israel with him. David's homecoming includes locking up the ten concubines Absalom violated, provided for and shut away for life, which the text records without comment. Amasa misses his first deadline, and Joab settles the succession question himself, with a greeting, a hand on the beard, and a sword he had let fall. Then another wise woman stops a war with a conversation.
2SA 21:1-22	Three years of famine are traced back to an old broken oath, and the Gibeonites, offered compensation, ask for seven of Saul's descendants instead. David hands them over, sparing the one he swore to protect. What the chapter is actually about is Rizpah, who takes sackcloth up onto the rock and keeps the birds and the animals off her sons' bodies for months, alone, until the rain comes. Her vigil, not the execution, is what moves David to bury the whole family properly.
2SA 22:1-51	A long psalm of thanks, and the striking thing is the weather. David calls, and God does not simply answer, he comes: the earth shaking, smoke out of his nostrils, darkness under his feet, a cherub used as transport, and the seabed exposed by his breath, all of it to pull one man out of deep water. The middle section is where a modern reader flinches, since David spends several verses on his blameless conduct, though he is talking about the wars rather than the roof.
2SA 23:1-39	The last words first, and they are about good government: a just ruler is morning light after rain, and the wicked are thorns you cannot pick up bare-handed. Then the veterans' list, which is where the book keeps its affection. One man killed eight hundred at a sitting. One fought until his fingers locked around the hilt. Three broke an enemy line to fetch a drink for a homesick king, and he poured it on the ground. The roll ends with Uriah the Hittite.
2SA 24:1-25	A census, which sounds harmless and is treated as a catastrophe, and even Joab, who is not squeamish, asks the king why he wants it. Nine months and twenty days of counting produce one million three hundred thousand swordsmen and an immediate attack of conscience. David's reasoning for picking the plague is sound: God's mercy is larger than any man's. Seventy thousand die anyway, and his line about the sheep comes far too late. He refuses a free threshing floor: an offering that costs nothing is not one.
ACT 1:1-26	Volume two opens with a recap and a question showing the disciples have learned little: are you restoring the kingdom now. No on timing, very large on geography. Then he goes up, and two men in white note that staring at the sky is not the assignment. The vacancy left by Judas is filled by lot.
ACT 2:1-24	Wind, fire and languages, and the detail Luke insists on is the audience: devout Jews from everywhere, each hearing his own dialect, listed at length. Two reactions, bafflement and drunkenness, and Peter answers the second first, since it is nine in the morning. The rest is Joel until the last two verses turn direct. Next time on Acts: an argument about a tomb.
ACT 2:25-47	The core of the sermon is close reading. David wrote about not being abandoned to the grave; David is still in his tomb up the road; therefore he meant someone else. The conclusion is blunt: the man they crucified has been made Lord and Christ. Three thousand in a day. Then teaching, meals, prayer, and selling property to cover other people's needs.
ACT 3:1-26	A man carried daily to the same gate to beg asks for money and is told there is none, then gets something else. Luke keeps the physical detail: the ankles, the standing, a grown man jumping in the temple. The crowd gathers to look at Peter, which Peter shuts down at once. His speech is unsparing about the trial, then calls it ignorance.
ACT 4:1-37	Arrested for teaching resurrection, the one doctrine the Sadducees cannot let pass, they spend the night in custody while the count of believers reaches five thousand anyway. Asked for their authority, Peter names the name and adds whom you crucified. The council's difficulty is procedural: the healed man is standing right there, so they warn instead of sentencing.
ACT 5:1-21	Two people sell property, keep part of the money, and present the rest as the whole. Nobody asked them to sell it, so the deception was unnecessary as well as dishonest. Ananias dies on the spot. Sapphira arrives three hours later, is given one chance to answer differently, and does not. Next time on Acts: an empty cell.
ACT 5:22-42	The best scene in the chapter is administrative. The officers report the prison locked, the guards at their posts, and nobody inside, and then somebody arrives to say the prisoners are teaching in the temple again. The high priest's complaint includes the phrase you intend to bring this man's blood on us, which is an admission. Gamaliel advises waiting; the council flogs them anyway.
ACT 6:1-15	The first internal complaint in the church is about catering, and it runs along a language line: Greek-speaking widows missed in the daily distribution. The twelve delegate it, in a sentence about not leaving the word to wait on tables that has been quoted uneasily ever since. Seven are appointed, all with Greek names, so the complainants got the department.
ACT 7:1-31	Asked a yes or no question, Stephen delivers the longest speech in Acts, and for a long while it is only history, told so agreeably nobody could object. Abraham, given a promise and not one square foot of land. Joseph, sold by his brothers, made governor of Egypt. The detail he lingers on is the rejection of Moses. Next time on Acts: the burning bush.
ACT 7:32-60	The history now has a thesis, and it is the same sentence twice: the man God sent was refused. Moses, refused, then made ruler and deliverer. The people, given living words, built a calf. Then a quotation arguing God was never housed in a building, which is the charge they arrested him for. Then he stops narrating and starts accusing.
ACT 8:1-40	The persecution scatters the church, which Luke reports as the mechanism by which it spreads, since the scattered ones preach on the way. The awkward part of Philip's Samaritan mission is Simon, a professional wonder-worker who believes, is baptized, then tries to buy the ability wholesale. Then the desert road, one Isaiah passage, one conversation, one baptism, and Philip is gone.
ECC 1:1-18	Everything is vapour, and he proves it with hydrology. The sun rises and hurries back to where it started. The wind goes round and comes back. Every river runs into a sea that never fills. Nothing is new, and nobody will remember. His credentials are the funniest part: wisest king Jerusalem ever had, and his finding is that knowing more hurts more.
ECC 2:1-26	A funded research project with a sample size of one. He tries laughter, which accomplishes nothing, then wine, then construction on an imperial scale. He denies himself nothing his eyes want and becomes the greatest man in Jerusalem's history, then files the whole portfolio as wind. What finally breaks him is inheritance: someone else gets it, and he may well be an idiot.
ECC 3:1-22	The famous poem, fourteen paired times, quoted at funerals and much stranger in context. His gloss is the interesting part: God put eternity in the human heart, which means wanting the whole design while built to see a fragment. Rejoice, do good, eat, drink. Then he looks at the courts and finds wickedness in the seat of justice.
ECC 4:1-16	He looks at oppression, and the phrase he cannot let go of is that they had no comforter, said twice in one verse. His conclusion is bleak: the dead are better off, the never born better still. Then he notices achievement runs on envy. And then the warmest passage in the book, two being better than one, and a cord of three strands.
ECC 5:1-20	Instructions for religion, and they amount to talking less. Go to listen. Keep your words few, on the strictly geographical grounds that God is in heaven and you are not. On corruption he is worldly rather than outraged: every official is being watched by a bigger official. The lover of silver is never satisfied, and the labourer sleeps while the rich man lies awake.
ECC 6:1-12	The horror here is not poverty but wealth you cannot use: riches, honour and every desire met, then no capacity to enjoy it while a stranger eats it. He escalates ruthlessly. A hundred children, a long life, no satisfaction and no grave, and a stillborn child is better off, having at least got rest.
ECC 7:1-29	A chapter of deliberately unwelcome preferences. A good name beats perfume, the day of death beats the day of birth, and the funeral beats the party, on the grounds that the funeral is where the useful information is. He forbids nostalgia outright. Then the strangest advice in the book, delivered straight: do not be excessively righteous, and also do not be excessively wicked.
ECC 8:1-17	Court etiquette from a man who clearly worked near power. Keep the command, do not storm out, and remember that nobody gets to ask the king what he thinks he is doing. Underneath runs the real subject, the absence of visible consequences: sentence against evil is not carried out quickly, so people simply go ahead. He does not resolve it. Eat, drink, be glad.
ECC 9:1-18	The great levelling chapter. One event comes to everybody, the good, the clean, the unclean. A living dog beats a dead lion, because the living at least know what is coming. From that he draws a list of pleasures rather than a warning: bread, wine, white clothes, oil, the wife you love, since Sheol has no projects.
ECC 10:1-20	Assorted observations, mostly about incompetence in high places. Dead flies ruining the perfumer's oil is the model: a small amount of folly outweighs a great deal of wisdom. The fool identifies himself to everyone simply by walking down the road. Then a run of workplace injuries, pits, walls, quarried stone, and a snake that bites before the charmer has started.
ECC 11:1-10	The Preacher's advice for acting under uncertainty, and it is nearly optimistic. Cast the bread on the water. Spread it across seven ventures and then eight. Anyone who stands about watching the wind will not sow. Nobody knows how bones grow in a womb, so sow morning and evening. Enjoy your youth, he says, then mentions the judgment on the way out.
ECC 12:1-14	The book ends on the finest description of ageing ever written, and it works by not naming what it describes. The keepers of the house tremble, the strong men bow, the grinders stop, the ones who look out of the windows go dark, a bird's voice wakes you, a grasshopper is a burden. Then the breakages: silver cord, golden bowl, pitcher at the spring.
EXO 1:1-22	Seventy people came down to Egypt and the roster has since gotten away from everyone. A new king who never knew Joseph decides the guests are a military problem and applies unlimited free labour, which only makes them multiply faster. Plan B is the midwives Shiphrah and Puah, who quietly decline and explain that Hebrew women are simply too quick for them. God rewards the lie with families of their own. Plan C is the river, and Pharaoh stops pretending this is about bricks.
EXO 2:1-25	A baby is hidden three months, then waterproofed and floated, which is how you obey the letter of an order to put your son in the Nile. Pharaoh's daughter adopts him, and his sister gets his own mother hired as the nurse. Grown up, Moses looks both ways before committing murder, which tells you he knew. It is known by morning anyway. He runs to Midian, defends seven sisters at a well, and acquires a wife and a son. God hears, remembers, sees.
EXO 3:1-22	A bush burns and does not burn down, so Moses turns aside, which is apparently the whole audition. Shoes off. God has seen, heard and come down, and would now like Moses to do the actual walking. Moses asks who am I, and gets an answer about who God is instead. Pressed for a name to give the elders, he receives I AM WHO I AM, which settles nothing and everything. The briefing includes a spoiler: Pharaoh will say no, repeatedly, and the people will leave carrying Egypt's jewellery.
EXO 4:1-31	Moses raises objections in ascending order of honesty. They will not believe me, so he is handed three signs, one of which has him running from his own staff. I am not eloquent, and God points out who made mouths. Out of excuses, he says please send someone else, and the divine patience thins. Aaron is drafted as a mouthpiece. Then a strange night at a lodging place where God moves to kill Moses and Zipporah settles it with a flint. The people, at least, believe.
EXO 5:1-23	Moses delivers the message and Pharaoh replies with the sentence the whole book exists to answer: who is Yahweh. He then diagnoses the problem as idleness, which is his read on people making bricks all day, and cancels the straw while keeping the quota. The foremen get beaten, appeal to Pharaoh, get told they are idle again, and come out to find Moses conveniently waiting. Everyone blames Moses. Moses blames God. Round one of the liberation has left everybody worse off, which the text does not soften.
EXO 6:1-30	God answers the complaint by restating everything more grandly, seven I wills and a name to go with them. One of the great speeches, and its audience is too exhausted to hear it. Moses returns to his one reliable excuse about his lips. At which point the narrative stops dead for a family tree, through Reuben and Simeon at speed, then slowly through Levi, because the real point is establishing who these two brothers are. It ends where it paused, with Moses still objecting.
ISA 1:1-31	Yahweh subpoenas heaven and earth, then opens with a comparison unfavourable to Israel: the ox knows its owner, you do not. He refuses the worship outright, since the hands lifted in prayer have blood on them. Wash, seek justice, and scarlet sins can go white as snow. The faithful city is now a prostitute serving watered wine, and God proposes to smelt her.
ISA 2:1-22	Two visions that disagree about the mood. First the mountain of Yahweh's house above the hills, nations queueing to be taught, swords becoming farm equipment. Then the present: silver, imported horses, gods people made with their own fingers. A day is coming for everything tall, and people bolt into caves and hand their gold idols to the moles and bats.
ISA 3:1-26	Jerusalem loses its supports, and the list is thorough enough to include the enchanters. Government collapses until owning a coat qualifies you to rule, and the man drafted declines. The elders are tried for grinding the faces of the poor. Then the women of Zion have their finery confiscated, each item replaced by its opposite: rope for a belt, baldness for a hairstyle.
ISA 4:1-6	It opens on a demographic horror: seven women to one man, willing to feed and dress themselves if he will lend them his name. Then the register changes. Zion's survivors are called holy, the filth washed off by a spirit of justice and burning, and God builds weather over Mount Zion, cloud by day and fire by night, doubling as a sunshade and an umbrella.
ISA 5:1-30	The love song about a friend's vineyard is a trap. Best vines, watchtower, winepress, and the crop is wild grapes, so the hedge comes down and the clouds are ordered to stop. He looked for justice and got bloodshed. Six woes: property accumulators, dawn drinkers, people who call evil good, judges for hire. He whistles, and a nation arrives that never gets tired.
ISA 6:1-13	A king dies, and Isaiah sees the real throne, the robe alone filling the temple, seraphim shouting holy hard enough to shake the doorposts. His first thought is that he is a man of unclean lips, a professional problem for a prophet, solved with a live coal to the mouth. He volunteers before hearing the job: preach to people guaranteed not to listen.
ISA 7:1-25	Two kings march on Jerusalem and Ahaz's nerve goes. Isaiah turns up with a son named Remnant Will Return and calls the invaders two smoking stubs of torch. Offered a sign anywhere from the depths to the heights, Ahaz piously declines and gets one anyway: a child called Immanuel, and before he can tell right from wrong both kingdoms are empty.
ISA 8:1-22	Isaiah writes Maher Shalal Hash Baz on a billboard-sized tablet, then names his newborn son the same thing. Reject the quiet waters of Shiloah and you get the River instead, up to the neck. He is warned off joining the general shout of conspiracy: God is a sanctuary to some and a tripwire to others. And mediums who chirp and mutter are not research.
ISA 9:1-21	The lights come up on Zebulun and Naphtali, the yoke snapped, the bloodied battle gear burned for fuel, and a child given titles that grow larger until they finish at Prince of Peace. Then the north's judgment resumes, and Samaria's answer to losing bricks is a promise to rebuild in cut stone. Manasseh and Ephraim turn on each other, and the hand is still out.
ISA 10:1-34	A woe for legislators who draft laws to turn widows into plunder. Then the strangest argument in the book: Assyria is the rod of God's anger and has no idea, its king boasting about gathering the earth like eggs from an unguarded nest. God asks whether an axe brags about the arm, and reduces Assyria's forest to a remnant a child could tally.
ISA 11:1-16	Out of a cut stump comes a shoot, equipped with wisdom, counsel and might, and notably not with the habit of judging by appearances. He judges the poor fairly and kills the wicked with his breath. Then the famous zoo: wolf boarding with lamb, lion on a straw diet, a toddler as both shepherd and snake handler. The River is split into seven wadeable streams.
ISA 12:1-6	Six verses of relief, which in this book counts as a holiday. The speaker thanks God for being angry and then stopping, calls him strength and song, and declares he will not be afraid. Water gets drawn from the wells of salvation, cheerfully. Then the song turns outward: tell the peoples, shout, because the Holy One of Israel is standing in the middle of town.
ISA 13:1-22	Babylon's turn. God musters an army from far off and the day of Yahweh arrives with the lights off, sun, moon and stars withheld. People become rarer than the gold of Ophir. The sack is described without softening: children killed in front of their parents, houses stripped, wives raped. The Medes arrive uninterested in bribes, and the glory of kingdoms ends as jackal real estate.
ISA 14:1-32	A funeral song for the king of Babylon, sung with enormous enjoyment. The earth goes quiet, and even the cedars are relieved nobody is coming with an axe. Sheol wakes specially to greet him, dead kings climbing down to ask whether he has gone weak too. His bedding is maggots. He said he would set his throne above the stars, and ends up dumped unburied.
ISA 15:1-9	Moab is destroyed overnight, and the oracle is mostly a map of people crying in specific places. Ar and Kir gone before morning, heads shaved, beards off, sackcloth on the roofs, the noise carrying from Heshbon to Jahaz. The prophet says his own heart cries out for Moab, which is not how these oracles usually go. A lion waits for the survivors.
ISA 16:1-14	It opens as diplomacy: lambs to Zion, and Judah asked to be shade at noon, hide the outcasts, don't hand over the fugitive. Then the reason it will fail: Moab's pride. So Moab mourns for Moab, and the loss named is the raisin cakes of Kir Hareseth. The vineyards get a tender lament, Heshbon watered with tears. Three years, counted as a contractor counts.
ISA 17:1-14	Damascus is told, in the passive voice, that it will stop being a city. The oracle then folds Israel in with it: what is left is gleanings, two or three olives at the top of the tree. Then people look at their Maker rather than at things their own fingers made. The nations roar like the sea and blow off as chaff.
ISA 18:1-7	An oracle for the land of rustling wings past the rivers of Ethiopia, which conducts its foreign policy in papyrus boats, addressed to a people described twice in identical words as tall and smooth. Everyone watches for the banner, and God announces that he intends to do nothing yet, waiting quietly like heat shimmer. The pruning comes just as the flower is becoming a grape.
ISA 19:1-25	Egypt's oracle begins with God arriving by fast cloud and Egypt promptly fighting itself, then consulting mediums instead of fixing anything. The Nile fails and the economy fails with it. Pharaoh's wise men at Zoan are described as stupid, staggering in their own vomit. Then the ending nobody set up: an altar to Yahweh inside Egypt and a highway to Assyria.
ISA 20:1-6	The year Assyria takes Ashdod, God gives Isaiah an assignment consisting of taking his clothes off. He walks naked and barefoot for three years: Egyptian and Ethiopian captives will be marched away exactly like that, with buttocks uncovered. It is aimed at everyone in Judah betting on Egypt. The coastland asks the right question: if that was our hope, what happens to us?
ISA 21:1-17	A vision arrives like a desert whirlwind and hits the prophet physically: labour pains, deafness, blindness. A banquet is being laid, and the order comes to oil the shield. A watchman stands day and night until he reports horsemen in pairs: fallen, fallen is Babylon. Then Dumah, where a voice asks what is left of the night and gets the least satisfying answer available.
ISA 22:1-25	A city on its rooftops, its dead not killed in battle, its rulers caught fleeing. What follows is competence pointed the wrong way: they audit the wall breaches, demolish houses for masonry, build a reservoir, and never look at the one who arranged the crisis. Called to sackcloth, they throw a dinner. Shebna, found chiselling himself a prestige tomb, is balled up and thrown.
ISA 23:1-18	Tyre falls, and the oracle grieves in the language of shipping. The ships of Tarshish howl because there is no harbour to come home to, and the news arrives from Kittim. Tyre was the market of nations, and Yahweh's stated purpose is to stain the pride of all glory. Seventy years forgotten, then the trade resumes, a prostitute walking the city with a harp.
ISA 24:1-23	The earth gets turned upside down, and the striking thing is how evenly: priest and people, master and servant, creditor and debtor. Wine mourns, the harps stop, the drink goes bitter. Whoever runs from the noise falls in the pit, and whoever gets out of the pit finds the snare. The earth sways like a hammock and does not get up.
ISA 25:1-12	Praise, on the grounds that a fortified city is now a heap that will never be rebuilt. He shelters the poor and ends the noise of the ruthless the way a cloud ends the glare. Then the banquet: every people on this mountain, rich meat, refined wine, death swallowed, tears wiped away. Then Moab, trodden into a dung pit doing a swimming stroke.
ISA 26:1-21	A song about a strong city whose fortifications turn out to be salvation, sung while another city is ground into dust under the feet of the poor. Steadfast minds get perfect peace; the wicked get favour and learn nothing from it. Then the nation admits it was in labour and delivered wind. Against that, the dead are told to wake up and sing.
ISA 27:1-13	The sword comes out for leviathan and the sea dragon. Then the vineyard from chapter five gets a rewrite: God as keeper, watering every moment, on guard night and day, insisting there is no wrath in him. Forgiveness has a physical form here, and it is grinding your own altar stones to chalk. Then the exiles are gathered one by one.
ISA 28:1-29	The drunks of Ephraim wear a crown of wilting flowers. Priests and prophets are drunk, and every table has vomit on it. They mocked Isaiah as baby talk, precept on precept, line on line, so God adopts their mockery as his method. Jerusalem's rulers claim a signed agreement with death; God offers a cornerstone. It ends in agronomy: nobody threshes cumin with a cart.
ISA 29:1-24	Ariel is besieged until it whispers out of the dust like a medium. The armies attacking Zion are a starving man dreaming of dinner and waking up hungry. The sealed-book joke is precise: the educated man cannot read it because it is sealed, the uneducated man simply cannot read. Afterwards the deaf hear the book and the blind read it.
ISA 30:1-18	They made an alliance and did not consult God. Treasure goes south through lion and viper country. God gives Egypt a nickname, Rahab Who Sits Still. This is a people who instruct their prophets to prophesy something nicer. Their guilt is a wall bulge that shatters so completely not one shard can carry coals. They preferred fast horses, so their pursuers will be faster.
ISA 30:19-33	The comforts here are mixed: still the bread of adversity, but teachers no longer hidden, and a voice behind you at every turn saying this is the way. The idols are stripped of their gold plating and thrown out as garbage. God arrives from far off in smoke. And every blow of the rod on Assyria falls in time with tambourines and harps.
ISA 31:1-9	The case against the Egyptian alliance is a category error: Egyptians are men, not God, their horses flesh, not spirit. One hand stretched out and the helper falls with the helped. Two images for God's defence, both meant: a lion unimpressed by shouting shepherds, and a bird over the nest. Assyria falls by a sword belonging to nobody.
ISA 32:1-20	A competent government is imagined, which in this book counts as a vision: a righteous king, just officials, and a man who functions as weather protection and shade. Vocabulary gets accurate again, and fools stop being addressed as noble. Then the comfortable women of Zion are told to strip and put on sackcloth, because the vintage will not arrive.
ISA 33:1-24	It opens on the destroyer nobody has yet destroyed and the betrayer nobody has betrayed, with the observation that both are a matter of scheduling. The roads are empty, the treaty broken, the peace envoys crying. Zion's sinners ask who can live with everlasting fire and get an answer with no mysticism in it: someone who refuses bribes.
ISA 34:1-17	A universal summons, followed by the most extravagant violence in the book: mountains melting with blood, the sky rolled up like a scroll, the stars falling like leaves. The sword, described as drunk, comes down on Edom, staged as a sacrifice. Then a careful list of birds, and its point is the last verse: none is missing her mate.
ISA 35:1-10	The desert blossoms and is handed Lebanon's glory as a gift, which is generous. Shaky knees get strengthened, and the fearful are told God is coming with vengeance, presented as good news for them specifically. Blind eyes, deaf ears, a lame man leaping, water in the sand. The ransomed come home singing, and sorrow and sighing do not merely stop, they run away.
ISA 36:1-22	Assyria takes every fortified city in Judah, then sends a spokesman with a speech, delivered where the wall can hear. His argument is not bad: Egypt is a bruised reed that goes through your hand, and the God you trust is the one whose altars your own king demolished. Next time on Isaiah: someone takes it to the temple.
ISA 37:1-21	Hezekiah tears his clothes and heads for the temple, describing the crisis as a labour that reached the birth with no strength to push. Isaiah's reply is short: he will hear a rumour, go home and die there. Hezekiah unfolds the Assyrian letter in front of God. Next time on Isaiah: God answers in verse, then in numbers.
ISA 37:22-38	God's reply to the letter is a poem. Zion is a girl shaking her head at him. His boasting gets quoted back, then dismantled: the cities he flattened were grass on a roof. He goes home by the nose, with a hook. A hundred and eighty five thousand dead overnight, and his own sons kill him mid-prayer in his god's temple.
ISA 38:1-22	Hezekiah gets a terminal diagnosis, turns to face the wall, and makes the case for himself in the third person, weeping. It works: fifteen years, plus a sundial shadow that goes backwards ten steps as a receipt. His account of the illness is good poetry, life cut off the loom, a voice reduced to a swallow's chatter. The treatment was a fig poultice.
ISA 39:1-8	Babylon sends a get-well card and a present, and Hezekiah, flattered, gives the delegation the complete tour, treasury to armoury, leaving nothing out. Isaiah's questions are those of a man who already knows. All of it, says Hezekiah. So all of it goes to Babylon, and your sons will be eunuchs there. Hezekiah: at least there will be peace in my time.
ISA 40:1-31	The book changes register here, and the first word is comfort, said twice. A highway gets graded through the desert. The crier's script is not encouraging, all flesh is grass, though the word outlasts the grass. God arrives as a conquering arm and, four verses later, as a shepherd carrying lambs. Even young men fall over, but those who wait get wings.
ISA 41:1-29	The islands are summoned: who raised up the conqueror from the east? The nations respond to fear by making an idol, and the scene is devastating: the carpenter encouraging the goldsmith, the finished god nailed down so it does not fall over. Israel is addressed as worm Jacob, then promoted to a threshing sledge with teeth. Then the gods are invited to predict anything. Silence.
ISA 42:1-25	The servant is introduced by everything he does not do: no shouting in the street, no snapping a bruised reed, no snuffing a wick. Just justice, without giving up. Then God, quiet a long time, decides to make noise, and the simile he picks for himself is a woman in labour. And then the sting: who is blind, but my servant?
ISA 43:1-28	The reassurance is specific: not that there will be no water or fire, but that neither will finish them. Egypt, Ethiopia and Seba go into the ledger as ransom. The witnesses called are blind people with eyes and deaf people with ears. Then a complaint that is almost domestic: I never asked much, and you brought me your sins.
ISA 44:1-28	Water on dry ground, Spirit on the children. Then the centrepiece, a workshop tour. The blacksmith gets hungry and faint making his god. The woodcutter plants a tree, burns half for heat and roast meat, announces that he is warm, and turns the offcut into something he begs to save him. The text asks the obvious question he never asks himself.
ISA 45:1-25	A foreign king is called anointed, with bronze doors broken open ahead of him, and the text twice points out that he does not know the God doing it. Then a claim most readers step around: I form light and create darkness, I make peace and create calamity. The clay is then invited to ask the potter what he thinks he is making.
ISA 46:1-13	The chapter opens on a logistics problem. Bel and Nebo, being statues, have to be evacuated by donkey, and they go into captivity with everyone else because they could not carry themselves. Set against that: Israel has been carried since the womb and will be carried to grey hair. The finished god, meanwhile, stays where it was shouldered, unable to answer.
ISA 47:1-15	Babylon, a pampered daughter, is put in the dust, at the millstones, and into the river with her skirt lifted, a humiliating picture of an empire doing labourer's work. She was handed the exiles and showed no mercy, yoking even the elderly. Her catchphrase, I am, and there is no one besides me, is a straight parody of God's.
ISA 48:1-22	His reason for announcing things in advance turns out to be pre-emptive: he knew they would credit their idols otherwise. Iron-necked, brass-browed, treacherous from the womb, and spared for the sake of his own reputation. Then a wistful line in the subjunctive, the peace that would have been like a river. And a last flat sentence with no comfort in it.
ISA 49:1-26	The servant introduces himself as a sword mouth and a polished arrow in the quiver, and then immediately files a complaint: I have laboured for nothing. The response is to enlarge the job, because Jacob alone was too small an assignment. Then Zion's line, he has forgotten me, answered by a nursing mother who cannot forget, and a name engraved on the palms.
ISA 50:1-11	God opens with paperwork: produce the divorce certificate, name the creditor I sold you to. There is none, which is the point. The servant's own testimony is quieter and much harder: an ear woken every morning to be taught, a back given to the people beating it, no face hidden from the spitting, a face set like flint.
ISA 51:1-23	The argument from precedent: look at the quarry you were cut from, look at Abraham and Sarah, one household before they were a nation. Heaven goes like smoke, the earth wears out like an old coat, and the people insulting you get eaten by moths. Then the cup of staggering is taken from Jerusalem and handed to the people who walked on her.
ISA 52:1-15	Three imperatives in a row, wake up, get dressed, sit up, and one that is easy to miss: untie the rope on your own neck. Sold for nothing, redeemed without money, which is a strange balance sheet and deliberately so. The departure is specifically unhurried, unlike the first exodus, with God at the front and also at the back.
ISA 53:1-12	Nothing about him invites attention: a shoot out of dry ground, no beauty, no presence, someone people look away from. The turn in the chapter is a change of pronoun. The sickness was ours; the assumption that God had struck him was ours too, and wrong. Then silence: a lamb led away, a sheep quiet in front of the shearers.
ISA 54:1-17	A woman who has never had a child is told to sing about her children, and then to enlarge the tent, because the guest list has grown. The apology, if it can be called that, is carefully proportioned: forsaken for a small moment, gathered with great mercies. The last argument is elegant: I made the blacksmith who makes the weapons.
ISA 55:1-13	The invitation is aimed at people with no money, which is a strange thing to advertise at a market: come and buy wine and milk for nothing. The sharpest question in the chapter is why you are paying for something that is not bread. The exit is a procession where the hills sing and the trees applaud.
ISA 56:1-12	Two people who had every reason to think they were excluded get addressed by category: the foreigner who assumes he will be sorted out, and the eunuch who calls himself a dry tree. The eunuch gets a name better than sons and daughters, the exact thing he does not have. Then the chapter savages the leadership: blind watchmen, guard dogs that cannot bark.
ISA 57:1-21	It opens on something quiet and awful, the righteous dying with nobody paying attention, reframed as mercy, since they are being spared what is coming. Then the indictment, the most graphic in the book: worship under every green tree, children killed in the ravines, a memorial hidden behind the door. The most exhausting detail is that they never once said it was not working.
ISA 58:1-14	The complaint is filed by the congregation: we fasted and you did not notice. The answer is that on the fast day they were driving their workers and using the fast as a run-up to a fistfight. Then the redefinition, entirely practical and containing no ritual at all: undo the yoke straps, feed the hungry, house the homeless poor. Also drop the pointing finger.
ISA 59:1-21	The chapter opens by ruling out the two easy explanations: the arm is not short and the ear is not dull. The problem is then stated as biology: they hatch adders' eggs, so eating one kills you and crushing one produces a viper, and they weave spider webs, which will never be clothing. God looks for an intercessor, finds nobody, and dresses for battle himself.
ISA 60:1-22	The chapter is a logistics fantasy, and the details are the point. Camels from Midian and Sheba, gold and frankincense, ships of Tarshish, Lebanon's timber, all converging on one city whose gates never close because the deliveries never stop. Foreigners rebuild the walls that foreigners knocked down. The upgrade schedule is stated in metals: gold for brass, silver for iron, peace as governor.
ISA 61:1-11	The job description is all verbs, and they are all reversals: good news to the humble, bandages for broken hearts, liberty for captives. The best line is a swap of one thing for another, a garland for ashes, oil of joy for mourning, praise for heaviness. Instead of shame they get double, which is the same arithmetic used earlier for the punishment.
ISA 62:1-12	The speaker refuses to shut up, and then instructs everyone else to refuse as well, giving God no rest until Jerusalem is a praise in the earth, which is a bold model of prayer. The renaming is the heart of it: not Forsaken, not Desolate, but Hephzibah and Beulah, delighted in and married. Then a concrete oath about grain and wine.
ISA 63:1-19	A figure arrives from Edom in red clothing and the question asked is the ordinary one, why are your clothes that colour. The answer is a winepress, trodden alone, and it is one of the most frightening images in the book precisely because it is domestic. The chapter ends as a complaint addressed upward, and it is not polite.
ISA 64:1-12	The request is for something violent, that God tear the sky open and come down hard enough to shake the mountains, on the grounds that he has done it before. What follows is one of the bleakest self-assessments anywhere: our righteousness is a filthy garment, we fade like leaves, and nobody is even bothering to pray. The last line is a question left open.
ISA 65:1-25	God complains about being found by people who were not looking, while he stood all day with his hands out for the ones who were supposed to be. The practices are itemised, gardens, bricks, graves, pig's meat, and the sting is the last: telling other people to keep back because you are holier. The reprieve is a wine-grower's instinct: do not throw out the cluster.
ISA 66:1-24	It opens by dismantling the premise of temple building: heaven is the throne and earth the footstool, so what exactly would a house be for. Sacrifice offered by the self-satisfied is put on a level with murder and pig's blood, deliberately. Then the tenderest image in the whole book, God comparing himself to a mother comforting a child. Then fire and chariots.
JDG 1:1-18	Joshua is dead and nobody is in charge, so Israel asks who goes first and is told Judah. Judah takes Simeon along, kills ten thousand at Bezek, and relieves Adoni-Bezek of his thumbs and big toes, exactly as he once did to seventy kings. Achsah, given dry land, asks for the water too. Next time on Judges: the list of what did not happen.
JDG 1:19-36	A conquest report that turns into a register of exceptions. Yahweh is with Judah, though apparently not against iron chariots. Bethel falls because an informant is bought with kindness. Then the tally: Manasseh did not, Ephraim did not, Zebulun did not, Asher did not, Naphtali did not, and Dan is shoved into the hills. Everyone settles for forced labour.
JDG 2:1-23	An angel reads out at Bochim the terms Israel agreed to and did not keep, and the people cry so hard the place is renamed Weeping. Joshua is buried, his generation dies out, and the next has never heard any of it. Then the book states its engine in advance: evil, oppression, groaning, a judge, peace, funeral, worse evil.
JDG 3:1-31	The leftover nations are retained as a test. Othniel handles Cushan Rishathaim in a verse and a half. Then Ehud, left-handed and so not frisked on the correct thigh, promises Eglon of Moab a message from God and delivers it into his stomach, where the fat closes over the handle. The servants wait outside, assuming the king is occupied.
JDG 4:1-24	Nine hundred iron chariots, twenty years of it, and the person actually running Israel is Deborah, holding court under a tree. Barak agrees to command on condition she come along, and is told the credit will therefore belong to a woman. It does. Sisera runs on foot and accepts hospitality from Jael, who gives him milk, tucks him in, and then uses a tent peg.
JDG 5:1-31	The same battle again, as poetry and considerably more pointed. Deborah credits the volunteers, then names who stayed home: Reuben deliberating among the sheepfolds, Dan lingering with the ships, Asher at his beach, Meroz cursed outright. The stars fight and the Kishon does much of the work. It closes on Sisera's mother at the lattice, telling herself that plunder takes time to divide.
JDG 6:1-21	Midian arrives annually like locusts and eats everything, so Israel lives in caves. A prophet is sent and never mentioned again. Then an angel finds Gideon threshing wheat in a winepress, which is where you thresh if you are hiding, and hails him as a mighty man of valour. Next time on Judges: the rock catches fire.
JDG 6:22-40	The offering goes up in flame and Gideon's first reaction is that he has seen too much and will now die. Reassured, he demolishes his father's Baal altar at night, because daylight was unthinkable. The town wants him executed; his father suggests a god who can defend himself should do so. Then he asks for the fleece test. Twice. In both directions.
JDG 7:1-12	Gideon has an army, and God's first note is that it is too big, because a comfortable win would be misreported. Anyone frightened may leave, and twenty-two thousand promptly do. Ten thousand is still too many, so a drinking test keeps the three hundred who lap like dogs. Next time on Judges: a loaf of bread with a prophecy in it.
JDG 7:13-25	Gideon reaches the enemy camp as a soldier is describing a nightmare about a barley loaf squashing a tent, which his friend identifies as Gideon. Encouraged by being baked goods, he hands out trumpets, jars and torches, and at midnight three hundred men make the noise of an army. Midian's army obligingly kills itself. The heads of Oreb and Zeeb are delivered like receipts.
JDG 8:1-17	Ephraim's grievance is not the war but the invitation, and Gideon defuses it with flattery. Then, faint yet pursuing, he asks Succoth for bread and is asked whether he has caught anyone yet. He promises to come back with thorns. Penuel agrees. He takes Zebah and Zalmunna at Karkor, then keeps both promises. Next time on Judges: why any of this was personal.
JDG 8:18-35	One question about Tabor reveals the whole campaign as a blood feud: they killed his brothers. He orders his son to do the execution, the boy freezes, and the kings ask to be killed by a professional. Offered a crown, Gideon gives a fine speech about Yahweh alone ruling, then asks for everyone's gold and makes an ephod Israel worships almost at once.
JDG 9:1-28	Abimelech's pitch to Shechem is that one king is more efficient than seventy brothers, and he funds the restructuring with temple money. Seventy die on one stone; the youngest hides. Jotham climbs Gerizim with a fable: the useful trees decline the crown because they have jobs, so the office goes to a thornbush that threatens arson. Next time on Judges: the fable's fire arrives.
JDG 9:29-57	Zebul plays both sides, cheering Gaal in public and writing to Abimelech at night. When Gaal spots the ambush coming, Zebul tells him he is seeing shadows. Shechem is taken, killed, salted, and a thousand burn in the tower. At Thebez a woman leans over a wall with a millstone, and Abimelech's last concern is the wording of the report.
JDG 10:1-18	Two judges pass with no incident, which in this book is a golden age, though Jair leaves thirty sons on thirty donkeys. Then Israel takes up the gods of Syria, Sidon, Moab, Ammon and Philistia, and is oppressed by two of them at once. The reply to their cry is: go try the gods you picked. They repent properly, and he cannot keep it up.
JDG 11:1-20	Jephthah is the son of a prostitute, disinherited by his half-brothers and running a gang of outlaws in Tob, which is why Gilead remembers him the moment a war starts. He points this out, and gets the job promised before God. Ammon says Israel stole the land; Jephthah replies with the exodus itinerary. Next time on Judges: a promise nobody should make.
JDG 11:21-40	Three hundred years is a long time to sit on a claim. Ammon does not reply. Then the Spirit comes on Jephthah and he promises to burn whatever walks out of his door first. What walks out is his daughter, an only child. He cannot unsay it, and she agrees. She asks for two months in the hills, comes back, and he keeps the vow.
JDG 12:1-15	Ephraim arrives late, with a complaint and a threat to burn Jephthah's house down. Jephthah, who has no flattery in him, fights them. Gilead takes the river crossings and screens for accent: say Shibboleth. Forty-two thousand die of a consonant. Three more judges are filed in a paragraph, and what survives is arithmetic: thirty sons, ten quiet years, seventy relatives on seventy donkeys.
JDG 13:1-25	Another forty years of Philistine rule, and the rescue plan is an angel visiting a childless woman in Zorah with a prenatal regimen: no wine, no unclean food, never cut the boy's hair. Manoah prays for a repeat performance, gets one, asks his own question, and receives his wife's answer verbatim. The angel then leaves via the altar flame. The boy is Samson.
JDG 14:1-20	Samson's first act is demanding a Philistine wife. He kills a lion barehanded, then scoops honey from the corpse and feeds it to his parents: a Nazirite handling a carcass, and a family eating it blind. He sets the wedding guests a riddle only he could answer; the groomsmen threaten his bride with fire, and he talks. Thirty strangers in Ashkelon settle the debt.
JDG 15:1-20	Samson turns up after the wedding holding a goat and finds his wife reassigned, her sister offered as a substitute. His reply is three hundred foxes tied in pairs with torches, and the harvest burns. The Philistines burn his wife and her father alive, and the feud stops being funny. Judah's contribution to national liberation is arresting its own strongman.
JDG 16:1-16	An ambush waits at the Gaza gate, and Samson leaves at midnight taking the gate with him, uphill toward Hebron. Then Delilah, and the lords with a price. Three rounds of the same conversation: he names a binding, she applies it, she shouts that the Philistines are on him, he walks out of it. Next time on Judges: he answers honestly.
JDG 16:17-31	He finally tells the truth, which is the one thing that could hurt him, and the money arrives before the razor does. He wakes planning to shake himself free, not having noticed that Yahweh had gone. Then blinding, bronze fetters, a mill in Gaza, and one quiet line: his hair began to grow. Dagon's festival brings him out as the entertainment.
JDG 17:1-13	Micah returns the silver he stole from his mother, who answers the confession with a blessing and then dedicates the money to Yahweh by having idols made from it. Two hundred of the eleven hundred reach the silversmith. He ordains a son, then upgrades to a wandering Levite at ten pieces a year, and concludes that God must now favour him.
JDG 18:1-15	Dan, which never took its allotted land, goes looking for someone else's. The five scouts stop at Micah's shrine, recognise the Levite by his voice, and ask a blessing on the expedition, which he supplies. At Laish they find a town unfortified and with no allies, and their report treats defencelessness as divine provision. Next time on Judges: the priest weighs up his options.
JDG 18:16-31	The theft is conducted with six hundred armed men at the gate. The priest asks what they think they are doing and is offered a promotion: better a tribe than one household. He carries the idols out himself. Micah gets one pitiable line, that they have taken his gods and his priest, before being told to lower his voice or die.
JDG 19:1-16	The book's last story opens with the same line about there being no king. A Levite goes to Bethlehem to fetch back the concubine who left him, and the visit turns into five days of a father-in-law who will not let anyone leave without one more meal. So he sets out too late. Next time on Judges: the night in Gibeah.
JDG 19:17-30	The old man takes them in. Then men of the city surround the house and demand the guest, and the host's counter offer is his own daughter and the concubine. The Levite settles it by putting her outside, where she is raped through the night. Her master's first words in the morning are an instruction to get up. There is no answer.
JDG 20:1-25	The Levite gives his account, in which the mob wanted to kill him and his concubine is simply dead, with no mention of who handed her over. Benjamin refuses to surrender Gibeah's guilty men, turning a criminal case into a civil war. Twenty-two thousand die on day one, eighteen thousand on day two. Next time on Judges: the third day.
JDG 20:26-48	This time they fast, sacrifice and ask again: go up, and tomorrow it is done. The tactic is Ai again, a feigned rout onto the roads while the ambush takes the empty city. Benjamin is still shouting that this is going as it did before when it turns and sees its town on fire. Israel does not stop at the army.
JDG 21:1-25	Having nearly destroyed a tribe, Israel weeps over the tribe being destroyed, then treats its own oath as an obstacle to engineer around. Jabesh Gilead skipped the assembly, so twelve thousand men kill everyone there except four hundred virgins. Two hundred short, the elders devise the loophole: carry off the girls who come out dancing at Shiloh, since nobody gave them.
JER 1:1-19	The job offer is retroactive: known before he was formed, appointed prophet to the nations before he had any say in it. Jeremiah's objection is that he is a child and cannot speak, which is overruled rather than answered. The portfolio is four verbs of demolition and only two of construction, in that order. Then a cauldron tilting off the fire from the north.
JER 2:1-19	God opens in the voice of someone recalling a wedding, the honeymoon in the wilderness, the following into unsown country. Then a market survey: check Kittim, check Kedar, find me one nation that has swapped its gods. The two evils are one image, the best in the chapter: they left a spring of living water to chisel their own cisterns, and the cisterns leak.
JER 2:20-37	The accusation moves through images faster than it can be summarised: a noble vine gone wild, a stain that survives lye and soap, a wild donkey in heat that nobody could turn back. They have precisely as many gods as they have towns. The comparison that stings is jewellery: a bride does not forget her outfit, and these people forgot him.
JER 3:1-25	The chapter quotes the law against a divorced wife returning, then tells her to return anyway. Israel waits by the roadside with a prostitute's forehead. Then the sisters: Israel got an actual divorce certificate, Judah watched and did the same, then came back in pretence. The verdict is that the backslider looks better than the hypocrite. The promise is oddly specific: shepherds with sense, and a Jerusalem so occupied by God that nobody misses the ark. The last verses are the people's own, and they are bleak.
JER 4:1-31	It begins with terms and conditions and turns into an air raid siren: trumpet, get behind walls, a lion already on the road. Jeremiah's interjection is the boldest thing here, an accusation that God let this people be promised peace with a sword at their throat. Then the vision, Genesis played backwards: earth waste and void, heavens unlit, mountains rocking, no people, not even birds. It closes on the city putting on scarlet and eye paint for lovers who came to kill her.
JER 5:1-31	The assignment is a search: find one person in Jerusalem doing justice and the city gets pardoned. Jeremiah tries the poor first, assuming they never learned the law, then the important people, who have all broken the yoke. Well fed stallions neighing at the neighbour's wife. Their forecast: nothing will happen, the prophets are wind. So the words become fire and the audience becomes firewood. The last verse indicts everybody: prophets lie, priests rule as they please, and my people love it that way.
JER 6:1-30	The attackers get dialogue, arguing whether to go up at noon or wait for dark. Jerusalem produces wickedness the way a well produces water, and Jeremiah is exhausted from holding wrath in. Then, again: they bandage the wound and say peace, peace, when there is no peace, and cannot manage a blush. Stand at the crossroads and ask for the old paths gets a one-line refusal, we will not walk in it. It ends in the smelter: bellows working, lead gone, silver rejected.
JER 7:1-19	The temple sermon, delivered in the temple gate. Its target is a slogan, repeated three times so you cannot miss that it is one: Yahweh's temple, Yahweh's temple, Yahweh's temple. You cannot steal, murder, commit adultery and burn incense to Baal, then stand in this building and announce you are safe; that makes it a den of robbers. Then the exhibit, Shiloh. And do not pray for them, the harshest thing said to Jeremiah yet. Next time on Jeremiah: a valley gets renamed.
JER 7:20-34	The argument turns to the paperwork: what I commanded coming out of Egypt was not burnt offerings, it was listen to my voice. Prophets sent daily ever since; necks stiffened each generation. Jeremiah is told in advance that nobody will listen to him either. Then the valley. They built Topheth to burn their own children, and God says it never entered his mind. Renamed the Valley of Slaughter, filled until there is no room to bury, and every wedding voice in Judah goes quiet.
JER 8:1-22	Grave robbing as judgment: bones laid out in front of the sun and stars they worshipped, unburied, left as fertiliser. The stork knows when to migrate; the turtledove, swallow and crane all keep the calendar; my people do not know my law. Then the false pen of the scribes, and peace, peace where there is no peace. It ends inside Jeremiah: heart faint, harvest past, summer over, not saved, and the question that outlived it all, is there no balm in Gilead.
JER 9:1-26	It opens with a wish for a bigger tear duct and then a wish to move out of town entirely. Nobody is trustworthy, brother included; tongues are bows bent for falsehood. God weeps too, for burned pastures with no cattle and no birds. Professional mourners are called in to teach their daughters to wail: death has come through the windows. Mid-chapter sits its most quoted verse: let the wise, the strong and the rich boast only about knowing the God who does justice.
JER 10:1-25	An extended comedy at the expense of manufactured gods, the funniest detail being the nail: they fasten it down so it cannot topple over. It must be carried, it cannot talk, and it cannot even do harm, presented as the final humiliation. Against it, weather: waters roaring, wind out of storehouses. Then the tone drops into the voice of somebody whose tent is down and whose children are gone. The last request is modest: correct me, but gently, not in anger.
JER 11:1-23	Jeremiah is made to read out the terms of a contract, his own response a formal amen, which puts him with the prosecution. The statistic: as many gods as cities, as many Baal altars as streets. For the second time he is forbidden to pray for these people. Israel is a green olive tree that God himself is setting fire to. Then it turns personal: Jeremiah gets a plot of his own, out of Anathoth, his hometown. The ultimatum: stop prophesying or die.
JER 12:1-17	Jeremiah opens with a courtroom courtesy, you are righteous, then asks anyway why the wicked prosper. Sharper than the complaint: you are near their mouths and far from their hearts. God's reply refuses the question and raises the stakes: if the footmen wore you out, wait until the horses, and if level ground unnerves you, wait for the Jordan thickets. Also, your brothers are in on it. Then God's own grief gets a turn, the vineyard trampled by shepherds, the whole land desolate because no one cares.
JER 13:1-27	The first assignment: buy a linen belt, wear it, never wash it, then bury it in a rock cleft by the Euphrates. He digs it up ruined, which is the entire sermon. The follow-up is deliberate banality, every bottle should be filled with wine, and the crowd says yes, we know, which is when it lands: the bottles are people and the filling is drunkenness. Then the proverb: can the Ethiopian change his skin, or the leopard his spots, applied to people well practiced at wrong.
JER 14:1-22	A drought chapter, with observational details: children back from the cistern with empty jars, plowmen with their heads covered, a doe walking away from her own fawn, wild donkeys panting on bare rock. The prayer asks why God behaves like a traveller stopped for one night. The reply is a third prohibition on praying for them. Jeremiah's objection: the other prophets promise assured peace. He never sent them, and they will starve alongside their audience. It ends where these chapters keep ending: no idol ever produced rain.
JER 15:1-21	Moses and Samuel could show up and it would not matter. Four destroyers are appointed, one of them dogs. Then: I am weary of showing compassion. The mother of seven has her sun go down at noon. Then Jeremiah's complaint, not about the nation at all: he never lent anybody money and is cursed anyway. He ate God's words and skipped every party. The reply offers no sympathy and a job: separate precious from vile, be my mouth, be a bronze wall.
JER 16:1-21	The prohibitions are the strangest thing asked of any prophet. Do not marry. Do not have children. Do not go to a funeral. Do not go to a wedding. Jeremiah's own life is being made into the sermon, which is cruel. Asked what exactly they did, the people get a straight answer and an unflattering comparison to their parents. Then the reversal: the oath will stop being about the exodus from Egypt and start being about the return from the north. Fishermen first, then hunters.
JER 17:1-27	Sin engraved with an iron pen and a diamond point, permanent ink before permanent ink. Then the contrast everyone remembers: the desert bush that never notices when good arrives, and the tree by the river whose leaf stays green through a drought. Then: the heart is deceitful above all things, who can know it. Ill-gotten wealth is a partridge brooding eggs she did not lay. Jeremiah's aside is bitter: people keep asking him where the disaster is. And it finishes on Sabbath freight regulations.
JER 18:1-23	A field trip to a workshop. The vessel goes wrong under the potter's hands, and the potter reshapes it into something else: the clay has no say, but the outcome is not fixed either. The rule runs both directions, so the threats are conditional too. Judah's reply is the flattest refusal in the book: it is useless, we will follow our own plans. Then the plot against Jeremiah, whose weapon is specified as the tongue, and his prayer in response, long and asking for no forgiveness at all.
JER 19:1-15	The prop this time is a clay bottle, and the venue is the valley where children were burned, which the text says never entered God's mind. Elders and priests come along as witnesses. The threatened siege includes cannibalism, stated plainly. Then the bottle is smashed, and the detail that carries it is that a broken pot cannot be made whole again, unlike the one on the potter's wheel a chapter earlier. Afterwards he walks into the temple court and says it all again, to a bigger audience.
JER 20:1-18	Jeremiah gets beaten and locked in the stocks by the temple's chief officer, and his response on release is to rename the man Terror On Every Side, which turns out to be both a prophecy and an excellent insult. Then comes the most exposed passage in the book. He accuses God of talking him into this, and admits that when he resolves to stop, the word turns into a fire in his bones. He manages a verse of praise, then curses the day of his own birth.
JER 21:1-14	The king sends a delegation to ask for a miracle, specifically one of the old wondrous works. God is on the other side of the wall: he will turn their own weapons back inward and fight them himself. Then the offer, phrased as a choice: stay in the city and die, or walk out and surrender and live. The most unpatriotic instruction any prophet ever gave, given publicly. The house of David gets one condition, do justice in the morning.
JER 22:1-30	A tour of failing kings. The terms, repeated across the book: justice, no violence to the foreigner, orphan or widow, no innocent blood. Then the review. One king is criticised for his renovations, wide rooms, cedar ceiling, painted red, built on wages he never paid, against a father who ate and drank and did justice. Jehoiakim gets a donkey's funeral, dragged out past the gates. And Coniah, described as a signet ring on God's own right hand, is pulled off and filed as childless.
JER 23:1-18	The shepherd metaphor is used as an audit: you scattered them and never once visited. God appoints himself to the job, then promises a Branch for David called Yahweh Our Righteousness. The oath is reissued, no longer about Egypt but about the north. Then the prophets, Jeremiah's reaction physical, bones shaking, staggering like a drunk. Samaria's prophets at least served Baal openly; Jerusalem's are worse, because they encourage evildoers to stay put. The last question: who among you has actually stood in the council and heard anything?
JER 23:19-40	God's complaint against the false prophets is not that they are wrong but that they never turned up. I did not send them, yet they ran. Then the dreams, quoted with visible impatience: I had a dream, I had a dream. Tell your dream, fine, but what is straw to wheat? His own word is fire and a hammer that breaks rock, offered as the test. And the phrase the message from Yahweh is banned outright, because everyone's own opinion has become his message.
JER 24:1-10	Two baskets of figs in front of the temple, one excellent and one so rotten it cannot be eaten. The interpretation is the interesting part, because it inverts what everyone in Jerusalem would assume. The exiles already hauled off to Babylon are the good figs, watched over, to be brought back, planted, given a heart to know him. The people still living in the city, king included, are the rotten ones. Surviving turns out to be no evidence of favour at all, which is why it stung.
JER 25:1-18	Jeremiah does the arithmetic on his own career: twenty three years of speaking, plus every prophet before him, same result. Nebuchadnezzar is introduced with the title that must have landed like a slap, my servant. The losses listed are domestic, not military: no wedding voices, no millstones turning, no lamplight. Seventy years, with an expiry date on Babylon too. Then the strangest prop yet: a cup of wrath carried nation to nation until they all stagger. Next time on Jeremiah: the rest of the guest list drinks.
JER 25:19-38	The cup makes its rounds, from Pharaoh through the Philistine cities, Edom, Moab, Ammon, Tyre, Sidon, the Arabian kings, Elam, the Medes, and eventually every kingdom on earth, with Sheshach politely last. The instruction is drink, be drunk, vomit, fall, do not get up. Refusing is not an option, and the argument is unnerving: judgment has already started at the city that carries God's own name, so nobody else has grounds for exemption. Then Yahweh roars, compared to a man shouting while treading grapes.
JER 26:1-24	The instruction comes with a warning: omit nothing. He does not, and priests, prophets and the crowd seize him, all agreed he should die. What follows is the best-documented trial in the book. His defence is short: God sent me, I am in your hands, and if you kill me the blood is yours. The princes acquit him, the elders citing precedent: Hezekiah did not kill Micah for saying much the same. Then Uriah, the case that went the other way, extradited from Egypt and killed.
JER 27:1-22	The costume this time is a yoke, worn on his own neck, with copies sent to five neighbouring kings whose envoys happen to be in town. The message is politically outrageous and theologically simple: the earth belongs to its maker, who has handed it to Nebuchadnezzar, so put your neck in the yoke and keep your land. Then a challenge to the temple optimists predicting a quick return of the vessels: if you are real prophets, pray that the ones still here stay here. They will not.
JER 28:1-17	A rival prophet stands up in the temple with a much better message: yoke broken, everything back within two years. Jeremiah does not take the bait. Amen, he says, I hope you are right. Then the test, devastatingly simple: prophets predict disaster, so anyone predicting peace only gets credit once it happens. Hananiah snaps the wooden yoke off Jeremiah's neck; Jeremiah leaves without a word. The reply comes later: you have broken bars of wood and replaced them with iron. Hananiah is told he will die this year.
JER 29:1-17	A letter to exiles, delivered by diplomatic bag, and its contents are the opposite of what anyone in Babylon wanted. Build houses. Plant gardens. Marry. Have children. And then the hardest line: seek the peace of the city that took you, and pray for it, because your peace is tied to its peace. Only after all that domestic advice comes the famous verse about thoughts of peace and a future, a promise about seventy years from now, not next spring. Next time on Jeremiah: two prophets get named.
JER 29:18-32	The correspondence turns personal and names names. Ahab son of Kolaiah and Zedekiah son of Maaseiah are to be handed over and executed in front of the exiles, their names becoming a curse formula. Then Shemaiah the Nehelamite, who wrote to the temple police complaining that nobody had put Jeremiah in the stocks yet, quoting Jeremiah's own letter back as evidence. Zephaniah the priest reads the complaint aloud to Jeremiah, which is how Jeremiah gets to answer it in writing, with a prophecy about Shemaiah's descendants.
JER 30:1-24	Write it in a book, a sign this material is meant to outlive the crisis. The terror comes first, the image chosen for grown men labour pains, hands on their waists. Then the medical assessment: the wound is incurable, and the one who inflicted it did so deliberately, like an enemy. Then, with no bridge, the same speaker promises to restore health and heal the wounds. The reason given is a nickname that got back to him: it is Zion, whom no man seeks after.
JER 31:1-20	Loyalty stated retroactively: I have loved you with an everlasting love, said to people currently in ruins. The return party includes the blind, the lame and women in labour, which is nobody's triumphal procession, and is the point. He who scattered Israel will gather him, and the text is content to leave that sitting there. Then Rachel, weeping in Ramah for children who are gone and refusing to be comforted, and God does not argue with her, he tells her the work will be repaid.
JER 31:21-40	Road signs first: mark the route home. Then a line nobody has fully explained, a woman will encompass a man. Jeremiah mentions waking up and that his sleep had been sweet, the only pleasant thing he says about his own night. Then the sour grapes proverb is retired: everyone answers for himself. Then the new covenant, whose novelty is location, the law moved from tablets to the inside of people, with no teaching required. Even the valley of corpses and ashes gets rezoned as holy.
JER 32:1-23	Jeremiah is in custody, mid-siege, for saying the city will fall, and there he is instructed to buy real estate. His cousin turns up with the offer on cue, which is how he knows it is genuine. The paperwork gets an accountant's care: seventeen shekels weighed out, deed signed, sealed, two copies, witnesses, handed to Baruch for a jar. The point is one sentence: houses and fields and vineyards will be bought in this land again. Next time on Jeremiah: he asks what the field was for.
JER 32:24-44	Jeremiah's prayer ends by pointing out the window: the ramps are up, the city is gone, and you had me buy a field, which is the most reasonable complaint in the book. God's reply quotes Jeremiah's own opening line back as a question: is there anything too hard for me. Then the charge sheet, ending on children passed through fire, which he says never entered his mind. Then the reversal, in the same registry-office language: fields bought, deeds signed, sealed, witnessed, across Benjamin and Judah.
JER 33:1-26	Still under guard, the second word opens with an invitation, not a verdict: call to me and I will show you great and difficult things. The houses pulled down for siege works will fill with bodies, and in the same breath he promises health, cure, peace and truth. The promise is a soundscape: a wedding heard again where there is now neither man nor animal. The guarantee offered is the sunrise: break day and night if you can, and then the covenant with David can be broken too.
JER 34:1-22	Zedekiah's sentence: the city burns and he goes to Babylon, but dies in peace with a royal funeral. The chapter's real subject is what follows. Zedekiah proclaims freedom for every Hebrew slave, everyone complies, then takes them back. God's response is a pun with teeth: you would not proclaim liberty, so I proclaim a liberty to the sword, plague and famine. The men who walked between the halves of the calf will be handed over.
JER 35:1-19	God's instruction: throw a wine party for a family famous for teetotalism, in a temple side room. The Rechabites decline: no wine, no houses, no crops, no vineyards, tents only, on the word of an ancestor named Jonadab, kept for generations. God's own instructions, delivered in person and by every prophet, have not held at all. The Rechabites get a promise of their own, the only good news in this stretch of the book.
JER 36:1-15	The whole chapter is about a document, lovingly recorded. Jeremiah dictates, Baruch writes, and being banned from the temple, Jeremiah sends him to read it on a fast day, when the crowd is biggest. It works. Baruch reads it in a scribe's room, one man walks down to the palace and reports. The officials, being officials, do not act on it; they send for the document and have it read again from the start. Next time on Jeremiah: the king has a fire going.
JER 36:16-32	The officials react with fear, and even ask the right procedural question: how was this written. They tell Baruch to disappear. The king, sitting by a brazier, listens to a few columns, cuts the scroll up with a penknife and burns it piece by piece. Nobody in the room tears their clothes. Three men try to stop him and are ignored. The reply is the neatest thing in the book: write it again, longer, with many similar words added.
JER 37:1-21	Egypt's army marches out and the Chaldeans lift the siege, and for a moment things look recoverable. Jeremiah's word: Pharaoh is going home, and even a wounded remnant of the besieging army would return and burn the city. He goes to see about his property, and is arrested at the gate as a defector. Beaten, dungeoned, then summoned for a private audience, his answer is the same one that got him arrested, plus a question: where are the prophets who said Babylon was not coming?
JER 38:1-14	The complaint against Jeremiah is now purely military: he is demoralising the troops.
