Zedekiah's reply to officials wanting him dead is one of the saddest lines any king gets: he is in your hand, I can do nothing. Jeremiah goes down a cistern on ropes into mud. Ebedmelech, an Ethiopian eunuch, tells the king the man will starve down there. He finds old rags to pad Jeremiah's armpits so the ropes do not cut him. Next time on Jeremiah: the king wants a private word.
JER 38:15-28	Jeremiah's opening position: answering will get him killed, and the advice will be ignored regardless. Under oath, he gives terms: walk out and surrender, and you and the city both survive. Zedekiah's real fear, it turns out, is not the city but being mocked by the men who defected before him. Jeremiah shows him the alternative: the palace women led out reciting a taunt, feet sunk in the same mud he was just pulled from. The chapter ends with a cover story agreed between them, and it holds.
JER 39:1-18	Everything Jeremiah spent forty chapters saying happens in eighteen verses, narrated flat, which makes it worse. Zedekiah runs at night through the garden gate and is caught on the Jericho plain. Nebuchadnezzar orders his sons killed in front of him first, then his eyes put out, so the last thing he ever saw was chosen for him. The city burns. Then two small mercies: the poorest are given fields and vineyards, and Nebuchadnezzar personally orders Jeremiah looked after. Ebedmelech, who found the rags, is told he will live.
JER 40:1-16	Jeremiah is released by a Babylonian officer who delivers a short, theologically accurate sermon about why Jerusalem fell. He is offered Babylon with a pension, or freedom to go anywhere, and chooses to stay with the governor at Mizpah. For a few verses things go well: refugees return, and there is a large harvest of wine and summer fruit, the only prosperity in the book. Then Johanan brings word of an assassination plot. Gedaliah's reply is the last mistake of his life: you are lying about Ishmael.
JER 41:1-18	Ishmael eats a meal with Gedaliah and then kills him at the table, along with the Jews present and the Chaldean garrison. What follows is worse: eighty pilgrims arrive in mourning for a temple that no longer exists, and Ishmael goes out to meet them weeping, then kills them and fills Asa's old cistern with the bodies. Ten survive by disclosing where the food is hidden. He takes the survivors toward Ammon, is intercepted at Gibeon, and the captives simply walk over to the other side.
JER 42:1-22	The delegation's oath is emphatic: whatever the answer is, good or bad, we will do it. Jeremiah takes ten days, which suggests he did not have the answer in advance. The answer is stay, with a reason unusual in this book, that God grieves over the harm he has brought. The alternative gets a neat inversion: the sword you are running from will meet you in Egypt. Jeremiah adds his own verdict, that they had already decided before they asked and dealt deceitfully with their own souls.
JER 43:1-13	The response to a ten-day-awaited answer is to accuse the prophet of taking dictation from his secretary. Then they do what they were told not to, and take Jeremiah and Baruch along, so the prophet ends up in Egypt against his own preaching. There he gets another prop job: bury stones at Pharaoh's door, and announce a Babylonian throne will stand there. The image for the conquest is domestic: Nebuchadnezzar will put Egypt on like a shepherd puts on his coat, and leave in peace.
JER 44:1-16	The argument is made from the evidence outside the window: you have seen what happened to Jerusalem, and here you are doing the same thing in a new country. The list of past offenders is unusually complete, including the kings' wives and their own. The threat is that the return they are dreaming about will not happen. Then the audience answers, which is rare in this book, and their answer is four words long: we will not listen. Next time on Jeremiah: they explain their reasoning.
JER 44:17-30	Their reasoning turns out to be economic: when we burned incense to the queen of the sky we had food, and things fell apart after we stopped. The women add that their husbands helped make the cakes too. Jeremiah concedes the timeline and reverses the causation. Then the cutting line: fine, keep your vows, perform them. What follows is erasure: no Judean in Egypt will ever again use God's name in an oath. The sign offered is Pharaoh Hophra, who will go the way Zedekiah went.
JER 45:1-5	Five verses, addressed to the man who did the writing. Baruch's complaint is quoted rather than described: sorrow added to pain, worn out with groaning, no rest. God does not deny any of it, but offers scale instead, that he is dismantling what he built across the whole land, so this is no time to seek great things for yourself. The one promise is small and, in context, enormous: wherever you go, you will escape with your life.
JER 46:1-28	The oracles against the nations open with a battle already lost, Carchemish, told in careful preparation: harness, polish, put on the mail. Mid-stanza the tone flips: why am I watching them run? Egypt floods like the Nile but the day belongs to Yahweh. The best line jabs at Egypt's famous export: go up to Gilead for balm, none of it works. Then Nebuchadnezzar invades, Egypt a heifer whose mercenaries bolt like stall-fed calves. Jacob is told not to fear.
JER 47:1-7	A short oracle, and its best feature is the detail about the fathers: at the sound of the chariots they do not turn back for their own children, because their hands have gone weak. That single image does more than the flood metaphor around it. Gaza gets a shaved head, Ashkelon gets nothing. Then the chapter does something unusual: it addresses the sword itself, asks how long before it will be quiet, and tells it to rest. The sword's answer is that it has orders and cannot.
JER 48:1-26	Moab's oracle is a gazetteer, one town destroyed per clause, with a joke buried in it: Madmen, an actual place, told it will be brought to silence. The best image is a wine cellar: Moab has sat undisturbed on his lees so long his taste is unchanged, so the punishment is decanting, men pouring him off and breaking the jars. Chemosh goes into captivity with his own staff. The aside about doing God's work negligently is chilling to find here.
JER 48:27-47	It opens with a grudge: you shook your head every time you mentioned Israel. Then the register shifts, and God says he will wail for Moab himself, weeping more than Jazer wept over the vine of Sibmah. The losses are agricultural: no shouting at the winepress. The terror, pit and snare sequence returns from Isaiah, and the closing line is one word of hope after forty seven verses of demolition.
JER 49:1-19	Ammon first: a property dispute, has Israel no heirs, that somebody else lives in Gad? Their god goes into exile with his priests, which happens to every god in these chapters. Then Edom, and the argument is thoroughness: grape pickers leave gleanings, thieves stop once satisfied, but Esau is stripped bare, hiding places opened. Tucked in the middle is an unexpected offer, that the orphans will be kept alive. The pride is located precisely: you nested as high as an eagle, and it will not help.
JER 49:20-39	Four short oracles, each with one good image. Edom's fall is loud enough to reach the Red Sea. Damascus is asked, almost fondly, how the city of praise has not already been abandoned, then her palaces burn. Kedar and Hazor are indicted for comfort: a nation at ease, with neither gates nor bars, which reads like an epitaph. Elam gets the four winds and a promise that no country will be without an Elamite refugee. Two of the four end with the captivity reversed.
JER 50:1-23	The instruction is publicity: raise a standard, publish it, Babylon is taken and its gods embarrassed. Israel's shepherds get blamed for the sheep wandering until they forgot home, and the predators are quoted offering a theological defence for eating them, the nastiest line here. Then the accounting: Assyria devoured Israel first, Babylon broke his bones last, so Babylon gets what Assyria got. The best promise is a failed search: Israel's guilt looked for and not found. Babylon, elsewhere the instrument, is now the broken hammer of the earth.
JER 50:24-46	The opening image is a trap already sprung: you are caught, Babylon, and did not notice. Then an armoury opens, and the attack orders are practical: come from the farthest border, open the storehouses, leave nothing. The middle runs the word sword down a list, princes, wise men, soldiers, treasures, then drought on the water, a country mad over idols. The new tenants are ostriches and wolves. The last note is acoustic: the earth trembles, and the cry is heard among the nations.
JHN 1:1-28	No genealogy, no manger, no shepherds. John starts before Genesis does, with the Word, and only reaches a human being in verse six: a witness, explicitly not the light. The tragedy is stated flatly, that the world made through him did not recognise him. Then the sentence the whole book turns on, that the Word became flesh. Next time on John: the Lamb of God.
JHN 1:29-51	John's line about the Lamb of God is delivered twice, and the second time two of his own disciples walk off after Jesus, which he seems to have intended. Recruitment then goes by word of mouth: Andrew fetches Simon, Philip fetches Nathanael, whose remark about Nazareth is the most honest line in the chapter. Jesus mentions a fig tree instead of arguing.
JHN 2:1-25	The first sign is a catering emergency. His mother reports the problem rather than asking, he objects that it is not his hour, and she instructs the staff anyway. Six jars meant for ritual washing come back full of good wine, and the compliment goes to the bridegroom. Then Jerusalem, where John puts the temple clearance at the start rather than the end.
JHN 3:1-36	Nicodemus comes at night, which John mentions for a reason. He takes being born again entirely literally and asks whether he is expected to re-enter a womb. Jesus points out that the teacher of Israel might have managed better. Then Nicodemus falls silent partway through and the speech continues without him. Judgment ends up defined as preferring the dark.
JHN 4:1-27	The longest conversation Jesus has with anyone is with a Samaritan woman at a well at noon, and it starts because he is thirsty with nothing to draw with. She notices the etiquette problem immediately. He offers water that stops thirst permanently, she takes it as a plumbing improvement, and the subject changes abruptly to her five husbands. Next time on John: the town.
JHN 4:28-54	She abandons the water jar, the detail John keeps, and pitches the town on a man who told her everything she did. The disciples try to get him to eat and get a speech about harvest. The town's final word to her is gracious and slightly rude: we believe now, but not because of you. In Galilee an official is sent home with one sentence.
JHN 5:1-22	A pool with five porches, a crowd waiting for water to move, and a man ill thirty-eight years with nobody to carry him in. Jesus asks whether he wants to get well, which is not rhetorical in that setting. He walks, and the first thing anyone says to him is that carrying a mat on a Sabbath is illegal. Next time on John: the evidence.
JHN 5:23-47	The argument turns legal. He concedes the rule that self-testimony does not count, then calls witnesses: John, a lamp they enjoyed while it was novel; the works; the Father. Then the charge back at them: you comb the scriptures looking for life and will not come to the one they point at. He declines to accuse them, on the grounds that Moses will.
JHN 6:1-34	The feeding opens with a test question, and Philip fails it with arithmetic: two hundred denarii, still not enough. Andrew produces a boy with five loaves and two fish and does not pretend it helps. Five thousand fed, and an instruction to collect the scraps so nothing is wasted. Their conclusion: make him king by force. Next time on John: the bread of life.
JHN 6:35-71	The bread claim gets harder. First he is the bread of life. Then he came down from heaven, which they answer by naming his parents. Then the bread is his flesh, and when they ask how anyone eats that, he repeats it with blood added. The only mass resignation in the gospels follows, and a note that one of the twelve is already a problem.
JHN 7:1-26	His brothers, who do not believe in him, advise him on publicity: nobody hides, go up to the feast and make a splash. He declines, then goes anyway, privately. The city is full of muttering, split between good man and fraud. Halfway through he starts teaching in the temple, and the first objection is about his credentials. Next time on John: the last day.
JHN 7:27-53	The argument about his origins is circular: they know where he is from, and the Christ's origin is a mystery, so knowing disqualifies him. Officers sent to arrest him come back without him, and their explanation is the best line in the chapter: nobody ever spoke like this. The crowd divides over Galilee versus Bethlehem, a geography argument standing in for a theological one.
JHN 8:1-29	The trap is built carefully: a woman caught in the act, a law that prescribes stoning, and a question with no safe answer. He writes in the dirt, twice, and hands the decision back with one condition attached, and the crowd thins from the oldest down. What he says to her is short and contains no lecture. Next time on John: Abraham comes up.
JHN 8:30-59	This starts with people who believe him and ends with them picking up rocks. Freedom is the flashpoint: they hear an insult, since Abraham's children were never slaves, and he means slavery to sin. They claim Abraham, he grants the bloodline and denies the resemblance. They claim God, he names the devil instead. Then the sentence that ends the argument: before Abraham was, I AM.
JHN 9:1-41	The disciples open with the question, whose fault it was, and get told it is the wrong question. Then mud made with spit, a pool called Sent, and a man who comes back seeing. What follows is a courtroom farce in four rounds: neighbours, Pharisees, frightened parents, and the man himself, whose testimony is the flattest in the gospel. I was blind, now I see.
JHN 10:1-42	The sheepfold speech goes over so badly he restarts it, and take two is stranger, since he is now the door as well as the shepherd. The contrast is with the hired hand, whose reaction to a wolf is rational and useless. At a winter festival they ask for a straight answer and get one: I and the Father are one. Stones come out.
JHN 11:1-31	Word arrives that a friend is dying, and Jesus waits two days, which John reports without excusing. The disciples object that the last visit to Judea involved stoning, and Thomas settles it: let us go and die with him. Jesus says Lazarus is asleep, they take it as good news, and he has to say the word dead. Next time on John: the tomb.
JHN 11:32-57	Mary says exactly what Martha said, and this time Jesus does not answer with theology. In the shortest verse in the book, he weeps. At the cave, Martha makes the practical objection about four days, out loud, in front of everyone. Then the coldest turn in the chapter: a committee meeting, and a high priest observing that one death is cheaper than a nation.
JHN 12:1-25	Dinner at Bethany with a man at the table who was recently dead. Mary uses a pound of nard on his feet, and Judas raises the charitable objection, which John undercuts by noting that Judas ran the money box and helped himself. Next day: palms, a donkey, and disciples with no idea what they were doing. Next time on John: a voice from the sky.
JHN 12:26-50	He admits his soul is troubled, then rules out asking to be spared. The voice from the sky reaches the crowd as weather, or possibly an angel. He tells them to use the daylight, then hides, and his public teaching ends there. John accounts for the unbelief from Isaiah, and adds the sting: plenty of the rulers believed and said nothing, preferring the praise.
JHN 13:1-38	There is no bread and cup in John. Instead the Lord and Teacher strips down, ties on a towel and washes twelve pairs of feet, one pair belonging to a man already committed to selling him. Peter's refusal is meant as respect and is overruled. Then the dipped bread, do it quickly, and Judas leaves. John ends the sentence: it was night.
JHN 14:1-31	The comfort speech is repeatedly derailed by the disciples, which is what makes it readable. Thomas admits they have no idea where he is going. Philip asks to be shown the Father, having spent years in the room, and gets a reply that is patient and faintly incredulous. Around those interruptions: many rooms, greater works, and a Counselor whose job is teaching and reminding.
JHN 15:1-27	The vine image is not sentimental. The Father runs it, unproductive branches are removed and productive ones cut back, and the point is output. Then the promotion: no longer servants, since a servant is not told what is going on, and they have been told everything. Then it turns cold. The world's hatred is not a risk but a schedule, and it is inherited.
JHN 16:1-33	The advance warning is specific: expulsion, and killers who believe they are performing a religious duty. Then the argument that his departure is an advantage, since the Counselor cannot come otherwise. He admits there is more he cannot tell them yet. The riddle about a little while they discuss among themselves without asking him, and he answers anyway with labour pains forgotten.
JHN 17:1-26	The whole chapter is one prayer, out loud, with the disciples listening. He reports the work finished, and asks for the glory he had before the world existed. His requests are narrow and repeated: keep them, keep them together, keep them from the evil one, and do not take them out of the world. One flat parenthesis: none lost, except one.
JHN 18:1-21	There is no agony in the garden in John, and no kiss. Jesus walks out to meet the arresting party, and when he answers his own question the whole detachment ends up on the ground. He negotiates the release of everyone else before letting himself be taken. Peter's sword produces one casualty, and John alone names him: Malchus, right ear. Next time on John: Pilate.
JHN 18:22-40	The reply about procedure earns him a slap, which he also queries. Then Peter's second and third denials, the last to a relative of the man he wounded, and the rooster. The delegation will not enter the Praetorium, since that would make them unfit to eat the Passover, and John records it without comment. A governor asks a legal question and gets a philosophical answer.
JHN 19:1-22	The flogging comes in the middle of the trial rather than at the end, as an attempt to satisfy the crowd. Pilate brings the result out with three words quoted ever since. The charge shifts from politics to the Son of God, which makes him more frightened, not less. The lever that finally moves him is Caesar's friendship. Next time on John: the cross.
JHN 19:23-42	The soldiers split the clothes, then decline to cut up the seamless coat and gamble for it instead, which is also just what soldiers would do. Four women stand there, three of them named Mary. From the cross he arranges his mother's housing, a startlingly practical thing to be doing. A spear replaces the leg-breaking, and the writer stops to insist he saw it.
JHN 20:1-31	The resurrection in John begins as a suspected theft. Mary's report is entirely reasonable and entirely wrong. The footrace to the tomb is recorded with the winner noted, and John keeps the detail nobody would invent: the head cloth folded on its own. She mistakes him for the gardener; it takes one word, her name. Thomas sets precise conditions and uses none of them.
JHN 21:1-25	An epilogue in which seven disciples, having seen the risen Lord twice, go fishing and catch nothing all night. The man on the beach asks the question fishermen least want to hear, then tells them where to cast. Peter's response to recognition is to get dressed and then jump in the water. Three questions, matching three denials, and Peter upset by the third.
JOB 1:1-22	Job is introduced as blameless, wealthy and slightly anxious, sacrificing for his children in case they sinned privately. Then the scene cuts to heaven, where Satan makes an audit point: piety is easy behind a hedge. God removes the hedge. Four messengers arrive one on top of another, each the sole survivor. Job shaves his head and blesses the name that took them.
JOB 2:1-13	Round two, and God notes that Job kept his integrity for nothing. Satan revises his theory to skin for skin and gets permission for everything short of killing him. Job ends up in the ash heap scraping his sores with a shard of pottery. His wife offers the shortest speech in the book. Three friends arrive and say nothing for a week.
JOB 3:1-26	Seven days of silence end with Job cursing his own birthday in detail: strike the date from the calendar, let no star rise on it, bring in the professional day-cursers. His argument for the grave is unexpectedly democratic, since kings, prisoners and slaves are all equally at rest there. Twice he asks why light is given to people who want the dark.
JOB 4:1-21	Eliphaz begins by asking whether Job would mind if he spoke, the classic opening of a man about to be unhelpful. He compliments Job on how well he used to comfort other people. Then the theory: nobody innocent ever perished, therefore. He backs it with lions who starve anyway and a ghost story, a spirit sliding past his face at night.
JOB 5:1-27	Eliphaz warms to the discipline theory: God wounds and then bandages the wound he made, so being corrected is really a compliment. He throws in a numerical flourish, six troubles and then seven, and a rural retirement package including peace with wild animals and a grave arrived at like grain in its season. Appalling timing, delivered to a man whose children are already buried.
JOB 6:1-30	Job asks for his misery to be weighed on actual scales, since it would outweigh the sea's sand. He defends complaining with livestock: nothing brays over good grass. His one request is that God finish him off. The friends get the best image here, a desert stream swollen in the cold months and gone by summer, so caravans navigate towards it and stand there confounded.
JOB 7:1-21	Job's model for human life is shift work, a hired man watching the sun for the shadow that means the day is over. His nights are the assignment, and his days go faster than a shuttle. He asks whether he is the sea or some monster that requires a guard, and notes that God's remedy for insomnia is worse dreams.
JOB 8:1-22	Bildad has no interest in Eliphaz's bedside manner. He opens by calling Job's speech wind, then says out loud what Eliphaz kept implying: your children sinned and God handled it. Having said that, he recommends prayer. His authorities are the ancestors, on the grounds that we ourselves know nothing, and his image is a spider's web the godless man intends to lean on.
JOB 9:1-35	Job concedes the theology and then finds the flaw. God is unanswerable, mountains moved before they notice, the Bear and Orion built to order. That is the problem: the opposing party is also the bailiff. Then the line the friends never recover from: he destroys the blameless and the wicked alike. He wants an umpire who can put a hand on both. There isn't one.
JOB 10:1-22	Having decided complaining is now his job, Job does it properly. He asks whether God has eyes of flesh and a mortal lifespan, since only someone short of time would hurry the investigation. His best argument is manufacturing: you poured me out like milk and curdled me like cheese, knit the bones in, and are now dismantling it.
JOB 11:1-20	Zophar's contribution is mostly annoyance at the word count. He would like God to answer Job personally, which is unwise of him, and offers the reassurance that God is charging Job rather less than he owes. There follows a fine passage on the unsearchable, higher than heaven and deeper than Sheol, immediately spent on the claim that he knows what it contains.
JOB 12:1-25	Job's patience gives out: no doubt wisdom will die with you. The man who prayed and was answered is the neighbourhood joke while robbers do fine. Ask the animals, ask the birds, ask the fish. Then he takes their favourite genre, the hymn to God's power, and turns it on the respectable: judges made fools, priests led away, chiefs groping in the dark.
JOB 13:1-28	Job informs the committee that he has read the material, thanks, and would prefer to argue with the Almighty directly. His review of their performance: forgers of lies, physicians of no value, and if they were completely silent they would be wise. Lying on God's behalf is still lying, and God audits character witnesses too. Their finest lines are proverbs of ashes.
JOB 14:1-22	The saddest speech so far, and it is mostly botany. A tree that gets cut down smells water and starts over. A man lies down and that is the end of the transaction, until the heavens are no more. Job's proposal is oddly tender: hide me in Sheol, wait out your own anger, then set a reminder and call me.
JOB 15:1-35	Eliphaz comes back with the gloves off. Job is full of east wind, and does he think he was born before the hills or sat in on God's private meetings? Then the set piece, a wicked man fat about the thighs, begging bread, living in houses that are already rubble. None of it is about Job, and all of it is about Job.
JOB 16:1-22	Job's review: miserable comforters, all of you. He could produce their speeches without effort if the chairs were swapped, head-shaking included, but claims he would have used his mouth for something useful. Then the imagery turns violent and stays there: God as an animal gnashing its teeth, as a wrestler taking him by the neck, as an archery range with Job in the middle.
JOB 17:1-16	The grave is ready and the mockers are still in the room. Job asks for a guarantor, someone to strike hands and post collateral, and looks around a circle of three men who will not. He is tired of being told the light is near while standing in the dark. If the worm is his sister, hope is a relative nobody has met.
JOB 18:1-21	Bildad is offended at being lumped in with the livestock, and answers with the most booby-trapped speech in the book. The lamp goes out, a net takes his feet, a snare his heel, a noose waits in the ground. The firstborn of death eats his limbs. No son, no grandson. It is a portrait, unsigned, of the man who has just buried ten children.
JOB 19:1-29	Job has been counting: ten reproaches, and nobody is embarrassed yet. God, in his account, has become a besieging army, walling the road, confiscating the crown, camping troops around the tent. Then an inventory of everyone who has left the room, down to a wife who cannot stand his breath. He wants it carved in rock, because he knows his Redeemer lives.
JOB 20:1-29	Zophar returns, announcing that he is answering in haste, which is at least honest. The thesis has not moved. What is new is the gastroenterology. Sin tastes sweet, he saves it under his tongue, and then it curdles in his gut into cobra venom, so the swallowed wealth comes straight back up. A bronze arrow exits through the liver.
JOB 21:1-34	Job requests the floor and grants permission to mock afterwards, then aims at the load-bearing wall of their system. The wicked, empirically, do fine. Kids established, bulls that never fail, tambourine and harp, and a quick painless drop into Sheol, all while telling God to keep his distance. So how often does that lamp get put out?
JOB 22:1-30	Eliphaz opens with a shrug of cosmic scale, since God gains nothing from anyone's virtue, and then, having run out of insinuation, produces actual charges. Seized pledges, water withheld from the weary, widows sent away with nothing, the arms of orphans broken. None of it happened, but it makes the theory work. His terms are almost generous: dump your gold in the streambed.
JOB 23:1-17	Job's problem is no longer theological, it is logistical. He would like a hearing, case in order, mouth full of arguments, and he is confident God would listen rather than out-muscle him. The difficulty is finding the courtroom. East, west, north, south, nobody there. He falls back on what he knows: tested, he will come out like gold.
JOB 24:1-25	Job's complaint becomes documentary. Boundary stones moved, the orphan's donkey taken, the widow's ox held as security, and the poor sent out to forage, gleaning the vineyards of the men who robbed them. The night shift gets its own section: a murderer at first light, an adulterer waiting for dusk, burglars tunnelling through walls. And the verdict: God keeps the powerful secure.
JOB 25:1-6	Bildad's third speech runs six verses and then he leaves the stage forever. Dominion, fear, uncountable armies, and therefore nobody born of a woman can be clean. His flourish is astronomical: the moon is not bright and the stars are not pure, so what does that make you. He answers with the word worm, twice, in case once was ambiguous.
JOB 26:1-14	Job thanks them warmly for the help rendered to the powerless and the wisdom supplied to a man who had none. Bildad can do a hymn. Job does a better one. God stretches the north over nothing and hangs the earth on nothing at all, packs water into clouds that somehow hold, and runs Rahab through. All of it a whisper at the fence.
JOB 27:1-23	Job swears an oath by the God who has denied him justice, a fair summary of the whole book, and refuses on the record to agree with his friends. Then he delivers their sermon himself. Children multiplied for the sword, silver heaped up like dust and worn by better men, a house built to the standards of a moth. The east wind takes him.
JOB 28:1-28	An interlude, and the finest poem in the book so far. Humans are astonishing at extraction: shafts sunk where nobody lives, men dangling on ropes, mountains turned over by the roots, down paths no falcon has seen. Then the pivot. Where is wisdom mined? The deep says not here. Destruction and Death have heard a rumour, which is the best lead available.
JOB 29:1-25	Job spends a chapter in the past tense. God's lamp on his head, children around the tent, oil coming out of rock, an extravagant way of saying business was good. At the gate the nobles' tongues stuck to their palates. He is careful to say why: eyes to the blind, feet to the lame, predators' jaws broken open.
JOB 30:1-31	The sequel, in the present tense, and the fall is measured socially rather than financially. He is now the entertainment for men whose fathers he would not have put with the sheep dogs, outcasts living in rock holes and chewing broom roots. The rabble comes through the breach in him as through a hole in a wall. He cries out and God looks at him.
JOB 31:1-40	The closing argument, structured as a series of self-curses. If I looked at a young woman, let my wife grind for someone else. His best line is about his servants: the same God made him in the womb, so what would he say when God stands up? Then he demands the indictment. He would wear it as a crown.
JOB 32:1-22	The three run out of speeches, and the narrator says why: Job is righteous in his own eyes. Enter Elihu son of Barachel the Buzite, who has apparently been standing there the whole time, furious at Job for defending himself instead of God and at the friends for condemning him without answering him. He is, by his own account, a wineskin about to burst.
JOB 33:1-33	Elihu opens with reassurance nobody asked for, promising he is only clay and will not be terrifying. He reads Job's position back to him, clean, innocent, feet in the stocks, and answers it: God is bigger and does not file reports. But he does speak, in dreams, steering sleepers off a course that ends in the pit. The bone pain is part of the curriculum.
JOB 34:1-37	Elihu opens by addressing the wise men, meaning himself and, generously, whoever agrees. He quotes Job accurately, which is new, then improves the quotation until Job sounds like he keeps bad company. The argument is procedural: nobody appointed God over the earth, so there is no authority he could be abusing. His sharpest question is whether Job has ever tried "I was guilty, teach me."
JOB 35:1-16	Elihu tackles the profit question, and his answer is oddly modern. Look up: the sky is higher than you. Your sin does nothing to God and your righteousness gives him nothing. Both land on other people. Then a fine line about the God who gives songs in the night, followed by this: when the oppressed cry out, nobody thinks to ask where he is.
JOB 36:1-33	Elihu wants more of everyone's time because a man perfect in knowledge is present, meaning himself. The friends' theology with better manners: fetters are a teaching device, ears opened, prosperity for whoever listens. His best line is that God delivers the afflicted by means of their affliction. Then he drifts into weather, and the storm arrives while he talks.
JOB 37:1-24	Elihu's last speech is a live weather broadcast, and his own heart is trembling. Thunder under the whole sky, lightning to the ends of the earth, nobody asked to comprehend it. Snow is told to fall, which shuts up every man's hands and puts the animals in their dens. Then, mid-storm, someone else starts speaking.
JOB 38:1-41	The whirlwind arrives, and God opens by asking who has been darkening counsel with words without knowledge. The questions go the other way now. Where were you when the foundations went in and the morning stars were singing? It is the best poetry in the book: the gates of death, the address of light, rain falling on wilderness just to make grass.
JOB 39:1-30	The interrogation moves to zoology and turns almost affectionate. Does Job supervise the birthing calendar of mountain goats? Who signed the wild donkey's release papers? The ostrich gets a strange, honest paragraph: eggs left in the dust, no fear because wisdom was simply not issued to her, and yet she leaves horse and rider behind.
JOB 40:1-24	God pauses long enough for an answer, and Job gives the shortest speech in the book: I am small, hand over mouth, I have said enough. It is not an exit. The question underneath the whole book gets asked out loud: will you condemn me so that you can be innocent? Then, instead of an argument, an animal.
JOB 41:1-34	A whole chapter on Leviathan, and God is clearly enjoying it. Can you land him on a hook, keep him as a pet for your daughters, sell him by the piece at market? Try it once and you will decline the rematch. Sealed armour, eyes like the eyelids of morning, torches out of the mouth. King over all the sons of pride.
JOB 42:1-17	Job's answer is six verses and concedes everything except the thing he was accused of: where he had heard of God secondhand he has now seen him. Then the twist nobody expected. God is angry at Eliphaz and the other two, because they did not speak rightly about him, as Job did. Their penance is asking the man they lectured to pray for them.
LUK 1:1-35	Luke opens like a historian, with a dedication. Then a childless elderly couple and a priest whose lot that week is the incense. Gabriel turns up with a son, a name, a job description drawn from Elijah. Zacharias asks for evidence, given his age, and gets nine months of silence. Mary asks the practical question. Next time on Luke: he answers it.
LUK 1:36-80	The angel's evidence is Elizabeth, six months pregnant, and Mary's answer is consent rather than argument. In the hill country a baby kicks on cue. Then the Magnificat, which is not a lullaby: thrones emptied, the proud scattered, the rich sent away with nothing. John's naming becomes a family dispute settled by a writing tablet, and his father's voice comes back.
LUK 2:1-25	An imperial paperwork exercise moves the story to Bethlehem. The birth gets one sentence and a feeding trough. The announcement goes to men working nights in a field, and the sign offered is deliberately unimpressive: a baby in a trough. Then the sky fills with an army singing. Next time on Luke: Simeon speaks.
LUK 2:26-52	Simeon had a promise that he would not die first, and now asks to be dismissed. His song is about a light for the nations, a wider audience than anyone at the temple that morning. Then he turns to Mary and adds the part nobody wants: a sword through her. Twelve years pass in one verse, and the family loses the boy.
LUK 3:1-38	Luke fixes the date, then hands the wilderness prophet a microphone. Isaiah supplies the landscaping metaphor. The unusual part is the follow-up: the crowds ask what to do and get concrete answers, one coat to whoever has none, tax collectors to stop padding the bill. The genealogy runs the other way from Matthew's and does not stop at Abraham.
LUK 4:1-23	Forty days, no food, and three offers: bread from a stone, the kingdoms of the world, then the temple parapet. He answers with scripture each time, and the devil leaves only until another time. At Nazareth he reads Isaiah, sits down, and says the sentence out loud: today, in your hearing. Next time on Luke: the room does the arithmetic.
LUK 4:24-44	The line that empties the room is not about himself. It is about two foreigners, Elijah's widow in Sidon and Naaman the Syrian. The congregation carries him to the edge of a cliff, and he walks out through them. At Capernaum the reaction is the same word every time, authority. Simon's mother-in-law has her fever rebuked as though it were a spirit.
LUK 5:1-39	The crowd on the shore forces him into a boat. Simon has fished all night for nothing, then nearly sinks two boats and asks Jesus to go away. He is recruited instead. With the law's experts seated and watching, a man arrives through the roof, and forgiveness comes before mobility, which starts the argument the healing then settles.
LUK 6:1-26	The grain field complaint gets David and the show bread. The withered hand, which Luke specifies is the right one, gets a staged confrontation: the man called into the middle. Before choosing the twelve, Luke has him pray on a mountain all night. Then a sermon in matched pairs, blessing and warning in the same rhythm. Next time on Luke: enemies.
LUK 6:27-49	The instructions get harder. Love enemies, bless people who curse you, offer the second cheek. Lend expecting nothing, because loving people who love you is a standard sinners already meet. Then the images: a blind man guiding a blind man, and the beam and the speck, which he calls hypocrisy outright. Fruit reveals the tree.
LUK 7:1-24	The centurion never appears in person. He sends the local elders first, who vouch for him, then sends friends to head Jesus off with the argument from chain of command. Then Nain, which is Luke's alone: a funeral, a widow burying her only son, and no request from anyone. Next time on Luke: he talks about John.
LUK 7:25-50	The question is what they went out to look at. The greatest man born of women is outranked by the least in the kingdom. The generation is children who refuse every game: John fasted, so demon; he eats, so drunk. Then dinner at a Pharisee's, interrupted by a woman who weeps on his feet, and a quiet audit of Simon's hospitality.
LUK 8:1-27	Luke names the funders, which nobody else does: Mary Magdalene, Joanna Susanna, paying for the whole operation out of their own property. Then the seed, in four conditions, and an explanation that names the cares and riches doing the choking. His family arrive, and he redefines the word rather than going out. Next time on Luke: a man with no clothes.
LUK 8:28-56	He asks the man's name and gets an answer that is not a name. The demons negotiate: not the abyss, the pigs. The town finds the man dressed, and is frightened enough to ask Jesus to go. Then two interruptions: Jairus on his knees about a twelve-year-old, and a woman with twelve years of bleeding.
LUK 9:1-31	The twelve go out with no staff, which leaves them entirely dependent on hospitality. Herod hears, and Luke lets him say the awkward part himself: John I beheaded, so who is this. The retreat to Bethsaida lasts as long. Five loaves, two fish, seating in fifties, twelve baskets. Then Peter's correct answer, immediately embargoed. Next time on Luke: the cloud arrives.
LUK 9:32-62	The three nearly sleep through it, and Peter suggests building, which Luke bluntly annotates as not knowing what he said. Then the cloud and the voice. While the crowd is admiring the majesty of God, Jesus says he will be handed over, and Luke notes they were too frightened to ask. Then James and John offer to burn down a Samaritan village.
LUK 10:1-21	Seventy more get sent out, and the brief is harder than the twelve's: no purse, no sandals, no greeting anyone on the road. Refused, you wipe the dust off and announce it anyway, less a threat than a delivery receipt. They come back thrilled about the demons, and are told the list their names are on matters more. Next time on Luke: a lawyer.
LUK 10:22-42	A lawyer arrives to test him and ends up answering his own question. His follow-up, asking who exactly counts as a neighbour, is what Luke calls self-justification. It gets him the story, and a Samaritan who does the whole job, including the bill. Asked which of the three was the neighbour, he cannot bring himself to say Samaritan.
LUK 11:1-28	Asked for a prayer, he gives a short one, then a story: a man wakes his neighbour at midnight and gets the bread not out of affection but because he will not stop knocking. After a mute demon, the crowd splits between Beelzebul and demanding a sign, and he notes that Satan purging Satan is a poor business model. Next time on Luke: Jonah.
LUK 11:29-54	The demand for a sign gets Jonah, plus two witnesses: a foreign queen who travelled to hear Solomon, and a city that repented for a reluctant prophet. The dinner that follows goes badly. Criticised for skipping the washing, he replies with cups polished on the outside, then works through the woes: herbs tithed while justice is skipped, front-row seats, unmarked graves.
LUK 12:1-30	The crowd is large enough to be trampling itself, and the first word to the disciples is a warning about hypocrisy: what is said in a back room ends up on the roof. Then the interruption Luke keeps: a man wants help getting his share of an inheritance, and is handed a warning about greed. Next time on Luke: lamps kept lit.
LUK 12:31-59	Sell things, give them away, and note that the heart follows the money. Then the waiting: lamps lit, a master back from a wedding who ends up serving the staff who stayed awake. Peter asks whether this applies to them or everyone, and is answered sideways, with the steward who assumed the boss was late and a sliding scale: knowing more means answering for more.
LUK 13:1-35	Two disasters are put to him and both times he refuses the arithmetic that makes the dead worse sinners, then makes it personal anyway. The fig tree gets a stay of execution. A woman folded over for eighteen years is straightened, and the synagogue ruler complains about the day. Warned Herod wants him dead, he calls him a fox and keeps his own timetable.
LUK 14:1-35	Dinner at a Pharisee's, and Luke says outright they were watching him, so he asks the question first, gets silence. Then he audits the seating plan: take the worst chair and let the host promote you. To the host: stop inviting people who can pay you back. A guest offers a pious line, and gets the banquet nobody attends.
LUK 15:1-32	The complaint that starts the chapter is about who he eats with, and the answer is three stories. A shepherd abandons ninety-nine to find one. Then two sons: one drafts a speech he never gets to finish, because his father is already running. The elder brother stands outside doing sums about a goat.
LUK 16:1-31	The strangest parable: a manager starts forging discounts into his master's ledgers so the debtors will owe him favours, and gets praised for it. The point is not the fraud but the foresight. Then a man in purple, a beggar at his gate, and a reversal in which the rich man, still giving orders, asks for Lazarus to be sent with water.
LUK 17:1-37	The forgiveness quota is seven times a day. The apostles ask for more faith, and are told a mustard seed's worth would relocate a tree. Ten lepers are cleansed, and the only one who comes back is the Samaritan, which Luke saves for the last word of the sentence. Noah and Lot are cited not for wickedness but for how normal the day looked.
LUK 18:1-43	The lesson on persistence rests on a judge who admits he fears neither God nor man. The lesson on humility rests on a Pharisee whose prayer is a list of other people's faults, and a tax collector who manages one sentence. A ruler with a clean record leaves sad. Then the third passion prediction, and Luke's note that they understood none of it.
LUK 19:1-23	A senior tax collector, rich, ends up in a tree. Jesus stops under it and invites himself to dinner. Zacchaeus's response is arithmetic: half of everything to the poor, four times over to anyone he cheated. Then, because everyone expects the kingdom on schedule, a parable about a man going abroad to be made king. Next time on Luke: the reckoning.
LUK 19:24-48	The coin goes to the servant who already has ten, and the king's enemies are executed before him. Then the entry: an unridden colt, cloaks on the road, disciples shouting about all they have seen. Told to quiet them, he says the stones will do it instead. Mid-parade he stops and weeps over a coming siege. He clears the temple, then simply teaches there daily, untouchable while the crowd listens.
LUK 20:1-47	The credentials question gets a counter-question about John. The vineyard parable follows, a murdered heir, and the crowd's horrified gasp only makes it land harder. Spies open with flattery about Caesar's tax; he asks whose face is on the coin. The Sadducees pose seven brothers and one widow, answered with Moses at the burning bush. A scribe compliments him and the questions stop. He asks his own about David, then names the scribes: long robes, best seats, widows' houses.
LUK 21:1-38	The chapter opens on a widow and two coins, weighed against everyone else's surplus and found heavier. The temple's admirers get told the stonework is coming down. Arrests come first: synagogues, prisons, kings, no rehearsed defense, since the words will be supplied. Families will inform on each other. Jerusalem surrounded means leave, and pregnant and nursing women get named plainly. Then sun, moon, stars, and a budding fig tree marking the season.
LUK 22:1-36	Luke says plainly that Satan entered Judas, who goes off to negotiate. Setting up the meal is cloak-and-dagger: follow a man with a water jar, take the upper room. At the table: a cup, bread, then the flat line that the betrayer's hand is on the table with him. The disciples argue about rank, and are told the greatest serves. Simon is warned he'll be sifted like wheat and told how long he has before he denies it. Next time on Luke: the Mount of Olives.
LUK 22:37-71	Two swords are produced, and he ends the subject with "that is enough." On the Mount of Olives he asks for the cup and defers; Luke alone records the angel and the sweat like blood. The disciples are asleep from grief. Judas names the kiss before it lands. An ear comes off and, only in Luke, goes back on. Then the courtyard, three denials, the rooster, and the detail only Luke has: Jesus looks at Peter.
LUK 23:1-28	The charges before Pilate are political, since blasphemy won't travel: tax refusal, a claim to kingship. Pilate finds nothing and ships the problem to Herod, who mocks him and sends him back; the two become friends that day. Pilate finds no capital offence and offers a flogging instead. The crowd asks for a convicted murderer, and their voices prevail, a verdict by volume. Next time on Luke: the Skull.
LUK 23:29-56	The warning to the mourning women is about their future, not his. At the Skull, crucified between two criminals, his first words ask that they be forgiven. The mockery comes in tiers: rulers, soldiers, one of the men beside him. It is the other criminal who says the only accurate thing anyone says all day: this man has done nothing wrong. He is promised Paradise. Then darkness, the torn veil. Luke's centurion says righteous rather than Son of God.
LUK 24:1-26	The women arrive with spices for a body that is not there, and get a question rather than an announcement: why look for the living among the dead. Luke names them, then reports the apostles treated it as nonsense. Peter runs anyway, finds folded linen, goes home baffled. Then the road to Emmaus, where two disciples explain the crucifixion to the crucified man himself. He calls them slow, and starts from the prophets. Next time on Luke: the bread.
LUK 24:27-53	He walks them through Moses and the prophets, then makes as if to keep going, and is talked into staying. Recognition happens over broken bread, and he vanishes in the same moment. Back in Jerusalem the eleven are already saying he has appeared to Simon. Then he is in the room; they assume ghost, so he eats grilled fish while they watch. The commission is short: repentance goes to every nation, you are witnesses, wait for power.
MAT 1:1-25	Matthew begins with paperwork: three tidy sets of fourteen generations, the list quietly including Tamar, Rahab, Ruth and the woman he will only call Uriah's wife. Then the ledger stops working. Mary is pregnant, Joseph is not the reason, and he plans a decent quiet exit. An angel interrupts mid-sleep and hands him the child's name.
MAT 2:1-23	Star-watchers from the east ask the sitting king where the new king is. Herod extracts Bethlehem from the professionals and asks them to report back so that he too may worship. Gold, frankincense and myrrh are handed over, then a dream sends them home another road. Joseph is dreamed into Egypt. Herod kills every boy in Bethlehem two and under. The family returns only after he dies, and only as far as Nazareth.
MAT 3:1-17	John works the wilderness in camel hair on locusts and honey, and the whole Jordan region comes out to be baptized. The religious professionals arrive and are greeted as a nest of vipers: Abraham buys nothing, and the ax is already at the roots. Then the man whose shoes he says he is not fit to carry joins the queue. John finds this backwards and is talked round. Sky opens, dove, voice.
MAT 4:1-25	Forty days without food, and the devil arrives with three reasonable offers: stones into lunch, a jump from the temple with the angel clause quoted back, and every kingdom on earth for one bow. Each is answered with a quotation rather than an argument. Angels bring the meal after all. John is arrested, so Jesus moves to Capernaum and preaches John's own sentence. Four fishermen leave nets, boat and father.
MAT 5:1-24	He climbs a hill, sits down, and starts congratulating the wrong people: the poor in spirit, the mourners, the gentle, the hungry for righteousness, the merciful, the pure in heart, the peacemakers, anyone slandered on his account. Salt that has stopped being salt is gravel. Nothing in the law is repealed, and then the bar goes past the scribes: contempt for a brother is filed with murder. Next time on Matthew: the standard keeps rising.
MAT 5:25-48	The escalation continues. Settle out of court while you still can. A lustful look has already done the deed, and an eye is a cheaper loss than the whole body. Divorce narrows to one exception, and oaths are abolished outright. Retaliation gives way to the offered cheek, the surrendered cloak and the second mile. Loving people who love you back is a trick tax collectors manage.
MAT 6:1-34	Three religious performances are dismantled in turn: giving with a trumpet, praying on the street corner, fasting with a carefully ruined face. All three have already been paid, in applause. Prayer gets a short model instead of volume, with an unwelcome footnote about forgiving other people first. Treasure on earth is moth food. Nobody serves two masters. Birds do not farm, lilies outdress Solomon, tomorrow can do its own fretting.
MAT 7:1-29	The measure you use gets used on you, and the speck in a brother's eye waits behind the beam in your own. Pearls are wasted on pigs. Ask, seek, knock: no father hands over a stone when asked for bread. The gate is narrow and badly attended. An impressive resume of miracles still gets "I never knew you." Two houses, same storm, different foundations.
MAT 8:1-34	Coming off the mountain he touches a leper. A centurion asks for a long-distance cure on military logic and is told his faith beats anything in Israel. Peter's mother-in-law is up and serving within a verse. Two volunteers are warned: no pillow, and the dead can bury each other. He sleeps through a storm and calls the sailors faithless before calming it. Two demoniacs negotiate their exit into a pig herd, and the town asks him to leave.
MAT 9:1-38	A paralyzed man arrives on a bed and gets forgiveness first, which the scribes silently rule blasphemy, so he heals the man to settle who has the authority. Then he hires a tax collector and prescribes mercy over sacrifice to the offended. On the way to a dead girl, a woman twelve years ill touches his coat. The professional mourners are laughing when he puts them out.
MAT 10:1-42	The twelve are named and handed authority over demons and disease, with Judas flagged at the end of the list in advance. The travel instructions are brutally light: no money, no bag, no spare coat, no staff. Then the job description turns honest. Councils, floggings, governors, kings, brothers turning in brothers, and no need to prepare a defence. Sparrows are cheap and still counted. He came with a sword, not peace.
MAT 11:1-30	John, in prison, sends to ask whether he backed the right man, which is a fair question from a cell. The answer is not yes but a list: blind, lame, lepers, deaf, dead, poor. Then Jesus praises the man who just doubted him as more than a prophet. John fasted and was called possessed, he eats and is called a drunk. Chorazin, Bethsaida and Capernaum are told even Sodom would have done better.
MAT 12:1-28	The disciples snack in a wheat field on the Sabbath and the Pharisees file a complaint, so Jesus cites David eating forbidden bread and priests who work the Sabbath every week. In the synagogue the trap is a withered hand and an innocent question, answered with the sheep any of them would haul out of a pit on a Saturday. The Pharisees leave to discuss killing him. Next time on Matthew: he takes their Satan theory apart.
MAT 12:29-50	You do not rob a strong man's house without tying him up first. Every sin is forgivable except speaking against the Spirit. The tree is known by its fruit, and every throwaway sentence goes on the record. Asked to perform a sign, he offers Jonah instead: three days inside a fish, three days inside the earth. Told his family is waiting outside, he redefines the word family without going out.
MAT 13:1-30	From a boat, he starts telling stories about dirt. Seed lands on the path, on rock, in thorns, and on soil that actually works. Asked why the riddles, he says the crowd hears without hearing, then decodes it anyway: snatched, shallow, choked, or fruitful. Story two is a sabotage case, and the owner will not pull the weeds in case the wheat comes too. Next time on Matthew: the sorting explained, plus an expensive pearl.
MAT 13:31-58	The comparisons keep getting smaller: a mustard seed that ends up a tree full of birds, yeast worked through three measures of flour. Indoors the disciples ask about the weeds and get an unusually literal key: sower, field, wheat, harvest, angels, furnace. Two more follow about liquidating everything for one item, then a net that catches everything. At home the verdict is that they know his mother and brothers.
MAT 14:1-36	Herod hears the reports and concludes, uneasily, that John is back. A birthday dance and an oath sworn in front of guests had ended with the head on a platter. Jesus leaves by boat and is followed on foot anyway. Told to send the crowd home for dinner, the disciples are told to feed them: five loaves, twelve baskets spare. Peter gets a few steps across the lake before noticing the wind.
MAT 15:1-39	A delegation arrives from Jerusalem about handwashing and gets a lecture on loopholes: declare your money devoted to God and you need never feed your parents again. The mouth, he tells the crowd, is an exit and not an entrance. A Canaanite woman asks for her daughter and gets silence, a jurisdictional excuse and a line about dogs, then out-argues all three with the crumbs. Four thousand are fed from seven loaves.
MAT 16:1-28	Asked for a sign from heaven, he notes they can forecast rain from a red sky but cannot read the moment they are standing in. He warns about the yeast of the Pharisees, and the disciples conclude they are in trouble for forgetting lunch. At Caesarea Philippi he asks the survey question, then the real one. Peter gets it right, is renamed the rock, then corrects his teacher about Jerusalem and is called Satan to his face.
MAT 17:1-27	On a high mountain his face turns to sunlight and his clothes to white, and Moses and Elijah turn up for a conversation. Peter, present at the most extraordinary moment of his life, proposes construction. A cloud settles the matter with a voice. On the way down: Elijah already came and was badly treated. At the bottom, a boy the disciples could not help, cured in one rebuke, and the temple tax arrives in the mouth of a fish.
MAT 18:1-35	The disciples ask which of them is greatest, which is its own answer, and get a child stood in the middle of the room. Trip up one of these little ones and the sea with a millstone is preferable. A shepherd leaves ninety-nine for one stray. Grievances go privately, then with witnesses, then to the assembly. Peter negotiates forgiveness down to seven, is quoted seventy times seven, and gets the man let off ten thousand talents who throttles a colleague over a hundred denarii.
MAT 19:1-30	The test question is whether a man can divorce his wife for any reason at all, and he answers from Genesis rather than the statute: Moses permitted it because hearts are hard. The disciples conclude marriage sounds inadvisable, which is not the lesson. Children are brought over, shooed off, and the shooing is overruled. A man with a clean record asks what he still lacks, is told to sell everything, and declines, being rich. Then camels, needles, and twelve thrones.
MAT 20:1-34	A vineyard owner hires all day and pays everyone a denarius, paying the latecomers first where the early crew can watch. They complain and are told generosity is not theft. On the road up to Jerusalem he spells out the ending: handed over, mocked, flogged, crucified, raised. James and John's mother requests the two best seats. The other ten seethe, and he explains that greatness here is a serving job. Then two blind men in Jericho shout over a crowd, and he stops for them.
MAT 21:1-23	The entry is staged and Matthew says so: a donkey and colt fetched on request, cloaks and branches on the road, Hosanna in the streets. Then the temple, where he clears the market, calling it a house of prayer. He heals in the wreckage. The chief priests complain about children shouting. He finds a fig tree with no figs and ends it, then talks about mountains and prayer. Next time on Matthew: he asks them a question instead.
MAT 21:24-46	He offers a trade: tell me where John's baptism came from and I will tell you where my authority comes from. They retreat to not knowing, and he keeps his answer too. Then two sons, one who says no and goes, one who says yes and does not. Then tenants who kill the servants sent for the rent, then murder the heir figuring the estate passes to them. The priests pass sentence on themselves, and only the crowd keeps them from arresting him.
MAT 22:1-46	A king throws a wedding, the invited guests are too busy, then violent, so the guest list is replaced from the roads. Then three attempts to trap him. The tax question comes wrapped in flattery, and he asks whose face is on the coin. The Sadducees arrive with a hypothetical about a woman and seven brothers, and are told they have misread the point. A lawyer asks for the greatest commandment and gets two. Then he asks his own about David calling his own son Lord.
MAT 23:1-39	The advice is to follow the teaching and ignore the teachers, who write heavy rules and carry none, and enjoy the good seats and being greeted as Rabbi. His own people are to collect no titles. Then the woes: shutting the door, tithing garden herbs while skipping justice and mercy, filtering gnats and swallowing camels, polishing the outside of the cup, tombs packed with bones, monuments to prophets their fathers killed. It ends not in fury but in grief: a hen, chicks that would not come.
MAT 24:1-27	The disciples point out the temple architecture and are told none of it will be standing. On the Mount of Olives they ask for a date and get, first, a warning about impostors. Wars, famines, plagues and earthquakes are dismissed as early labor pains. What follows for them is arrest, execution, universal hatred, love going cold, with the good news reaching every nation first. When Daniel's abomination stands where it should not, leave Judea. Next time on Matthew: what the real thing looks like.
MAT 24:28-51	The real thing needs no announcement: vultures find a carcass, lightning crosses the sky. Sun and moon go dark, stars fall, the Son of Man arrives, angels gathering the chosen. A fig tree's leaves tell the season, not the date: not even the angels know that. Noah is the model for ordinary life continuing right up to the flood. A burglar keeps no appointment, so stay awake, like the servant caught feeding the household rather than beating it.
MAT 25:1-25	Ten bridesmaids wait on a bridegroom who takes his time, and the only difference between the five who make it and the five who don't is a spare flask of oil. Everybody sleeps. At midnight the door shuts on the unprepared five while they're out shopping. Watch, he says. Then the accounts: three servants get five, two and one talents, and the first two double theirs to identical praise. The third buries his, blaming a hard master. Next time on Matthew: how that lands.
MAT 25:26-46	It lands badly: the money should at least have earned bank interest, the master says. His talent goes to the one already holding ten. Then the courtroom scene: the Son of Man in glory, sorting every nation the way a shepherd separates sheep from goats. The criteria are entirely unspiritual: food, water, welcome, clothes, a sick visit, a prison visit. Both groups are baffled and get the same answer from opposite ends: the least of these was him.
MAT 26:1-39	Two days from Passover, Jesus states plainly what is coming, and the priests plot to take him quietly. At Bethany a woman empties a jar of ointment on his head; the disciples price it against charity, and he calls it burial preparation. Judas names his own figure, thirty pieces of silver. At the table he names his betrayer and gives bread and wine that reframe both, then tells Peter how many hours he has left. In Gethsemane he asks for the cup to pass. Next time on Matthew: the arrest.
MAT 26:40-75	He prays three times and finds his closest friends asleep each time. Judas arrives with armed men and marks him with a kiss. A sword comes out, an ear comes off, and Jesus stops it: twelve legions of angels are available and not called. Every disciple runs. The trial finds no testimony until two witnesses cite a line about the temple. Under oath, his answer makes the high priest tear his own clothes. They spit on him and strike him. Peter denies him three times in the courtyard and weeps.
MAT 27:1-32	Morning brings the transfer to Pilate. Judas tries to give the money back, is told that's his affair, throws it into the sanctuary and hangs himself; the priests spend it on a burial field for strangers. Before Pilate, Jesus answers once, then stops. Pilate offers a swap and the coached crowd picks Barabbas. His wife sends a warning from a dream. He washes his hands, which changes nothing. Soldiers rob and crown him, then pull a bystander from Cyrene to carry the cross. Next time on Matthew: Golgotha.
MAT 27:33-66	He refuses the drugged wine and is crucified between two robbers, clothes gambled for. Onlookers say: come down and we will believe. Three hours of darkness end in a cry asking why he has been forsaken, misheard as a call for Elijah, then he dies. The temple curtain tears top to bottom, the ground shakes, tombs open. The centurion reaches a conclusion nobody at the trial did. The women watch from a distance. Joseph of Arimathaea gives up his own tomb; the priests seal it.
MAT 28:1-20	Two women arrive at first light to an earthquake, an angel who rolls the stone aside and sits on it, and guards stiff as corpses. The message is short: not here, risen, go tell them Galilee. They leave frightened and delighted, and Jesus meets them himself. The guards report to the priests, who buy a story admitting they slept on duty while the body vanished. The eleven worship him in Galilee, and some doubt. The last words: a commission to every nation and a promise to stay to the end.
PRO 1:1-33	The book states its purpose up front like a syllabus: wisdom, justice, prudence for the simple, plus the ability to handle a riddle. Fear of Yahweh is the entry requirement. Then a father's first practical lesson, on peer pressure and startlingly bloody: if the boys invite you to pool the proceeds in one purse, decline, because the ambush is their own.
PRO 2:1-22	A single long conditional sentence, built so the payoff matters more than the clauses. Dig for wisdom the way people dig for silver, and the fear of Yahweh becomes something you understand rather than recite. After that, discretion works as a bodyguard against two hazards: men who genuinely enjoy doing evil, and a house with a downhill exit and no return traffic.
PRO 3:1-35	The chapter everyone can quote half of. Trust Yahweh with all your heart, decline to lean on your own understanding, and the paths get straightened out, which sounds simple until you try it. Discipline is reframed as affection, a thought that lands differently depending on the father. Then the practical part: pay the debt today rather than telling your neighbour to come back tomorrow.
PRO 4:1-27	The father cites his sources, which is a nice touch: this is what his own father told him. The advice is to acquire wisdom at any cost, up to and including everything you own. On bad company he is uncompromising: do not walk carefully on that road, go around, because those men cannot sleep until they have made somebody fall.
PRO 5:1-23	The adultery chapter, and it works by aftertaste. Her lips drip honey and her mouth is smoother than oil, and the review of the experience is wormwood and a two-edged sword. There is even a scripted deathbed regret provided in advance: how I hated instruction, how I ignored every teacher. The counter-proposal is all cisterns and springs that should stay on your own property.
PRO 6:1-35	A chapter of miscellaneous emergencies. Co-signing a stranger's loan is treated as a hostage situation: do not sleep until you are out of it. The sluggard is sent on a field trip to the ant, who runs no management structure whatsoever and still has the summer stores done, while his own ruin arrives dressed as an armed robber after a little folding of the hands.
PRO 7:1-27	The father drops the abstractions and tells a story, from the window, through the lattice, a detail he did not have to include. A young man with no understanding takes the route past a particular corner at dusk. The woman who meets him has the best speech in the chapter: my husband is out of town with a bag of money until the full moon.
PRO 8:1-36	Wisdom takes the podium herself and makes a considerable case. Nothing crooked in her mouth, better value than silver and rubies, and a genuine boast that kings reign by her. Then the section theologians never stopped arguing about: she was there before the depths, before the mountains were set down, before there was dust, the craftsman at his side.
PRO 9:1-18	Two dinner invitations, printed in the same font on purpose. Wisdom builds the house, carves seven pillars, mixes the wine and sends the staff to shout for the simple. Folly sits in her doorway in the same high places and uses word for word the same call, her only menu item being that stolen water is sweet. Her guests are already among the departed.
PRO 10:1-32	The one-liners begin, and the format is relentlessly binary: righteous on the left, wicked on the right, no third option. The recurring interests are work and mouths. Sleeping through harvest is presented as a character flaw rather than a scheduling issue. Best line: the sluggard is vinegar on the teeth and smoke in the eyes of whoever sent him.
PRO 11:1-31	It opens, pointedly, with weights and measures, because nothing reveals a person's theology faster than a rigged scale. Gossip is defined precisely, as betraying a confidence, which is more useful than calling it a sin. The generosity sayings run on arithmetic that should not work: scatter and you increase, hoard the grain and the neighbourhood curses you by name.
PRO 12:1-28	The collection settles into its rhythm and starts calling names, since hating reproof is not unfortunate here, it is stupid. There is a quiet, genuinely humane line about the righteous man looking after his animal. The lazy hunter is caught out not by failing to hunt but by not bothering to cook what he caught.
PRO 13:1-25	Mouth, money, and the rod. Guard your mouth and you guard your life; open your lips wide and you are finished. There is a sharp pair about pretending, one man performing wealth on nothing and another performing poverty on a fortune. The famous one lands here: hope deferred makes the heart sick, and fulfilled longing is a tree of life.
PRO 14:1-35	This chapter keeps admitting that the system is not tidy. The heart knows its own bitterness and refuses to hand it to a stranger. Laughter can be covering sorrow. There is a way that looks entirely correct and ends in death, which undercuts every confident proverb around it. With no oxen the stall stays clean, and also nothing gets harvested.
PRO 15:1-33	A chapter about tone of voice, sustained for thirty-three verses. The gentle answer turning away wrath opens it, and a word at the right time is described as a pleasure rather than a duty. Then the comparison that carries the whole book's economics: better a dinner of herbs where there is love than a fattened calf served with hatred.
PRO 16:1-33	A chapter about the gap between intending and happening. You plan the route, Yahweh moves your feet. Everyone's ways look clean to the person walking them, which is why somebody else weighs the motives. Then the greatest hits, practically back to back: pride before destruction, pleasant words as a honeycomb. The best of them is the quietest: ruling your own temper beats taking a city.
PRO 17:1-28	It opens on the most persuasive argument in the book, that a dry crust eaten in peace beats a banquet with a fight going on. There is an admirably concrete rule about conflict: starting a quarrel is breaching a dam, so shut it before the water is moving. Then the strong preference for meeting a bear robbed of her cubs over meeting a fool mid-folly. And the last verse is generous to fools: keep your mouth shut and people will assume you are wise.
PRO 18:1-24	Mostly a chapter about talking, and it is unusually sharp about how arguments actually work. A fool takes no pleasure in understanding, only in expressing his opinion, a sentence that has aged extremely well. And there is a real insight into procedure: whoever speaks first sounds convincing right up until somebody cross-examines him. The rich man's wall is unscalable in his own imagination, a phrase doing considerable damage in six words.
PRO 19:1-29	Poverty is the running subject and the treatment is unsentimental to the point of bleak. Wealth collects friends; the poor man loses his relatives too. The counterweight is one line: pity the poor and you have lent to Yahweh, who settles his accounts. The observation about the hot-tempered man is unnervingly practical, since rescuing him from the consequences guarantees you will be doing it again. And then the sluggard, whose hand is in the dish and cannot see the point of the return trip.
PRO 20:1-30	Wine is a mocker and beer is a brawler, which is the whole hangover in six words. The genuinely interesting thread is about how little a person knows himself: who can claim a pure heart, and the spirit of man described as Yahweh's lamp going through the drawers. There is one straight piece of comedy, the buyer who says it is no good, it is no good, and then boasts about the deal once he is safely round the corner.
PRO 21:1-31	The chapter opens by describing a king's heart as plumbing, a watercourse turned wherever Yahweh wants it. Doing justice is rated above sacrifice, a bolder claim than it looks in a book this pious. There is a good deal of housing advice: the corner of a roof, or failing that actual desert, both beat sharing a house with a contentious woman. The closing verse is the whole book in miniature: prepare the horse anyway, but the outcome is not yours.
PRO 22:1-29	A good name is worth more than money, and rich and poor share a manufacturer, the kind of levelling this book does without fuss. There is the flat economic observation that the borrower is the lender's servant, which needs no theology. The sluggard reappears with the finest excuse in scripture, a lion, outside, in the street. Then the format changes and the collection announces itself as thirty sayings, the best of which warns that Yahweh will take the poor man's court case personally.
PRO 23:1-35	Dining etiquette for the ambitious, and it is bleak. If your host is stingy, the meal he presses on you is food you will vomit, along with your compliments. Do not exhaust yourself getting rich, because money grows eagle wings and leaves. And finally the wine passage, the best writing about drinking anywhere: who has woe, sorrow, bruises he cannot account for, red eyes. It closes on the man who cannot feel the beating he took and is already planning the next round.
PRO 24:1-34	A house built by wisdom, rooms furnished by knowledge, wars won by having enough advisers, all reasonable. Then the chapter does something the rest of the book mostly avoids and demands action: rescue the people being led away to death, and do not try the line about not having known, because the one who weighs hearts has already considered it. And the closing tour of the sluggard's vineyard, thorns, nettles, collapsed wall, is a whole sermon delivered as landscape.
PRO 25:1-28	A second collection, credited to Hezekiah's copyists, and the images sharpen considerably. It begins at court, with the useful advice not to seat yourself high, since being invited up beats being moved down where the prince can watch. Then the metaphors arrive in a run: a word fitly spoken as apples of gold in silver settings, a boaster as cloud and wind that produce no rain, and singing cheerfully at a grieving person as removing their coat in cold weather.
PRO 26:1-28	Twenty-eight verses on fools, and the editor has stopped being polite. Honour on a fool sits like snow in summer. Two verses in a row give flatly opposite instructions about answering him, which is either a scribal joke or an admission that neither option is good. Best of the images: the fool going back to his folly is a dog returning to its vomit, a comparison nobody has improved on. The sluggard, meanwhile, turns on his bed like a door on hinges.
PRO 27:1-27	A chapter of unusually good sentences. Do not boast about tomorrow, and let someone else do the praising. Open rebuke beats hidden love, and the wounds of a friend are called faithful, the kindest thing this book says about criticism. A near neighbour beats a distant brother on the day things go wrong. Iron sharpens iron. Sheol, Abaddon and the human eye are the three things never satisfied. Grind a fool in a mortar and you still have a fool.
PRO 28:1-28	It opens with the wicked running from nobody at all, the most psychologically astute image in the collection. From there it is largely about money and how it distorts the people holding it. There is a rare pastoral note: hiding your sins does not work, but confessing and dropping them finds mercy. And a political line that has not dated at all: a wicked ruler over helpless people is a roaring lion and a charging bear.
PRO 29:1-27	Effectively a chapter on governance. The people rejoice or groan depending entirely on who is in charge; a king who judges the poor fairly keeps his throne, one who takes bribes dismantles the country. Elsewhere the observations are personal and sharp: flattery is a net laid for your feet, and a fool empties out all his anger while a wise man holds it in. The line about no revelation and people casting off restraint is famous. The line about hasty speech being worse than folly deserves to be.
PRO 30:1-33	Agur son of Jakeh arrives, the most interesting voice in the book, opening by declaring himself the most ignorant man present and asking who has gathered the wind in his fists. His prayer is one of the few genuinely modest requests in scripture: no lies, and neither poverty nor riches, because full men deny God and hungry men steal. Then he turns to lists: things that never say enough, things too wonderful to grasp, small creatures wise anyway, including the lizard that has somehow got himself into the palace.
PRO 31:1-31	King Lemuel's mother gets the last word, and she is direct. Not women, not wine, since a drinking king forgets the law. Open your mouth for people who have none. The closing poem on the worthy woman reads as domestic and is mostly commercial: she evaluates a field and buys it, plants a vineyard, trades linen with the merchants, while her husband is well thought of at the gate. Charm is deceptive and beauty is vapour; the request at the end is that she be paid and credited in public.
SIR 1:1-30	Sirach opens the way a scribe opens a career, by establishing that the material is not his: all wisdom comes from the Lord. The unanswerable questions come first, since nobody counts the sand or the raindrops. Wisdom predates everything and was poured out over God's works, an oddly industrial image. Then the phrase this book will not let go of, the fear of the Lord, called wisdom's beginning, its fullness, its crown and its root inside twenty verses.
SIR 2:1-18	Advice for anyone who has just decided to serve God, and the first item is that it will go badly: prepare your soul for temptation. Gold is tried in fire, acceptable men in the furnace of humiliation, which is honest and no comfort at all. He then challenges the reader to name one person from earlier generations who trusted and was shamed. The woes that follow are aimed at hesitation rather than wickedness: fearful hearts, faint hands, and anyone who has run out of patience.
SIR 3:1-31	A chapter on parents that is unusually practical about what honour actually costs. The famous part is a father's decline: help him, do not grieve him, and if his understanding fails, be patient rather than dishonouring him while you still have your strength. That is the sort of instruction that only comes from someone who watched it happen. The reward is described beautifully, sins melting like ice in fair weather. Then humility: the greater you are, the more you should humble yourself.
