SIR 4:1-31	The first half is about poverty and it is uncompromising. Do not make hungry eyes wait. Do not add trouble to a heart already provoked. Give nobody a reason to curse you, because the one who made him is listening. Then wisdom appears as an instructor of alarming methods, walking her student through crooked ways first, tormenting him with discipline until she decides she can trust him, and only afterwards coming back with the secrets.
SIR 5:1-15	A list of sentences not to say out loud, which makes it one of the more entertaining chapters here. Do not say your goods are sufficient. And do not say the one everybody has thought, that you sinned and nothing happened, since the correct reading of that silence is patience, not permission. Indecision gets an agricultural image: do not winnow with every wind, do not walk in every path. If you have no understanding, put your hand over your mouth.
SIR 6:1-37	The friendship chapter, and the most clear-eyed thing written on the subject in the ancient world. Have plenty of people at peace with you, and one counsellor in a thousand. Test a friend first, because the taxonomy is unforgiving: the friend who is in it for his own occasion, the friend who converts to enemy and takes the quarrel public, and the table companion who cannot be found when things go wrong. A faithful friend is a medicine of life.
SIR 7:1-36	A very long list of things not to do, from a man who has watched people ruin themselves in every one of these ways. Do not angle for preeminence. Do not volunteer to be a judge, in case you turn out to be afraid of a powerful face. Do not repeat your words in prayer, which is a shrewd note. The household section is brisker and less comfortable, but it does insist on paying a hired man properly.
SIR 8:1-19	A chapter entirely about picking your fights, and its conclusion is to pick almost none. Not the mighty man, not the rich man, not the man full of tongue, because heaping wood on that fire is exactly what he wanted. Do not go to law with a judge, since the verdict follows his honour rather than the evidence. The kindest lines are about ageing and dying: do not mock the old or gloat over the dead.
SIR 9:1-18	The chapter every modern reader trips over, since the advice about women is relentlessly framed as hazard management: do not gaze, do not linger, do not drink with a married woman. The reasoning at least locates the problem in the looker: beauty has led many astray. Then it pivots, and the second half is genuinely warm. Never trade an old friend for a new one, because a new friend is new wine and only worth drinking once it has aged.
SIR 10:1-31	Political theory, and the central claim is that a people takes its character from whoever is in charge: as the ruler is, so is the whole city. An uninstructed king ruins his people. Then a long and enjoyable assault on pride, tracing empires changing hands because of injustice, violence and greed. Best pair of lines in the chapter: he is a king today and tomorrow he dies, and what he inherits is creeping things and worms.
SIR 11:1-34	A chapter about not judging by the surface, and it makes the case with a bee, small among flying things and nonetheless the maker of the best thing on the table. Kings have ended up sitting on the ground and total unknowns have worn diadems. Then the parable of the saver, who scrimps for years, announces he will now enjoy his goods, and turns out not to know how much time is left. The best image here is the proud man as a decoy partridge in a cage.
SIR 12:1-18	Two subjects, generosity and enemies, and the first is uncomfortable. Help the godly and the lowly, and specifically withhold bread from the ungodly, on the practical grounds that he will use it against you. Then the enemy section, a small masterpiece of suspicion. His wickedness is rust on brass, and even if he crouches and humbles himself you will find, on wiping the mirror, that the rust never came off. He talks sweetly while surveying you for a pit,.
SIR 13:1-26	The class chapter, and it is unsparing. Keep company with the powerful and rich and you are the earthen pot next to the kettle, and only one of you survives the collision. The portrait of the patron reads like reportage: he trades on you while you are useful, humiliates you with his hospitality until he has stripped you two or three times, then laughs. A rich man who falls gets a queue of helpers; a poor man who falls gets a lecture.
SIR 14:1-27	The miser's portrait, and it is a study in self-harm rather than greed. His eye is never satisfied with his own portion, he grudges the bread on his own table, and everything he saves by depriving himself is money other people will enjoy. Nobody is worse off, the author says, than the man who envies himself. The counter-advice is unexpectedly generous: be good to yourself, be generous to your friend while you are still alive, and do not let a good day go by.
SIR 15:1-20	Wisdom is described in domestic terms, meeting the man who keeps the law like a mother, and opening his mouth in the assembly, which for a scribe is the real reward. Then the argument the chapter exists for. Do not say the Lord made you fall, because he has no use for a sinful man. God made humans and left them in the hand of their own counsel. Fire and water are set out; reach for whichever you like.
SIR 16:1-30	The opening is bleak about family: better to die childless than to raise ungodly children. Then the historical evidence, delivered as a docket: the giants who revolted in their strength, the neighbours Lot had to live among, and the six hundred thousand footmen who managed it through hardness of heart. Somebody is going to object that he cannot possibly be noticed in a creation this size, and the answer is that heaven, the deep and the earth all move when God visits.
SIR 17:1-32	A compressed anthropology. Man is made of earth and returns to it, with days issued by number, and a set of instruments listed like equipment: counsel, tongue, eyes, ears, heart. He was shown good and evil and is watched continuously, since all his works are as the sun before God. The best image is administrative and lovely: a man's almsgiving is kept like a signet. And the argument for repenting now is practical rather than pious, since the dead do not give thanks.
SIR 18:1-33	It opens with a fine statement of the limits of the whole wisdom enterprise: when a man has finished, he is only at the beginning. A hundred years at the outside, a drop from the sea, a pebble from the sand, and God's patience follows directly from noticing how short we are. Then the practical section, which contains one of the best lines in the book, that a word is better than a gift. Also, do not throw banquets on credit.
SIR 19:1-30	It opens with a hard economic fact, that a drunk workman does not get rich, and adds that despising small things fails you gradually rather than all at once. Then the discretion chapter proper. Never repeat what you were told, because he heard you and watched you and will get round to hating you at a convenient moment. A fool labours with a secret like a woman in childbirth, and it sits in him like an arrow in the thigh.
SIR 20:1-32	A chapter organised almost entirely around silence, and it distinguishes the kinds. One man is quiet because he has nothing to say, another because he knows his moment has not come, while the braggart talks straight through his. Then a run of paradoxes: profit that turns into loss, a gift that repays nothing and a gift that repays double, a bargain that costs sevenfold. The fool's generosity gets a full portrait: gives little, scolds much, asks for it back the next day, then complains he has no friends.
SIR 21:1-28	Sin gets the serpent treatment, with lion's teeth attached, and the point is proximity: it only bites if you draw near. There is a fine line about a poor man's prayer arriving at God's ears with the judgment following quickly. But most of the chapter is a portrait gallery of the fool: his insides are a broken vessel that will not hold knowledge, instruction sits on him like handcuffs while it hangs on a wise man like gold.
SIR 22:1-27	The chapter opens by comparing a lazy man first to a defiled stone and then to something off a dunghill, which suggests the author had somebody specific in mind. Teaching a fool is described as gluing a potsherd back together, or waking a man from deep sleep who then asks what it was. Then the line that gets quoted: mourn the dead for seven days and a fool for his whole life. Sand, salt and a lump of iron are easier to carry.
SIR 23:1-28	A prayer opens it, and what he asks not to be given is precise: a proud look, greed, a shameless mind. Then the discipline of the mouth, with casual swearing as the target, since a man who names God constantly is like a servant beaten constantly. The rest concerns adultery, and the best passage is the monologue he writes for the adulterer: the walls hide me, it is dark, who sees me. The answer is that God's eyes are ten thousand times brighter than the sun.
SIR 24:1-34	The centrepiece of the book, and Wisdom delivers it herself. She came out of the mouth of the Most High, covered the earth like mist, walked the circuit of heaven and the floor of the abyss, and then went looking for an address. The Creator assigned her one: Jacob, Israel, Zion. The self-description that follows is entirely botanical, cedar, cypress, palm, roses, cinnamon, myrrh, and it is the loveliest writing in Sirach. And all of this turns out to be the law of Moses.
SIR 25:1-26	It starts warmly, with three beautiful things, relatives who get along, neighbours who are friends, then three men he cannot stand: the arrogant poor man, the lying rich man, and the elderly adulterer with no sense. And then the second half arrives, the most misogynistic passage in the book: he would rather live with a lion than a wicked wife, and sin and death both started with a woman. There is no way to make that funny, and no reason to try.
SIR 26:1-29	The chapter opens generously, with a good wife doubling her husband's days, and produces two of the loveliest images in the book, her beauty ordering a house like sunrise and a face in ripe age like the lamp on the holy candlestick. In between sits the author's own list of terrors, entirely social: slander of a city, a hostile assembly, a false accusation. It ends on three things that anger him: a soldier living in poverty, and men of understanding treated as rubbish.
SIR 27:1-30	The commercial observation first, and it is memorable: sin drives itself in between buying and selling the way a nail goes between two stones. Then the assaying imagery Sirach loves, sieve and furnace, applied to conversation, so that you praise nobody before you have heard them reason. The heart of the chapter is betrayed confidences, and the tone is unusually wounded. Break a secret and the friend is a bird already out of your hand. Wounds get bound and insults patched up, but this does not.
SIR 28:1-26	An argument about grudges, framed as a question the reader cannot answer: you are nourishing wrath against a man made of the same flesh, and you were planning to ask for mercy yourself? Remember your last end and drop it. Strife burns in proportion to its fuel, and a spark can be blown up or spat out by the same mouth. Then the tongue: it has scattered nations, pulled down fortified cities, and where a whip marks the skin the tongue breaks bones.
SIR 29:1-28	On lending, and the portrait of the borrower is drawn from life: he kisses your hands until the money is in them, then the due date arrives and he discovers the times are hard, and if you ever see half of it back you should count yourself lucky. Which is why, the author notes, people have stopped lending at all. Then the dignity section: a shelter of logs of your own beats good food in a house where you dare not open your mouth.
SIR 30:1-25	The child-rearing half has not aged well and there is no point pretending otherwise, since the recommended tools are stripes, a bowed neck and no liberty, on the theory that an unbroken horse turns stubborn. Its stated aim is at least affectionate: a well taught son continues the father, so the father dies and somehow has not died. Then the chapter changes subject entirely. Health beats all the gold there is, and death is preferable to a bitter life. Put sorrow away, because worry brings old age early.
SIR 31:1-31	Money first, and the observation is medical: worrying about riches eats the flesh and takes the sleep away. His test of a wealthy man is hard to pass, since it is not whether he has money but whether he had the opportunity to sin and declined. Then the etiquette section, the most entertaining thing in Sirach. Do not reach wherever your eye goes. Stop first, for manners. On wine he is admirably balanced, calling it as good as life in measure.
SIR 32:1-24	A guide to conducting yourself at a party, and it is charming. Made master of the feast? Do not get grand about it, look after the guests and sit down last. Nobody is to talk over the music, which gets two of the book's better similes, a carbuncle in gold and an emerald in gold. The advice to the young man is the best of it: speak only if you are needed, hesitate even when asked twice, and be someone who knows and holds his tongue.
SIR 33:1-31	A chapter about distinction. Why is one day better than another when all the daylight comes from the same sun? Because God separated them, hallowing some and leaving others ordinary, and the author applies the same logic to people, who are clay in the potter's hand. The fool gets a good image, a heart like a cartwheel and thoughts like a rolling axle. Then advice for old age about not signing anything over early, since it is better that your children ask you than that you ask them.
SIR 34:1-26	The dream chapter, and the debunking is brisk: setting your mind on a dream is grabbing at a shadow and chasing wind. What he offers instead is experience, and he cites his own, including several occasions of being nearly killed while travelling. The final third turns severe and is the best of it. Bringing an offering out of the goods of the poor is like killing a son in front of his father. Withholding a labourer's wages is shedding blood.
SIR 35:1-20	The argument here is that ordinary conduct counts as liturgy: keeping the law multiplies offerings, returning a favour is fine flour, almsgiving is a thank offering, and simply stopping doing wrong works as atonement. He still wants the actual offering, cheerfully given. Then the part that matters. Do not imagine the gift buys anything, because the judge takes no bribes. The widow's tears are described as a cry against whoever caused them, which is a legal formulation, not a sentimental one.
SIR 36:1-26	The chapter opens in a completely different register, as a national prayer: send your fear on the nations, show new signs, crush the heads of the rulers who say there is none but us, gather the tribes and have compassion on Jerusalem. It is the closest Sirach comes to a psalm. Then, without transition, we are back to domestic advice. An understanding heart tastes false speech the way a mouth tastes game, which is the sharpest thing in the chapter.
SIR 37:1-31	On advice, and it is the most cynical and useful thing in the book. Everyone claims to be your friend. One enjoys your good times and disappears in the bad, another works with you strictly for the meals. Then the counsellors: every counsellor recommends taking counsel, so establish his interest first, because he may advise in his own favour and then stand next to you to watch how it turns out. Never consult a coward about war, a merchant about exchange rates or a sluggard about work.
SIR 38:1-34	It begins by defending doctors: God made the physician and made medicines out of the earth, so pray, make the offering, and then get out of the doctor's way. The mourning advice is startlingly practical, prescribing real weeping and a proper burial, then a limit of a day or two so the neighbours cannot complain. The last third is a magnificent portrait of skilled labour, the smith wrestling iron in the furnace heat and the potter turning the wheel with his feet.
SIR 39:1-35	The scribe's job description, and it is unmistakably a self-portrait: study the ancients, work through the dark sayings, serve among great men, travel abroad, get up early to pray, and be rewarded with a name that outlasts the world. The author then admits he is filled like the moon at the full, the least modest sentence here. Nobody may ask what this is or why that is, because everything has a use and a season. Winds and hail and famine are created and waiting for orders.
SIR 40:1-30	The opening is the bleakest passage in Sirach: a heavy yoke on everyone from the womb to burial in the earth, which he calls the mother of all things, and the fear of death reaching the man on the throne and the man in the hemp shirt alike. Even sleep offers nothing, since he wakes from it like a man just out of the front line. Then a game of comparisons, each good thing topped by a better. It ends coldly on begging.
SIR 41:1-24	The opening is the best thing in the chapter, because it refuses to pick a side: death is bitter to a comfortable man with an appetite and welcome to a man who is old, failing and out of patience. Both are true, and he says both. There is no inquisition of life in the grave. A good name outlasts a thousand treasures of gold. The rest is an inventory of shame, sorted by who is watching: lying to a prince, stealing from your hosts, leaning your elbow on the table.
SIR 42:1-25	The matching list of things not to be ashamed of, and it is unexpectedly commercial: the law, exact weights, settling accounts, and putting everything in writing by number and weight, the most practical verse in the book. Then a father's insomnia over a daughter, worried at every stage of her life, and another run of hostile remarks about women of the sort this book keeps producing. The second half changes register into a hymn on God's knowledge: everything is made double, each thing against its opposite, nothing left imperfect.
SIR 43:1-33	A hymn to the weather, Sirach at the top of his form. The sun dries out the country at noon, burning three times hotter than a furnace. The moon names the month; the stars stand in order and never faint on watch. A whole meteorology follows: the rainbow as a circle of glory, snow sprinkled down like birds, hail broken small, ice buckling a breastplate onto the water. Sailors report the sea and its monsters. Say as much as you like, the sum of it is that he is all.
SIR 44:1-23	The famous roll of honour begins, and its most striking verse is about the men who did not make the list, who have no memorial and are as though they had never been born. He includes not only rulers and prophets but composers and writers of verse. Then the parade starts. Enoch pleased the Lord and left. Noah was found righteous and, in a fine phrase, taken in exchange for the world. Abraham was proved faithful and got the oath about dust and stars and inheritance.
SIR 45:1-26	Moses first, beloved of God and men, who stopped the wonders with a word and took his commandments face to face. Then Aaron, who gets three times the space, and almost all of it is clothing: linen breeches, ephod, golden pomegranates and bells, the Urim and Thummim, and a gold plate on the mitre reading HOLINESS. The revolt of Dathan, Abiram and Korah is dealt with in two verses of fire. Aaron gets the firstfruits and no land, because God is the portion. Phinehas takes third place, David is mentioned.
SIR 46:1-20	Joshua gets the highlights package, with the sun going back so one day became two, and hailstones landing on the enemy at exactly the right moment. Of six hundred thousand men on foot, only two came into the land, the ones who stood up against the murmuring. Caleb keeps his strength into old age. The judges get a collective blessing. Then Samuel, whose chosen detail is not a battle but an audit: before he died he stated publicly that he had never taken so much as a shoe from anybody.
SIR 47:1-25	David gets the fat separated from the peace offering, a compliment here, plus lions handled like livestock, the sling stone and Goliath, and credit for organising the temple singers. Solomon is praised at length, the river of understanding, gold gathered like tin, and then the sentence turns: you bowed your loins to women, you blemished your honour. The kingdom divides as consequence, with Rehoboam and Jeroboam named as the men who finished it.
SIR 48:1-25	Elijah arrives as fire, pure momentum: a famine, the sky shut, fire three times, a dead man raised, kings brought down. His job description is the best line, to turn the heart of the father to the son. Elisha inherits the spirit, and his corpse prophesies after death. Then the flat verdict: none of it worked, the people did not repent, and they were carried off. Hezekiah closes the chapter cutting rock for water, delivered from Sennacherib through Isaiah.
SIR 49:1-16	Josiah gets the loveliest compliment in the book, a memory like compounded incense. The accounting on the monarchy is brutal: except for David, Hezekiah and Josiah, all the kings of Judah failed. Jeremiah, sanctified in the womb, gets a job description of four verbs, two of them destructive. Ezekiel gets the cherubim in a single line, the twelve prophets are handled as a group. Then the rebuilders, Zerubbabel and Nehemiah, reaching back past everybody to Adam.
SIR 50:1-29	The book's climax is not a battle but a liturgy. Simon son of Onias comes out of the sanctuary and the author piles up images for a page: morning star, full moon, a rainbow, roses, frankincense, a cypress in the clouds. Then the ritual: the robe, kindred standing round him like palm trees around a cedar, wine at the altar's foot, trumpets, everyone face down. After that the author signs his name, and adds a sour aside about three neighbouring peoples.
SIR 51:1-30	A closing thanksgiving, and the emergency it thanks God for is reputational, not military: a slanderous tongue, forged lies, slander carried to the king. That is the danger a court scribe faces. Then his own account of getting wisdom, warmer than anything else in the book: he asked for her at the temple as a boy, and the reward he names is a tongue. The pitch that follows: come lodge in the house of instruction, get her without money, for he worked little and found much rest.
SNG 1:1-17	The woman speaks first, which is worth noticing in a book this old, and she does not ease into it: kiss me, your love is better than wine. Her defence to the staring daughters of Jerusalem is practical, since the sun did this while she worked the vineyards her brothers assigned her. Their roof beams are trees.
SNG 2:1-17	They trade images and neither is modest about it. She is a lily; he is the one apple tree in a whole wood of ordinary timber, and she sits in its shade on purpose. Being faint with love and needing raisins is either romantic or a request for a snack. Then his invitation, the best writing here: the winter is over, the figs are coming.
SNG 3:1-11	A night scene that reads like a dream and probably is one. She looks for him from her bed, then out in the city, repeating that she sought him and did not find him. The watchmen turn up and she asks them, which is either brave or dream logic. Then the poem cuts to a parade: myrrh, frankincense, Solomon's cedar carriage, sixty armed men.
SNG 4:1-16	The long compliment, and a genuinely different aesthetic. Hair like a flock of goats coming down a mountain is about movement, teeth like a freshly washed flock is about completeness, a neck like David's armoury tower is about bearing rather than size. Then the poem becomes a garden: locked, sealed, saffron, cinnamon, myrrh. Her reply is one line: north wind, blow the fragrance out.
SNG 5:1-16	The garden visit is accomplished, and then the mood turns. She is asleep with her heart awake when he knocks, hair damp with night dew. Her hesitation is entirely domestic, since she is undressed and her feet are washed, and by the time she reaches the door he is gone. The watchmen, merely inconvenient in chapter three, beat her and take her cloak.
SNG 6:1-13	The daughters volunteer to join the search, and she deflects: he is off gathering lilies. Then the thesis again, I am his and he is mine. His praise escalates unusually, since alongside Tirzah and Jerusalem she is awesome as an army with banners, and he asks her to look away because her eyes are winning. Then the arithmetic: sixty queens, eighty concubines, one dove.
SNG 7:1-13	The third and most enthusiastic inventory, and this one starts at the sandals and travels upward, the reverse of chapter four. A waist like a heap of wheat with lilies round it, and a nose like the tower of Lebanon facing Damascus, a compliment nobody would attempt now. He announces his intention to climb the palm tree. Then she takes over: fields, villages, vines.
SNG 8:1-14	She opens with a workaround for public affection, wishing he were her brother so a kiss in the street would raise no eyebrows. Then the verse the whole book has been building towards, sounding less like love poetry than a verdict: love is strong as death, jealousy cruel as the grave, its flashes a flame of Yahweh. She informs her brothers she is a wall.
WIS 1:1-16	The book opens by addressing the judges of the earth, which tells you what it thinks of its readers. Wisdom will not move into a soul that is planning something. The surveillance imagery is specific: an ear of jealousy catches every murmur. Then the line that makes the book worth reading: God did not make death. People befriended it themselves.
WIS 2:1-24	The author hands the microphone to the ungodly and lets them argue their case, the boldest move in the book, because they argue it well. Life is short, reason is a spark that goes out, so: wine, perfume, rosebuds crowned before they wilt. It is nearly persuasive until the same logic turns into torture the righteous man to see whether God turns up.
WIS 3:1-19	The rebuttal, and its central move is a change of vantage point. The souls of the righteous are in God's hand, so what looked like ruin was peace, and the suffering is reclassified as assay work: gold in the furnace. They will run about like sparks in stubble and judge nations. Then a childless woman and a eunuch are promised better than children.
WIS 4:1-20	An argument against measuring a life by its length or its descendants. Numerous ungodly offspring are shoots with no root, flourishing a season and taken out by wind. Against that, the famous formulation: understanding is grey hair, and an unspotted life is ripe old age. Then the delicate part, the good person dying young, taken out early before wickedness could work on his understanding.
WIS 5:1-23	The courtroom scene, and the author clearly enjoys writing the losing side's statement. Their regret speech is genuinely good: this is the one we made a byword, we called his life madness, the sun never rose for us. Then the images of their own vanishing: a ship leaving no track, a bird leaving nothing in the air, an arrow whose path closes behind it.
WIS 6:1-25	The lecture to management continues, and its point is one no ruler enjoys hearing: your authority was issued to you, the issuer keeps records, and the audit is proportional to the office. Mighty men will be searched out mightily. Then a courtship register: wisdom does the seeking herself, so the man who gets up early finds her already sitting at his gate.
WIS 7:1-30	The speaker, generally taken to be Solomon, opens by insisting on how ordinary he is. Ten months in a womb, first sound the same wail as everybody, because no king ever had a different beginning. Then he prayed and got wisdom, rating gold as sand in her presence. The syllabus he claims runs from solstices and star positions to the virtues of roots.
WIS 8:1-21	A chapter of open courtship, and the author is not embarrassed: he sought her from youth and wanted her as his bride. The argument then becomes a sales presentation with headings. Want riches? She works all things. Want understanding? She is the architect of everything that exists. The last move is the honest one: none of it is available unless God hands her over.
WIS 9:1-18	The prayer, and it is the most modest thing in the book. He asks for wisdom, the one seated beside God's own throne, and grounds the request in his limitations rather than his office: a servant, the son of a handmaid, weak and short-lived. Then the sentence the whole tradition kept: a corruptible body weighs down the soul.
WIS 10:1-21	A tour of Genesis and Exodus in which Wisdom does everything and nobody gets named. A man who left her in anger died in the rage that killed his brother. She steered a righteous man through the flood on a poor piece of wood, a startlingly dismissive way to describe the ark. She stayed in the dungeon until the sold man had a sceptre.
WIS 11:1-26	The author starts working a single idea very hard: the same thing that punished Egypt supplied Israel. The Egyptians got clotted blood where a river should be, and the Israelites got water out of flint. Then a wonderful digression on restraint: God could have sent bears, lions or newly invented beasts breathing fire, and chose measure, number and weight instead.
WIS 12:1-27	An extended argument about why judgment is slow. The old inhabitants are described in the worst possible terms, and then the author insists God still spared them, sending hornets ahead of the army so they would have somewhere to repent. He also maintains their wickedness was inborn, a difficulty he does not resolve. Being sovereign over his own strength, God judges gently.
WIS 13:1-19	The anti-idol argument opens with genuine generosity. People who mistook fire, wind, stars and water were at least looking at something real, and their blame is small, since they were searching. Then the detail turns merciless: the woodcutter cooks his dinner over the offcuts, then carves the last knotty scrap, paints it red, and bolts it to the wall.
WIS 14:1-31	The wood argument continues. A man boarding a ship prays to a plank more rotten than the vessel under him, when a raft once carried the entire future of the species. Then a genealogy of idolatry: a grieving father honours an image of his dead child, princes make statues compulsory, and ambitious artists improve the likeness until people are worshipping a man.
WIS 15:1-19	A breather addressed to God before the author returns to the workshop. This time it is the potter: out of one batch of clay he makes vessels for clean uses and vessels for the opposite, and then makes a god. The man doing this is earth, and has to hand the borrowed soul back. What is unbearable is the professional pride.
WIS 16:1-29	The parallel gets worked past the point of subtlety. Egypt gets vermin, Israel gets quails. When Israel is bitten by serpents the cure is explicitly not medical: not herb, not plaster, but a word. Then the physics goes strange, with fire burning harder inside water. The manna is the best detail: bread that tasted like whatever the eater wanted, and melted under a weak sunbeam.
WIS 17:1-21	The plague of darkness, retold as a study in fear. The Egyptians who thought they held a holy nation captive become prisoners themselves, cut off from each other by a curtain of forgetfulness. The only illumination is a self-kindled glimmer that frightens them worse than the dark. The magicians who sold terror-removal services are themselves sick with fear.
WIS 18:1-25	Light for one side, darkness for the other. Egypt hears Israelite voices without seeing anybody and concludes those people are fortunate. The reciprocity is stated plainly: they planned to kill infants, and now their own children are gone. Then the image the chapter is famous for: peaceful silence over everything, night at mid course, and God's word leaping off the throne as an armed warrior.
WIS 19:1-22	The last chapter tidies up the argument. God knew Egypt would change its mind, and it does, chasing after the people it had begged to leave, while still standing at the graves. Creation is refashioned for the occasion: dry land coming up out of the Red Sea, and Israel crossing while skipping like lambs. Both Sodom and Egypt went blind at the door.
1KI 19:1-21	One message from Jezebel undoes the man who beat four hundred and fifty prophets, which is what exhaustion does. He walks into the desert and asks to die, and God's response is not a rebuke but food and sleep, twice. Then Horeb, where wind, earthquake and fire all fail to contain God and a thin quiet voice does. Elijah answers identically before and after: I alone am left. He is not argued out of it. He is given work and one correction, seven thousand others.
1KI 20:1-23	Ben Hadad demands everything Ahab owns, and Ahab agrees immediately, which is the most revealing thing about him. Only when the terms rise to a house-by-house search do the elders stop him. The insults are excellent: a boast about not enough dust to go round, answered by the best line Ahab ever speaks, that a man putting his armour on should not brag like one taking it off. A prophet promises victory with two hundred and thirty-two provincial young men. Next time on 1 Kings: the plain.
ACT 9:1-21	Saul is introduced still breathing threats, on his way to Damascus with paperwork authorising arrests. Then the light, the fall, and a question rather than an accusation: why are you persecuting me. His escort hears the sound and sees nothing. The best part is Ananias, who receives a clear divine instruction and argues with it at length, citing the man's arrest warrants, and is told to go anyway. He opens with the word brother. Next time on Acts: he leaves the city over the wall, in a basket.
ACT 9:22-43	The first consequence of the conversion is that Saul has to be smuggled out of a city in a basket, which sets the tone for the rest of his career. In Jerusalem nobody believes he is a disciple, reasonably, and Barnabas has to vouch for him. Then he argues with the Hellenists until he has to be shipped to Tarsus for his own safety. Then two Peter episodes: a man bedridden eight years, and the widows of Joppa with the clothes Tabitha made.
ACT 10:1-25	Two visions arranged to meet in the middle. Cornelius, a Roman officer with a good reputation, gets an angel, a name and an address. Peter, hungry on a roof, gets a sheet full of everything he has spent his life not eating and an instruction to help himself. He refuses, and Luke lets the argument happen three times before the sheet goes back up. The knock at the gate lands while he is still working out what it meant. Next time on Acts: the sermon.
ACT 10:26-48	Cornelius greets him by falling at his feet, and is told to get up. Peter's opening line acknowledges out loud that he is not supposed to be in the house. His summary of the whole story runs about six sentences and notes that the risen Jesus was shown to chosen witnesses rather than to everybody. He does not get to the end of it. The Spirit arrives mid-sermon, and Peter's question about refusing water to people who already have the Spirit is not really a question.
JER 51:1-31	Babylon's oracle is enormous, and the best line is medical sarcasm: fetch balm for her pain, perhaps she may be healed, immediately followed by we would have healed her and she is not healed. She was a golden cup that made the whole earth drunk. Mid-chapter a hymn about the God who brings wind out of storehouses turns up unaltered from chapter ten. Babylon gets called God's battle axe, used to break horses, chariots and farmers alike, before being handed the bill for all of it.
JER 51:32-64	Zion is finally given a voice: he swallowed me like a monster, filled his mouth with my delicacies, and threw the empty dish away. God's reply promises litigation, that he will plead her cause, and dry the fountain. The image for Babylon's end is a banquet: set the feast, get them drunk, let them sleep permanently. The last act is theatrical: Jeremiah writes it out, hands it to a quartermaster heading to Babylon, with instructions to read it aloud and drop it in the Euphrates.
JER 52:1-17	An appendix, written flat, the right choice. Dates do the work: tenth month, forts built, the siege running into the eleventh year, and by the fourth month, no bread at all. Then the escape at night through the garden gate, the capture on the Jericho plain, and the sentence executed in order so the sons die while their father can still see. Temple and palace burn. The last people mentioned are the poorest, left to keep the vineyards. Next time on Jeremiah: the inventory is taken.
JER 52:18-34	The final chapter of a book of poetry closes with a packing list, and that is the point. Pots, shovels, snuffers, basins, spoons, gold and silver counted separately, Solomon's pillars measured to the finger, bronze past weighing. Then the executions at Riblah, named by office. Then the deportation arithmetic, three totals adding to four thousand six hundred, small for the end of a kingdom. And the strangest ending in the prophets: a king let out after thirty seven years, given clean clothes and dinner every day for life.
1KI 20:24-43	Syria's theological fix is administrative: swap the kings for professional captains and fight downhill. Israel camps opposite like two little flocks of goats, the narrator precise rather than modest. A man of God announces the hills-only slander will be answered in a valley. The numbers are enormous, and the wall of Aphek adds twenty-seven thousand. Then Ahab, handed a victory on a point of doctrine, meets Ben Hadad's men in sackcloth and calls the man his brother. A prophet in a bandage traps him into sentencing himself.
1KI 21:1-29	Ahab offers a fair price for a vegetable garden. Naboth says no, the land is not his to sell, and Ahab goes to bed and refuses dinner. Jezebel points out that he is the king, then does it properly: letters in his name, a fast, Naboth in the place of honour, two hired liars, a stoning. The forms are all observed, which is the horror of it. Elijah waits in the vineyard with a matching sentence: dogs for dogs. Ahab puts on sackcloth, and the verdict is deferred a generation.
1KI 22:1-26	Three quiet years, then Ahab wants a border town back, and Jehoshaphat agrees first, asks about God second. Four hundred prophets say yes, one with iron horns as a visual aid. Jehoshaphat asks whether there is a real prophet anywhere, and gets Ahab's most honest answer ever: there is one, and I hate him, he never tells me what I want. Micaiah's first answer is a flawless parody, until the king demands the truth and regrets it. Next time on 1 Kings: an arrow with nobody's name on it.
1KI 22:27-53	Micaiah's parting line, that if the king comes back safely God never spoke through him, stakes the prophetic office on one battle. Ahab's counter-plan is a disguise, with Jehoshaphat left conspicuously in royal robes, an arrangement he agrees to for reasons the text does not explore. Syria's orders are to ignore everyone but Israel's king, so the decoy nearly works. Then an anonymous archer shoots and finds the one gap in the armour. Ahab is propped upright so the line holds, and bleeds into the chariot until sunset.
2KI 1:1-18	Ahab's son injures himself falling through his own window lattice, and his first instinct is to send abroad for a medical opinion from Baal Zebub of Ekron. Elijah meets the messengers with the obvious question, whether Israel has run out of gods, and a diagnosis: you are not getting off that bed. Two captains and their fifties are burned off the hillside for delivering the summons in the wrong tone. A third arrives on his knees and is spared for it.
2KI 2:1-25	The last day is structured as three refusals. Three times Elijah tells Elisha to stay behind, three times Elisha answers with the same oath, and at every stop a delegation of prophets helpfully announces that his master is being taken today. The Jordan opens for a rolled-up coat. Elisha's request, a double portion, is the inheritance share of a firstborn son, and the condition is simply that he keep watching. The coat comes back down, the river opens a second time, and the succession is settled.
2KI 3:1-27	Jehoram is graded on a curve: evil, but not like his parents, since he did at least take down the Baal pillar. Moab's tribute is quantified in livestock, which explains why losing it matters. The coalition marches seven days through desert and runs out of water, at which point Jehoram concludes God has arranged this personally. Elisha makes clear he is only present out of respect for Judah. The trick that wins the battle is optical: dawn light on new water, read as blood.
2KI 4:1-24	Two women and two problems. The first is a debt about to cost a widow her sons, solved by an inventory question, what do you have in the house, and one pot of oil that pours exactly as long as there are borrowed jars to fill. The miracle stops when the containers do. The second is the Shunammite, who feeds Elisha out of courtesy and builds him a room. Gehazi notices the thing she has not asked for. Next time on 2 Kings: it is well.
2KI 4:25-44	She tells Gehazi everything is well, which is not a lie so much as a refusal to discuss it with the assistant. Then she grabs the prophet's feet and asks the only question that matters: did I ask you for this. Elisha's first move, sending his staff ahead, fails completely, and the text lets that stand. What works is unglamorous and physical, a shut door, prayer, and a man lying full length on a child until warmth comes back.
2KI 5:1-14	The most important person here is a kidnapped girl with no name, waiting on the wife of the man whose army took her, who mentions in passing that there is a prophet who could help. The remark travels up two royal courts and arrives as a diplomatic incident, complete with a king of Israel tearing his clothes because he thinks he is being set up. Elisha then declines to come outside at all, delegating a one-line instruction to a messenger. Next time on 2 Kings: the invoice.
2KI 5:15-27	Naaman comes back a convert and negotiates the terms of it with unusual honesty: he will worship Yahweh only, he would like two mules' worth of Israelite soil to do it on, and he asks in advance to be excused for bowing in the temple of Rimmon. Elisha gives him the shortest possible answer and takes nothing. Gehazi, who has watched a fortune walk away, invents two hungry young prophets. The disease Naaman lost is the disease Gehazi acquires, which is the tidiest arithmetic in the book.
2KI 6:1-18	A building project, a borrowed axe head in the river, and a miracle scaled to the fact that the man who lost it did not own it. Then the register changes without warning. Syria cannot hold a war council without Israel knowing the outcome, and the king's first theory is a mole, until a servant explains that the leak is a prophet who hears what is said in the bedroom. Next time on 2 Kings: a guided tour.
2KI 6:19-33	Elisha walks a blinded army to the wrong city on purpose, restores their sight in the middle of Samaria, and answers the king's eager question about killing them with an order for dinner. It works: the raids stop. Then the siege, and the tone drops away entirely. The famine is priced out first, a donkey's head at eighty shekels, then given a voice, when a woman appeals to the king in a dispute between two mothers who agreed to eat their children. Hers was eaten.
2KI 7:1-20	Elisha's forecast is a commodity price, flour and barley at a shekel by tomorrow, which after eighty-shekel donkey heads is the least believable thing he has ever said. The officer says as much and receives a precise sentence: you will see it, you will not eat it. The relief itself is achieved by four men with leprosy, sitting at the gate because they are not allowed inside, reasoning that surrender is the only move with any upside.
2KI 8:1-29	The Shunammite gets a property case, and the timing is absurd in the best way: she walks in to petition the king at the exact moment Gehazi is telling him the story of her son. The land comes back with seven years of back rent. Then the register darkens. Ben Hadad sends Hazael to ask a prophet for a prognosis, and Elisha answers in two halves: tell him he lives, he dies. The wet cloth arrives the next morning.
2KI 9:1-18	The anointing is run like a hit: one young prophet, one vial of oil, a private room, and explicit instructions to open the door and run. He delivers a sentence on a whole dynasty and then does exactly that. The officers outside ask what the lunatic wanted, in the tone soldiers use about prophets. Cloaks go down on the bare stairs, a trumpet sounds, and the coup exists. Jehu's first act as king is a news blackout. Next time on 2 Kings: the second rider.
2KI 9:19-37	The watchman's second report is the best line in the chapter: he recognises the man by his driving. Joram, wounded, rides out to meet a coup in person and asks whether it is peace, twice, and the answer names his mother. The place where he is shot and dumped is Naboth's field, which Jehu makes sure his captain notices. Then Jezebel does her eyes and her hair before appearing at the window, and is thrown out of it by her own household. Jehu goes inside and eats.
2KI 10:1-17	Jehu's letter to Samaria is a dare he knows will be refused: you have chariots, walls and seventy candidates, so pick one and fight me. The guardians do the arithmetic on two dead kings and surrender in writing. The second letter takes them at their word, and seventy heads arrive in baskets. Stacking them at the gate is theatre, and so is the speech, where Jehu concedes his own conspiracy and then asks who did this. Next time on 2 Kings: the great sacrifice.
2KI 10:18-36	The sting is elegant and entirely without conscience. Ahab served Baal a little, Jehu will serve him much, so bring every priest in Israel, attendance compulsory. He hands out ceremonial robes so the congregation is easy to identify, and sweeps the building for worshippers of Yahweh. The offering finishes, and so does the congregation. The temple becomes a latrine. Then the verdict: Baal gone, the calves at Bethel and Dan untouched, four generations promised, no obedience beyond the killing.
2KI 11:1-21	The queen mother's response to her son's death is to eliminate every possible heir, which very nearly works. What defeats her is an aunt, a nurse and a bedroom, then six years of a child living in the temple while she rules a country that does not know he exists. Jehoiada's coup is staged with care: shift rotations, spears out of David's own armoury, a ring of men nobody may breach. Athaliah walks into a completed fact and shouts treason at it, correctly and far too late.
2KI 12:1-21	A king who does right for exactly as long as his priest is telling him what right is. The interesting part is the accounting. Joash tells the priests to fund temple repairs out of the offerings, and twenty-three years later nothing has been repaired, so the money is taken off them. Jehoiada's fix is a chest with a hole drilled in the lid, counted by the scribe and the high priest. Then Hazael turns toward Jerusalem and the restored treasury leaves as a bribe.
2KI 13:1-25	Two kings of Israel in one chapter and the same verdict on both, which by now is the point. Syria squeezes Jehoahaz until his army is fifty horsemen, ten chariots and ten thousand men on foot. He prays, God sends relief, nothing changes. Then Elisha's deathbed, where the arrow scene is a test disguised as a ritual: shoot east, that is victory, now strike the ground. He hits it three times, which is not wrong, only unenthusiastic, and the prophet is furious because the number was the answer.
2KI 14:1-29	Amaziah does one genuinely notable thing, which is to execute his father's murderers and leave their children alone, on the explicit authority of a text. Then he beats Edom and immediately writes to Israel proposing they look each other in the face. Jehoash answers with a fable: a thistle in Lebanon asks a cedar for its daughter and is trodden flat by a passing animal. Amaziah goes anyway, and the result is a captured king, four hundred cubits of wall pulled down, and the treasury moved to Samaria.
2KI 15:1-19	Judah gets one king in this stretch and Israel gets four, which tells you where the rot is. Azariah reigns fifty-two years and spends part of them in a separate house with leprosy while his son runs the country. Then Israel, at speed. Zechariah lasts six months and is killed in front of everyone, using up Jehu's four generations exactly. Shallum lasts a month. Menahem takes Samaria. Next time on 2 Kings: who pays for it.
2KI 15:20-38	The answer is fifty shekels a head from every rich man in Israel, and Assyria goes home, for now. After that the chapter is a list of assassinations with a formula attached to each. Pekahiah, two years, killed by his own captain in the palace fortress. Pekah, twenty years, during which Tiglath Pileser simply takes the north and deports the population. Then Hoshea kills Pekah and will be the last. Judah, in parallel, has Jotham, who is fine.
2KI 16:1-20	Ahaz is the first king of Judah the formula refuses to grade at all, and the detail it gives is his son in the fire. Besieged by Syria and Israel, his solution is to buy an empire: temple silver, palace gold, and a letter to Tiglath Pileser signed your servant and your son. It works, in the sense that Damascus falls. Then Ahaz visits, likes the altar there, and sends the plans home by courier for Urijah to build.
ACT 11:1-30	Peter gets home and is met not with congratulations but with a complaint about his dining arrangements. His defence is simply to tell the whole story again in order, sheet and all, which Luke reproduces at length rather than summarising. The closing line, who was I that I could withstand God, ends the discussion. Then the scene shifts to Antioch, where the same thing is happening without anyone asking permission, and Barnabas is sent, who fetches Saul out of Tarsus. The nickname Christian is coined here.
ACT 12:1-25	James is killed in half a verse and Peter arrested in the next, because it polled well. Herod assigns sixteen soldiers to one fisherman, who spends the night before his execution asleep. The rescue is domestic: get dressed, sandals, take your coat. Peter assumes he is dreaming until the angel leaves him alone in a street. The prayer meeting held for his release then refuses to believe he is at the door and settles on his ghost, while Peter keeps knocking. Herod dies at Caesarea.
ACT 13:1-25	The list of teachers at Antioch includes Herod's foster brother. Barnabas and Saul are commissioned mid-fast and sail for Cyprus, where a magician with the working name Elymas tries to keep a Roman governor away from the faith. Saul, who becomes Paul in the same sentence, blinds him, and the governor believes. John Mark quits at Perga, a detail that will cost people a friendship later. Then the first set-piece sermon: Egypt, wilderness, judges, Saul, David. Next time on Acts: he reaches the point.
ACT 13:26-52	The second half turns on a piece of close reading. David was promised his Holy One would not see decay; David demonstrably did decay; therefore the psalm was talking about someone else. It is an argument built entirely out of a text his audience already accepts, which is why it works on some of them and enrages the rest. The whole city turns up the following Sabbath, and the jealousy starts. The turn to the Gentiles is announced as a formal decision with a scripture attached.
ACT 14:1-28	Lystra is the strangest scene in the book. A man lame from birth stands up, and the crowd, speaking Lycaonian so the apostles cannot follow, concludes the gods have come down and starts leading oxen to the gate. Barnabas gets to be Jupiter; Paul, being the talker, gets Mercury. The counter-sermon makes no appeal to scripture at all, only rain and harvests, because this audience has never heard of Moses. The same crowd is talked round by visitors from Antioch and stones Paul, Luke's quietest comment on public enthusiasm.
ACT 15:1-41	The first church council convenes because visitors from Judea told Antioch that salvation has a surgical prerequisite. Luke calls the exchange no small discord, his way of saying shouting match. Peter's argument is that God settled it by handing out the Spirit without checking anyone's paperwork, and his line about an unbearable yoke is unusually candid. James supplies a prophecy and four rules, none of them the one being demanded. Then the chapter undercuts itself: having agreed the largest question in the book, they split permanently over John Mark.
ACT 16:1-18	Fresh from a council that ruled circumcision unnecessary, Paul circumcises Timothy, and Luke reports this without a flicker. Then refusals: forbidden Asia, blocked from Bithynia, herded to the coast until a Macedonian appears in a dream. Note the pronoun in verse ten turning into we. Europe begins not with a crusade but with a riverside Sabbath conversation and a businesswoman in the purple trade. Then a slave girl advertises them accurately for days, and Paul exorcises the spirit from irritation. Next time on Acts: somebody notices the revenue.
ACT 16:19-40	The charge mentions no fortune-telling, only Jews pushing un-Roman customs, a money grievance in court dress. Beaten without trial and put in the stocks, they are singing by midnight with the prison as audience. The earthquake opens every door, and the jailer's first thought is his own execution. His question, what must I do to be saved, comes from a man still holding a sword. He spends the night washing the wounds his colleagues inflicted. Offered quiet release, Paul mentions he is a Roman citizen and declines.
ACT 17:1-34	Three cities, three receptions, in ascending order of civility. Thessalonica supplies a riot and the accusation that these men say there is another king. Beroea gets Luke's only compliment, for checking claims against the text daily. Athens dismisses Paul as a babbler before inviting him to explain himself anyway. The Areopagus speech quotes no scripture, starts from an altar addressed to a nameless god, and borrows two lines of Greek poetry. It works until he mentions resurrection, and the room politely breaks up.
ACT 18:1-28	Corinth is where the pace slows: eighteen months, a day job making tents, and a landlord who moves one door when the synagogue turns hostile. The vision Paul receives is not a commission but reassurance. Gallio's ruling is the most consequential shrug in the book: an internal argument about words and names, not worth his hearing. A crowd beats Sosthenes in front of the bench, and the proconsul can't be bothered to look up. Apollos arrives eloquent but out of date, and is quietly taken aside by two tentmakers.
ACT 19:1-21	Paul opens at Ephesus with a diagnostic question and gets the flattest possible answer: we have not heard there is a Holy Spirit. Twelve men are rebaptized and the lecture moves into a rented hall belonging to Tyrannus, running daily for two years. Cloth off Paul's body heals people, and freelance exorcists decide the name of Jesus is a formula anyone can use. The spirit's reply, that it knows Jesus and Paul but not them, is the best line in the book. Next time on Acts: the silversmiths weigh in.
ACT 19:22-41	A riot organised on economic grounds and conducted in religious language. Demetrius gathers the trade, mentions profits first and the goddess second, and within a verse the city is in the theatre chanting. Most did not know why they had come. Paul wants to address the mob and is physically prevented by his friends. Alexander is put forward, recognised as a Jew, and the chanting resumes for two hours. What ends it is an official noting the courts are open and these men have stolen nothing.
ACT 20:1-38	The travelogue is thick with names and ports, and the memorable part happens because Paul will not stop talking. Eutychus falls asleep in a window during a sermon past midnight, falls out, and is picked up dead. Paul restores him, continues until dawn, and walks to the next port alone. The Miletus speech is his only recorded address to believers, a farewell said twice: they will not see his face again, and wolves will come from inside the room. He appeals to his hands as proof he took nothing.
ACT 21:1-22	The voyage south is one long attempt to talk Paul out of it. Disciples at Tyre say through the Spirit he must not go; the congregation walks him to the beach to kneel and pray. At Caesarea, Philip's four prophesying daughters are mentioned and dropped. Agabus ties himself with Paul's belt to show whose hands it will be. Paul's answer, why break his heart weeping, is the closest he comes to admitting the cost. Jerusalem raises the real problem: not doctrine, a rumour. Next time on Acts: the PR fix.
ACT 21:23-40	The proposed solution is a photo opportunity: sponsor four men's vows, be seen paying for them, and the rumour dies. It works exactly until it does not. Visitors from Asia spot him, and the charge they shout has one detail, that he brought a Greek into the temple, drawn from seeing Trophimus with him in the city. The doors are shut, the beating begins, and what saves his life is the Roman garrison arriving as the city riots. Carried up the steps, Paul asks the commander for a word.
ACT 22:1-30	The defence is his conversion told again, and the differences from chapter nine are the point: this version is aimed at the crowd holding the rocks. He opens in Hebrew, buying silence, and describes Ananias as a devout man well spoken of by every Jew in Damascus. The crowd stays with him through the light, the voice and the blindness. The word that ends it is Gentiles, and the dust is in the air. Paul waits until tied to the post to mention his citizenship, born not bought.
ACT 23:1-35	Paul manages one sentence before being punched, calls the high priest a whitewashed wall, and retracts it on learning who he is. His next move is tactics: he identifies the two parties and announces he is on trial for the resurrection, setting Sadducees and Pharisees against each other and getting carried out by soldiers. Forty men swear off food and water until he is dead, a vow they will regret. The plot is undone by a nephew nobody knew he had. The commander sends hundreds of soldiers for one prisoner.
ACT 24:1-27	Tertullus opens with flattery for a governor history remembers as a brute, then reaches charges that are grand and unsupported. Paul's reply is a lawyer's: twelve days, checkable, nobody saw him arguing, and his accusers have not bothered to appear. Felix decides nothing. When the subject reaches judgment he gets frightened and ends the meeting, the most honest reaction anyone in the book has to a sermon. He keeps sending for Paul anyway hoping for a bribe, then leaves him chained as a parting favour.
ACT 25:1-27	A new governor, the same request, the same road ambush waiting behind it. Festus keeps the venue in Caesarea, then asks the question that forces Paul's hand: would he rather be tried in Jerusalem? The appeal to Caesar is two words and irreversible; Festus grants it, and the rest of the book follows from that sentence. He then explains the case to Agrippa, the most useful summary in the book because it is baffled: a dispute about their religion and one Jesus who died and whom Paul insists is alive.
ACT 26:1-32	The third telling of the conversion, and the most polished, because the audience is a king. He flatters Agrippa's expertise and grounds the argument in the hope of the twelve tribes, an internal Jewish question. Only this version includes the line about kicking against the goads, a Greek proverb aimed at a Greek-speaking court. Festus interrupts to shout that he has read himself insane. Agrippa's answer is famously hard to translate, and sounds like a man declining politely. He could have walked free, had he not appealed.
ACT 27:1-21	Luke's shipwreck is the most detailed sea narrative from antiquity. The harbour is poor for wintering; the prisoner objects; the centurion follows the captain, and the majority votes. A gentle south wind confirms them wrong. Then the wind gets a name, Euroclydon: boat hauled aboard, cables under the hull, gear thrown overboard by hand. Fourteen days with no sun or stars means no navigation, and hope is gone. Next time on Acts: I told you so, then something useful.
ACT 27:22-44	Having got the reproach out of the way, Paul becomes the calmest man aboard. He spots sailors faking an anchor drop to steal the lifeboat and has soldiers cut it loose, stranding everyone together deliberately. Then he makes two hundred seventy-six starving people eat breakfast: took bread, gave thanks, broke it. The wreck is technical and unsentimental. The last threat is standing orders: soldiers who let prisoners escape die in their place, so killing them looks sensible. The centurion overrules it to save one man, and everyone gets ashore.
ACT 28:1-31	The islanders build a fire for soaked strangers, then watch a snake bite Paul and revise their opinion of him twice in one paragraph, murderer to god. Malta gives him three months, a healing or two, and provisions for the last leg. The approach to Rome is quiet: brothers walking out to meet him. The ending is deliberately anticlimactic: no trial, no Caesar, no martyrdom. Instead a rented house, an open door, two years arguing with people who half agree, and the last word of the book: unhindered.
EXO 7:1-25	Moses is eighty, Aaron eighty-three, and the two of them are sent to bully an empire. Aaron's rod becomes a serpent, the court magicians match the trick immediately, and then Aaron's rod eats theirs. Pharaoh is unimpressed. So the Nile turns to blood, fish dead, river foul, and the magicians match that too, making the water worse rather than better. Pharaoh goes home and, in the text's driest line, doesn't even take this to heart. Egypt digs holes by the river for a week.
EXO 8:1-32	Frogs, everywhere, in the ovens and the dough, and the magicians helpfully produce more frogs. Pharaoh cracks and lets Moses name the hour of relief; he says tomorrow, which is a strange choice when you are sleeping among frogs. They die in heaps and the land stinks, and with relief comes Pharaoh's usual amnesia. Lice next, and this time the magicians cannot copy it: this is God's finger. Then flies, with Goshen carved out, and Pharaoh begins negotiating. The flies go, the answer reverts to no.
EXO 9:1-18	Livestock die across Egypt while Israel's do not, and Pharaoh sends someone to verify it, gets it confirmed, and changes nothing, which is a kind of achievement. Then furnace ashes flung skyward turn into boils, and the magicians, whose whole role has been showing up to compete, cannot stand in the room. The next speech shifts register: God could have ended Egypt in one stroke and has instead kept Pharaoh upright as an exhibit. Next time on Exodus: the worst storm since Egypt was a country.
EXO 9:19-35	The warning comes with an evacuation notice, and the text quietly sorts Pharaoh's officials into those who believed it and those who left their staff outside. Hail, thunder and lightning flatten everything standing in the fields, except in Goshen. Pharaoh manages the closest thing to a confession he ever gets to: I have sinned, Yahweh is righteous. Moses stops the storm, then tells him he still doesn't fear God, which the next verse proves. A footnote explains the wheat survived because it hadn't come up yet.
EXO 10:1-29	Pharaoh's own officials stage a mild intervention: how long is this man going to be a snare to us. Pharaoh offers the men only, then has Moses thrown out. Locusts arrive on an east wind and eat what the hail missed. An apology, a prayer, a west wind, no locusts. Then three days of darkness thick enough to feel. The last offer is everything but the animals; Moses replies that not a hoof stays, and Pharaoh ends it with a death threat.
EXO 11:1-10	One plague left, and the instruction that goes with it is to go ask the neighbours for their silver and gold, which they do, because Egypt now likes Israel and thinks a great deal of Moses. The announcement is short and terrible: at midnight every firstborn dies, from the throne to the mill to the cattle, and the cry will be unlike anything before or since. Against Israel not even a dog will bark, a precise image for total quiet. Moses walks out in hot anger.
EXO 12:1-22	Before the last plague comes the recipe. A year-old lamb without defect, killed at evening on the fourteenth, blood on the doorposts and lintel, roasted whole, eaten with bitter herbs and flat bread, leftovers burned. And eaten standing up with your belt on and your stick in your hand, which is the detail that gives the meal away: this is not dinner, it is a departure. Seven days of unleavened bread are legislated before the escape has happened. Next time on Exodus: the night itself.
EXO 12:23-51	A note on how to answer your children sits just before the event that will need explaining. At midnight every firstborn in Egypt dies, from the palace to the dungeon, and the great cry Moses promised arrives, with no house untouched. Pharaoh, who spent ten chapters saying no, now says go, and adds bless me also. The Egyptians hurry them out so fast the bread has no time to rise, which is why the flat bread was legislated first. Four hundred and thirty years end to the day.
EXO 13:1-22	Every firstborn belongs to God now, which is either a claim on Israel or a reminder of what happened in Egypt. The firstborn donkey gets a lamb substituted or its neck broken, sitting right beside the redemption of sons. Twice Moses scripts the conversation for when a child asks what this is. Then a note of realism: God takes the long road because these people would turn back at the first sight of an army. Joseph's bones come along, four hundred years late.
EXO 14:1-31	The trap is deliberate: Israel camps against the water so Pharaoh will conclude they are lost, which he obligingly does. Six hundred chariots come out. The people's first instinct is a sarcastic complaint about there being no graves in Egypt. Moses tells them to stand still, and God tells Moses to stop praying and get them moving. The cloud holds the armies apart all night. An east wind opens the sea, Israel walks, Egypt follows, the wheels come off, the water closes.
EXO 15:1-27	The escape gets a song, and a good one: horse and rider thrown into the sea, the enemy quoted mid-boast about dividing the plunder just before the wind arrives. It then lists everyone who will be terrified, Philistia through Canaan, none of whom have been met yet. Miriam takes a tambourine and the women dance the whole thing again in one line. Then three waterless days, and bitter water at Marah, and the singing stops abruptly. A tree sweetens it. Elim has twelve springs and seventy palms.
EXO 16:1-16	Six weeks after the sea, the congregation remembers Egypt as a place with meat pots and all the bread you could eat, a remarkable revision of slavery. God's answer is bread from the sky on a strict daily ration, explicitly as a test. Moses points out twice that the complaint is not really against him. Quail arrive at evening, something flaky appears with the dew, and the people ask what is it, which is the word they will call it forever. Next time on Exodus: the leftovers.
EXO 16:17-36	The economics are the miracle: gather greedily or gather badly, and the measure comes out the same for everyone. Told not to keep any overnight, some keep it overnight, and it goes wormy and rank, which is either a lesson about trust or about food safety. On the sixth day it doubles and keeps perfectly, since the Sabbath is being introduced by pastry logistics rather than by commandment. It tastes like honey wafers, and they eat it for forty years, which is a long time for one flavour.
EXO 17:1-16	No water at Rephidim, and Moses reports to God that the people are approximately one hour from stoning him. He strikes a rock with the rod that struck the Nile, water comes out, and he names the place after the argument. The question they asked, is God among us or not, is the sort of thing you ask while drinking miraculous rock water. Then Amalek attacks, and the battle is decided by whether an eighty-year-old can hold his arms up, so Aaron and Hur prop them like scaffolding until sunset.
EXO 18:1-27	Jethro turns up with the wife and two sons Moses had sent home at some unspecified point, which the text mentions and declines to explain. He blesses God and holds a sacrifice, a Midianite priest presiding over Israel's worship without anyone finding this odd. Next day he watches his son-in-law hear every dispute in the nation personally and delivers the first management consultancy in scripture: this is not good, you will wear away, delegate. Moses takes the note without a word.
EXO 19:1-25	Sinai, three months out. The offer comes in the grandest possible terms, eagles' wings and a kingdom of priests, and the people agree sight unseen, a habit they will not break. Then the preparation: wash, wait three days, and do not touch the mountain, on pain of being shot from a distance so nobody else has to touch you either. The mountain does everything at once. Moses climbs up and is sent straight back down.
EXO 20:1-26	Ten commandments, and the striking thing is how short they are: four about God, six about the neighbours, no penalties attached yet. The last is the odd one out, since coveting is the only item nobody can see you do. Having heard exactly one speech directly, the people ask that all future speeches be forwarded through Moses. Then, after the most famous text in the book, a practical note about altars: no stairs, for reasons of modesty.
EXO 21:1-36	After the thunder and the mountain, the fine print. Six years of service then freedom, unless the master supplied the wife, in which case she and the children stay and the man walks out alone, which is what produces a servant volunteering to stay forever and getting his ear nailed to the doorpost. Then homicide, parents, kidnapping, and the eye for eye formula, a cap on revenge rather than an invitation. Your bull gets one free goring; the second time it is your life.
EXO 22:1-31	The theft tariff is oddly specific, five oxen for an ox but four sheep for a sheep, and nobody explains the discount. A burglar killed in the dark is nobody's fault; the identical burglar killed in daylight is manslaughter. Then pages of who pays when something borrowed dies, settled by oath. The tone shifts hard at the end: do not wrong the foreigner, because you were foreigners, and give a man his coat back by sundown, because what else is he going to sleep in.
EXO 23:1-33	A short course in courtroom integrity, including the unexpected instruction not to favour a poor man in his lawsuit, since justice bent kindly is still bent. Then the inconvenient rule that your enemy's escaped ox goes back to him, and his collapsed donkey gets your help. Sabbath extends to the land every seventh year. Three feasts, and one baffling ban on boiling a young goat in its mother's milk. The conquest will be slow on purpose, so the country does not go feral.
EXO 24:1-18	The covenant is ratified with an unusual ceremony: half the sacrificial blood on the altar, the book read aloud, the people agreeing for the third time in one chapter, and then the other half thrown over them. Then seventy-four men climb the mountain and see the God of Israel, and the narrator describes him entirely by what was under his feet: pavement like sapphire, clear as the sky. Nobody died, and they had lunch. Moses goes higher, into the cloud, for forty days.
EXO 25:1-40	A fundraising appeal, strictly voluntary, for gold, silver, dyed cloth, acacia, spices and gemstones, on the stated grounds that God intends to move in. Then specifications exact to the half cubit. The ark's carrying poles are never to be taken out of their rings, a small detail with a large implication: this is furniture for a God who is not planning to settle down. The lampstand is beaten from a single talent of gold in one piece, blossoms and all.
EXO 26:1-18	Textiles. Ten embroidered linen curtains with cherubim, joined five and five by fifty gold clasps, the point being that the tent is one object rather than two. Above that, eleven curtains of goats' hair, and the eleventh is the interesting problem: an odd number over an even one, solved by folding the spare double across the front like a valance. Then two waterproof layers, and acacia boards ten cubits tall, twenty along the south. Next time on Exodus: what holds them up.
EXO 26:19-37	The load-bearing chapter, and it is all silver: ninety-six sockets of it, two under every board, holding the structure upright in sand. Six boards close the west end and two odd double boards handle the corners, a detail that only appears in instructions written by someone who has actually built a corner. Gold-plated bars slot through gold rings, one of them running the full length inside the boards where nobody will ever see it. Then the veil, whose entire job is to keep two rooms apart.
EXO 27:1-21	The altar is bronze rather than gold, hollow, with horns growing out of the corners as one piece, and it comes with a full kitchen inventory of pots, shovels, basins, forks and fire pans, because this is a building where a great deal of meat is going to be burned. Then the courtyard fence, a hundred cubits by fifty, bronze down to the last peg. The chapter ends on its smallest item, a standing order for olive oil to keep a lamp lit forever.
EXO 28:1-24	Aaron and four sons are made priests and then, at length, dressed. The stated purpose of the wardrobe is glory and beauty, which is not a phrase the rest of this legislation goes in for. The ephod carries two engraved onyx stones with six tribal names each, so the high priest wears the whole nation on his shoulders. The breastplate does it again in twelve gemstones, four rows of three. Nobody explains why the names appear twice. Next time on Exodus: the fastenings, and the bells.
EXO 28:25-43	The engineering problem of the chapter is keeping the breastplate from swinging, solved with gold rings and a blue cord. Inside it go the Urim and Thummim, which the text names and never once describes. The robe's hem alternates gold bells with woven pomegranates, and the reason given is startling: the sound is heard when he goes in and comes out, so that he does not die. Then the last instruction is linen underwear, also on pain of death.
EXO 29:1-23	Ordination, and it is a long messy day. Wash, dress, anoint, then three animals: a bull as a sin offering whose remainder goes outside the camp, a ram burned entirely, and a second ram that gets the strangest treatment in the chapter. Its blood goes on the right earlobe, right thumb and right big toe of every man being ordained, marking what he will hear, do and go with. Then oil and blood together, over the men and their brand new clothes. Next time on Exodus: dinner.
EXO 29:24-46	The consecration food is put in the priests' hands and waved, which is how a portion is formally handed over and handed back. The breast goes to Moses; breast and thigh become the priesthood's permanent cut of everyone else's peace offerings, a salary paid in meat. The rest is eaten at the tent door by the ordained only, and anything left at dawn is burned. Seven days of this, then two lambs every day forever. The point arrives last: I will dwell among them.
EXO 30:1-18	A second altar, small and gold, for incense only, and the prohibitions are unusually blunt: no strange incense, no burnt offering, no grain, no drink offering, nothing but the one thing. Then a rule that sits oddly among the furniture: counting people is dangerous, so each man over twenty pays half a shekel as a ransom to head off a plague. The rich pay no more and the poor no less, the flattest tax in the Bible. Then a bronze washbasin. Next time on Exodus: why it matters.
EXO 30:19-38	The basin turns out to be lethal equipment: wash your hands and feet or die, stated twice in three verses. Then two recipes, an anointing oil and an incense, each given exact ingredients and weights like a perfumer's formula, both protected by the same penalty for copying. The incense clause is sharper: anyone who mixes it just to enjoy the smell is cut off from his people. It is possible to be exiled for the wrong perfume.
EXO 31:1-18	After six chapters of specifications, someone has to build it, and God names him: Bezalel of Judah, filled with the Spirit of God for metalwork, stonecutting and carpentry, a striking use of the phrase. Oholiab of Dan assists, and every willing worker gets skill too. Then, right after ordering a vast construction project, God insists on the Sabbath, on pain of death, less a change of subject than a warning about enthusiasm. The chapter ends with the two stone tablets, written with God's finger.
EXO 32:1-16	Moses is late, and forty days is apparently the limit of the people's attention span. Aaron's contribution to religious history is asking for everyone's earrings. The calf is barely cool before it is credited with the entire exodus, and Aaron announces a feast to Yahweh in front of it, a compromise nobody asked for. God offers to start over with Moses alone. Moses talks him out of it in three moves, the last being that the Egyptians will laugh. Next time on Exodus: he looks.
EXO 32:17-35	Joshua hears fighting; Moses, who already knows, corrects him: that is singing. Then he sees it, and the tablets God wrote go to pieces on the rocks. He burns the calf, grinds it, scatters it on the water and makes them drink it. Aaron's defence is one of the great evasions in scripture: I threw the gold in the fire, and out came this calf. About three thousand die. Moses then offers to be erased from God's book in their place. The offer is declined.
EXO 33:1-23	The offer on the table is everything Israel was promised, minus God, who explains that travelling with them at close range would end badly. The people take off their jewellery and stay in mourning, the first time in the book they read a room correctly. Moses meets God in a tent outside camp, face to face, as a man speaks to his friend. Moses refuses the deal: if you do not come, do not send us. He wins, then asks to see the glory, and gets God's back.
EXO 34:1-18	Round two, this time Moses supplies the stone himself. God passes by and proclaims his own name in what becomes the Bible's most quoted description of him: merciful, gracious, slow to anger, abundant in loving kindness, yet by no means clearing the guilty. Moses asks him to come along anyway, granting in advance that the people are impossible. The covenant is renewed with a sharper warning: no treaties, no intermarriage, no cast idols, because his name is Jealous. Next time on Exodus: a face nobody can look at.
EXO 34:19-35	The renewed terms are mostly familiar: firstborns, sabbath, three pilgrim feasts, firstfruits, and once again the ban on boiling a young goat in its mother's milk. One new clause is quietly reassuring, a promise that nobody will covet your land while every man is away at a festival. Forty days, no bread, no water. Then Moses comes down not knowing his face is shining, everyone backs away from him, and he ends up wearing a veil in public, taking it off only to go back in and talk to God.
EXO 35:1-35	Before a single board is cut, Moses reasserts the Sabbath, and adds a clause not heard before: no lighting a fire anywhere you live. Given that the project ahead involves smelting gold, the timing is deliberate.
