The trap identified is the sentence my own power got me this, and the antidote is a memory of scorpions, flint and thirst.
DEU 9:1-29	A rousing pre-invasion speech that turns into a character reference, and not a flattering one. You are not getting this land for being righteous, says Moses, you are stiff-necked, and here is the file: Horeb, the calf, forty days on the mountain, and God offering to wipe them out and restart the nation through Moses alone. The tablets are broken on arrival, the calf burned, powdered and poured into a stream. The argument that worked was Egypt's likely opinion of God.
DEU 10:1-22	The tablets are reissued, this time with Moses supplying the stationery and a wooden box to keep them in. A parenthesis handles Aaron's death and Eleazar's promotion in one sentence, and explains why Levi owns nothing: God is his inheritance, which is either generous or a pay dispute. Then the whole law summarised in four verbs, fear, love, walk, serve. The reason given for loving foreigners is memory. You were foreigners. Also, circumcise your heart.
DEU 11:1-32	Moses is careful about his audience: he is talking to the people who actually watched the sea close and the ground open under Dathan and Abiram, not to their children. The agricultural argument is the sharp part. Egypt you irrigate by foot, this land drinks rain, which makes the weather the enforcement mechanism and a shut sky the penalty. The doorpost instructions repeat. The chapter ends by assigning the blessing to Gerizim and the curse to Ebal, geography as liturgy.
DEU 12:1-16	Centralisation. Every hilltop shrine is to be dismantled, and the oddity worth naming is the line that follows: you shall not do so to Yahweh your God, meaning do not treat him as one shrine among many. Offerings go to a single chosen place and are eaten there as a meal, Levite included. The stated problem with the present arrangement is every man doing what is right in his own eyes. Next time on Deuteronomy: the fine print on blood.
DEU 12:17-32	The concession is generous and plainly worded: if the sanctuary is too far and you want meat, have meat. What cannot travel is the sacred portion, tithes and firstborn and vows, to be eaten at the chosen place with the household and the Levite, who is mentioned twice in case anyone forgets him. The blood rule finally gets its reason, the blood is the life. The chapter closes by naming what these nations did to their children, and forbidding curiosity about their methods.
NUM 31:1-23	The last order Moses receives before he dies is a war order. A thousand men per tribe, Phinehas carrying the holy vessels, and Midian loses every male, its five kings, and Balaam, whose blessing business did not extend to himself. The army returns with captives and is met with fury rather than thanks: Moses asks why the women are alive, and the order he gives is not softened by the text. Purification rules follow. Next time on Numbers: the spoils, counted to the last donkey.
NUM 31:24-54	Cleanliness resolved, the chapter becomes a spreadsheet. The plunder is halved, taxed at one in five hundred for the priest and one in fifty for the Levites, then itemized: sheep, cattle, donkeys, and thirty-two thousand young women, listed in the same column as the animals. The officers arrive with a remarkable statistic, not one Israelite casualty, and hand over sixteen thousand seven hundred fifty shekels of gold as atonement money. It goes into the Tent of Meeting as a memorial, which is one way to file a receipt.
NUM 32:1-20	Reuben and Gad look at the grass in Gilead and develop strong feelings about real estate. Their request to settle east of the Jordan sounds reasonable until Moses hears the spy report in it, and he says so at length: your fathers discouraged Israel once, and it cost them the land and forty years. The two tribes counter with a compromise, families in fortified cities, fighting men out front until everyone else is settled. Next time on Numbers: the fine print.
NUM 32:21-42	The deal closes on Moses' terms: cross the river, fight until the land is subdued, then come home guiltless. The alternative is put memorably, be sure your sin will find you out. Gad and Reuben agree, and Moses hands enforcement to Eleazar, Joshua and the tribal heads, since he will not be there to do it himself. Sihon's and Og's kingdoms are divided, cities rebuilt and renamed, and a man called Nobah takes a town and names it Nobah.
NUM 33:1-56	A travel log, some forty stops, most of them a name and a departure. The list has two moments of texture: Elim, with twelve springs and seventy palm trees, and Rephidim, remembered only for having no water. Aaron dies on Mount Hor at a hundred twenty-three, dated to the month. Then instructions for the far bank: clear out the inhabitants, smash the images, allot the land by lot and by size. Anyone left behind becomes pricks in the eyes and thorns in the sides.
NUM 34:1-29	A surveyor's chapter. The land is described as a perimeter, corner by corner: Zin and Edom to the south, the great sea west, Mount Hor and Hazar Enan north, the Jordan and the Salt Sea east. Nine and a half tribes only, because two and a half made other arrangements. Then the commission that will divide it: Eleazar, Joshua, and one prince per tribe, mostly men never heard from again, headed by Caleb, who has waited forty years for exactly this job.
NUM 35:1-17	The Levites, having no territory of their own, are given forty-eight towns with a measured ring of pasture, and the measurement is oddly insistent: a thousand cubits from the wall, then two thousand on each of the four sides. Six of the towns are cities of refuge, open to Israelites and foreigners equally, where a man who kills by accident can outrun the avenger of blood long enough to get a hearing. Next time on Numbers: how the court tells the difference.
NUM 35:18-34	The line is drawn by method and by motive: wood, hatred, ambush, a shove given in hostility, all murder, and the avenger of blood carries out the sentence himself. Accidental death gets a trial and internal exile in a refuge city, and the sentence expires when the high priest dies, which makes one man's freedom depend on another man's health. Step past the city limits early and the avenger is within his rights. Two witnesses minimum, no cash settlements.
NUM 36:1-13	Numbers ends on a property loophole. The men of Gilead point out that if Zelophehad's daughters marry out, the land they won leaves Manasseh for good at the jubilee. Moses agrees, and the ruling is a masterpiece of qualified freedom: they may marry whomever they like, so long as he is a cousin. The five sisters marry their father's brothers' sons, the land stays put, and the book closes where it has been camped for chapters, the plains of Moab.
DEU 13:1-18	The chapter on treason, and it escalates deliberately. First the prophet whose sign actually works, which the text handles by saying the accuracy was the test, not the credential. Then the private case, brother, child, wife, the friend who is as your own soul, with the instruction that the informer's own hand goes first. Then a whole city, subject to a genuine investigation, inquire and search and ask diligently, before being burned and left a heap forever.
DEU 14:1-29	The dietary list, and it is a list, but two things in it are worth stopping on. Anything that dies of itself may be given or sold to a foreigner, which is either hospitality or its opposite. And the tithe has a travel clause: if the sanctuary is too far, sell the produce, carry the cash, and spend it there on cattle, sheep, wine or strong drink, whatever your soul asks for. Every third year the tithe stays home for the Levite, the orphan and the widow.
DEU 15:1-23	Debt cancellation every seventh year, with the anticipated loophole named and blocked: do not look at your poor brother, notice the release year is close, and keep your hand shut. The concession that the poor will never cease out of the land sits oddly beside the earlier promise that there shall be no poor among you. A freed slave leaves supplied, not empty. One who prefers to stay gets an awl through the ear into the doorpost, permanence made audible.
DEU 16:1-22	The festival calendar, and what it keeps repeating is the guest list. Passover in Abib, at the one chosen place, with the unleavened bread named the bread of affliction. Weeks, counted from the first swing of the sickle. Tents, after the wine press. Each time the same people are itemized: sons, daughters, servants, the Levite, the foreigner, the orphan, the widow. Then judges, with a memorable reason for the bribery ban, a bribe blinds the eyes of the wise.
DEU 17:1-20	Court procedure, and then a constitution. Capital cases need two witnesses minimum, and the witnesses have to throw the first stones, which is an interesting way to discourage perjury. Hard cases go up to the central court, whose ruling is final in the strongest sense. Then the king, permitted rather than commanded, and hedged about: no cavalry buildup, no harem, no treasury, and a homework assignment, since he copies out this law by hand and reads it every day of his life.
DEU 18:1-22	Clergy pay, then a ban on the competition. The priests' cut is itemized down to the cheeks and the inner parts, and a Levite who relocates to the sanctuary gets an equal share. Then the forbidden practices, which read like a directory of the entire ancient consulting industry: diviners, sorcerers, charmers, mediums, necromancers. In their place, one prophet like Moses, requested by the people themselves because the voice from the fire frightened them. The test for a true prophet is refreshingly empirical.
DEU 19:1-21	Refuge cities again, with roads to be prepared, which is thoughtful, and one memorable illustration: the axe head that slips off its handle in the woods. The point of the short distance is emotional, since the avenger's heart is hot and the road is long. A genuine murderer gets extradited by his own elders. Then landmark theft, then witnesses, two minimum, and a proved liar receives the punishment he intended for the accused, followed by the eye for eye formula in full.
DEU 20:1-20	Draft exemptions, and they are strikingly humane: a new house not yet dedicated, a vineyard not yet tasted, a fiancee not yet married, and, remarkably, plain fear, on the grounds that it is contagious. Distant cities get a peace offer first. The nations in the land get no such offer, with the reason stated as cultural quarantine. And then the line that stops the chapter, do not axe the fruit trees, for is the tree of the field a man, that you should besiege it.
DEU 21:1-23	Four unrelated laws, and the first is the strangest: an unsolved murder is settled by the nearest town breaking a heifer's neck in an unploughed valley and washing their hands over it while swearing they did not do it. Then the captive bride, given a shaved head, a month of mourning, and, if rejected, freedom rather than sale. Then inheritance, where the unloved wife's son keeps the double portion regardless of feelings. Then a stoned son, and a body that cannot hang overnight.
DEU 22:1-30	A chapter that starts in neighbourly small print and ends somewhere much heavier. The early laws are charming in their specificity: return the lost donkey, help the fallen one up, put a railing on your roof, let the mother bird fly before taking the eggs, no wool woven with linen. Then the marriage and sex laws, graded carefully by circumstance: a false accusation costs a hundred shekels, adultery is fatal to both, and a woman forced in a field is explicitly compared to a murder victim.
DEU 23:1-25	Membership rules first, with a long grudge: Ammon and Moab excluded for ten generations over bread, water and the hiring of Balaam, while Edom and Egypt are readmitted in three. Then the camp sanitation law, which is the one everybody remembers, carry a trowel with your weapons and bury it, because God walks through the camp. An escaped slave is not to be returned to his master, which is remarkable for the period. And you may eat your neighbour's grapes, just not bag them.
DEU 24:1-22	Domestic and economic law, and its logic is consistently about margins. A newly married man is excused from the army for a year specifically to cheer his wife. A millstone cannot be taken as security, because that is taking a livelihood. A poor man's cloak comes back before dark so he can sleep in it. Wages are paid the same day, because he is counting on them. And harvest is to be done badly on purpose: what you forget belongs to the foreigner, the orphan and the widow.
DEU 25:1-19	Assorted rules with unusually specific reasoning. Forty lashes maximum, and the stated reason is dignity rather than pain. Do not muzzle the working ox. Then levirate marriage, whose refusal ceremony is pure public shaming: the widow pulls off his sandal in front of the elders and spits in his face, and his family is known thereafter as the house of the unsandalled. Honest weights and measures. And Amalek, remembered for attacking the exhausted at the back of the column.
DEU 26:1-19	The harvest liturgy, and its script is worth noticing: the worshipper does not recite doctrine, he recites history in the first person, my father was a Syrian ready to perish, we went down to Egypt, we were oppressed, we cried out. The basket goes down and then everybody eats, Levite and foreigner included. The third-year tithe comes with a sworn declaration that none of it was misused. The chapter closes on a mutual announcement, each party declaring the other its own.
DEU 27:1-26	Instructions for a ceremony: plastered stones on Mount Ebal with the entire law written on them very plainly, and an altar built of stones no iron has touched. Then the tribes are divided across two mountains, six for blessing and six for cursing, and the Levites shout twelve curses while the whole nation answers amen to each. The list is a fair index of the book's anxieties: idols made in secret, moved landmarks, misleading a blind man, and murder for hire.
DEU 28:1-25	The blessings come as matched pairs, city and field, body and ground, basket and kneading trough, coming in and going out, and they end on the promise of being the head and not the tail. Then verse fifteen turns the same list inside out, item by item, and keeps going. Pestilence, fever, blight, mildew. A sky like brass, ground like iron, rain that falls as dust. Defeat, and scattering among the kingdoms. Next time on Deuteronomy: the list does not stop there.
DEU 28:26-50	The list continues, and its cruelty is in the specifics: you will betroth a wife and another man will take her, build a house and not live in it, plant a vineyard and not drink from it. Boils that do not heal, madness, blindness, groping at noon. Children carried off while their parents watch, powerless. Locusts in the grain, worms in the vines, olives dropping. The foreigner rises and Israel sinks. Next time on Deuteronomy: the siege.
DEU 28:51-68	The last section is the siege, and it is unrelieved. The walls that were trusted come down, and hunger inside them drives a father and a mother, described in the text as tender and delicate, to eat their own children in secret. Then plagues of long duration, all the diseases of Egypt, a nation once numerous as the stars reduced to a few. Scattered to the ends of the earth with a trembling heart, wishing morning were evening. And back to Egypt by ship, with no buyers.
DEU 29:1-29	A second covenant, made in Moab on top of the one at Horeb. Moses reviews the evidence and then says something startling: God has not given you a heart to know, or eyes to see, to this day. The oath includes everyone present, down to the foreigner who chops the wood and draws the water, and also those not present. The threat is aimed at the private defector who tells himself he will be fine. And a closing line worth keeping, the secret things belong to God.
DEU 30:1-20	The turn. Having spent a chapter on ruin, Moses assumes it happens and keeps going: from the uttermost parts of the heavens, the scattered get gathered. The promised repair is internal, a circumcised heart, which is the only way the rest of the book works. Then an argument against excuses, this is not in heaven, not across the sea, it is in your mouth. And the verse the whole book has been driving at, delivered as an imperative: choose life.
DEU 31:1-16	Moses at a hundred and twenty, opening with a plain statement of limits: I can no more go out and come in. The handover is public, Joshua charged in front of everyone to be strong and courageous, and the law is written down and given to the priests, with an instruction to read the whole thing aloud every seven years to everybody, children and foreigners included. Then God calls them both to the Tent. Next time on Deuteronomy: the commission, and a song.
DEU 31:17-30	God's forecast is blunt: they will eat, grow fat and go elsewhere, and he will hide his face until they notice the connection themselves. The remedy is a song, on the reasoning that a song is not forgotten out of the mouths of descendants, which is a shrewd theory of memory. Moses writes it, commissions Joshua, finishes the book, and has it filed beside the ark as evidence against them. His parting assessment: you rebelled while I was alive, so I have no illusions.
1CH 1:1-54	Nine generations pass in nine words, Adam to Noah, before anyone gets a verb. Nimrod gets one: he began to be mighty, and that is the whole biography. Peleg is named for the earth being divided, with nothing said about the dividing. The line narrows to Abraham, widens into Ishmael, Keturah and Esau, then hands the chapter to eight Edomite kings who ruled before Israel had one, each dying so the next can be listed.
1CH 2:1-55	Judah's line, and the Chronicler cannot list it without pausing to say who was wicked: Er, killed by Yahweh, and Achar, named the troubler of Israel over the devoted thing. Jesse's sons are counted out to David, seventh. Hezron fathers a child at sixty. Sheshan, having only daughters, marries one to Jarha his Egyptian servant, and ten generations descend from that. The chapter closes on the scribal families who lived at Jabez.
1CH 3:1-24	David's sons, sorted by mother: six in Hebron, nine more in Jerusalem, four of them from Bathshua including Solomon, with no word about how that marriage came about. Tamar is named as their sister and nothing else. Then the kings of Judah in one unbroken chain, Solomon to Josiah, into exile with Jeconiah the captive, and out the far side through Zerubbabel for six more generations of men who ruled nothing, ending at Anani.
1CH 4:1-43	More of Judah, and buried in it are the potters and the fine linen workers who lived where they did because the king had work for them. Jabez, whose name is his mother's pain, asks for blessing and a wider border and gets both inside two verses. Simeon's entry is openly comparative: sixteen sons for Shimei, but the tribe never multiplied like Judah. Under Hezekiah they take fat quiet pasture at Gedor, and five hundred men clear Mount Seir of the last Amalekites.
1CH 5:1-26	Reuben was the firstborn, and the chapter says so twice before admitting he lost the birthright over his father's couch: it went to Joseph's sons, and the ruler came out of Judah anyway. The eastern tribes field forty-four thousand seven hundred sixty men, beat the Hagrites, and come home with fifty thousand camels and a hundred thousand captives, because they trusted God in the battle. Then they take up the local gods, and God stirs two Assyrian kings to carry them off. Same explanation both times.
1CH 6:1-48	The high-priestly line runs from Aaron down to Jehozadak, and only two men in it get anything said about them: Azariah, who served in the house Solomon built, and Jehozadak, who went into captivity. Then Levi's three families, with Samuel appearing quietly among the Kohathites. It closes with David's music appointments, Heman in the middle, Asaph on his right, Ethan on his left, each traced all the way back to Levi to prove he belongs there. Next time on 1 Chronicles: the priests get an address list.
1CH 6:49-81	Aaron's sons do the altars and the atonement, exactly as Moses commanded, and the rest of the day is real estate. Thirteen towns to the priests, ten to the other Kohathites, thirteen to Gershom, twelve to Merari, all drawn by lot. Hebron goes to Aaron's sons, but the fields around it go to Caleb, the one line where two claims meet. Every entry arrives with its suburbs, and the cities of refuge are folded into the same list.
1CH 7:1-40	Issachar, Benjamin, Manasseh, Ephraim and Asher, mostly as muster figures, while Naphtali gets one verse and nothing else. Zelophehad appears with the note that he had daughters, no more. The one story: Ephraim's sons Ezer and Elead are killed rustling cattle from Gath, Ephraim mourns for days, and the next son is called Beriah because of the trouble. His daughter Sheerah builds three towns, more than most men on the page manage. The line runs on to Joshua.
1CH 8:1-40	Benjamin again, at much greater length, because the Chronicler has a destination in mind. Shaharaim fathers a second family in Moab after sending his wives away. Elpaal's sons build Ono and Lod, and Beriah and Shema chase the people of Gath out of Aijalon. Then columns of names, heads of households living in Jerusalem, until the chapter arrives where it was always going: Kish fathered Saul. Jonathan's line is then traced nine more generations to Azel's six sons.
1CH 9:1-20	Everyone was on a genealogy, and Judah went to Babylon anyway for disobedience: one verse manages both. Then the first to move back into their own towns, counted by family. Six hundred ninety from Zerah, nine hundred fifty-six Benjaminites, and a thousand seven hundred sixty priests, whom the text calls very able men for the work. Levites follow, then the gatekeepers with Shallum in charge and Phinehas named as their ruler long ago. Next time on 1 Chronicles: the door staff get their duty roster.
1CH 9:21-44	Two hundred twelve gatekeepers, ordained by David and Samuel the seer, posted on all four sides and relieved every seven days by brothers coming in from the villages. Four chiefs hold the storerooms and open the house each morning. Vessels go out by count and come back by count. One man is specifically over the things baked in pans. The singers live on site and are excused everything else, being on duty day and night. Then Saul's genealogy again.
1CH 10:1-14	Chronicles opens its history with a defeat, having skipped Saul's entire reign to get here. Israel breaks on Gilboa, three of Saul's sons die, and Saul, cornered by archers, asks for a sword and ends up using his own. The Philistines nail his head up in Dagon's temple and take the good news round to their idols. Jabesh Gilead retrieves the bones and fasts a week. Then the verdict: he died for consulting a medium instead of asking Yahweh, and the kingdom passed to David.
1CH 11:1-18	All Israel arrives at Hebron at once, no civil war in between, Chronicles has no interest in seven years of rival kings. They anoint David, and Jerusalem falls to a job advertisement: first to strike the Jebusites gets the command, which Joab duly wins. Then the mighty men: Jashobeam kills three hundred with one spear, Eleazar defends a barley patch alone. Three of the thirty hear David wish aloud for water from Bethlehem's well and cut through an army to get it. Next time on 1 Chronicles: nobody drinks it.
1CH 11:19-47	David will not drink the water, on the grounds that it is the blood of the men who fetched it, and pours it out instead, which is either magnificent or a waste of a very difficult errand. Abishai captains the three without belonging to the three, a distinction the text insists on twice. Benaiah's record includes a lion, in a pit, on a snowy day. Then the full roster by name and home town, with Uriah the Hittite listed among the loyal, some chapters early.
1CH 12:1-20	The defections arrive, and the Chronicler notes each tribe with care. Benjaminites first, Saul's own relatives, ambidextrous with sling and bow. Then Gadites with faces like lions who cross the Jordan at flood stage, the weakest of them rated at a hundred men. When men of Benjamin and Judah turn up, David asks outright whether this is help or betrayal, and Amasai answers in verse. Manassites join on the road from a battle the Philistines would not let David fight. Next time on 1 Chronicles: everybody brings food.
1CH 12:21-40	A muster roll, tribe by tribe, and two entries are doing something other than counting. Benjamin sends three thousand, with the note that most of them had stayed loyal to Saul's house until then. Issachar sends two hundred whose stated qualification is understanding the times and knowing what Israel ought to do, which is an odd thing to field in an army. Zebulun's fifty thousand are specifically not of double heart. Then three days of eating, figs and raisins arriving by donkey, camel, mule and ox.
1CO 1:1-31	Paul opens with a compliment about how gifted they are, which turns out to be the runway for the complaint. Chloe's household has been talking, and the church has organised itself into fan clubs: team Paul, team Apollos, team Cephas, and one group loftily claiming team Christ. Paul is relieved he baptised almost nobody there. His counter is that the cross is embarrassing by design, foolishness to Greeks and offensive to Jews, and that God recruited the unimpressive on purpose.
1CO 2:1-16	Paul reminds a city that admired rhetoric that he arrived with none of it: no polished delivery, no clever argument, visibly nervous, and talking about an execution. That was deliberate, so their confidence would not rest on how good he was at speaking. The wisdom he does claim is hidden, older than the world, and completely missed by the authorities, whom he notes would have thought twice about the crucifixion had they seen it coming.
1CO 3:1-23	Paul tells them they are still on milk, and that their fan clubs are the evidence. He and Apollos get demoted to farm labour: one planted, one watered, and the growth was never theirs. Then a building site, with a foundation already laid and a fire coming to test whether anyone framed the walls in gold or in straw. The builder survives either way, singed. The punchline is that boasting about leaders is absurd, since the leaders belong to them.
1CO 4:1-21	Paul says he does not much mind their verdict on him, since the only review that counts is not yet in. Then the most sarcastic paragraph in the letter: you are rich, you are full, you already reign, how splendid, while the apostles are hungry, beaten, homeless and mopping the floor of the world with themselves. He softens it by claiming fatherhood, mentions Timothy is on his way, and asks whether they would like him to bring the rod.
1CO 5:1-13	A man is living with his father's wife, and Paul's complaint is not only the arrangement but that the church seems rather pleased with itself about how broadminded it is. He has convicted the man in absentia and tells them to expel him. The metaphor is yeast: a small amount changes the whole batch, so throw out the old batch entirely. He also corrects a misreading of his earlier letter, since avoiding every immoral person outside the church would require leaving the planet.
1CO 6:1-20	Corinthian believers are dragging each other into pagan courts, and Paul finds this humiliating: people slated to judge angels cannot referee a property dispute. His suggestion is to simply lose. Then a list of who is out of the kingdom, and the observation that this used to describe several of them. He has no patience for the slogan that everything is permitted, and answers it with prostitution, one flesh, and a temple you did not pay for. Next time on 1 Corinthians: a question about marriage.
1CO 7:1-22	A letter has arrived asking about sex and marriage, and Paul answers with unusual care about what is command and what is opinion. Celibacy is good, marriage is fine, and neither spouse withholds, since each owns the other's body. Better to marry than to burn. Mixed marriages should hold if the unbeliever is content, and dissolve peacefully if not. His running theme: nobody needs to change their situation, circumcised or not, slave or free, stay where you were called. Next time on 1 Corinthians: Paul admits he is improvising.
1CO 7:23-40	Paul flags this section as his own opinion rather than a command from the Lord, which is refreshingly honest. His advice, shaped by an expected crisis, is to stay put. Marrying is not sin but it comes with trouble, and the whole arrangement is temporary anyway: weep as though not weeping, buy as though owning nothing, since the set is being taken down. He concedes that a man who marries off his daughter does well, then cannot resist adding that not doing so does better.
1CO 8:1-13	Idol meat. Paul concedes the Corinthians' theology outright, since idols are nothing and there is one God, and then points out that being right is not the same as being useful: knowledge inflates, love constructs. Food changes nobody's standing. What it can do is embolden someone with a shaky conscience to eat as a worshipper and ruin himself. Paul, who has just conceded the argument, ends by volunteering to give up meat forever, which is not a concession the winners usually make.
1CO 9:1-27	Paul spends most of a chapter proving he is entitled to a salary, marshalling soldiers, vineyards, flocks, an ox that should not be muzzled, temple staff eating from the altar, and a direct instruction from the Lord. Having established the right beyond argument, he announces he has never once used it, because preaching for free is the only thing he has to boast about. Then the training metaphor: runners, boxers, one prize, and a body he treats as an opponent.
1CO 10:1-33	Paul reaches for Israel in the wilderness as a cautionary tale, and it is an unflattering comparison: everyone got the miracle, most did not get to the other end. Idolatry, immorality, testing God, and simply complaining all made the casualty list. Then back to idol meat, where he takes a harder line than in chapter eight: the pagan sacrifices go to demons, and you cannot hold two cups. Butcher shop meat, however, requires no interrogation. Ask no questions unless your host volunteers the information.
1CO 11:1-34	Two problems with how Corinth conducts a meeting. First, head coverings, argued from headship, from creation, from the angels, and finally from what nature obviously teaches, before Paul cuts it off by noting that anyone still arguing is being contentious. Second, and much worse: their communion has become a class dinner, where the rich arrive early and get drunk and the poor go hungry. He quotes the words of Jesus back at them, then gives the practical instruction, which is to wait.
1CO 12:1-31	Corinth has been ranking spiritual gifts, so Paul lists nine and attributes every one to the same Spirit, handed out as it pleases rather than as anyone earned. Then the body, at length, with a foot resigning because it is not a hand and an eye informing the hand it is surplus. His sharpest point: the parts nobody displays get the most careful treatment. The chapter ends by hinting there is something better than the whole argument. Next time on 1 Corinthians: the excellent way.
1CO 13:1-13	The famous wedding reading, which in context is a rebuke to a church proud of its gifts. Speak in every language including the angelic and you are a cymbal. Prophesy, know everything, move mountains, give away all you own, burn: nothing. Then the definition, mostly by what love refuses to do, which is a long list of things Corinth has been doing. Gifts expire because they are partial, like childhood and like a poor mirror. Three things last, and love outranks the other two.
1CO 14:1-40	Paul's problem with tongues is not theology but acoustics: nobody can say amen to a prayer they did not understand. He works the point through musical instruments with no distinct notes and a trumpet that cannot decide whether to sound the charge. He mentions, not entirely innocently, that he speaks in tongues more than any of them, and would still rather say five words people can follow. Then the meeting rules, all aimed at the same complaint, and the closing verdict: decently, and in order.
1CO 15:1-29	Paul recites the earliest summary of the gospel, complete with a witness list that runs from Cephas through five hundred people at once to James, and then to himself, whom he describes with an unflattering metaphor about being born at the wrong time. Some Corinthians have been denying the resurrection of the dead, so Paul takes their premise and follows it downhill: no resurrection, no risen Christ, no gospel, no forgiveness, and a set of apostles who are simply liars. Next time on 1 Corinthians: what sort of body, exactly?
1CO 15:30-58	Someone has asked the practical question, and Paul calls the asker foolish before answering it properly. A seed has to die and comes up as something unrecognisably different, and glory varies anyway, since the sun, moon and stars are not equally bright. Sown perishable, raised imperishable. Then the mystery: not everybody dies, but everybody changes, in the time it takes to blink. He taunts death by name, quoting Hosea, then finishes with the least glamorous instruction possible, which is to keep working.
1CO 16:1-24	Business at the end of the letter. Weekly saving for the Jerusalem collection, so no scramble when he arrives. Travel plans through Macedonia, possibly a winter in Corinth, but Ephesus until Pentecost because a door has opened and, he adds in the same breath, there are many opponents. Timothy is coming and should not be looked down on. Apollos declined the trip, which Paul reports without comment. Then greetings, a note that he is writing this bit himself, one startlingly hard line, and a prayer for the Lord to come.
1TH 1:1-10	Paul's warmest opening, and it is almost entirely compliments: faith that works, love that labours, hope that endures. What he actually praises is the church's reputation, which has travelled so far ahead of him that he claims there is nothing left for him to say, and then keeps writing. The summary of their conversion is admirably compact: they dropped the idols, took up the living God, and are now standing around waiting for his Son to arrive from the sky.
1TH 2:1-20	Paul defends his conduct in Thessalonica against charges nobody in the letter has made, which tells you someone made them. No flattery, no greed, no fishing for honours, and he waived his right to be supported and worked nights instead. His two metaphors for himself in the same paragraph are a nursing mother and a father, which he does not attempt to reconcile. He also says he tried to come back twice and blames Satan for the delay, then calls the readers his trophy.
1TH 3:1-13	The most anxious chapter Paul ever wrote. Twice he says he could not stand it any longer, so he stayed alone in Athens and sent Timothy to check whether the church had survived. His fear is specific and unflattering to himself: that the tempter had got there and his own work had been wasted. Timothy comes back with good news, and Paul's relief is almost physical, since he says he only lives if they are standing. Then a prayer that their love overflow.
1TH 4:1-18	Practical instructions, and the most unexpectedly modest ambition in the collection: aim to live quietly, mind your own affairs, and work with your hands. He also tells them what to do with grief, which is not to stop it but to stop it being hopeless. Then the passage that has launched a thousand charts: a shout, an archangel's voice, a trumpet, the dead in Christ rising first, and the living collected into the clouds. Paul offers it as something to comfort each other with.
1TH 5:1-28	The day of the Lord arrives like a burglar, precisely when everyone is congratulating themselves on peace and safety. Paul's response is not a timetable but an instruction to stay awake and sober, since drunkenness and sleep are both night activities. The armour here is only two pieces, which suggests he had not yet worked the image up to its Ephesians length. Then a rapid volley of one-line commands, three of which have been embroidered onto more cushions than the rest of the letter combined.
1TI 1:1-20	Timothy has been left in Ephesus with an unenviable job: telling specific men to stop teaching. What they are teaching involves myths and endless genealogies, which Paul dismisses without engaging, noting that they want to be teachers of the law and understand neither the law nor their own sentences. Then he turns the light on himself, claiming the title of chief sinner as a demonstration of how patient God can be. It ends with two names and a shipwreck, since Paul does not believe in anonymising the opposition.
1TI 2:1-15	Pray for kings, says Paul, whose stated goal is a quiet life, which is a modest ambition for a man who keeps getting arrested. Then the mediator line, one God and one man in the middle, delivered like a formula because it probably is one. The instructions get specific fast: men are to pray without arguing first, and women are to skip the gold and the elaborate hair. Then the notoriously contested paragraph on women teaching, argued from the order of creation and the garden.
1TI 3:1-16	A job description, and the striking thing is how ordinary it is. An overseer needs to be sober, not violent, not greedy, hospitable, able to teach, and capable of running his own house, since a man who cannot manage a family is an odd choice to manage a congregation. No recent converts, on the grounds that promotion goes to the head. Deacons get tested first. Paul says he hopes to visit, then quotes a hymn as though he ran out of prose.
1TI 4:1-16	The opponents' programme finally becomes visible: no marriage, and no certain foods. Paul's counter is cheerfully material, since anything God made is fine if you say grace over it. Then the line about physical training having some value, which is the ancient world's most quoted concession, immediately outranked by godliness. The rest is advice to a young man told to command people older than him: do not let them despise your youth, read publicly, keep teaching, and watch yourself as closely as you watch the doctrine.
1TI 5:1-25	Church administration, and it is unexpectedly granular. Treat the older ones as parents and the younger as siblings, and then a full policy on the widows' list, complete with a minimum age of sixty, a required record of good works, and a frank observation that younger widows enrolled with nothing to do become house-to-house gossips. Elders who work get paid, and an accusation against one needs witnesses. Buried at the end is the least theological sentence in the letter: have a little wine for your stomach.
1TI 6:1-21	Paul's target here is teachers who treat religion as a business, and his answer is the line everyone half remembers: the love of money is a root of all kinds of evil, not money itself. His logic is disarmingly simple, since nobody brought anything in and nobody takes anything out, so food and clothing should do. The doxology in the middle is the grandest in the letters, all unapproachable light. Then the rich get their instruction, which is not to sell up but to share.
2CO 1:1-24	This letter opens not with thanks for the readers but with thanks for comfort, which tells you what has happened since the last one. Paul mentions a disaster in Asia without describing it, saying only that he despaired of life and felt sentenced to death. Then the real business: apparently someone has accused him of being unreliable because he changed his itinerary. His defence runs from his own integrity up to God's promises, which he insists are Yes rather than a politician's yes and no.
2CO 2:1-17	Paul explains the cancelled visit: he did not want a second unpleasant trip, so he sent a letter instead, written in tears, which he now half apologises for. The man who caused the trouble has been disciplined enough, and Paul asks them to forgive him before grief finishes the job, adding that he knows Satan's playbook. He also admits he could not concentrate at Troas because Titus never turned up, an opened door notwithstanding. The chapter ends with the church as a smell: perfume to some, decay to others.
2CO 3:1-18	Rival teachers have evidently arrived with credentials, so Paul says his credentials are the Corinthians themselves, a letter written in Spirit rather than ink and readable by anyone. Then the comparison he cannot resist: if a covenant chiselled into rock left Moses' face too bright to look at, the newer arrangement should be brighter still. He reads Moses' veil as a fading act being covered up, and says the same veil still sits over the old reading, lifting only when someone turns.
2CO 4:1-18	Paul insists he has never doctored the message, which is the kind of thing a man says because someone has claimed otherwise. His self-description is deliberately unimpressive: treasure in cheap pottery, so nobody mistakes where the power comes from. Then the famous pairs, each conceding the blow and refusing the conclusion, pressed but not crushed, struck down but not destroyed. The outer man is falling apart on schedule while the inner one is renewed, and the affliction is filed under light and momentary, which is a bold accounting.
2CO 5:1-21	Paul calls the body a tent, which nobody has ever called a permanent structure, and describes groaning for the building that replaces it. He would rather be away from the body and at home with the Lord, though the judgment seat is on the itinerary either way. Then a run of the letter's biggest lines: one died for all, anyone in Christ is a new creation, and God has handed his messengers the job of reconciliation, which makes them ambassadors doing the begging.
2CO 6:1-18	Paul's credentials, unlike his rivals', are a list of things that happened to him: beatings, prisons, riots, no sleep and no food, alongside the harder-to-verify purity and sincere love. Then a run of paradoxes where every insult he has been given gets an answer attached, called deceivers yet true, dying yet alive, poor yet enriching people. He tells them his heart is wide open and that the restriction is at their end. Then, abruptly, a warning about being yoked to unbelievers.
2CO 7:1-16	Paul finally tells us what he was so anxious about: he reached Macedonia and could not rest, fighting outside and fear inside, until Titus arrived with good news. He then discusses his own harsh letter with unusual candour, admitting he did regret sending it and now does not, since the grief it caused was the productive kind. Godly sorrow leads to repentance; worldly sorrow just kills. He also confesses he had talked them up to Titus and was relieved to be proved right.
2CO 8:1-24	A fundraising letter, and Paul opens with the oldest technique there is: telling one church how generous another church was. The Macedonians were broke, gave beyond their means, and had to be allowed to. The Corinthians, who excel at everything by their own account, are invited to excel at this too. He notes they started a year ago and might consider finishing. Then a paragraph about sending three men with the money, so nobody can accuse him of anything. Next time on 2 Corinthians: the same subject, more delicately.
2CO 9:1-15	Paul says he does not need to write about the collection and then writes about it for fifteen verses, which is the joke of the chapter. He is sending the brothers ahead so nothing has to be extracted from them in a rush while Macedonian visitors watch. The farming logic follows: sparse sowing, sparse harvest. God loves a cheerful giver, which quietly rules out the reluctant one. He ends on thanksgiving, having asked for money as gracefully as it can be asked for.
2CO 10:1-18	Paul quotes the actual insult, which is a brave editorial choice: strong letters, weak presence, and a speaking voice nobody rates. His answer is that the absent version and the present version are the same man, and they may find that out. He also declines to enter the comparison game, noting that his rivals measure themselves against themselves and grade generously. He will stay inside the territory he was given, which is a pointed remark about people setting up shop in a church someone else planted.
2CO 11:1-33	Paul announces he is about to talk nonsense, then does it deliberately. He casts himself as the father who arranged their engagement to Christ and is watching the bride get chatted up. His rivals are false apostles, on the grounds that Satan also dresses well. Then the boast, done in the only currency he has: five floggings, three beatings with rods, a stoning, three shipwrecks, a night in open water, hunger, cold, and the daily anxiety of the churches. It ends with him leaving Damascus in a laundry basket.
2CO 12:1-21	The one vision Paul will mention he tells in the third person, twice admitting he does not know whether he was in his body, and he refuses to repeat what he heard. Then the thorn, which he never identifies, and three prayers that got the same answer: grace is enough, power works best in weakness. So he boasts about the wrong things on purpose. He then notes that his sole crime against Corinth was not charging them for anything, and asks to be forgiven for it.
2CO 13:1-14	Third visit, and Paul says this time he will not be gentle, citing the legal rule about witnesses. They want proof Christ speaks through him, so he flips the test around: examine yourselves, and see whether you pass. He points out he would rather look weak and have them be strong, which undercuts the whole contest. The letter that has spent thirteen chapters arguing ends by asking them to be at peace, and closes with the warmest three-part blessing in the whole collection.
2TH 1:1-12	The opening thanksgiving starts warm and then turns into flaming fire and mighty angels rather faster than the reader expects. Paul's argument to a persecuted church is that the persecution itself proves God's judgment is coming, which is comfort of a very specific kind: those doing the afflicting will get theirs, and the afflicted will get relief, at the same event. The prayer at the end is short and modest by comparison, asking only that they be found worthy of what they already have.
2TH 2:1-17	Somebody has been circulating a letter in Paul's name saying the day of the Lord has already happened, and Paul is here to deny it in writing. His counter-timetable is the strangest passage in either Thessalonian letter: a rebellion, a man of sin installing himself in God's temple as God, and something or someone currently restraining him that Paul declines to name, on the grounds that he already told them in person. The reader, who was not there, is left guessing. Next time on 2 Thessalonians: the busybodies.
2TH 3:1-18	Paul deals with a practical side effect of expecting the world to end: some people have stopped working. His response is not theological but blunt, and he quotes his own house rule back at them, that anyone unwilling to work does not eat. He points at his own record of paying for his meals while working nights. The offenders are described as not working at all but very busy, which is an accurate description of a certain kind of person. They are to be shamed, but still counted family.
2TI 1:1-18	This one reads like a last letter, and Paul writes it to Timothy the way you write to family, remembering his tears and crediting his faith to two women by name, his grandmother and his mother. The advice is to fan the gift back into flame, which implies it had cooled. Then the sting: everyone in Asia walked away, and Paul supplies two names. Against that he sets Onesiphorus, who went around Rome asking after a prisoner until he found one, which was not a safe hobby.
2TI 2:1-26	Three quick images for endurance, and none of them is heroic: a soldier who stays undistracted, an athlete who bothers to read the rules, a farmer who eats first. Then a warning against arguing about words, which Paul compares to gangrene, an image nobody improves on. Hymenaeus and Philetus get named for teaching that the resurrection already happened. The instruction to correct opponents gently sits a little awkwardly next to naming them in a letter, but Paul has never worried about that.
2TI 3:1-17	A vice list of nineteen items, and the sharpest one is last: keeping the appearance of godliness while having quietly abandoned its power. Paul reaches for two names out of Jewish tradition, Jannes and Jambres, as the pattern for opponents whose folly eventually becomes obvious to everybody. Then he tells Timothy to stay with what he learned, and where he learned it, which was at home from infancy. The famous line about all Scripture being God-breathed arrives here as an argument for staying put.
2TI 4:1-22	Paul's charge to keep preaching is followed by the reason: audiences will start shopping for teachers who tell them what they want, which has aged extremely well. Then he writes his own obituary in three clauses, fought the fight, finished the course, kept the faith, and it lands. The rest is heartbreakingly domestic. Demas left. Only Luke is here. Bring Mark, bring my cloak from Troas, and bring the books. Nobody turned up at his first hearing, and he asks that it not be held against them.
COL 1:1-29	Paul is writing to a church he has never visited, on the strength of a report from Epaphras. The prayer is standard until it turns into the biggest claim in the letter: Christ as the image of the invisible God, the agent of everything visible and invisible, and the thing holding the universe together. The scale then collapses back down to the readers, who used to be enemies. Paul closes on the mystery hidden for ages, which turns out to be four words long.
COL 2:1-23	Something is going around Colossae and Paul is circling it carefully: philosophy, human tradition, dietary rules, festival calendars, angel worship, and a fashionable severity toward the body. His counter is that the whole fullness of deity is already present bodily in Christ, so there is nothing left to add. The best image in the chapter is legal and violent at once: the record of debts against them nailed up on the cross, and the powers stripped and marched through the streets like defeated troops.
COL 3:1-25	Set your mind above, says Paul, and then spends the chapter on entirely earthly things. Two wardrobes: take off anger, slander, lying and greed, put on compassion, kindness, humility and patience, with love as the belt holding it together. In between, the list of categories that no longer exist, which includes Scythian, apparently the outer limit of the ancient insult. Then the household instructions, briskly delivered, ending with the reminder that whoever does wrong gets it back, with no favouritism.
COL 4:1-18	The chapter opens by telling slaveowners they have an owner too. Then Paul asks for prayers, not for release but for a door to keep talking through, which is a specific ask from a man in chains. Speech should be seasoned with salt, which is better advice than most. The rest is the roll call: Tychicus, Onesimus, Aristarchus, Mark, Luke the doctor, and Demas, whose later career the reader may know. Then instructions to swap letters with Laodicea, a nudge for Archippus, and Paul's own handwriting.
DEU 32:1-26	The song opens by subpoenaing the sky. God is the Rock, faultless; Israel is the crooked generation, and the contrast is the entire argument. There is a tender stretch about an eagle stirring up her nest, honey out of rock and oil out of flint, the finest wheat, and then the turn: Jeshurun grew fat and kicked. Prosperity, as ever, was the problem. The reply is fire down to Sheol, arrows, hunger and vipers.
DEU 32:27-52	The only thing staying God's hand is public relations: he will not have the enemy taking credit for it. Then comes the famous line about vengeance, and a flat challenge to the other gods, who ate the fat of the sacrifices and are now invited to earn them. Moses finishes the recital with Joshua beside him and tells Israel this is no vain thing, it is their life. Then he is told to go up Nebo, look, and die there.
DEU 33:1-29	Moses blesses the tribes one by one, and the ratios are telling. Joseph gets a paragraph of dew, deep water, sun, moon and ancient mountains; Dan gets nine words about being a lion's cub. Reuben's blessing amounts to please do not die out. Levi is commended for failing to recognise his own family. It closes on the everlasting arms and Israel called happy, which is the warmest thing Moses has managed in a while.
DEU 34:1-12	He gets the view and not the land. God supplies a panoramic tour of everything his descendants will inherit, then repeats that he is not going over. Moses dies at a hundred and twenty with his eyesight clear and his strength unspent, which somehow makes it worse, and is buried in a grave nobody has ever located. Thirty days of weeping, Joshua steps up, and the obituary says plainly that there has been no one like him since.
EPH 1:1-23	The letter opens with a sentence that refuses to end, piling up chosen, predestined, adopted, redeemed and sealed without pausing for breath, all of it decided before the world existed. There is no complaint in it anywhere, which makes Ephesians unusual in the collection. The second half is a prayer for the readers to have their hearts' eyes opened, and it climbs steadily upward until Christ is seated above every authority that has a name, in this age or the next.
EPH 2:1-22	Paul starts his readers off dead, which is a bracing way to describe your audience, and has God rescue corpses out of sheer mercy. Saved by grace, through faith, not by works, specifically so nobody gets bragging rights, and then immediately: made for good works anyway. The second half addresses the Gentiles about the wall. Whatever hostility the law's regulations maintained between the two groups, Paul says Christ demolished it and built one new person out of the pieces, then used the rubble as a temple.
EPH 3:1-21	Paul mentions in passing that he is writing from custody, then declines to make anything of it beyond asking them not to be discouraged on his behalf. The mystery he was given is remarkably simple for something hidden for ages: the Gentiles are in, on the same terms, in the same body. He calls himself the least of all the saints, which sits oddly beside being God's chosen lecturer to the cosmic powers. Then a prayer about the dimensions of a love he says exceeds knowing.
EPH 4:1-32	A chapter built on the word one, listed seven times in three verses, which is Paul's way of ending an argument before it starts. Church leaders exist to work themselves out of a job, equipping everyone else so the congregation stops behaving like a toddler blown about by whatever teaching came through last. Then the practical list, and it is refreshingly small-scale: stop lying, deal with your anger before bedtime, get a job instead of stealing, and stop saying corrosive things about each other.
EPH 5:1-33	Paul asks for imitation of God and then gets specific about what that rules out, including coarse joking, which is a harder ask than most of the list. Once darkness, now light, so stop keeping company with what you used to do. Do not get drunk, he says, get filled with the Spirit and sing instead. Then the household code, where the instruction to wives is short and the instruction to husbands runs three times longer and ends with a man being asked to die for someone.
EPH 6:1-24	Children, parents, slaves and masters each get a line, and the slaveowners get told to stop threatening people on the grounds that their own Master is watching and shows no favouritism. Then the armour, itemised piece by piece, which sounds martial until you notice the enemy is explicitly not human and the objective is only to remain standing. Every weapon is defensive except the sword, which is a word. Paul closes by asking for prayers that he keep talking, and mentions Tychicus is bringing the news.
GAL 1:1-24	Every other letter Paul writes opens with thanks for the readers. This one opens with astonishment, which is how you know the mood. Anyone preaching a rival gospel is cursed, and he includes hypothetical angels in that, twice, in case once was ambiguous. The rest is a deposition on where his message came from: not from Jerusalem, since he pointedly went to Arabia instead, and did not meet Peter for three years, and then for a fortnight. He swears he is not lying, which suggests someone said he was.
GAL 2:1-21	Paul's account of the Jerusalem meeting is a masterpiece of grudging respect: the men reputed to be pillars, whatever they were, added nothing to him, and shook his hand. Titus stayed uncircumcised, which he reports as a victory. Then the scene at Antioch, where Peter ate happily with Gentiles until people arrived from James and he quietly changed tables, taking Barnabas with him. Paul says he confronted him in front of everyone, and the speech he quotes himself giving runs on until it becomes the letter's argument.
GAL 3:1-29	Paul opens by calling them foolish and asking one question they cannot answer without conceding the case: how did you get the Spirit, by law or by hearing? Abraham is the precedent, since he had faith long before there was law to keep, and the law itself arrived four hundred and thirty years after the promise. His image for it is a childminder, useful and temporary. It ends with the categories dissolving: no Jew, no Greek, no slave, no free, no male or female.
GAL 4:1-31	The argument becomes an inheritance case: a minor heir is legally indistinguishable from a slave until the father's appointed day. Paul is baffled that people freed from that are voluntarily going back to watching the calendar. Then the personal appeal, which is the softest thing in the letter: he was ill when he first preached to them, they treated him like an angel, they would have handed him their own eyes, and now apparently he is the enemy. Then Hagar and Sarah, read as an allegory, which he says outright.
GAL 5:1-26	Paul's argument against circumcision is practical: accept it and you have signed for the entire law, all of it, forever. He notes they were running well and asks who tripped them, then delivers the letter's most unrepeatable wish about what his opponents should do to themselves. Freedom, he adds quickly, is not permission, since the whole law reduces to loving your neighbour, and biting each other tends to end in mutual consumption. Two lists follow, and the second one, the fruit, has no law against it.
GAL 6:1-18	Practical closing instructions, and the tone drops to something almost gentle: restore the person who fell, and watch yourself while you do it. Carry each other's loads, and also carry your own, which he does not attempt to reconcile. Then Paul takes the pen off the scribe and the letters get bigger. His parting shot at the circumcision party is that they want a persecution-free reputation and a tally of Galatian foreskins. He would rather point at his own scars.
HEB 1:1-14	No greeting, no name, no address: Hebrews simply starts talking, and the first sentence is one of the grandest in the collection. God used to speak in instalments through prophets and has now said it all at once in a Son. Then the method that will run the whole book, which is a stack of scripture quotations arranged as an argument, all of them here aimed at proving the Son outranks the angels. The angels come out of it as staff: winds, flames, and errand-runners for the heirs.
HEB 2:1-18	The first of the book's warnings, and its logic is arithmetic: if the law delivered by angels had teeth, ignoring something delivered by the Son is worse. Then a psalm about humanity crowned with glory, which the writer concedes does not describe the world as anyone currently finds it, and applies to Jesus instead. The best line is the reason for the incarnation: he took flesh and blood because the children have it, to break the hold of a devil whose only weapon was fear of dying.
HEB 3:1-19	Moses gets a compliment and a demotion in the same breath: faithful, certainly, but faithful as a servant inside a house that somebody else built. Then the wilderness, quoted at length, where a whole generation heard the good news and refused to believe it and spent forty years annoying God about it. The writer works the passage like a lawyer, asking who rebelled, with whom was God displeased, and who was shut out, and answering his own questions until only one conclusion is left standing.
HEB 4:1-16	The rest, he argues, is still open, and the proof is chronological: David says today long after Joshua had already handed out the land, so whatever Joshua gave them was not it. The Sabbath rest remains, which is a strange thing to have to work for. Then the sword image, which cuts between soul and spirit and joint and marrow, followed immediately by the gentlest turn in the book: a high priest who has been through it, so approach the throne without flinching.
PHM 1:1-25	The shortest letter Paul wrote, and the most tactical. Onesimus, Philemon's runaway slave, has ended up with Paul in prison, and Paul is sending him back with a letter addressed not only to Philemon but to the whole church that meets in his house, which is not an accident. He says he could command and will beg instead, offers to cover any debt in his own handwriting, mentions that Philemon owes him rather more, and asks that a guest room be got ready.
PHP 1:1-30	A letter from prison that spends most of its opening being pleased about things. Paul reports his chains have been good publicity, since the whole palace guard now knows why he is there. Some rivals are preaching Christ specifically to annoy him, and he decides he does not care, which is either magnanimous or the most efficient way to win. Then the genuinely odd dilemma: he would prefer to die and be with Christ, has weighed it, and concludes he will stay because they still need him.
PHP 2:1-30	Paul asks a church with an ego problem to consider everyone else better than themselves, then quotes what is probably a hymn: God's own form, not clutched at, exchanged for a servant's, obedient all the way down to a cross, and then the name every knee bends to. He follows this cosmic descent and ascent with the instruction to stop grumbling. The chapter ends in ordinary logistics: Timothy is coming, and Epaphroditus is being sent home early because he nearly died and the Philippians heard about it.
PHP 3:1-21	Paul lists his pedigree in full, tribe, sect, blamelessness and all, purely so he can throw it away in the next sentence, where he calls it refuse. He is careful to say he has not arrived: no perfection, just forward motion, forgetting what is behind. He weeps over the enemies of the cross, whose god he identifies as the stomach. Then the closing image of an outpost colony: citizenship elsewhere, waiting for a Saviour who will refit these humiliating bodies to match his own.
PHP 4:1-23	Two women in the church are not speaking, and Paul puts their names in a letter that will be read aloud, which is one way to handle it. Then the greatest hits: rejoice always, be anxious about nothing, pray about everything, and think about what is true and lovely instead. The thank-you note is careful almost to the point of comedy: he is delighted with the gift, has learned to be content without it, and reminds them they were the only church that ever sent anything.
PSA 51:1-19	The full-dress repentance psalm. It asks not for a lighter sentence but for laundry: wash me, purify me with hyssop, whiter than snow. He admits the sin was against God alone, a bold framing, then asks outright for a clean heart and for the holy Spirit not to be withdrawn. It closes by noting God does not want sacrifice, only a broken heart, and then requests bulls on the altar once Jerusalem's walls are rebuilt.
PSA 52:1-9	Addressed to one specific mighty man with a razor for a tongue, who loves lying more than truth. The psalmist predicts he will be plucked out of his tent by the roots while the righteous stand around, frightened, then laughing, and narrating the moral aloud about the man who trusted his money. He himself is a green olive tree in God's house, which is the smugger half of the comparison.
PSA 53:1-6	Nearly Psalm 14 again, lightly redeployed. The fool says in his heart there is no God; God leans out of heaven to audit humanity and finds nobody doing good, not one, while the wicked eat his people like bread. One strange line stands out: they were in great fear where no fear was. It ends wishing salvation would come out of Zion.
PSA 54:1-7	Short and businesslike. Strangers and violent men who never set God before them are after him, so he asks for vindication, states flatly that God is his helper, and asks for his enemies to be destroyed. Then he promises a freewill offering and reports, in the past tense, that he has already been delivered and has already watched his enemies go down. The rescue is announced before it arrives.
PSA 55:1-23	A betrayal psalm, and a shaky one: terrors of death, trembling, horror. He wishes for wings like a dove so he could fly off and lodge in the wilderness, because the city has malice prowling its walls day and night. The real wound is specific. Not an enemy, but his own companion, the man he walked into God's house beside. He asks that they go down alive into Sheol. Butter mouth, war heart.
PSA 56:1-13	Enemies want to swallow him whole, twist his words all day, and lurk watching his steps. His refrain is a piece of self-instruction repeated twice: when I am afraid, I will trust, and what can flesh do to me. The one image nobody else has is the accounting: God keeps his wanderings counted and his tears collected in a bottle, entered in a book. He ends paying thank offerings for a deliverance he treats as already filed.
PSA 57:1-11	He is hiding in the shadow of God's wings until the disaster passes, which sounds cosy until you see where he is hiding from: men whose teeth are spears and whose tongues are swords, a soul lying down among lions. They dig a pit and fall into it themselves. Then the tone flips entirely and he starts tuning instruments, announcing that he will wake up the dawn rather than wait for it.
PSA 58:1-11	An indictment of crooked judges, who plot injustice while claiming to speak righteousness and have been going wrong since the womb. They are deaf cobras, immune to the best snake charmer alive. What follows is a curse in careful detail: break their teeth, blunt their arrows, let them melt away like a snail. The verdict is that people will see the righteous wash their feet in blood and conclude God judges the earth after all.
PSA 59:1-17	He insists the ambush is not his fault: no sin of his, just mighty men lying in wait. His enemies are dogs that come back at evening, howl, and prowl the city with swords in their lips, sure that nobody hears them. God, meanwhile, is laughing. Then a strikingly practical request: do not kill them, or my people will forget, just scatter them. The dogs get a second lap in the closing verses, still hungry.
PSA 60:1-12	A national complaint after a defeat. God has rejected them, torn the land, and made them drink wine that leaves them staggering. Then God answers from his sanctuary with a land survey of conquest, and the register descends into insult: Gilead and Manasseh mine, Judah my scepter, Moab my wash basin, and Edom something to throw a shoe at. The psalm still has to ask who will get them into the fortified city.
PSA 61:1-8	A brief, quiet prayer, calling from the end of the earth with an overwhelmed heart. The one request that carries it is oddly precise: lead me to the rock that is higher than I, since the trouble with self-reliance is elevation. God has already been a tower and a tent, and the wings are back. It then turns aside to pray for the king's years to run for generations, and ends promising to pay its vows daily.
PSA 62:1-12	An exercise in waiting in silence, which the psalmist has to tell his own soul to do twice, along with the line about not being shaken. His enemies bless him out loud and curse him inwardly, and mean to shove him over like a leaning wall. The weighing image is the good one: put the low and the mighty on the scales together and they come out lighter than a breath. Riches get the same verdict.
PSA 63:1-11	Thirst as the whole argument. In a dry land with no water, his body wants God the way a body wants a drink, and the satisfaction he expects is described as rich food. He thinks about God in the night watches, on his bed, which is a more domestic setting than most of these get. Then it turns: those hunting him go down into the earth, fall to the sword, and end as jackal food.
PSA 64:1-10	The enemies here are entirely verbal: tongues sharpened like swords, words aimed like arrows, shot at innocent men from ambush. Their real crime is smugness. They lay snares in private, ask who will ever see, and congratulate themselves on a perfect plan. God's counterstrike uses the same weapon, an arrow arriving suddenly, and their own tongues finish the job. The onlookers shake their heads, which is the psalm's idea of a verdict.
PSA 65:1-13	It starts in Zion with vows and answered prayer, and God stilling the roaring of the seas and the turmoil of the nations in the same breath, as though they were the same noise. Then it becomes a farming psalm and stops wanting anything: God visits the earth, drenches the furrows, softens the ground with showers and crowns the year with bounty until the hills wear gladness and the valleys, dressed in grain, sing.
PSA 66:1-20	An invitation psalm: come and see, then come and hear. The sea turned to dry land gets its usual mention, and then the thing is unusually frank about the cost. God refined them like silver, which in practice meant prison, burdens on their backs, men riding over their heads, and a trip through fire and water before anyone reached the place of abundance. The vows made in distress are duly paid, in rams, bulls and goats.
PSA 67:1-7	Seven verses, and two of them are the same line. All it asks for is the blessing and the shining face, and not for its own sake either: the point is that God's way gets known everywhere and the nations sing, since he judges the peoples with equity. The one concrete item in it is the harvest, the earth having yielded its increase, which is quietly the evidence for everything else.
PSA 68:1-35	The processional psalm, and it will not sit still. Enemies driven off like smoke and melted like wax, God as father of the fatherless who sets the lonely in families and walks prisoners out singing, then the wilderness march with Sinai shaking and rain coming down. The strangest picture in the Psalter follows: a dove sheathed in silver and gold while soldiers sleep among the campfires, and snow falling on Zalmon. Chariots by the tens of thousands, a parade with tambourines, and Egypt and Ethiopia stretching out their hands.
PSA 69:1-36	A drowning psalm. The water is at his neck, the mire has no floor, and his throat has given out from crying. The humiliation is social as much as physical: a stranger to his own brothers, discussed by the men at the gate, the subject of drunkards' songs, handed gall to eat and vinegar to drink. Then the curse, which is thorough: darkened eyes, bent backs, emptied tents, and names struck out of the book of life.
PSA 70:1-5	Five verses, and four of them are some form of hurry up. No scene setting, no history, just deliver me quickly and do not delay, bracketing the whole thing. The one detail that gives it a face is the taunt he wants silenced, a pair of enemies standing there going "Aha! Aha!" He signs off calling himself poor and needy, which is the argument as well as the description.
PSA 71:1-24	Old age is the whole subject. He has leaned on God since before he was born and would rather not be dropped now that his strength is going, especially since his enemies have started saying God has already let go and nobody is coming. What he asks for is not comfort but time: long enough to tell the next generation about God's strength. Harp and lyre at the end, from a man who says his flesh has been through bitter troubles.
PSA 72:1-20	A coronation wish list, and unusually it is mostly about the poor: judge them fairly, rescue the needy who have nobody, break the oppressor in pieces. The good king arrives like rain on mown grass, which is a gentler image than kings normally get. Then the map expands, sea to sea, tribute from Tarshish and Sheba and Seba, enemies licking dust. A closing note records that the prayers of David, son of Jesse, stop here.
PSA 73:1-28	The most honest psalm about envy. He nearly slipped, he says, watching the wicked do well: no struggle in their death, eyes bulging with fat, pride worn like a necklace, asking out loud whether the Most High knows anything. He decides he has kept his hands clean for nothing. Then he goes into the sanctuary and sees where they are standing, which is somewhere slippery, and the whole thing looks like a dream someone is about to wake up from.
PSA 74:1-23	Written after the temple has already burned. The enemies are described as woodcutters going at a thicket, breaking the carved work with hatchets and hammers, and the psalm's worst line is not about the fire: no signs, no prophet, and nobody who knows how long. So it reaches further back, to the sea split open and the heads of Leviathan broken and handed out as food. Then it asks God to at least remember he is the one being mocked.
PSA 75:1-10	Thanks first, and then God takes over the microphone for most of it: I pick the time, I hold the pillars of the earth, stop lifting that horn. The geography lesson is nice, promotion does not come from the east, the west or the south, and the missing fourth direction is the point. Then the image nobody else uses: a cup of foaming spiced wine in God's hand, poured out, which the wicked have to finish, dregs included.
PSA 76:1-12	Twelve verses of aftermath. God broke the arrows, the shield and the sword at Salem, and what is left on the field is described as men who have slept their last sleep, plundered, hands unable to lift. Chariot and horse go down into a dead sleep at a single rebuke. The earth's response to judgment from heaven is silence, which is the psalm's best detail. Then a brisk instruction: make your vows, keep them, bring presents.
PSA 77:1-20	Insomnia as theology. His hand is stretched out all night, his eyelids are held open, and he is too troubled to speak, so he lies there asking the questions nobody puts so bluntly: has the loving kindness run out, has God forgotten how to be gracious. His fix is deliberate remembering, and it works: the waters writhing at the sight of God, thunder in the whirlwind, lightning, and a path through the sea whose footprints nobody could find.
PSA 78:1-35	The national history retold as a warning to the children, which means the embarrassing parts stay in. Ephraim turned and ran with bows in hand. The sea was split, rocks poured out rivers, and they still asked whether God could set a table in the wilderness, which he answered with manna, called here the bread of angels, and then quail carried in on the east wind until birds lay thick as sand in the camp. Their food was still in their mouths when the anger arrived.
PSA 78:36-72	Part two, and the grievance is flattery: right words, wrong hearts, and God forgiving anyway because he remembers they are only wind that does not come back. The plagues get a second pass, blood and flies and frogs and hail and frost, and then the settlement, and then the high places and the carved images. Shiloh is abandoned, the glory goes into captivity, and widows cannot even weep. Then God wakes up like a man shouting from wine, picks Judah, and fetches a shepherd out of a sheepfold.
PSA 79:1-13	Written over rubble. The temple is defiled, the city is in heaps, and the detail that stings is administrative: bodies left for birds and animals because there was nobody left to bury them. The complaint is partly about reputation, since the neighbours are now asking where their God is. It asks not to inherit the fathers' debts, asks that the prisoners' sighing be heard, and requests repayment at a precise sevenfold rate.
PSA 80:1-19	Three times it stops and repeats the same refrain: turn us again, shine your face, and we are saved. In between, a diet of tears served by the pint, and neighbours enjoying themselves. The long image is a vine carried out of Egypt and planted with the ground cleared for it, which grew until mountains sat in its shade. Then someone knocked the wall down, and now every passer-by helps himself and a wild boar is in the vineyard.
PSA 81:1-16	It opens like a festival, tambourine and lyre and trumpet at the new moon, and then God interrupts to do the talking. He remembers taking the basket out of their hands and answering from the secret place of thunder. His one instruction is unusually generous: open your mouth wide and I will fill it. They did not, so he let them walk after their own counsel, and the psalm ends on what they missed, the finest wheat and honey out of a rock.
PSA 82:1-8	Eight verses set in a divine courtroom, where God is prosecuting the other gods for judicial misconduct. The charge sheet is entirely social: you show partiality, defend the weak, maintain the rights of the poor, rescue the needy. They understand nothing and stumble about in the dark while the foundations of the earth shake. Then the sentence, which is the whole psalm: you are gods, sons of the Most High, and you will die like men.
PSA 83:1-18	The psalm's opening request is that God stop being quiet. Then a full list of the alliance, ten names deep, from Edom and the Ishmaelites through Amalek and Tyre to Assyria, all agreed that Israel's name should be forgotten. The precedents are named too, Midian and Sisera and Jabin, Oreb and Zeeb and Zebah and Zalmunna, which is history used as a threat. Make them tumbleweed, chase them with a storm.
PSA 84:1-12	Homesickness for a building. He notices, with something close to envy, that the sparrow and the swallow have managed to nest by the altars and he has not. The pilgrimage image is the good one: the valley of Weeping turned into a place of springs by the people walking through it, going from strength to strength. His summary offer is the memorable part, a day in the courts beats a thousand anywhere else, and he would take the doorkeeper's job over a comfortable tent in wickedness.
PSA 85:1-13	It argues from precedent. You forgave before, you turned from your anger before, so do it again, and stop being angry to all generations. The psalmist then does something rare and simply waits to hear what God will say, expecting peace with one condition attached: no going back to folly. The ending is the famous personification, mercy and truth meeting up, righteousness and peace kissing, truth coming up out of the ground.
PSA 86:1-17	A modest prayer that keeps calling itself poor and needy. It reasons out loud, twice, from God's character rather than from need: you are good and ready to forgive, you are slow to anger, therefore listen. Its sharpest request is not rescue but a single mind, an undivided heart. And its closing ask is a little pointed: a visible sign of your goodness, specifically so the people who hate me have to look at it.
PSA 87:1-7	Seven verses, and it is essentially a birth registry.
