NEH 12:31-47	The dedication is staged as a circuit: two enormous choirs put up onto the wall itself and sent round the city in opposite directions, Ezra leading one and Nehemiah walking behind the other, past every gate they had rebuilt. They meet at the temple and the noise carries for miles. Then, characteristically, the chapter ends with storeroom appointments and a note that the tithes were actually paid, which for this book is the happy ending.
NEH 13:1-15	Nehemiah goes back to Persia for a while, and the whole thing comes apart while he is away. He returns to find that the priest in charge of the storerooms has cleared one out and given it to Tobiah, the man who spent six chapters trying to stop the wall. Nehemiah throws the furniture into the street personally. Then he discovers the Levites have not been paid in so long they have gone back to farming. Next time on Nehemiah: the Sabbath market.
NEH 13:16-31	The complaints keep coming. Winepresses working on the Sabbath, Tyrian fishmongers trading in the city, so Nehemiah has the gates locked from dusk and threatens to lay hands on the merchants who camp outside waiting. Then the intermarriage problem, and his response is not a dignified one: he curses them, hits some of them and pulls out their hair, citing Solomon as the precedent. The high priest's grandson, married to Sanballat's daughter, is simply run off. Remember me for good, he says, and the book stops.
OBA 1:1-21	One chapter, one target, no digressions. Edom lives up a cliff and asks who could possibly bring it down, which in this genre is a question that answers itself. Even thieves leave something, the argument runs, and Edom will be left nothing, betrayed by the allies who ate at its table. The actual charge is small and damning: on the day Jerusalem fell, Edom stood on the other side and watched, then joined in. Whatever you did comes back on your own head.
REV 1:1-20	John is on Patmos, which he mentions without complaint, and hears a trumpet voice behind him telling him to write. He turns and gets the full description: robe to the feet, hair white as snow, eyes of fire, feet like brass out of a furnace, a voice like many waters, seven stars in one hand and a sword coming out of the mouth. John responds by falling over like a corpse, which seems reasonable. He is told not to be afraid.
REV 2:1-29	The letters begin, and each opens with I know your works, which is never entirely comforting. Ephesus works hard, sniffs out fake apostles, and has stopped loving anybody. Smyrna gets no rebuke, only a warning about prison. Pergamum lives next to Satan's throne, kept the faith when Antipas was killed, and still has Balaam enthusiasts in the pews. Thyatira is improving, apart from tolerating a self-declared prophetess the letter simply calls Jezebel. Prizes: hidden manna, a white stone, a private name.
REV 3:1-22	Three more assessments, and the compliments run out. Sardis has a reputation for being alive, which the letter dismantles in one clause. Philadelphia has little strength and is handed a door nobody can shut, plus the offer of becoming architecture. Laodicea is the famous one: neither hot nor cold, so it gets spat out. It considers itself rich and self-sufficient, and is told it is blind and naked and should buy some eye salve. Then the knock at the door.
REV 4:1-11	A door opens in heaven and John is invited up to look. Throne, gemstone light, an emerald rainbow, twenty-four elders in white with gold crowns, thunder coming out of the furniture, and a floor of glass like a frozen sea. Four creatures covered in eyes front and back, shaped as lion, calf, man and eagle, six wings each, saying holy holy holy without pause, ever. The elders' one action is to throw their crowns down. Nobody in the room is impressed with themselves.
REV 5:1-14	A scroll with seven seals, and a challenge nobody answers. John, who has just seen the throne room of heaven, bursts into tears because the document cannot be opened. An elder tells him to look at the Lion of Judah, and the thing John turns to see is a Lamb that appears to have been killed, with seven horns and seven eyes. It takes the scroll. The harps come out, and the singing widens until every created thing is doing it.
REV 6:1-17	The seals open and the horses ride: conquest, slaughter, and famine with a set of scales and a voice quoting grain prices while carefully protecting the luxury goods. The fourth rider is simply named Death, with Hades trailing behind him like an assistant. The martyrs under the altar want to know how long, and are handed robes and told to be patient. Then the sky rolls up like a scroll and the powerful of the earth ask the mountains to fall on them.
REV 7:1-17	An interruption before the seventh seal. Four angels hold the winds still so nothing moves, while the servants of God get stamped on the forehead, and the count comes in tribe by tribe at a hundred and forty-four thousand. Then John looks again and sees an uncountable crowd from every nation in white with palm branches, which is a different sort of number entirely. An elder asks who they are, John declines to guess, and is told they came out of the great tribulation.
REV 8:1-13	The seventh seal opens and nothing happens for half an hour, which after six seals of catastrophe is the loudest silence in the book. An angel mixes incense with the prayers of the saints, then loads the same censer with fire and throws it at the earth. Four trumpets follow, each taking exactly a third: earth, sea, rivers and sky. The poisoned star is named Wormwood. An eagle flies overhead announcing that the remaining three trumpets are worse.
REV 9:1-21	A fallen star unlocks the abyss and the smoke turns into locusts, which are described in detail and get stranger with every clause: horse bodies, gold crowns, human faces, women's hair, lion teeth, scorpion tails. They are forbidden to touch grass and permitted to torment people for five months without killing them, in a period when death goes conspicuously missing. Their king has a name in two languages. Then two hundred million cavalry, and the survivors change nothing.
REV 10:1-11	An angel comes down dressed in a cloud with a rainbow on his head, plants one foot in the sea and one on land, and roars. Seven thunders answer intelligibly, and John, reaching for his pen, is told to seal it up and write none of it, which is the one thing in this book kept back from the reader. The angel swears time is up. Then John eats a book, on instructions, and finds it sweet going down and bitter afterwards.
REV 11:1-19	John is handed a measuring reed and told which parts not to measure. Two witnesses in sackcloth spend 1,260 days breathing fire at anyone who bothers them and shutting off the rain, which makes them extremely unpopular. The beast kills them, and the world throws a party and sends each other gifts over the corpses in the street. Three and a half days later they stand up and the mood changes considerably. Seventh trumpet: heaven announces the management has changed.
REV 12:1-17	A pregnant woman dressed in the sun, and a seven-headed red dragon waiting under her to eat the baby the moment it arrives. The child is snatched up to the throne, the woman runs for the desert, and Michael settles the matter upstairs by throwing the dragon out of heaven altogether. Down here he is furious and short of time. He chases her, spits a river after her, and the ground drinks it. So he goes after her other children instead.
REV 13:1-18	A beast walks out of the sea assembled from spare parts, leopard, bear, lion, with seven heads and a fatal wound that healed, which is precisely what the world finds impressive. The dragon hands over his authority and everyone asks who could possibly fight this. Then a second beast, horns like a lamb and a voice like a dragon, does the marketing: fire from the sky, a statue that talks, and a mark without which nobody buys anything. The number, we are told, is a man's. 666.
REV 14:1-20	Relief first: the Lamb on Zion with the sealed crowd singing a song nobody else is able to learn. Then three angels fly past in turn with an eternal announcement, a funeral notice for Babylon, and a very specific warning about the mark. A voice interrupts to bless the dead. Then the harvest imagery, which starts pastoral and does not stay that way: the earth is reaped, the grapes are trodden, and the blood comes out to the height of a horse's bridle.
REV 15:1-8	A short chapter that is mostly staging. Seven angels appear with the last seven plagues, and the people who beat the beast are standing on a sea of glass shot through with fire, playing harps and singing Moses' song about the nations coming to worship, which is a generous thing to sing at that moment. The temple opens, the angels come out dressed for a ceremony, and a living creature hands them the bowls. Then smoke, and nobody may go in.
REV 16:1-21	The bowls empty out in sequence, and the trumpets' careful one third gives way to all of it. Sores, then a sea like a dead man's blood, then rivers, and the angel of the waters pauses to say the punishment fits. The fourth turns the sun up, and people respond by cursing God rather than repenting, which becomes the chapter's refrain. Frogs come out of three mouths to recruit kings for Megiddo. The seventh brings hailstones weighing a talent apiece.
REV 17:1-18	A woman on a scarlet seven-headed beast, dressed in purple, gold and pearls, holding a beautiful cup full of filth, with her own identity written across her forehead in case anyone missed it. John admits he stared. The angel then delivers the most puzzle-like passage in the book: seven heads are mountains and also kings, five down, one standing, the beast is an eighth and also one of the seven. The ending is the twist: the horns turn on the woman and eat her.
REV 18:1-24	Babylon's funeral, sung by the people who did business with her. The kings weep from a safe distance, the sailors throw dust on their heads, and the merchants mourn hardest of all, over an inventory that begins with gold and silk and cinnamon and ends, without changing tone, with bodies and human souls. She had said in her heart that she was a queen and no widow. An angel drops a millstone into the sea to demonstrate the manner of her going.
REV 19:1-21	Heaven celebrates, at length and loudly, and announces a wedding. John, overwhelmed, tries to worship the angel and is told off for it briskly. Then the sky opens: a rider named Faithful and True, eyes on fire, wearing many crowns and a name he alone knows, robe already bloodstained before the battle, followed by cavalry in white linen. An angel calls the birds to dinner in advance. The fight itself takes two verses. The sword is his mouth.
REV 20:1-15	An angel arrives with a key and a chain and files the dragon away for a thousand years, with the odd detail that he must afterwards be released for a while. The martyrs reign in the interval. Then Satan is let out, does exactly what he was locked up for, gathers Gog and Magog, and is finished off by fire without a battle. The great white throne follows: books opened, the sea handing back its dead, and Death itself thrown into the lake of fire.
REV 21:1-27	Everything is replaced, including the sea, and the city comes down dressed as a bride. The promise is domestic rather than cosmic: God moving in, and tears wiped off faces. Then the angel produces a golden measuring reed and the vision turns into a surveyor's report, a cube twelve thousand stadia on a side, jasper walls, twelve foundations in twelve named stones, gates cut from single pearls. No temple, and no sun, both for the same reason.
REV 22:1-21	The book ends where the story started, with a tree and a river, except the tree is now public and its leaves are medicine for whole nations. John, having learned nothing from chapter nineteen, tries to worship the angel again and is corrected again. Daniel was told to seal his book; John is told not to, because the time is at hand. Then come quickly three times, an invitation to anyone thirsty, and a stern note against editing.
ZEC 1:1-21	It opens with a warning not to repeat the family history, followed by a pointed question: your fathers, where are they, and the prophets, do they live forever. Then the visions start. A man on a red horse standing in a ravine among myrtle trees, with more horses behind him, all reporting that the world is calm, which is precisely the problem for a ruined city. An angel asks how long is seventy years supposed to be. Four horns follow, and four craftsmen arriving to knock them off.
ZEC 2:1-13	A surveyor sets out to measure Jerusalem, and an angel is dispatched at a run to tell him not to bother. The city is going to outgrow walls entirely, so God offers to be the wall himself, made of fire, and the glory inside it as well. Then a call to everyone still comfortable in Babylon to get out. The phrase that lands is that whoever touches them is touching the apple of his eye. It ends by telling all flesh to be quiet, because God has just got up.
ZEC 3:1-10	A courtroom scene, and the defendant is the high priest, standing there in filthy clothes with an accuser at his elbow. The verdict skips the argument entirely: the accuser is rebuked and the clothes are simply taken away and replaced. Zechariah, who has been silent all book, interrupts to request a clean turban as well, and gets it. Then a stone with seven eyes on it, a servant called the Branch, and the promise that a land's guilt can be removed in a single day.
ZEC 4:1-14	The angel has to wake him for this one. A gold lampstand, seven lamps, seven pipes each, two olive trees flanking it, and twice the angel asks whether he knows what it means and twice he admits he does not, which is refreshingly honest for a visionary. The message inside it is the famous one, not by might nor by power but by my Spirit, along with a line for anyone unimpressed by a small rebuilding project: who despises the day of small things.
ZEC 5:1-11	Two of the strangest visions in the book, back to back. First a scroll the size of a room flying over the country, itemised as a curse on thieves down one side and perjurers down the other, which moves into the guilty man's house and stays there until the house is gone. Then a grain basket with a woman called Wickedness inside it, shoved back down and capped with a lead weight, and flown off to Shinar by two women with stork wings.
ZEC 6:1-15	Four chariots come out between two bronze mountains, with colour-coded horses, and are identified as the four winds sent to patrol the world. The ones heading north are reported to have settled God's spirit up there, which is a very restrained way to describe whatever they did. Then the vision stops and the practical instruction arrives: take the gold that three returning exiles brought back, make crowns, and put them on the high priest's head, with a speech about the Branch who will build the temple.
ZEC 7:1-14	A delegation arrives with a simple administrative question: are we still doing the fifth-month fast? The answer never addresses it. In seventy years of fasting, God asks, was any of that for me, and when you eat, is that not also for yourselves. Then the actual requirement, quoted from the old prophets: true judgement, kindness, and leaving the widow, the orphan, the foreigner and the poor alone. Their fathers stopped their ears, so when they called, nobody answered. The question about the fast is not returned to.
ZEC 8:1-23	The vision of restored Jerusalem is refreshingly small in scale. Not thrones or armies, but old men and old women sitting out in the street with walking sticks, and children playing in it. God notes that if the remnant find this marvellous, he does not. The reversal is stated flatly: I meant to do harm and did not relent, and now I mean to do good. The four fasts become feasts, and the chapter ends with ten foreigners grabbing one Jew's coat to be taken along.
ZEC 9:1-17	The oracle works down the coast. Tyre, praised for its cleverness, piled up silver like dust and gold like street mud, and burns anyway. The Philistine cities lose their kings, and Ekron is quietly annexed. Then the tone changes completely for the arrival of a king who is deliberately unimpressive: righteous, bringing salvation, and riding a donkey, with the chariots and the battle bow abolished rather than deployed. The prisoners come up out of a pit with no water in it and are promised double.
ZEC 10:1-12	The advice is to ask God for rain rather than the household idols, whose speciality is comforting lies, which has left everyone drifting about like sheep with nobody in charge. God's complaint is aimed squarely at the shepherds and the male goats. Judah is then promoted from flock to warhorse, and supplies its own cornerstone, tent peg and battle bow. The gathering runs from Egypt to Assyria and ends with the pleasant problem of a country too small for the number of people coming home.
ZEC 11:1-17	This is the bleakest chapter in the book, and it is acted out rather than seen. Zechariah is told to shepherd a flock already sold for slaughter, whose traders thank God for the margins. He carries two staffs named Favor and Union, disposes of three shepherds in a month, then resigns. Asked for his wages, he is weighed thirty pieces of silver, which God describes as a handsome price, in the tone of someone who means the opposite, and it gets thrown to the potter.
ZEC 12:1-14	Jerusalem becomes two things at once: a cup that makes everyone around it stagger, and a stone so heavy that whoever tries to lift it does himself an injury. Horses are struck with terror, riders with madness, and the weakest man in the city is rated as good as David. Then the chapter drops abruptly into mourning, family by family, wives kept separately, over the one they pierced. The public triumph and the private grief are placed side by side without explanation.
ZEC 13:1-9	Idols are abolished, and so, remarkably, is prophecy: the profession becomes so disgraced that a prophet's own parents threaten him and he denies ever having been one, claiming to be a farmer and blaming his scars on friends. Read that alongside the hairy mantle he is no longer willing to wear and it is a whole small comedy of embarrassment. Then the sword against the shepherd, the sheep scattering, and two thirds of the land cut off while the last third goes into the fire to be refined.
ZEC 14:1-21	The final vision goes for maximum scale. All nations besiege Jerusalem, the city falls, and then God arrives and stands on the Mount of Olives, which obligingly splits in half and opens an escape route. The day is described as neither day nor night, with the light arriving in the evening. The plague on the attackers is genuinely horrible, and the ending is oddly domestic: cooking pots and horse bells all stamped holy, and no trader left in the temple.
ZEP 1:1-18	Zephaniah opens at maximum volume and does not come down: everything swept off the earth, people and cattle and birds and even the fish, which is a detail nobody asks for. The day of Yahweh is described as a sacrifice with the guests already invited, which is one way to phrase it. The best image is God going through Jerusalem with lamps, looking for the men settled comfortably on their dregs who have concluded he does nothing either way. Their silver will not help.
ZEP 2:1-15	A brief window is offered first: seek righteousness and humility, and you may be hidden. Then the tour. The Philistine coast becomes sheep pasture, Moab and Ammon go the way of Sodom and Gomorrah for the crime of insulting the neighbours, and Assyria is left dry. The finish is the pleasure of the chapter: Nineveh, the city that said I am, and there is no one besides me, ends up with birds calling through its windows and passers-by shaking their fists at the ruins.
ZEP 3:1-20	The city's leadership is described in animals: princes as roaring lions, judges as evening wolves who leave nothing for the morning, and prophets and priests who have simply given up pretending. God notes that his own justice turns up every single morning and nobody is embarrassed by the contrast. Then everything turns. The proud are removed and what is left is a poor, afflicted people who tell no lies. The last image is unexpected: God rejoicing over them with singing.
3MA 5:1-28	Hermon, master of the elephants, is ordered to drug five hundred with neat wine and frankincense and turn them loose on the Jews at dawn. The king goes off to dinner with the people who hate Jews most. Hermon does his job. The king is given sleep and does not surface until mid afternoon, demands to know why the Jews live, blames Hermon, and reschedules. The crowd gathers again before dawn, and the king arrives having entirely forgotten what the elephants are for. Next time on 3 Maccabees: Hermon explains.
3MA 5:29-51	Hermon reports the elephants ready, and the king, his memory rearranged, turns on him and says his own parents and children would have made better food for the beasts than these loyal Jews. Everyone leaves quietly. At the next banquet he orders it again, and his relatives ask how long he intends this, since the city is close to riot. He swears to go through with it, and to burn Judea too. At dawn the elephants go out, and the Jews say goodbye to each other.
3MA 6:1-20	Eleazar, an old priest, quiets the elders and prays a list of precedents: Pharaoh drowned, Sennacherib demolished, the three friends in the furnace, Daniel from the lions, Jonah from the sea monster. His one condition is striking: if we must die, kill us yourself, but do not let the heathen say their god could not deliver them. He finishes exactly as the king arrives with the elephants, whereupon two terrifying angels come down, visible to everybody except the Jews. Next time on 3 Maccabees: the elephants turn around.
3MA 6:21-41	The elephants turn around and trample the army behind them. The king bursts into tears, rounds on his own friends, accuses them of governing badly and of plotting against his throne, and orders the prisoners released as sons of the living God. He pays for a seven day feast on the very ground meant to be their grave, turned into banqueting booths with dancing and psalms. The men who had cheerfully registered them for death go home in disgrace. The days become an annual commemoration.
3MA 7:1-23	The king writes to his commanders: certain friends had urged him to destroy the Jews, those friends were the problem all along, and he himself never really meant it. The God of heaven, he notes, shields them as a father shields his sons, and anyone troubling them answers to him, not the palace. Before leaving, the Jews ask permission to deal with their own who had abandoned the law, and kill three hundred. Then a seven day feast at Ptolemais, and home with their goods returned.
4MA 1:1-35	The author opens by announcing his thesis, which is that religious reasoning is the absolute master of the passions, and promises to prove it from the deaths of Eleazar, seven brothers and their mother. First, definitions. Reasoning, wisdom, prudence, justice, manliness, temperance. Pleasure and pain are the two roots: lust comes before pleasure and joy after, fear before pain and sorrow after. Reasoning is a gardener who prunes rather than uproots. His first proof is that a hungry man can look at forbidden food and decline it.
4MA 2:1-24	The worked examples. Joseph, young and available, talked himself out of it. A miser under the law lends without interest and writes off debts at the Sabbath. A grasping farmer leaves his gleanings anyway. Law overrules love of parents, spouse and friends when they go wrong, and even hatred, since you may not cut down an enemy's crops. Moses was angry with Dathan and Abiram and did nothing about it. Jacob cursed the anger of Simeon and Levi, proving anger can be governed.
4MA 3:1-21	The clarification: reasoning does not uproot the passions, it opposes them. The illustration is David, who after a day of fighting developed an irrational craving for water from the enemy's camp. Two of his men broke through the lines and fetched a pitcher of it. David, parched, reckoned that a drink worth a man's blood was too dangerous to his soul, and poured it out to God. Then the author turns to history: peace, royal funding, and then some men with new ideas.
2ES 1:1-20	Sixteen generations of genealogy before anyone says anything, which establishes that Ezra is a priest and that the author has a list habit. Then God gives him the assignment: go tell the people what they have done, and their children too. What follows is a grievance ledger, sea parted, Moses supplied, pillar of fire provided, quails delivered, manna served, rock split for water, each item paired with the murmuring it received. Next time on 2 Esdras: the patience runs out.
2ES 1:21-40	The ledger closes and the verdict arrives. God remembers sweetening the bitter river and clearing out the Canaanites, asks what else could possibly be done, and then withdraws: no more mercy, no more listening, and the feasts, new moons and offerings are all declined. The prophets they killed are itemised. The house goes to a people who have seen no signs and believe anyway, arriving from the east under Abraham, Isaac, Jacob and the entire roster of minor prophets.
2ES 2:1-25	The nation is now a widowed mother telling her children to leave and go ask mercy elsewhere, which is bleaker than any threat so far. Assur gets a woe and a reminder of what happened at Sodom. The kingdom meant for Israel is reassigned, complete with everlasting tents and the tree of life. The mother is consoled with twelve fruit trees, springs of milk and honey and seven mountains of roses. Next time on 2 Esdras: a crowd on Zion, and a very tall young man.
2ES 2:26-48	The consolation runs on, promising the mother her sleeping children back out of the earth. Ezra mentions, almost in passing, that he was commissioned at Horeb and that Israel would not have him, so he addresses the nations instead and tells them to watch for their shepherd. Then the register changes entirely: a multitude on Mount Zion too large to count, a young man taller than all of them setting crowns on heads, and an angel identifying him as the Son of God.
2ES 3:1-36	Thirty years after the city fell, Ezra cannot sleep, because Zion is rubble and Babylon is thriving. He answers this by taking God through the entire record: Adam given one rule and breaking it, the wicked heart passed down intact, the flood, Noah, Abraham, Sinai delivered through gates of fire and earthquake, David, and the city eventually handed over. Then the question the rest of the book exists to answer, whether Babylon is any better, with a request to weigh both on scales.
2ES 4:1-25	Uriel arrives and immediately puts Ezra on the back foot with three assignments: weigh me some fire, measure me some wind, fetch back yesterday. Ezra concedes the point. The angel presses it with a fable in which the forest declares war on the sea and the waves plan to annex the plain, both campaigns ending badly, and asks Ezra to judge. He judges correctly, then is told to apply the same verdict to himself. Next time on 2 Esdras: the harvest, and how long it takes.
2ES 4:26-52	The world is in a hurry to end, Uriel says: one grain of evil seed went into Adam and the crop is still coming up. Nothing can be reaped early. The righteous dead ask the same impatient question and get the same answer from Jeremiel: the count must be filled first. Then two demonstrations, an oven that leaves smoke and a storm that leaves drops, meaning most of history is behind us. Ezra asks if he will live to see it. Uriel does not know.
2ES 5:1-31	The signs, increasingly strange: faith gone from the land, the sun out at night, blood from wood, stones talking, the Dead Sea throwing up fish, monstrous births, salt water in the sweet, friends turning on each other. Ezra wakes shaking. Phaltiel turns up wanting to know where his leader has gone, and is told to come back in a week. Ezra lists what God chose, one vine, one lily, one dove, one people, and asks why it was handed over. Next time on 2 Esdras: an answer, of a kind.
2ES 5:32-56	The angel opens with the question that ends most such arguments: do you love this people more than their maker? Ezra says he is only grieving, and rather wishes he had not been born. He then gets a second round of impossible errands: count the unborn, gather scattered drops, show a voice's image. Judgment is a ring, with no first or last. Births come one at a time for a reason, and the world is now elderly, which is why everyone is shorter than their ancestors.
2ES 6:1-30	The reply starts as far back as it is possible to start, before the winds, the thunder, paradise, the earthquake and the angels, all made by God alone and to be ended by him alone. The hinge of the ages sits between a hand and a heel. Then a voice like many waters reads out the signs: books opened in the sky, year-old infants talking, fields found unsown, storehouses found empty, springs stopping for three hours. Next time on 2 Esdras: another fast, and the whole creation recited back.
2ES 6:31-59	Another week of fasting, with a compliment about his chastity thrown in, and Ezra returns to the argument by going all the way back to day one. He recites the creation week in order, including the detail that the sea got one seventh of the earth, and that Behemoth was housed on the dry land while Leviathan took the wet part, both kept for eating later. Then the point of the recitation: if the world was made for us, why do the nations reckoned as spittle run it?
2ES 7:1-37	Two thought experiments: a huge sea reached only through one narrow inlet, and a city of good things whose only approach is a single-file path with fire on one side and deep water on the other. Israel's inheritance, says the angel, works the same way, and it is Adam's fault. Ezra questions it and is reminded of his rank. Then the timetable: the hidden city appears, four hundred years of rejoicing, everyone dies, seven days of silence, and the resurrection. Next time on 2 Esdras: what that day looks like.
2ES 7:38-73	The day of judgment has no sun, no moon, no weather at all, just the glory of the Most High, and runs about a week of years. Ezra points out that on these terms almost nobody qualifies. The angel replies with a jeweller's argument: nobody pads rare stones with lead, and clay is commoner than gold. He will rejoice over the few and not mourn the rest. Ezra takes that badly and envies cattle, who face no judgment. Next time on 2 Esdras: what happens the moment you die.
2ES 7:74-102	Ezra asks the practical question, whether the dead wait quietly or start suffering immediately, and gets a very orderly answer: seven ways the scornful spirits grieve, culminating in shame before the glory they offended, and seven orders of rest for the just, ending with faces shining like stars and an eagerness to see the one they served. Everyone gets seven days to look before being filed into their dwellings. Ezra, undeterred, asks whether the righteous can put in a word for the rest. Next time on 2 Esdras: no.
2ES 7:103-140	Nobody intercedes on that day, says the angel, because nobody can be sick or eat on another's behalf either. Ezra produces his precedents, Abraham over Sodom, Moses, Joshua, Samuel, David, Solomon, Elijah, Hezekiah, and is told those all belong to an age that has not ended yet. He replies that Adam should never have been made, or should have been stopped. The angel calls it the terms of the fight. Ezra then talks himself round, listing every merciful title God holds, since otherwise almost nobody would be left.
2ES 8:1-31	The angel opens with a comparison from mining: the earth gives up a great deal of clay and very little of the dust that becomes gold, and people are the same. Ezra counters with the work God puts into one person, nine months in the womb, milk on schedule, an upbringing under the law, and asks the point of it if it ends in swift destruction. Then he prays, at length, for God to look at the faithful and skip past everyone else.
2ES 8:32-63	Mercy only counts, Ezra argues, when it lands on people with nothing to show for themselves, since the righteous are already paid. The angel concedes some of it and reaches for a farming analogy: not everything sown comes up. Ezra objects that seed fails when the rain does, hardly the seed's fault, and that this seed is made in God's image. He is told, kindly, that he does not love creation more than its creator does, and should stop asking about the lost.
2ES 9:1-24	Watch the signs and do your own arithmetic, says the angel: earthquakes, peoples in uproar, nations scheming, rulers wobbling. He then redirects the whole conversation, telling Ezra to stop asking how the wicked will be punished and start asking how the righteous are saved. Ezra will not let it go, and says the lost outnumber the saved as a wave outnumbers a drop. The reply is that God kept one grape from the cluster and one plant from the forest. Next time on 2 Esdras: a week of eating flowers.
2ES 9:25-47	Ezra goes to a field called Ardat and lives on flowers for a week, which he reports as satisfying. Then he takes up the argument again: God sowed the law and the law is fine, it is the ground that failed. Normally the container outlasts the contents; here it is the other way round. Mid-thought he notices a woman on his right in torn clothes and ashes, crying. She tells him: thirty years barren, thirty years of prayer, one son at last, raised, married with a feast.
2ES 10:1-27	The woman finishes her story: the son died on his wedding night, and she has come out here to starve herself to death in peace. Ezra's response is not conventional comfort. He tells her she is grieving one boy while Zion has lost everything, itemises the ruin, and tells her to go home to her husband. She refuses. Then her face turns to lightning, she screams loudly enough to shake the ground, and she is gone. Next time on 2 Esdras: where she went.
2ES 10:28-59	Ezra, flat on his back and shouting for Uriel, is picked up by the hand and told to stand like a man. The explanation follows: the woman was Zion. Thirty barren years stand for three thousand years without an offering, the son is the city Solomon built, and a death on the wedding night is the fall of Jerusalem. The city he now sees is her glory, and he was sent to an empty field because God's city cannot be shown where a man has already built something.
2ES 11:1-24	The second night brings a dream: an eagle out of the sea with twelve feathered wings and three heads, spreading over the earth while every wind blows against her and nothing dares object. A voice, from the middle of her body rather than any head, sets out the rota. The wings are to rule in shifts, the heads held back for last. So they do, one at a time, each vanishing on cue, some barely getting a turn. Next time on 2 Esdras: the heads wake up.
2ES 11:25-46	The remaining small wings make their attempt and last no time at all. Then the middle head, the largest of the three, wakes up, recruits the other two, eats the two ambitious wings, and runs the world with more oppression than any wing managed, before disappearing as abruptly as the rest. The right head eats the left. Then a lion walks out of the wood, speaks in a human voice, informs the eagle it is the fourth beast and a bad judge, and orders it to stop existing.
2ES 12:1-27	The last head goes, the two remaining wings get a brief chaotic turn, and the whole eagle burns; Ezra wakes shattered and asks for a translation. It is the fourth kingdom from Daniel's vision, he is told, and helpfully, Daniel never got this much detail. Twelve wings are twelve kings, the second ruling longest, the eight small wings are eight brief reigns, and the three heads are three kingdoms that will out-oppress everything before them. Next time on 2 Esdras: who the lion was.
2ES 12:28-51	The remaining heads die by the sword, and the two surviving wings are a small, troubled kingdom held back for the end. The lion, it turns out, is the anointed one from David's line, who will read the last kingdom its own charge sheet before destroying it and dealing mercifully with everyone else. Ezra is told to write it down, hide the book, and only show the wise. Then the people arrive to ask why their last prophet has moved to a field. He sends them home.
2ES 13:1-27	Third dream, and the register goes up again. A wind churns the sea, something in the shape of a man rises out of it and flies on the clouds, and everything shakes when he looks at it. A countless army musters from the four winds to fight him. He carves himself a mountain, flies onto it, and carries no weapon of any kind, then annihilates the entire force with fire and breath and sparks out of his own mouth, leaving ash. Next time on 2 Esdras: who he is.
2ES 13:28-58	The explanation: nations will turn on each other, and then the Son appears, standing on Zion, which arrives ready-built like the mountain cut by no hand. He destroys the assembled nations with the law, likened here to fire, and without lifting a weapon. The peaceful crowd is the ten tribes, who after Shalmaneser deported them walked a year and a half into an empty country called Arzareth to keep the law properly, and are coming back across a river God stops for them twice. Three more days to wait.
2ES 14:1-23	Day three, and a voice comes out of a bush, a repeat performance, the last one having been for Moses. Same instruction too: publish some of it, hide the rest. Ezra learns he is being taken out of circulation to stay with the Son, and that the world is now ten and a half twelfths used up. He raises the practical objection nobody else has: the law was burned, so who will teach anyone anything. Next time on 2 Esdras: five scribes and a fiery drink.
2ES 14:24-48	The solution is administrative. Five fast writers, Sarea, Dabria, Selemia, Ethanus and Asiel, a stack of tablets, and a forty-day embargo on visitors. Ezra addresses the people first, summarising their history as a gift given and then withdrawn, then goes to the field and is handed a cup of something that looks like water and is the colour of fire. He drinks, his mouth opens and does not shut for forty days, and ninety-four books come out of it. Twenty-four go public, seventy to the wise.
2ES 15:1-32	The tone changes and stays changed. Ezra is told to write prophecy down and not worry what unbelievers think, since they will die unbelieving. Then the catalogue: sword, famine, death, Egypt due for another round of plagues, crops ruined by blasting and hail and a terrible star, neighbours robbing each other over bread, and kings summoned to turn on one another. It closes with something out of the east, the dragons of Arabia with chariots, hissing loud enough to be heard everywhere. Next time on 2 Esdras: Babylon's turn.
2ES 15:33-63	Storm fronts arrive from all four directions and collide, raining down a destroying star, fire, hail, flying swords and enough water to fill every plain, with blood reaching the horses' bellies. The armies grind on to Babylon and finish her. Then Asia is addressed directly for having copied Babylon and dressed her daughters accordingly, and the sentence is unsparing: widowhood, famine, sword, children dying of hunger, and people in the hills eating their own flesh. No comedy is available here.
2ES 16:1-39	Babylon, Asia, Egypt and Syria are each handed a woe and told to get into sackcloth. Then a run of rhetorical questions with the same answer: nobody turns back a sword, quenches a fire in stubble, or calls home an arrow already loosed. Famine and war follow, corpses go out like dung, and the fruit ripens with nobody left to pick it. Ten survivors from a city, two from a field, like olives missed on a tree. Next time on 2 Esdras: how to live meanwhile.
2ES 16:40-78	The practical advice is bleak: sell as though running, buy as though about to lose it, build a house you will not live in, sow a field you will not reap, marry expecting no children. Strangers get the harvest either way. The more a city decorates itself, the worse its account. Then a reminder of who is keeping that account, the one who named the stars and hung the earth on the waters, and knows what is in a head. The elect are tried like gold and delivered.
4MA 4:1-26	An informant with a grudge cannot beat the high priest in an argument, so he tries treason instead, reporting to Syria that Jerusalem is sitting on the king's money. Apollonius arrives to collect and is met by cavalry angels, after which he lies on the ground begging the people he came to rob to pray for him. They do. Then Seleucus dies, Antiochus Epiphanes arrives, the high priesthood goes to the highest bidder, and a gymnasium goes up on the citadel.
4MA 5:1-38	The king holds court and makes his case to Eleazar: the animal in question is one of nature's better gifts, and no reasonable deity would hold a forced meal against anyone. Eleazar, a lawyer by profession, answers like one. A small breach of the law slights it exactly as much as a large one. He adds that his philosophy trains temperance, courage, justice and piety, then invites the king to prepare the wheels and build the fire hotter.
4MA 6:1-35	They scourge Eleazar and he takes it, the text says, as one tortured in a dream. Some of the guards, moved by his age, propose a workaround: he can eat meat prepared by his own people and simply say it was pork. He turns that down harder than the beating, on the grounds that the young would learn the wrong lesson from an old man's convenient fiction. He dies in the fire asking that his blood count for his people.
4MA 7:1-25	A whole chapter of eulogy, most of it nautical. Eleazar's reasoning is a pilot holding the rudder through the breakers of torture until it docks in the harbour of victory. He is also a besieged city, a jutting promontory, and a second Aaron walking into fire with a censer. Someone raises the obvious objection, that plenty of people never master their passions, and the author replies that those people have simply not thought hard enough. Then he points at the boys.
4MA 8:1-27	Antiochus tries a different approach on the next group: seven brothers and their mother, whom he compliments on their looks before offering government posts and the Greek lifestyle. Then he has the hardware wheeled in, wheels, racks, hooks, cauldrons and iron hands, as a visual aid. The author writes out in full the sensible, cowardly speech the brothers might have given, excuses included, and then reports that not one of them thought it. They answer together.
4MA 9:1-32	The brothers open by telling the king not to pity them more than they pity themselves, and to note that Eleazar's death taught him nothing. He takes the point badly. The eldest is scourged and broken on the wheel, refuses the standard offer of release in exchange for one mouthful, tells his brothers to hold their station, and dies. The second, torn with iron hands, informs Antiochus that the man losing this contest is the one giving the orders.
4MA 10:1-21	The third brother's argument is one of family arithmetic: same father, same mother, same upbringing, therefore the same answer. He adds that the machinery can reach his body and nothing else, and is proved right. The fourth says no fire is hot enough to make him a coward, so Antiochus, running out of arguments, has his tongue cut out. The reply, delivered before the fact, is that God hears the silent, and that reasoning is not kept in the tongue.
4MA 11:1-27	The fifth does not wait to be dragged, walking up himself so the tyrant will owe heaven for one more crime. He asks what the charge actually is, since worshipping the Maker of all things is not usually an offence, and is broken on the catapult. The sixth, barely more than a child, points out that being youngest changes nothing about how he was raised. Dying, he tallies the score: six boys, one defeated tyranny, cold fire.
4MA 12:1-20	The last and youngest is brought out in chains, and Antiochus, who has watched six of these, tries the offer one more time: friendship, a post in government. He also sends for the mother, on the theory that grief will make her useful. It does not. The boy asks to be released so he can address the court, which the delighted guards allow, uses the freedom to tell the king exactly what is coming to him, and jumps into the pans himself.
4MA 13:1-27	Argument time. Seven boys did not break, therefore right reasoning rules the passions, and the author supplies a harbour metaphor in which their shared resolve is a seven-towered breakwater. He quotes the encouragement they passed along the line, complete with references to the furnace in Assyria and to Isaac. Then a digression on why brothers love each other, traced through shared womb, shared milk and shared schooling, which is offered as the measure of what watching cost them.
4MA 14:1-20	Having finished with the brothers, the author notes that they beat not only pain but their own fondness for each other, and that there were seven of them, like the days of creation, which he considers significant. Then the camera turns to the mother, who watched all seven. To measure what that took, he goes through the animal kingdom: birds defending nests on roofs and cliffs, circling helplessly when they cannot, bees stinging intruders to death. She did not flinch.
4MA 15:1-32	The tyrant offered the mother her sons' lives on one condition, and she declined. The author lays out how heavily nature was weighted against that choice, seven labours, seven children raised well, and then reports flatly what she watched, one son after another burned and torn apart, without weeping or calling any of them back. He stages her decision as a council chamber where nature and religion each hold a vote, and ends by comparing her to the ark riding out the flood.
4MA 16:1-25	The thesis, restated with the woman as proof: if she endured this, reasoning rules the passions. As with the brothers, the author drafts the speech she did not make, seven childbirths wasted, no grandchildren, nobody to bury her, and then reports she said nothing of the kind. What she actually said, in Hebrew and while Eleazar was still being tortured, was that an old man was managing it and they were younger, followed by Abraham, Daniel and the furnace.
4MA 17:1-24	The guards' own report is that the mother, about to be seized, went into the fire herself rather than be handled. The author calls her a house standing on the pillars of her children, sets her in the sky with them, and drafts the inscription he would put on the tomb: one aged priest, one aged woman, seven sons, one tyrant. He frames the whole affair as an athletic contest with the world watching, and observes that Antiochus later recommended their endurance to his troops.
4MA 18:1-24	The closing address: keep the law, because devout reason rules the passions, and look what it bought, peace restored and Antiochus off to fight the Persians instead. Then the mother speaks, first on her own blameless marriage and then on what her late husband used to read the boys, which turns out to be a full reading list, Cain and Abel, Isaac, Joseph, Phinehas, Daniel, the furnace, Isaiah, David, Solomon, Ezekiel, Moses. The book signs off with a doxology.
